## The Social Contract

*Or, Principles of Political Right*

*Fœderis æquas / Dicamus leges.*
"Let us set down fair terms for our compact." (Vergil, *Aeneid* XI)

### Foreword

This short treatise is part of a longer work that I began many years ago, before I understood the limits of my abilities, and that I abandoned long since. Of the various fragments that might have been salvaged from what I wrote, this is the most substantial and, I believe, the least unworthy of being offered to the public. The rest no longer exists.

## Book I

My aim is to ask whether, in civil society, there can be any sure and legitimate rule of government, taking men as they are and laws as they might be. Throughout this inquiry I will try always to join what right permits with what interest demands, so that justice and utility are never set against each other.

I begin without first proving the importance of my subject. I will be asked whether I am a prince or a legislator, that I should write about politics. I answer that I am neither, and that is exactly why I write. If I were a prince or a legislator, I would not waste my time saying what ought to be done. I would do it, or else keep silent.

I was born a citizen of a free state and a member of its sovereign body. However slight the influence my voice may have on public affairs, my right to vote on them makes it my duty to study them. And whenever I reflect on systems of government, I am glad to find that my inquiries always give me fresh reasons to love the government of my own country.

### Chapter I: Subject of the First Book

Man is born free, and everywhere he is in chains. One man believes himself the master of others, yet he remains more of a slave than they are. How did this change come about? I do not know. What can make it legitimate? That question I believe I can answer.

If I considered only force and what follows from it, I would say: "So long as a people is compelled to obey, and does obey, it does well. As soon as it can throw off the yoke, and does throw it off, it does even better. In recovering its liberty by the same right that took it away, either the people is justified in taking it back, or those who took it had no justification to begin with." But the social order is a sacred right that forms the basis of all other rights. This right, however, does not come from nature. It must therefore rest on conventions, that is, on agreements. Before turning to those agreements, I must prove what I have just asserted.

### Chapter II: The First Societies

The oldest of all societies, and the only natural one, is the family. Even here, children stay bound to their father only as long as they need him to survive. Once that need ends, the natural bond dissolves. The children, freed from the obedience they owed their father, and the father, freed from the care he owed his children, return alike to independence. If they remain together, they do so no longer by nature but by choice, and the family itself is then held together only by convention.

This shared liberty follows from human nature. Man's first law is to provide for his own preservation, and his first concerns are those he owes to himself. As soon as he reaches the age of reason, he alone judges how best to preserve himself, and he therefore becomes his own master.

The family, then, may be called the first model of political societies. The ruler corresponds to the father and the people to the children, and all of them, having been born free and equal, give up their liberty only for their own benefit. The only difference is this: in the family, the father's love for his children repays him for the care he gives them, whereas in the state, the pleasure of commanding takes the place of a love that the ruler cannot feel for the peoples beneath him.

Grotius denies that all human power is established for the benefit of the governed, and he cites slavery as an example. His habitual method is to derive right from fact. As the Marquis d'Argenson observes in his *Essay on the Interests of France in Relation to Its Neighbors*, "Learned inquiries into public right are often nothing more than the history of past abuses, and taking the trouble to study them too deeply is a fruitless obsession." This is exactly what Grotius has done. A more logical method could certainly be used, but none could be more favorable to tyrants.

According to Grotius, then, it remains uncertain whether the human race belongs to a hundred men, or those hundred men to the human race. Throughout his book he seems to lean toward the first alternative, which is also the view of Hobbes. On this account, humanity is divided into so many herds of cattle, each with a ruler who guards it only in order to devour it.

Just as a shepherd is of a higher nature than his flock, so the shepherds of men, their rulers, would be of a higher nature than the peoples they govern. According to Philo, this is how the Emperor Caligula reasoned, and from it he concluded, with equal logic, either that kings were gods or that men were beasts.

Caligula's reasoning matches that of Hobbes and Grotius. Aristotle, before any of them, had said that men are by no means naturally equal, but that some are born for slavery and others for rule. Aristotle was right, but he mistook the effect for the cause. Nothing is more certain than that every man born into slavery is born for slavery. Slaves lose everything in their chains, even the desire to escape them. They come to love their servitude, just as the companions of Ulysses, turned into swine by the sorceress Circe, came to love their brutish condition, a theme Plutarch takes up in a short treatise entitled "That Animals Reason." If there are slaves by nature, then, it is because there were first slaves against nature. Force made the first slaves, and their cowardice kept them in that condition.

I have said nothing of King Adam, or of Emperor Noah, father of the three great monarchs who divided the universe among themselves, as Saturn's sons Jupiter, Neptune, and Pluto did. Some scholars have in fact identified Noah's sons with those gods. I trust I will be duly thanked for my restraint. After all, I am a direct descendant of one of these princes, perhaps even of the eldest line, and for all I know an examination of titles might reveal me to be the rightful king of the human race. In any case, there can be no doubt that Adam was sovereign of the world, just as Robinson Crusoe was sovereign of his island, for as long as he was its only inhabitant. And this empire had one advantage: its monarch, secure on his throne, had no rebellions, wars, or conspirators to fear.

### Chapter III: The Right of the Strongest

The strongest is never strong enough to remain master forever unless he transforms his strength into right and obedience into duty. Hence the "right of the strongest," a phrase that sounds ironic but is in fact laid down as a fundamental principle. Will no one ever explain what it means? Force is a physical power, and I cannot see what moral effect it can produce. Yielding to force is an act of necessity, not of will. At most it is an act of prudence. In what sense could it be a duty?

Suppose for a moment that this so-called right exists. I maintain that the only result is a heap of incomprehensible nonsense. If force creates right, the effect changes with the cause: any force greater than the first inherits its right. As soon as one can disobey without punishment, disobedience becomes legitimate, and since the strongest is always in the right, the only thing that matters is to make oneself the strongest. But what sort of right vanishes when force fails? If we must obey because we are forced to, we need not obey because we ought to, and if we are not forced to obey, we are under no obligation to do so. Plainly, the word "right" adds nothing to force. In this context, it means nothing at all.

"Obey the powers that be." If this means "yield to force," it is sound advice but superfluous, and I can vouch that it will never be broken. All power comes from God, I grant, but so does all sickness. Does that mean we are forbidden to call the doctor? Suppose a robber surprises me at the edge of a wood. I must hand over my purse under compulsion, but if I could keep it hidden, am I also bound in conscience to give it up? After all, the pistol he holds is also a power.

Let us agree, then, that force does not create right, and that we are obliged to obey only legitimate powers. In that case, my original question returns.

### Chapter IV: Slavery

Since no man has natural authority over his fellows, and since force creates no right, we must conclude that agreements are the basis of all legitimate authority among men.

If an individual, says Grotius, can alienate his liberty and make himself the slave of a master, why could a whole people not do the same and make itself the subject of a king? This passage contains plenty of ambiguous words that would need explaining, but let us limit ourselves to the word *alienate*. To alienate is to give or to sell. A man who becomes another's slave does not give himself. He sells himself, at the very least for his subsistence. But what does a people sell itself for? Far from supplying his subjects with their subsistence, a king draws his own entirely from them, and, as Rabelais remarks, kings do not live on nothing. Do subjects, then, hand over their persons on condition that the king takes their property as well? I fail to see what they would have left to protect.

It will be said that the despot guarantees his subjects civil peace. Granted. But what do they gain if the wars his ambition brings upon them, his insatiable greed, and the harassment of his ministers weigh on them more heavily than their own quarrels would have? What do they gain if the very peace they enjoy is one of their miseries? There is peace in dungeons too, but is that enough to make them pleasant places to live? The Greeks trapped in the Cyclops's cave lived there quite peacefully while they waited their turn to be eaten.

To say that a man gives himself away for nothing is to say something absurd and inconceivable. Such an act is void and illegitimate for the simple reason that whoever performs it is not in his right mind. To say the same of an entire people is to imagine a nation of madmen, and madness creates no right.

Even if each man could alienate himself, he could not alienate his children. They are born human and free. Their liberty belongs to them, and no one but they has the right to dispose of it. Before they reach the age of reason, their father may set conditions on their behalf for their preservation and welfare, but he cannot give them away irrevocably and unconditionally, for such a gift runs contrary to the purposes of nature and exceeds the rights of fatherhood. To make an arbitrary government legitimate, therefore, the people of each generation would have to be free to accept or reject it, but in that case the government would no longer be arbitrary.

To renounce one's liberty is to renounce one's humanity, to surrender the rights of a human being and even its duties. No compensation is possible for someone who gives up everything. Such a renunciation is incompatible with human nature, for to strip a man's will of all freedom is to strip his actions of all morality. Finally, any agreement that grants absolute authority to one side and demands unlimited obedience from the other is empty and self-contradictory. Is it not clear that we can owe nothing to a person from whom we have the right to demand everything? And does this condition alone, with no equivalence and no exchange, not render the act void? What right could my slave have against me, when everything he has belongs to me? His right being mine, a right of mine against myself is a phrase without meaning.

Grotius and the others find in war another origin for the so-called right of slavery. Since the victor, in their view, has the right to kill the vanquished, the vanquished may buy back his life at the price of his liberty, and this agreement is all the more legitimate because it benefits both parties.

But this supposed right to kill the conquered clearly cannot be deduced from the state of war. While men live in their original independence, they have no relations with one another stable enough to constitute either a state of peace or a state of war, and for that reason alone they cannot be natural enemies. War arises from a relation between things, not between persons. Since a state of war cannot spring from merely personal relations, but only from relations concerning things, private war, the war of one man against another, can exist neither in the state of nature, where there is no settled property, nor in society, where everything is under the authority of the laws. Individual fights, duels, and skirmishes are acts that cannot constitute a state of war. As for the private wars permitted by the Establishments of Louis IX, King of France, and suspended by the Peace of God, they were abuses of feudalism, a system as absurd as any that ever existed, contrary both to the principles of natural right and to all sound government.

War, then, is a relation not between man and man but between state and state. Individuals are enemies only by accident: not as men, nor even as citizens, but as soldiers; not as members of their country, but as its defenders. Finally, each state can have only other states as enemies, not men, since there can be no real relation between things of fundamentally different natures.

The Romans understood and respected the right of war better than any other nation on earth, and they carried their scruples on this point so far that no citizen was allowed to serve even as a volunteer without expressly binding himself against the enemy, and against a particular enemy named in his oath. When the legion in which the younger Cato was serving his first campaign under Popilius was reorganized, the elder Cato wrote to Popilius that if he wished his son to go on serving under him, he must administer a new military oath, because the first had been annulled and the young man could no longer bear arms against the enemy. The same Cato wrote to his son, warning him to take great care not to go into battle before swearing the new oath. I know that the siege of Clusium and other isolated incidents can be cited against me, but I am citing laws and customs. No people has broken its laws less often than the Romans, and none has had such good ones.

This principle also agrees with the established rules of every age and the constant practice of all civilized peoples. Declarations of war are notices addressed less to governments than to their subjects. A foreigner, whether king, private individual, or people, who robs, kills, or detains subjects without declaring war on their prince is not an enemy but a bandit. Even in open war, a just prince seizes everything in enemy territory that belongs to the public, but he respects the lives and property of individuals, because he respects rights on which his own are founded. Since the aim of war is to destroy the enemy state, one has the right to kill its defenders so long as they bear arms. But as soon as they lay down their arms and surrender, they cease to be enemies or instruments of the enemy and become simply men again, and no one has any right to take their lives. It is sometimes possible to kill a state without killing a single one of its members, and war confers no right beyond what is necessary to achieve its aim. These principles are not those of Grotius. They do not rest on the authority of poets. They are derived from the nature of things and grounded in reason.

The right of conquest has no foundation other than the right of the strongest. If war does not give the conqueror the right to massacre conquered peoples, then the right to enslave them cannot rest on a right that does not exist. No one has the right to kill an enemy except when he cannot make him a slave, so the right to enslave him cannot be derived from the right to kill him. It is therefore an unfair bargain to make the captive buy his life, over which the victor has no right, at the cost of his liberty. Is it not obvious that founding the right of life and death on the right of slavery, and the right of slavery on the right of life and death, is a vicious circle?

Even if we grant this terrible right to kill everyone, I maintain that a slave taken in war, or a conquered people, owes nothing to its master except obedience to the extent that it is forced to obey. By taking an equivalent for the captive's life, the victor has done him no favor. Instead of killing him to no purpose, he has killed him profitably. Far from gaining any authority over him beyond that of force, the victor remains in a state of war with him. Their relationship is itself the product of that war, and exercising the rights of war does not imply a peace treaty. An agreement has indeed been made, but far from ending the state of war, it presupposes that the war continues.

Thus, from whatever angle we consider the question, the right of slavery is null and void, not only because it is illegitimate, but because it is absurd and meaningless. The words *slave* and *right* contradict each other and are mutually exclusive. It will always be equally foolish for a man to say to another man, or to a people: "I make an agreement with you that is entirely at your expense and entirely to my benefit. I will keep it as long as I please, and you will keep it as long as I please."

### Chapter V: That We Must Always Go Back to a First Convention

Even if I conceded everything I have been refuting, the champions of despotism would be no better off. There will always be a vast difference between subduing a multitude and governing a society. If scattered individuals, however numerous, were enslaved one after another by a single man, I would still see nothing but a master and his slaves, and certainly not a people and its ruler. I would see what might be called an aggregation, but not an association, for there would as yet be neither a public good nor a body politic. That man, even if he had enslaved half the world, would still be merely an individual, and his interest, separate from that of others, would still be a purely private one. When he dies, his empire is left scattered and without unity, like an oak that collapses into a heap of ashes once the fire has consumed it.

A people, says Grotius, can give itself to a king. By Grotius's own account, then, a people is already a people before it gives itself away. The gift is itself a civil act and implies public deliberation. Before examining the act by which a people gives itself to a king, it would be better to examine the act by which it became a people in the first place, for this act, being necessarily prior to the other, is the true foundation of society.

Indeed, without some prior agreement, and unless the choice were unanimous, where would the minority's obligation to submit to the majority's choice come from? By what right do a hundred men who want a master vote on behalf of ten who do not? The rule of majority vote is itself established by agreement, and it presupposes unanimity on at least one occasion.

### Chapter VI: The Social Compact

I assume that men have reached the point at which the obstacles to their survival in the state of nature have grown stronger than the resources each individual can muster to maintain himself in that state. That original condition can then no longer continue, and the human race would perish if it did not change its way of life.

But since men cannot create new forces, only combine and direct those that already exist, their only means of self-preservation is to form, by joining together, a sum of forces great enough to overcome that resistance. These forces must be set in motion by a single driving power and made to act in concert.

Such a sum of forces can arise only where many people come together. But since each man's strength and liberty are the chief instruments of his self-preservation, how can he pledge them without harming his own interests and neglecting the care he owes himself? As it bears on my present subject, this difficulty may be stated as follows:

"The problem is to find a form of association that will defend and protect, with the whole common force, the person and property of each associate, and in which each, while uniting himself with all, may still obey only himself and remain as free as before." This is the fundamental problem to which the social contract provides the solution.

The clauses of this contract are so fixed by the nature of the act that the slightest change would render them void and ineffective. Although they may never have been formally stated, they are everywhere the same and everywhere tacitly accepted and recognized, until the social compact is violated. At that point each person recovers his original rights and resumes his natural liberty, while losing the conventional liberty for which he gave it up.

Properly understood, these clauses all come down to one: the total surrender of each associate, together with all his rights, to the whole community. In the first place, since each person gives himself entirely, the conditions are the same for everyone, and since they are the same for everyone, no one has any interest in making them burdensome for others.

Moreover, since the surrender is made without reservation, the union is as complete as it can be, and no associate has any further claim to make. If individuals kept certain rights, there would be no common superior to settle disputes between them and the public. Each, being his own judge on one point, would soon claim to be so on all. The state of nature would then persist, and the association would necessarily become either ineffective or tyrannical.

Finally, each man, in giving himself to all, gives himself to no one. And since there is no associate over whom he does not gain the same right that he grants others over himself, he receives the equivalent of everything he loses, along with greater force to preserve what he has.

If, then, we strip the social compact of everything inessential, we will find that it comes down to the following terms:

"Each of us places his person and all his power in common under the supreme direction of the general will, and, as a body, we receive each member as an indivisible part of the whole."

At once, in place of the individual personality of each contracting party, this act of association creates a moral and collective body, made up of as many members as the assembly has votes, and receiving from this act its unity, its common identity, its life, and its will. This public person, formed by the union of all the others, once bore the name of *city*, and now bears that of *republic* or *body politic*. Its members call it the *state* when it is passive, the *sovereign* when it is active, and a *power* when it is compared with others of its kind. Those who are associated in it are known collectively as the *people*. Individually they are called *citizens*, as sharers in sovereign authority, and *subjects*, as persons bound by the laws of the state. These terms, however, are often confused and used interchangeably, and it is enough to know how to tell them apart when they are used with precision.

The true meaning of the word *city* has been almost entirely lost in modern times. Most people mistake a town for a city and a townsman for a citizen, not realizing that houses make a town, but citizens make a city. The same mistake once cost the Carthaginians dearly. I have never read of the title of citizen being granted to the subjects of any prince, not even to the ancient Macedonians, or to the English of today, although the English stand closer to liberty than anyone else. Only the French use the word *citizen* freely and familiarly everywhere, and they do so because, as their dictionaries show, they have no idea what it means. Were it otherwise, they would be guilty, in claiming the title, of *lèse-majesté*, the crime of offending against the dignity of the sovereign. Among the French, the word names a virtue, not a right. When Bodin wrote about our citizens and townsmen, he blundered badly by confusing the one class with the other. M. d'Alembert avoided that error: in his article on Geneva he clearly distinguished the four orders of people (or five, if mere foreigners are counted) who live in our town, only two of which make up the Republic. No other French writer, so far as I know, has grasped the real meaning of the word *citizen*.

### Chapter VII: The Sovereign

This formula shows that the act of association involves a mutual commitment between the public and its individual members. Each individual, in contracting, so to speak, with himself, is bound in two capacities: as a member of the Sovereign, he is bound to individuals, and as a member of the State, he is bound to the Sovereign. The maxim of civil law that no one is bound by promises made to himself does not apply here, for there is a great difference between taking on an obligation to yourself and taking one on toward a whole of which you are a part.

It should also be noted that public deliberation can bind all subjects to the Sovereign, because each of them can be regarded in two different capacities. For the opposite reason, however, it cannot bind the Sovereign to itself. It is therefore contrary to the nature of the body politic for the Sovereign to impose on itself a law it cannot break. Since the Sovereign can regard itself in only one capacity, it is in the position of an individual making a contract with himself. It follows that there is not, and cannot be, any kind of fundamental law binding on the body of the people, not even the social contract itself. This does not mean that the body politic cannot enter into commitments with others, provided those commitments do not violate the contract, for with respect to what lies outside it, the body politic becomes a simple being, an individual. But the body politic, or the Sovereign, draws its entire existence from the sanctity of the contract. It can therefore never bind itself, even toward an outsider, to do anything that would undermine the original act, such as giving away any part of itself or submitting to another Sovereign. To violate the act by which it exists would be to annihilate itself, and what is itself nothing can create nothing.

Once a multitude has been united into a single body in this way, no one can injure one of its members without attacking the body, and still less can anyone injure the body without its members feeling the wound. Duty and interest alike therefore oblige the two contracting parties to help each other, and the same men should seek to combine, in their double capacity, all the advantages that this capacity brings.

Moreover, because the Sovereign is formed entirely of the individuals who compose it, it neither has nor can have any interest contrary to theirs. The sovereign power therefore need offer no guarantee to its subjects, since a body cannot wish to harm all its members, and we shall see later that it cannot harm any one of them in particular either. By the mere fact of being what it is, the Sovereign is always what it ought to be.

The same cannot be said of the subjects' relation to the Sovereign. Despite their common interest, the Sovereign would have no assurance that its subjects would keep their commitments unless it found some means of securing their loyalty.

Each individual, as a man, may in fact have a particular will that is contrary to, or different from, the general will he has as a citizen. His private interest may speak to him in a very different voice from the common interest. His absolute and naturally independent existence may lead him to regard what he owes to the common cause as a gratuitous contribution, whose loss would harm others less than its payment burdens him. He may also treat the moral person that constitutes the State as a *persona ficta*, a merely fictitious person, because it is not a flesh-and-blood man, and so wish to enjoy the rights of a citizen without being willing to fulfill the duties of a subject. If such an injustice were allowed to persist, it would inevitably bring about the ruin of the body politic.

So that the social compact may not be an empty formula, it tacitly includes one commitment that alone gives force to all the others: whoever refuses to obey the general will shall be compelled to do so by the whole body. This means nothing less than that he will be forced to be free. This is the very condition that, by giving each citizen to his country, protects him against all personal dependence. Here lies the key to the working of the political machine. This alone makes civil obligations legitimate; without it, they would be absurd, tyrannical, and open to the most appalling abuses.

### Chapter VIII: The Civil State

The passage from the state of nature to the civil state produces a remarkable change in man. It substitutes justice for instinct in his conduct and gives his actions the moral quality they previously lacked. Only when the voice of duty replaces physical impulse, and right replaces appetite, does man, who until then had considered only himself, find himself compelled to act on different principles and to consult his reason before heeding his inclinations. In this new state he gives up some of the advantages that nature gave him. In return, however, he gains others so great, with his faculties so exercised and developed, his ideas so broadened, his feelings so ennobled, and his whole soul so elevated, that he would have reason to bless without ceasing the happy moment that drew him out of nature forever and turned him from a dull, unimaginative animal into an intelligent being and a man. The only exception is that the abuses of this new condition often degrade him below the one he left behind.

Let us reduce the whole balance sheet to terms that can be easily compared. What man loses through the social contract is his natural liberty and an unlimited right to anything that tempts him and that he can manage to get. What he gains is civil liberty and the ownership of everything he possesses. If we are to weigh the one against the other without error, we must clearly distinguish natural liberty, which is limited only by the individual's strength, from civil liberty, which is limited by the general will. We must likewise distinguish possession, which is merely the effect of force or the right of the first occupant, from property, which can be founded only on a positive legal title.

To all that man acquires in the civil state we might add moral liberty, which alone makes him truly master of himself. To be driven by appetite alone is slavery, while obedience to a law we prescribe for ourselves is liberty. But I have already said too much on this subject, and the philosophical meaning of the word *liberty* is not our concern here.

### Chapter IX: Real Property

At the moment the community is founded, each member gives himself to it just as he is, with all the resources at his command, including the goods he possesses. This act does not change the nature of possession when it changes hands; possession does not become property merely by passing into the hands of the Sovereign. But because the forces of the city are incomparably greater than those of any individual, public possession is in fact stronger and more secure against revocation, though it is no more legitimate, at least in the eyes of foreigners. In relation to its own members, the State is master of all their goods by virtue of the social contract, which within the State is the foundation of all rights. In relation to other powers, however, it is master only by the right of the first occupant, a right it derives from its members.

The right of the first occupant, though more real than the right of the strongest, becomes a true right only after the right of property has been established. Every man naturally has a right to everything he needs, but the positive act that makes him owner of one thing excludes him from everything else. Having received his share, he should keep to it, and he has no further claim against the community. This is why the right of the first occupant, so weak in the state of nature, commands the respect of every member of civil society. In honoring this right, we respect not so much what belongs to someone else as what does not belong to us.

In general, three conditions are required to establish a first occupant's right to a piece of land. First, the land must not yet be inhabited. Second, a man must occupy only as much as he needs to live on. Third, he must take possession not by some empty ceremony but by labor and cultivation, the only mark of ownership that others ought to respect in the absence of a legal title.

In granting the right of first occupancy to need and labor, are we not already stretching it as far as it can go? Can such a right be left without limits? Is it enough to set foot on a piece of common ground to call oneself its master on the spot? Is it enough to have the strength to drive others off for a moment in order to establish a right to keep them from ever coming back? How can a man or a people seize an immense territory and hold it against the rest of the world except by a punishable usurpation, since that act robs everyone else of the dwelling place and the means of subsistence that nature gave to all in common? When Núñez de Balboa stood on the shore and took possession of the South Seas and all of South America in the name of the crown of Castile, was that enough to dispossess all their actual inhabitants and to shut out every other prince in the world? If so, such ceremonies are multiplied to no purpose. The Catholic King, as the King of Spain was styled, need only take possession of the entire universe at a single stroke from his private chambers, adding afterward a reservation for whatever already belonged to other princes.

We can imagine how the lands of individuals, lying side by side and being joined together, became public territory, and how the right of sovereignty, extending from the subjects to the lands they held, became at once real and personal, a right over land as well as over persons. This made the landholders more dependent and turned their own resources into a guarantee of their loyalty. The ancient monarchs seem not to have grasped this advantage. They called themselves Kings of the Persians, of the Scythians, or of the Macedonians, and appear to have thought of themselves as rulers of men rather than masters of a country. Today's monarchs more shrewdly call themselves Kings of France, of Spain, of England, and so on; by holding the land, they are quite confident of holding its inhabitants.

What is peculiar about this surrender is that the community, in taking over the goods of individuals, far from despoiling them, only secures their lawful possession, turning usurpation into a genuine right and mere use into ownership. The possessors are then regarded as trustees of the public good. Their rights are respected by every member of the State and defended against foreign aggression by all its forces. Through a cession that benefits the public and benefits themselves still more, they have, so to speak, acquired everything they gave up. This paradox is easily explained by the distinction between the rights that the Sovereign and the owner hold over the same estate, as we shall see later. It may also happen that men begin to unite before they own anything. Later, occupying a stretch of country large enough for all, they may either enjoy it in common or divide it among themselves, equally or according to proportions fixed by the Sovereign. However the land is acquired, each individual's right to his own estate is always subordinate to the right the community holds over all of it. Without this, there would be neither stability in the social bond nor real force in the exercise of sovereignty.

I shall close this chapter and this book by noting a fact on which the entire social system ought to rest. Rather than destroying natural inequality, the fundamental compact substitutes an equality that is moral and legitimate for whatever physical inequality nature may have placed among men. Men who may be unequal in strength or intelligence thus all become equal by convention and by right.

Under bad governments, this equality is merely apparent and illusory. Its only effect is to keep the poor man in his poverty and the rich man in the position he has usurped. In fact, laws always benefit those who own something and harm those who have nothing. It follows that the social state is advantageous to men only when all of them have something and none of them has too much.

## Book II

### Chapter I: That Sovereignty Is Inalienable

The first and most important consequence of the principles established so far is that the general will alone can direct the State according to the purpose for which it was instituted, namely the common good. If the clash of particular interests made the founding of societies necessary, it was the agreement of those same interests that made it possible. What these different interests have in common forms the social bond, and if there were no point on which they all agreed, no society could exist. It is solely on the basis of this common interest that every society ought to be governed.

I maintain, then, that sovereignty, being nothing other than the exercise of the general will, can never be alienated, and that the Sovereign, which is nothing but a collective being, can be represented only by itself. Power may indeed be transferred, but not will.

It may not be impossible for a particular will to coincide with the general will on some point, but it is at least impossible for that agreement to be lasting and constant, since the particular will tends by its very nature toward partiality, and the general will toward equality. It is more impossible still to have any guarantee of such agreement. Even if it were always to exist, it would be the product not of design but of chance. The Sovereign may well say, "I now will what this man actually wills, or at least what he says he wills." But it cannot say, "Whatever he wills tomorrow, I too shall will," because it is absurd for the will to bind itself for the future, and no will is obliged to consent to anything that is not for the good of the being who wills. If, then, the people simply promises to obey, by that very act it dissolves itself and loses what makes it a people. The moment there is a master, there is no longer a Sovereign, and from that moment the body politic has ceased to exist.

This does not mean that the commands of rulers cannot pass for expressions of the general will, so long as the Sovereign, being free to oppose them, does not do so. In such a case, universal silence is taken to imply the people's consent. This will be explained later.

### Chapter II: That Sovereignty Is Indivisible

For the same reason that sovereignty is inalienable, it is indivisible. A will either is general or it is not: it is the will either of the body of the people or of only a part of it. To be general, a will need not always be unanimous, but every vote must be counted, and any exclusion breaks its generality. In the first case, the declared will is an act of sovereignty and constitutes law. In the second, it is merely a particular will, or an act of magistracy, and at most a decree.

Our political theorists, however, unable to divide sovereignty in its principle, divide it in its object. They split it into force and will; into legislative power and executive power; into the rights of taxation, of justice, and of war; into domestic administration and the power to make treaties with foreign states. Sometimes they lump all these parts together, and sometimes they separate them. They turn the Sovereign into a fantastic creature assembled from various pieces, as if they were constructing a man out of several bodies, one with eyes, another with arms, another with feet, and each with nothing else. We are told that the conjurers of Japan cut a child to pieces before the eyes of their audience and toss the limbs one after another into the air, and the child falls back to earth alive and whole. The tricks of our political theorists are much the same. First they dismember the body politic with a sleight of hand worthy of a country fair, and then they put it back together by some means no one can explain.

This error arises from a lack of precise ideas about sovereign authority, and from mistaking for parts of it what are only emanations from it. Declaring war and making peace, for example, have been treated as acts of sovereignty. They are not, because these acts do not constitute law. They are merely the application of law, particular acts that determine how the law applies in a given case, as will become clear once the idea attached to the word *law* has been defined.

If we examined the other divisions in the same way, we would find that wherever sovereignty appears to be divided, we are being deceived by an illusion. The rights taken to be parts of sovereignty are all in fact subordinate to it, and always presuppose supreme wills whose execution they merely authorize.

It would be impossible to measure how much obscurity this lack of precision has cast over the conclusions of writers on political right, when they have tried to use their own principles to judge the respective rights of kings and peoples. Anyone can see, in the third and fourth chapters of the first book of Grotius, how that learned man and his translator, Barbeyrac, tangle and tie themselves in their own sophistries, for fear of saying too little or too much of what they think and so offending the interests they needed to placate. Grotius, a refugee in France, discontented with his own country and eager to win favor with Louis XIII, to whom his book is dedicated, spares no effort and no device to strip the peoples of all their rights and confer them upon kings. This would have suited Barbeyrac's taste as well, for he dedicated his translation to George I of England. Unfortunately for him, however, the expulsion of James II, which he called an "abdication," forced him to be guarded, to evade and equivocate, so as not to make William out to be a usurper. Had these two writers adopted the true principles, every difficulty would have vanished and they would have remained consistent throughout. But it would have been a sad truth for them to tell, and it would have won them favor with no one but the people. Truth, moreover, is no road to fortune, and the people hands out neither ambassadorships, nor professorships, nor pensions.

### Chapter III: Whether the General Will Is Fallible

It follows from what has been said that the general will is always right and always tends toward the public good. It does not follow, however, that the people's deliberations are always equally sound. We always will our own good, but we do not always see what it is. The people is never corrupted, but it is often deceived, and only then does it seem to will what is bad.

There is often a great difference between the will of all and the general will. The general will looks only to the common interest, while the will of all takes private interests into account and is nothing more than a sum of particular wills. But take away from these same wills the pluses and minuses that cancel one another out, and the general will remains as the sum of the differences.

As the Marquis d'Argenson observes, "Every interest has different principles. The agreement of two particular interests is formed by opposition to a third." He might have added that the agreement of all interests is formed by opposition to the interest of each. If there were no differing interests, the common interest would scarcely be felt, since it would never meet an obstacle. Everything would run of its own accord, and politics would cease to be an art.

Suppose a sufficiently informed people were to deliberate while its citizens had no communication with one another. The grand total of their small differences would then always yield the general will, and the decision would always be good. But when factions arise and partial associations form at the expense of the great association, the will of each such association becomes general with respect to its members while remaining particular with respect to the State. One may then say that there are no longer as many votes as there are men, but only as many as there are associations. The differences become fewer and yield a less general result. Finally, when one of these associations grows so large that it prevails over all the rest, the result is no longer a sum of small differences but a single difference. At that point there is no longer a general will, and the opinion that prevails is purely particular.

If the general will is to be able to express itself, it is essential that there be no partial societies within the State, and that each citizen think only his own thoughts. Such was the sublime and unique system established by the great Lycurgus. But if partial societies do exist, it is best to have as many of them as possible and to prevent inequality among them, as Solon, Numa, and Servius did. These precautions are the only ones that can ensure that the general will is always enlightened and that the people never deceives itself.

Machiavelli makes a similar point in the seventh book of his *History of Florence*: "There are, in fact, some divisions that harm a republic and some that benefit it. Those that stir up sects and parties are harmful; those that involve neither are beneficial. Since, then, the founder of a republic cannot prevent enmities from arising, he ought at least to keep them from growing into sects."

### Chapter IV: The Limits of the Sovereign Power

If the State is a moral person whose life consists in the union of its members, and if its most important concern is its own preservation, then it must possess a universal and compelling force capable of moving and arranging each part in whatever way best benefits the whole. Just as nature gives each man absolute power over his limbs, the social compact gives the body politic absolute power over all its members; and it is this power, directed by the general will, that bears, as I have said, the name of sovereignty.

But beyond the public person, we must also consider the private persons who compose it, whose life and liberty are by nature independent of it. We are therefore obliged to distinguish clearly between the respective rights of the citizens and of the Sovereign, and between the duties the citizens must fulfill as subjects and the natural rights they ought to enjoy as men. Attentive readers, I ask you not to rush to accuse me of contradicting myself here: the poverty of the language made this terminology unavoidable. Wait and see.

I grant that by the social compact each man surrenders only that portion of his powers, goods, and liberty which it matters to the community to control; but it must also be granted that the Sovereign alone judges what matters.

A citizen owes the State every service he can render, as soon as the Sovereign demands it. The Sovereign, for its part, cannot impose on its subjects any restraint that is useless to the community; it cannot even wish to, for under the law of reason, no less than under the law of nature, nothing happens without a cause.

The commitments that bind us to the social body are obligatory only because they are mutual, and their nature is such that in fulfilling them we cannot work for others without also working for ourselves. Why is the general will always right, and why do all constantly will the happiness of each, unless it is because there is no one who does not take the word "each" to mean himself, and who does not think of himself when he votes for all? This shows that equality of rights, and the notion of justice it produces, spring from the preference each man gives to himself, and therefore from human nature itself. It shows that the general will, to be truly general, must be general in its object as well as in its essence; that it must both come from all and apply to all; and that it loses its natural rectitude when it is directed toward some particular and determinate object, because we are then judging something foreign to us and have no true principle of equity to guide us.

Indeed, the moment a question of particular fact or right arises on a point that no prior general agreement has settled, the matter becomes contentious. The individuals concerned form one party and the public the other, yet I can see neither the law that ought to govern the case nor the judge who ought to decide it. It would be absurd, in such a case, to propose referring the question to an express decision of the general will, for that decision could only be the conclusion reached by one of the parties; to the other party it would be merely an external and particular will, inclined on this occasion toward injustice and liable to error. Just as a particular will cannot stand in for the general will, so the general will in turn changes its nature when its object is particular: being general, it cannot pass judgment on a single man or a single fact. When the people of Athens, for instance, appointed or removed its rulers, voted honors to one citizen and penalties to another, and through a multitude of particular decrees exercised every function of government indiscriminately, it no longer had a general will in the strict sense; it was acting not as Sovereign but as magistrate. This will seem contrary to prevailing opinion, but I must be given time to set out my own.

It should be clear from all this that what makes the will general is less the number of voters than the common interest that unites them. Under this system each person necessarily submits to the conditions he imposes on others, and this admirable harmony between interest and justice gives the common deliberations a fairness that vanishes the moment any particular question is discussed, since there is then no common interest to unite the ruling of the judge with that of the party and make them one.

From whatever direction we approach our principle, we arrive at the same conclusion: the social compact establishes among the citizens an equality such that all bind themselves under the same conditions and should therefore all enjoy the same rights. By the very nature of the compact, then, every act of sovereignty, which is to say every authentic act of the general will, binds or benefits all citizens equally, so that the Sovereign recognizes only the nation as a body and draws no distinctions among those who compose it.

What, then, strictly speaking, is an act of sovereignty? It is not an agreement between a superior and an inferior, but an agreement between the body and each of its members. It is legitimate because it rests on the social contract; equitable because it is common to all; useful because it can have no object but the general good; and stable because the public force and the supreme power stand behind it. So long as subjects are bound only by agreements of this kind, they obey no one but their own will; and to ask how far the respective rights of the Sovereign and the citizens extend is to ask how far the citizens can commit themselves to themselves, each to all and all to each.

We can see from this that sovereign power, absolute, sacred, and inviolable as it is, does not and cannot exceed the limits of general agreements, and that every man may dispose freely of whatever goods and liberty these agreements leave him. The Sovereign therefore never has the right to burden one subject more heavily than another, because the matter would then become particular and pass outside its competence.

Once these distinctions are granted, it becomes clear how false it is to say that the social contract involves any real renunciation on the part of individuals. On the contrary, their situation after the contract is genuinely preferable to what it was before. Instead of a renunciation, they have made an advantageous exchange: in place of an uncertain and precarious existence, a better and more secure one; in place of natural independence, liberty; in place of the power to harm others, security for themselves; and in place of their own strength, which others might overcome, a right that the social union makes invincible. Their very life, which they have pledged to the State, is constantly protected by it; and when they risk that life in the State's defense, what are they doing but giving back what they received from it? What are they doing that they would not have to do more often, and at greater peril, in the state of nature, where they would inevitably have to fight battles at the risk of their lives to defend the very means of their preservation? All must indeed fight when their country needs them; but then no one ever has to fight for himself. Do we not gain something by running, on behalf of what keeps us secure, only some of the risks we would have to run for ourselves the moment we lost it?

### Chapter V: The Right of Life and Death

People often ask how individuals, who have no right to dispose of their own lives, can transfer to the Sovereign a right they do not possess. The difficulty of answering seems to me to lie in the way the question is posed. Every man has the right to risk his own life in order to preserve it. Has anyone ever said that a man who throws himself from a window to escape a fire is guilty of suicide? Has anyone ever laid that crime to the charge of a man who dies in a storm, on the grounds that he knew the danger when he went aboard?

The social treaty has as its end the preservation of the contracting parties. Whoever wills the end also wills the means, and the means must involve some risks, and even some losses. Whoever wishes to preserve his life at the expense of others must also be ready, when necessary, to give it up for their sake. Moreover, the citizen is no longer the judge of the dangers to which the law wishes him to expose himself. When the prince says to him, "It is expedient for the State that you should die," he ought to die, because only on that condition has he lived in security until now, and because his life is no longer merely a gift of nature but a gift made conditionally by the State.

The death penalty inflicted on criminals can be viewed in much the same light: it is in order not to fall victim to an assassin that we consent to die if we ourselves become assassins. Under this treaty, far from disposing of our own lives, we think only of securing them, and it is not to be supposed that any of the parties expects, at the time, to end up on the gallows.

Furthermore, every wrongdoer who attacks social rights becomes, by his crime, a rebel and a traitor to his country. By violating its laws he ceases to be a member of it; he even makes war upon it. In that case the preservation of the State is incompatible with his own, and one or the other must perish; when the guilty man is put to death, it is less as a citizen than as an enemy. The trial and the judgment are the proof that he has broken the social treaty and is therefore no longer a member of the State. And since he has acknowledged himself a member by living there, he must be removed from it, either by exile as a violator of the compact or by death as a public enemy; for such an enemy is not a moral person but merely a man, and in that situation the right of war is to kill the vanquished.

It will be objected that the condemnation of a criminal is a particular act. I grant it; but such condemnation is not a function of the Sovereign. It is a right the Sovereign can confer without being able to exercise it itself. All my ideas hang together, but I cannot set them all out at once.

We may add that frequent punishments are always a sign of weakness or negligence on the part of the government. There is no wrongdoer who could not be made good for something. The State has no right to put anyone to death, even to make an example of him, whom it can leave alive without danger.

The right to pardon, or to exempt the guilty from a penalty imposed by the law and pronounced by the judge, belongs only to the authority that stands above both judge and law, which is to say the Sovereign. Even its right in this matter is far from clear, and the occasions for exercising it are extremely rare. In a well-governed State there are few punishments, not because pardons are many, but because criminals are few; it is when a State is in decay that the very multitude of crimes guarantees impunity. Under the Roman Republic, neither the Senate nor the consuls ever attempted to grant a pardon; even the people never did so, though they sometimes revoked their own decisions. Frequent pardons announce that crime will soon have no further need of them, and anyone can see where that leads. But I feel my heart protesting and holding back my pen. Let us leave these questions to the just man who has never transgressed and would himself never stand in need of pardon.

### Chapter VI: Law

Through the social compact we have given the body politic existence and life; through legislation we must now give it movement and will. The original act by which the body is formed and united does not yet determine in any respect what it must do to preserve itself.

What is good and in accordance with order is so by the nature of things, independently of human agreements. All justice comes from God, who alone is its source; but if we knew how to receive so lofty an inspiration, we would need neither government nor laws. There is doubtless a universal justice that springs from reason alone; but to be acknowledged among us, this justice must be mutual. Speaking in human terms, the laws of justice are ineffective among men for want of natural sanctions: they work only to the advantage of the wicked and the ruin of the just, when the just man observes them toward everyone and no one observes them toward him. Agreements and laws are therefore needed to join rights to duties and to direct justice to its proper object. In the state of nature, where everything is held in common, I owe nothing to a person to whom I have promised nothing, and I recognize as belonging to others only what is of no use to me. In the civil state, where all rights are fixed by law, the case is different.

But what, after all, is a law? As long as we are content to attach purely metaphysical ideas to the word, we will go on arguing without coming to any understanding; and even once we have defined a law of nature, we will be no closer to defining a law of the State.

I have already said that there can be no general will directed at a particular object. Such an object must lie either inside the State or outside it. If outside, a will that is foreign to the State cannot be general in relation to it. If inside, the object is part of the State, and a relation then arises between whole and part that turns them into two separate beings: the part is one, and the whole minus that part is the other. But the whole minus a part is not the whole; so long as this relation holds, there is no whole, only two unequal parts, and it follows that the will of one is in no respect general in relation to the other.

But when the whole people decrees for the whole people, it considers only itself; and if a relation then arises, it is between two aspects of the entire object, with no division of the whole. In that case the matter on which the decree is made is general, just like the will that makes it. It is this act that I call a law.

When I say that the object of laws is always general, I mean that the law considers subjects collectively and actions in the abstract, never a particular person or a particular action. Thus the law may well decree that there shall be privileges, but it cannot grant them to anyone by name. It may establish several classes of citizens and even specify the qualifications for belonging to each, but it cannot assign this or that person to them. It may establish a monarchical government and hereditary succession, but it cannot choose a king or designate a royal family. In short, no function that has a particular object belongs to the legislative power.

On this view, we see at once that there is no longer any need to ask whose business it is to make laws, since they are acts of the general will; nor whether the prince is above the law, since he is a member of the State; nor whether the law can be unjust, since no one is unjust to himself; nor how we can be both free and subject to the laws, since the laws are merely records of our own wills.

We see further that, since the law combines universality of will with universality of object, what any man commands on his own initiative, whoever he may be, cannot be a law; and even what the Sovereign commands concerning a particular matter is no closer to being a law. It is a decree, an act not of sovereignty but of magistracy.

I therefore give the name "republic" to every State governed by laws, whatever the form of its administration, for only then does the public interest govern and the *res publica*, the "public thing," become a reality. Every legitimate government is republican. By this word I mean not merely an aristocracy or a democracy, but in general any government directed by the general will, which is the law. To be legitimate, a government must be not one and the same with the Sovereign but its minister, and in that case even a monarchy is a republic. This will become clearer in the following book, where I will explain what government is.

Laws are, properly speaking, nothing but the conditions of civil association. The people, being subject to the laws, ought to be their author; the conditions of a society should be regulated only by those who come together to form it. But how are they to regulate them? By common agreement, or by sudden inspiration? Does the body politic have an organ through which to declare its will? Who can give it the foresight to formulate and publish its acts in advance? Or how is it to announce them in the hour of need? How can a blind multitude, which often does not know what it wills because it rarely knows what is good for it, carry out on its own so great and difficult an undertaking as a system of legislation? Left to itself, the people always wills the good, but by no means always sees it. The general will is always right, but the judgment that guides it is not always enlightened. It must be made to see things as they are, and sometimes as they ought to appear to it; it must be shown the good path it is seeking, protected from the seductive influence of individual wills, taught to see events in their sequence across time and place, and made to weigh the appeal of present, tangible advantages against the danger of distant and hidden evils. Individuals see the good and reject it; the public wills the good but does not see it. All are equally in need of guidance. Individuals must be compelled to bring their wills into line with their reason; the public must be taught to know what it wills. When this is done, public enlightenment produces a union of understanding and will in the social body: the parts work together precisely, and the whole is raised to its greatest strength. This is what makes a legislator necessary.

### Chapter VII: The Legislator

To discover the rules of society best suited to nations would require a superior intelligence that saw all the passions of men without feeling any of them. This intelligence would have to bear no relation to our nature while knowing it through and through; its happiness would have to be independent of us, yet it would have to be willing to concern itself with ours; and finally, looking ahead through the course of time to a distant glory, it would have to be able to labor in one century and enjoy the reward in the next. It would take gods to give men laws.

The same argument that Caligula drew from fact, that rulers are of a higher nature than those they rule, Plato made on the basis of right when, in the dialogue called the *Statesman*, he defined the civil or kingly man. But if great princes are rare, how much rarer are great legislators? The prince has only to follow a model that the legislator must design. The legislator is the engineer who invents the machine; the prince is merely the mechanic who assembles it and sets it running. "At the birth of societies," says Montesquieu, "it is the leaders of republics who create the institutions; afterward it is the institutions that shape the leaders."

Whoever dares to undertake the founding of a people's institutions must feel capable, so to speak, of changing human nature: of transforming each individual, who by himself is a complete and solitary whole, into part of a larger whole from which he in some sense receives his life and being; of altering man's constitution in order to strengthen it; and of substituting a partial and moral existence for the physical and independent existence that nature has given us all. In a word, he must take from man his own resources and give him in their place new ones that are foreign to him and that he cannot use without the help of others. The more completely these natural resources are extinguished, the greater and more lasting are the acquired ones, and the more stable and perfect the new institutions. If each citizen is nothing and can do nothing without all the others, and if the resources acquired by the whole equal or exceed the sum of the resources of all the individuals, then legislation may be said to have reached the highest perfection it can attain.

The legislator holds an extraordinary position in the State in every respect. If he is extraordinary by reason of his genius, he is no less so by reason of his office, which is neither magistracy nor sovereignty. This office, which founds the republic, has no place in its constitution. It is a singular and superior function with nothing in common with dominion over men; for if the one who commands men ought not to command the laws, the one who commands the laws ought no more to command men. Otherwise his laws would become instruments of his passions and would often do nothing but perpetuate his injustices, and his private aims would inevitably corrupt the sanctity of his work.

When Lycurgus gave laws to his country, he began by giving up the throne. Most Greek cities made it a practice to entrust the drafting of their laws to foreigners. The republics of modern Italy often followed this example; Geneva did the same and profited by it. Rome, at the height of its prosperity, saw all the crimes of tyranny revived within it and was brought to the brink of destruction because it placed legislative authority and sovereign power in the same hands.

Yet even the decemvirs, the board of ten men Rome appointed to draw up its laws, never claimed the right to enact any law on their own authority alone. "Nothing we propose to you," they told the people, "can become law without your consent. Romans, be yourselves the authors of the laws that are to make you happy."

Whoever drafts the laws, therefore, has, or ought to have, no legislative right, and the people cannot divest itself of this nontransferable right even if it wishes to. Under the fundamental compact, only the general will can bind individuals, and there can be no assurance that a particular will conforms to the general will until it has been submitted to the free vote of the people. I have said this before, but it bears repeating.

Thus in the work of legislation we find two things that seem incompatible: an undertaking beyond human strength, and, to carry it out, an authority that is no authority.

There is a further difficulty that deserves attention. Wise men who try to address the common people in their own language rather than the people's cannot possibly make themselves understood. There are a thousand kinds of ideas that cannot be translated into popular language. Conceptions that are too general and objects that are too remote lie equally beyond its reach: each individual, having no taste for any plan of government other than one that suits his own particular interest, struggles to see the advantages he might hope to draw from the constant sacrifices that good laws impose. For a young people to appreciate sound principles of political theory and follow the fundamental rules of statecraft, effect would have to become cause. The social spirit that these institutions are meant to produce would have to preside over their founding, and men would have to be, before the laws existed, what the laws were meant to make them.

Since the legislator can appeal neither to force nor to reason, he must turn to an authority of a different order, one able to compel without violence and to persuade without convincing.

This is why, in every age, the founders of nations have been driven to invoke divine intervention and to credit the gods with their own wisdom. They did so in order that the people, submitting to the laws of the State as they submit to the laws of nature, and recognizing the same power at work in the founding of the city as in the creation of man, might obey freely and bear the yoke of the public good without resistance.

This sublime reason, which lies far beyond the grasp of ordinary people, is the reason whose decisions the legislator places in the mouths of the immortals, so that divine authority may move those whom human prudence could not. Machiavelli makes the same observation in his *Discourses on Livy*: "In truth, there has never been, in any country, an extraordinary legislator who has not had recourse to God, for otherwise his laws would not have been accepted. There are, in fact, many useful truths that a wise man may know, which do not carry within themselves reasons clear enough to convince others."

But not just anyone can make the gods speak, or win belief when he proclaims himself their interpreter. The legislator's greatness of soul is the only miracle that can prove his mission. Any man can carve tablets of stone or bribe an oracle; he can pretend to hold secret conversations with some divinity, train a bird to whisper in his ear, or find other crude ways of deceiving the people. A man whose knowledge goes no further may perhaps gather a band of fools around him, but he will never found an empire, and his extravagances will soon die with him. Empty tricks create a passing bond; only wisdom can make it last. The law of the Jews, which still endures, and the law of Ishmael's descendant, Muhammad, which for ten centuries has governed half the world, still bear witness to the great men who established them. While the pride of philosophy or the blind spirit of faction sees in them nothing more than lucky frauds, the true political thinker admires, in the institutions they created, the great and powerful genius that presides over works built to endure.

We should not conclude from this, as the English bishop William Warburton did, that politics and religion share a common purpose among us. We should conclude instead that, in the earliest periods of nations, one is used as an instrument of the other.

A people becomes famous only when its legislation begins to decline. We do not know for how many centuries the system of Lycurgus made the Spartans happy before the rest of Greece took any notice of it.

Those who know Calvin only as a theologian greatly underestimate the range of his genius. The codification of our wise Genevan edicts, in which he played a large part, does him no less honor than his *Institutes of the Christian Religion*. Whatever revolution time may bring to our religion, so long as the spirit of patriotism and liberty lives among us, the memory of this great man will be blessed forever.

### Chapter VIII: The People

Before raising a large building, an architect surveys the site and tests the ground to see whether it will bear the weight. In the same way, the wise legislator does not begin by drafting laws that are good in themselves; he first investigates whether the people for whom they are intended is fit to receive them. Plato refused to legislate for the Arcadians and the Cyrenaeans because he knew that both peoples were rich and could not tolerate equality. And in Crete, good laws and bad men were found side by side, because Minos had imposed discipline on a people already weighed down by vice.

A thousand nations have achieved worldly greatness that could never have endured good laws, and even those that could have endured them could have done so only for a very brief part of their long history. Most peoples, like most men, are teachable only in youth; as they grow old, they become incorrigible. Once customs are established and prejudices have taken root, any attempt at reform is both dangerous and futile. The people, like foolish and cowardly patients who rage at the sight of a doctor, can no longer bear to have anyone lay a hand on its faults in order to cure them.

There are, it is true, moments in the history of States when something happens to a whole people that resembles what certain illnesses do to individuals. Just as some fevers unsettle a man's mind and erase his memory of the past, periods of violence and revolution can do the same to nations, except that horror of the past takes the place of forgetting. The State, set ablaze by civil war, is reborn, as it were, from its own ashes, and emerges from the jaws of death with the renewed vigor of youth. Such was Sparta in the time of Lycurgus, Rome after the expulsion of the Tarquins, and, in modern times, Holland and Switzerland after they drove out their tyrants.

But such events are rare. They are exceptions, and their cause always lies in the particular constitution of the State concerned. They cannot even happen twice to the same people, for a people can make itself free so long as it remains barbarous, but not once its civic energy is spent. At that stage, upheavals may destroy it, but revolutions cannot restore it; it needs a master, not a liberator. Free peoples, remember this maxim: liberty may be won, but it can never be recovered.

Youth is not infancy. Nations, like men, pass through a period of youth, or maturity if you prefer, before which they should not be made subject to laws. But a people's maturity is not always easy to recognize, and if it is anticipated, the work is ruined. One people can accept discipline from its very beginning; another cannot after ten centuries. Russia will never be truly civilized, because it was civilized too soon. Peter the Great had a gift for imitation, but he lacked true genius, the kind that is creative and makes everything out of nothing. He did some good things, but most of what he did was misplaced. He saw that his people was barbarous, but he did not see that it was not yet ready for civilization. He tried to civilize it when it needed only to be toughened. His first ambition was to make his subjects into Germans or Englishmen, when he ought to have been making them into Russians; and by persuading them that they were what they were not, he prevented them from ever becoming what they might have been. A French tutor works the same way when he turns his pupil into a child prodigy who, for the rest of his life, amounts to nothing at all. The Russian Empire will aspire to conquer Europe, and will itself be conquered. The Tartars, its subjects or neighbors, will become its masters and ours, through a revolution I regard as inevitable. Indeed, all the kings of Europe are working together to hasten its arrival.

### Chapter IX: The People (continued)

Nature has set limits to the height of a well-formed man, and beyond those limits it produces only giants or dwarfs. In the same way, a State's constitution is at its best within certain limits of size: it must be neither too large to be well governed nor too small to sustain itself. Every body politic has a maximum strength that it cannot exceed and that it only loses by growing larger. Every extension of the social bond loosens it, and, as a general rule, a small State is proportionally stronger than a large one.

A thousand arguments support this principle. First, distance makes administration more difficult, just as a weight grows heavier the farther it sits from the fulcrum on a lever. Administration therefore becomes more burdensome as distances increase. Each town has its own administration, paid for by the people; each district has its own, again paid for by the people; then come the provinces, and then the great governments, satrapies, and viceroyalties, each costing more the higher you go, and always at the expense of the unfortunate people. Last of all comes the supreme administration, which outweighs all the rest. These layers of expense are a constant drain on the subjects. Far from being better governed by all these different tiers of authority, they are governed worse than if there were only a single authority over them. Meanwhile, barely enough resources remain to meet emergencies, and when the State must draw on them, it is always on the brink of ruin.

Nor is that all. Not only does the government have less energy and speed in enforcing the laws, preventing abuses, correcting wrongs, and forestalling seditious plots hatched in distant places; the people also has less affection for its rulers, whom it never sees, for its country, which seems to it as vast as the world, and for its fellow citizens, most of whom are strangers. The same laws cannot suit so many different provinces, each with its own customs, spread across the most varied climates, and incapable of tolerating a uniform government. Yet different laws breed only trouble and confusion among peoples who live under the same rulers, are in constant contact with one another, mix and intermarry, and, falling under the sway of new customs, never know whether they can call their own inheritance their own. Among such a multitude of men who do not know one another, all gathered at the seat of central administration, talent goes unnoticed, virtue unrecognized, and vice unpunished. The leaders, buried in business, see nothing for themselves; the State is governed by clerks. Finally, the measures required to uphold central authority, which all these distant officials try either to evade or to exploit, consume all the public's energy, leaving none for the people's happiness. There is scarcely enough left to defend the State when the need arises, and so a body too large for its constitution gives way and collapses under its own weight.

On the other hand, a State must secure a firm foundation if it is to remain stable and withstand the shocks it will inevitably suffer, as well as the efforts it will be forced to make to preserve itself.
