Every people exerts a kind of centrifugal force that sets it continually against the others and drives it to expand at its neighbors' expense, much like the vortices in Descartes's physics, the great whirlpools of matter he believed filled the heavens, each pressing outward against those around it. The weak therefore run the risk of being swallowed up before long, and it is almost impossible for any State to survive except by establishing a kind of equilibrium with all the others, so that the pressure upon it is roughly equal on every side.

There are, then, reasons for expansion and reasons for contraction, and no small part of a statesman's skill lies in finding the point between them that best favors the preservation of the State. In general, the reason for expansion, being merely external and relative, ought to yield to the reasons for contraction, which are internal and absolute. A strong and healthy constitution is the first thing to seek, and it is wiser to rely on the vigor that comes from good government than on the resources a vast territory provides.

It may be added that some States have been so constituted that the need for conquest was built into their very constitution, and that, to sustain themselves, they were forced to expand without end. Perhaps they congratulated themselves on this fortunate necessity. Yet it revealed to them, along with the limits of their greatness, the inevitable moment of their fall.

### Chapter X: The People (continued)

A body politic can be measured in two ways: by the extent of its territory or by the size of its population. Between these two measures there is a proper ratio that makes a State truly great. Men make up the State, and the land sustains the men. The proper ratio, therefore, is for the land to be sufficient to support its inhabitants, and for there to be as many inhabitants as the land can support. In this proportion lies the maximum strength of a given population. If there is too much land, it is hard to guard and poorly cultivated, produces more than is needed, and soon leads to defensive wars. If there is too little, the State depends on its neighbors for whatever it lacks, and this soon leads to wars of aggression. Any people whose situation leaves it no choice but trade or war is inherently weak. It depends on its neighbors and on circumstance, and its existence can never be anything but brief and precarious. Either it conquers others and changes its situation, or it is conquered and reduced to nothing. Only insignificance or greatness can keep it free.

No fixed ratio can be stated between the size of a territory and the population suited to it. Land differs in quality, in fertility, in the nature of what it produces, and in the influence of its climate, and the people who live on it differ in temperament: some, in a fertile country, consume little, while others, on harsh and unrewarding soil, consume a great deal. One must also take into account the greater or lesser fertility of women, the conditions in each country that favor or hinder population growth, and the influence the legislator can hope to exert through his institutions. The legislator, therefore, should judge not by what he sees but by what he foresees, and he should consider not so much the present size of the population as the size it ought naturally to reach. Finally, there are countless cases in which particular local conditions require or permit the acquisition of more territory than seems necessary. Thus a mountainous country will need a large territory, because its natural products, woods and pastures, require less labor; because experience shows that women there are more fertile than on the plains; and because a great expanse of slope yields only a small stretch of level ground that can be relied on for crops. A coastal country, by contrast, can manage with less, even on rocky land or nearly barren sand, because fishing makes up for much of the shortfall in produce, because the inhabitants must live closer together to fight off pirates, and because it is easier there to relieve the country of its surplus population by founding colonies.

To these conditions of lawgiving must be added one more, which cannot replace the others but renders them all useless when it is missing: the enjoyment of peace and plenty. The moment when a State puts its house in order is like the moment when a battalion is forming its ranks; it is then that the body is least able to resist and easiest to destroy. A better defense could be mounted in a state of complete disorder than at a moment of ferment, when everyone is absorbed in his own position and not in the danger. If war, famine, or sedition breaks out at this critical time, the State will inevitably be overthrown.

It is true that many governments have been established in the midst of such storms, but in those cases the governments themselves destroy the State. Usurpers always provoke or choose times of turmoil so that, under cover of public terror, they can pass destructive laws that the people would never adopt in calmer moments. The timing of a founding is one of the surest ways to tell the work of a legislator from the work of a tyrant.

What people, then, is fit to receive laws? One that is already bound together by some common origin, interest, or agreement, but has never yet borne the true yoke of law. One with no deeply rooted customs or superstitions. One in no danger of being overwhelmed by sudden invasion, which can stay out of its neighbors' quarrels, resist each of them on its own, or enlist the help of one to drive off another. One in which every member can be known by every other, and no man need be given a burden too heavy for a man to bear. One that can do without other peoples, and that all other peoples can do without. One that is neither rich nor poor, but self-sufficient. And, finally, one that combines the steadiness of an ancient people with the teachability of a new one. Legislation is made difficult less by what must be built than by what must be torn down, and success is rare because natural simplicity is so seldom found together with the needs of society. All these conditions are seldom found together, and that is why so few States have good constitutions.

Consider the requirement that two peoples should not depend on each other. If one of two neighboring peoples could not do without the other, its position would be very hard for the first and very dangerous for the second. Any wise nation in such a case would hasten to free the other from its dependence. The Republic of Tlaxcala, enclosed within the Mexican Empire, chose to go without salt rather than buy it from the Mexicans, or even accept it from them as a gift. The Tlaxcalans were wise enough to see the trap hidden beneath such generosity. They kept their freedom, and that small State, shut up inside that great Empire, became in the end the instrument of the Empire's ruin.

There is still one country in Europe capable of receiving laws: Corsica. The courage and persistence with which that brave people has won back and defended its liberty fully deserve that some wise man should teach it how to preserve what it has won. I have a feeling that one day that little island will astonish Europe.

### Chapter XI: The Various Systems of Legislation

If we ask what exactly constitutes the greatest good of all, which ought to be the goal of every system of legislation, we will find that it comes down to two principal objects: liberty and equality. Liberty, because every form of private dependence subtracts that much strength from the body of the State; and equality, because liberty cannot exist without it.

I have already defined civil liberty. By equality, we should not understand that everyone must have exactly the same degree of power and wealth. We should understand that no one's power should ever be great enough to be used for violence, and that power should always be exercised by virtue of rank and law; and, with respect to wealth, that no citizen should ever be rich enough to buy another, and none so poor as to be forced to sell himself. This requires moderation of property and position on the part of the great, and moderation of greed and envy on the part of the common people.

If the aim is to give the State stability, the two extremes should be brought as close together as possible: allow neither the very rich nor beggars. These two conditions, which naturally go together, are equally fatal to the common good. From the one come the supporters of tyranny, and from the other, tyrants. It is always between these two that public liberty is put up for auction: one buys, and the other sells.

Such equality, we are told, is an impractical ideal that cannot exist in reality. But if its abuse is inevitable, does it follow that we should not at least regulate it? It is precisely because the force of circumstances always tends to destroy equality that the force of legislation should always tend to preserve it.

These general objects of every good system of legislation must, however, be adapted in each country to its local situation and to the character of its inhabitants, and these circumstances should determine, in each case, the particular set of institutions that is best, not perhaps in itself, but for the State for which it is intended. If, for instance, the soil is barren and unproductive, or the land too crowded for its population, the people should turn to industry and the crafts, and trade what they produce for the goods they lack. If, on the other hand, a people lives on rich plains and fertile hillsides, or occupies good land with too few inhabitants, it should devote all its attention to agriculture, which causes men to multiply, and should banish the crafts, which would only depopulate the country by concentrating its few inhabitants in a handful of places. If a nation lives along a long and accessible coastline, let it cover the sea with ships and cultivate trade and navigation; its life will be short and glorious. If its shores are washed by a sea that touches only nearly inaccessible rocks, let it remain barbarous and live on fish; its life will be quieter, perhaps better, and certainly happier. In short, beyond the principles common to all, every nation contains within itself something that gives those principles a particular application and makes its legislation distinctly its own. Thus, among the Jews of old and more recently among the Arabs, the chief object was religion; among the Athenians, letters; at Carthage and Tyre, commerce; at Rhodes, seafaring; at Sparta, war; and at Rome, virtue. Montesquieu, the author of *The Spirit of the Laws*, has shown through many examples the art by which the legislator directs the constitution toward each of these ends.

The Marquis d'Argenson made a related point about trade: "Any branch of foreign commerce brings, on the whole, only an apparent advantage to the kingdom in general. It may enrich some individuals, or even some towns, but the nation as a whole gains nothing by it, and the people is no better off."

What makes the constitution of a State truly solid and lasting is a careful respect for what is fitting, so that natural conditions and the laws always agree on every point, and the law does no more, so to speak, than secure, accompany, and correct them. But if the legislator mistakes his object and adopts a principle different from the one circumstances naturally point to, if his principle leads toward servitude while circumstances lead toward liberty, toward wealth while they lead toward population, or toward peace while they lead toward conquest, then the laws will gradually lose their force, the constitution will change, and the State will know no rest from trouble until it is either destroyed or transformed, and nature has resumed her invincible rule.

### Chapter XII: The Division of the Laws

If the whole is to be put in order, and the commonwealth given the best possible form, several distinct relations must be taken into account.

First, there is the action of the whole body upon itself: the relation of the whole to the whole, or of the Sovereign to the State. As we shall see, this relation is made up of the relations among the intermediate terms.

The laws that govern this relation are called political laws. They are also called fundamental laws, and not without reason, provided they are wise. If each State has only one good system, then a people that possesses it should hold fast to it. But if the established order is bad, why should laws that prevent people from being good be treated as fundamental? In any case, a people is always free to change its laws, however good they may be; for if it chooses to do itself harm, who has the right to stop it?

The second relation is that of the members to one another, or to the body as a whole. The first of these should be as slight as possible and the second as strong as possible, so that each citizen is entirely independent of all the others and at the same time thoroughly dependent on the city. Both conditions are always secured by the same means, since only the strength of the State can guarantee the liberty of its members. Civil laws arise from this second relation.

We may also consider a third kind of relation, between the individual and the law: the relation of disobedience to its penalty. This gives rise to criminal laws, which at bottom are less a separate class of law than the sanction standing behind all the others.

Alongside these three kinds of law there is a fourth, the most important of all. It is engraved not on tablets of marble or bronze but on the hearts of the citizens. It forms the true constitution of the State. It gains new strength every day, and when other laws grow old or die out, it revives them or takes their place. It keeps a people on the path it was meant to follow, and by imperceptible degrees it replaces authority with the force of habit. I mean morality, custom, and above all public opinion. Political thinkers have overlooked this power, yet success in everything else depends upon it. The great legislator attends to it in secret, even while he appears to confine himself to particular regulations. Those regulations are only the curved span of the arch. Morals and customs, which take longer to form, become in the end its immovable keystone, the stone at the crown that locks the whole structure in place.

Of these different classes of law, only the political laws, which determine the form of government, bear on my subject.

## Book III

Before discussing the different forms of government, let us try to fix the exact meaning of the word, which has never yet been explained with much clarity.

### Chapter I: Government in General

I warn the reader that this chapter must be read with care, and that I cannot make myself clear to anyone who refuses to pay attention.

Every free action is produced by two causes working together. One is moral: the will that decides on the act. The other is physical: the power that carries it out. When I walk toward an object, I must first will to go there, and my feet must then carry me. If a paralyzed man wills to run and an able-bodied man wills not to, both will stay where they are. The body politic has the same two motive powers. In it, too, force and will are distinguished: will under the name of legislative power, and force under the name of executive power. Nothing is done, or should be done, without the two acting together.

We have seen that legislative power belongs to the people and can belong to the people alone. From the principles already laid down, it is equally plain that executive power cannot belong to the general body acting as legislature or Sovereign. Executive power consists entirely of particular acts, which lie outside the scope of law and therefore outside the competence of the Sovereign, whose acts must always be laws.

The public force therefore needs an agent of its own to bring it together and put it to work under the direction of the general will. This agent provides a means of communication between the State and the Sovereign, and does for the collective person roughly what the union of soul and body does for a human being. Here lies the basis of government within the State. Government is often wrongly confused with the Sovereign, whose minister it is.

What, then, is government? It is an intermediate body set up between the subjects and the Sovereign to keep them in mutual correspondence. It is charged with executing the laws and with maintaining liberty, both civil and political.

The members of this body are called magistrates or kings, that is, governors, and the body as a whole bears the name of prince. At Venice, for example, the governing College is addressed as "Most Serene Prince" even when the Doge is absent. Those who hold that the act by which a people places itself under a prince is not a contract are therefore entirely right. It is simply and solely a commission, a form of employment, in which the rulers, as mere officials of the Sovereign, exercise in their own name the power that the Sovereign has placed in their keeping. The Sovereign can limit, modify, or take back this power whenever it pleases, because surrendering such a right outright would be incompatible with the nature of the social body and contrary to the purpose of association.

I therefore call government, or supreme administration, the legitimate exercise of executive power; and I call prince or magistrate the person or body entrusted with that administration.

Government contains the intermediate forces whose relations make up the relation of the whole to the whole, or of the Sovereign to the State. This last relation can be represented as the relation between the two outer terms of a continuous proportion, in which the first term stands to the middle as the middle stands to the last, with government as the mean proportional between them. The government receives from the Sovereign the orders it gives to the people. For the State to be properly balanced, there must be, when everything is taken into account, an equality between the product or power of the government taken by itself (the middle term multiplied by itself) and the product or power of the citizens, who are sovereign in one respect and subjects in another.

Moreover, none of these three terms can be altered without destroying that equality at once. If the Sovereign tries to govern, or the magistrate tries to make laws, or the subjects refuse to obey, disorder replaces regularity. Force and will no longer act in concert, and the State dissolves into despotism or anarchy. Finally, since any given ratio has only one mean proportional, there is only one good government possible for a State at any one time. But because countless events can alter a people's circumstances, different governments may be good not only for different peoples but also for the same people at different times.

To give some idea of the various relations that can hold between these two outer terms, I shall take as my example the size of a people's population, since it is the easiest to express in numbers.

Suppose the State is made up of ten thousand citizens. The Sovereign can only be considered collectively, as a body, while each member, as a subject, is regarded as an individual. The Sovereign therefore stands to the subject as ten thousand to one. Each member of the State holds only a ten-thousandth share of the sovereign authority, yet is wholly subject to it. If the population grows to a hundred thousand, the subject's position does not change: each person remains entirely under the authority of the laws, while his vote, now reduced to a hundred-thousandth share, has one-tenth as much influence in framing them. Since the subject always remains a single unit, the ratio between him and the Sovereign grows as the number of citizens grows. It follows that the larger the State, the less liberty it allows.

When I say the ratio grows, I mean that it becomes more unequal. The greater the ratio in the geometric sense, the less relation there is in the ordinary sense of the word. In the geometric sense, a ratio is considered in terms of quantity and expressed by the quotient; in the ordinary sense, it is considered in terms of identity and judged by likeness.

Now, the less the particular wills correspond to the general will, that is, the less morals and customs correspond to the laws, the more the force of restraint must be increased. To be good, therefore, a government must be relatively stronger as the people becomes more numerous.

At the same time, as the State grows, those entrusted with public authority meet with more temptations and more opportunities to abuse their power. So the more force the government needs to keep the people in check, the more force the Sovereign needs in turn to keep the government in check. I am speaking here not of absolute force but of the relative force of the different parts of the State.

It follows from this double relation that the continuous proportion among the Sovereign, the prince, and the people is by no means an arbitrary notion; it is a necessary consequence of the nature of the body politic. It also follows that, since one of the outer terms, the people as subject, is fixed and represented by the number one, whenever the duplicate ratio increases or decreases, the simple ratio increases or decreases with it and changes accordingly. From this we see that there is no single, unique, and absolute form of government, but as many governments differing in nature as there are States differing in size.

Someone might mock this system by saying that, on my account, to find the mean proportional and give shape to the body of government one need only take the square root of the population. I would answer that I use this number here only as an example. The relations I am describing are measured not by the number of people alone but, more generally, by the quantity of action, which is the combined result of a multitude of causes. And although I borrow the language of geometry for a moment to save words, I am well aware that moral quantities do not admit of geometric precision.

Government is, on a small scale, what the body politic that contains it is on a large one. It is a moral person endowed with certain faculties, active like the Sovereign and passive like the State, and it can itself be broken down into further relations of the same kind. This produces a new proportion, and within it yet another, according to the arrangement of the various magistracies, until we arrive at an indivisible middle term: a single ruler or supreme magistrate. He can be pictured at the center of this progression as the unit that stands between the series of fractions and the series of whole numbers.

Without burdening ourselves with this multiplication of terms, let us be content to regard government as a new body within the State, distinct from both the people and the Sovereign and intermediate between them.

There is one essential difference between these two bodies: the State exists by itself, whereas the government exists only through the Sovereign. The ruling will of the prince is, or ought to be, nothing but the general will, that is, the law. His force is only the public force concentrated in his hands. As soon as he tries to base some absolute and independent act on his own authority, the bond that holds the whole together begins to loosen. If the prince should eventually come to have a particular will more active than the will of the Sovereign, and should use the public force in his hands to carry out that particular will, there would be, so to speak, two Sovereigns, one legitimate and the other actual. At that moment the social union would vanish, and the body politic would be dissolved.

Nevertheless, if the government is to have a true existence and a real life that distinguish it from the body of the State, and if its members are to act in concert and fulfill the purpose for which it was established, it must have a particular identity of its own: a sensibility shared by its members, and a force and will of its own directed toward its preservation. This separate existence implies assemblies, councils, the power to deliberate and decide, and rights, titles, and privileges belonging exclusively to the prince, which make the office of magistrate more honorable in proportion as it is more burdensome. The difficulty lies in arranging this subordinate whole within the larger whole so that it never alters the general constitution by asserting its own. It must always keep separate the particular force it possesses, which is meant to help preserve itself, from the public force, which is meant to preserve the State. In short, it must always be ready to sacrifice the government to the people, and never the people to the government.

Furthermore, although the artificial body of the government is the creation of another artificial body, and has, as it were, only a borrowed and subordinate life, it can still act with greater or lesser vigor and speed, and enjoy, so to speak, better or worse health. Finally, without departing outright from the purpose for which it was established, it may stray from that purpose to a greater or lesser degree, depending on how it is constituted.

All these differences give rise to the various relations that the government ought to bear to the body of the State, according to the accidental and particular relations by which the State itself is modified. Often the government that is best in itself becomes the most harmful once its relations have shifted to match the defects of the body politic to which it belongs.

### Chapter II: The Constituent Principle in the Various Forms of Government

To explain the general cause of these differences, we must distinguish here between the government and its principle, just as we earlier distinguished between the State and the Sovereign.

The body of the magistrate may have more members or fewer. We said that the ratio of the Sovereign to the subjects grows as the people becomes more numerous, and by a clear analogy we can say the same of the ratio of the government to the magistrates.

But the total force of the government, being always the force of the State, does not change. The more of this force it spends on its own members, therefore, the less it has left to apply to the people as a whole.

It follows that the more numerous the magistrates, the weaker the government. Since this principle is fundamental, we must do our best to make it clear.

In the person of the magistrate we can distinguish three essentially different wills. The first is the individual's private will, which aims only at his personal advantage. The second is the common will of the magistrates, which concerns only the advantage of the prince. This may be called the corporate will: it is general with respect to the government, but particular with respect to the State, of which the government is a part. The third is the will of the people, or the sovereign will, which is general both with respect to the State considered as the whole and with respect to the government considered as a part of that whole.

In a perfect system of legislation, the individual or particular will should count for nothing; the corporate will of the government should hold a very subordinate place; and the general or sovereign will should therefore always prevail and be the sole guide of all the others.

In the natural order, however, these wills grow more active the more concentrated they are. The general will is thus always the weakest, the corporate will comes second, and the individual will is the strongest of all. Within the government, each member is first himself, then a magistrate, and only then a citizen: exactly the reverse of the order the social system requires.

Granting this, if the entire government is in the hands of one man, the particular will and the corporate will are completely fused, and the corporate will consequently reaches its highest possible intensity. Since the use made of force depends on the degree of will behind it, and since the absolute force of the government never changes, it follows that the most active government is government by one man.

Suppose, on the other hand, that we join the government to the legislative authority, making the Sovereign the prince as well and every citizen a magistrate. The corporate will, now merged with the general will, can be no more active than the general will is, and it leaves the particular will at full strength. The government, still possessing the same absolute force, will then be at the lowest point of its relative force, or activity.

These relations are beyond dispute, and further considerations confirm them. We can see, for instance, that each magistrate is more active within his body than each citizen is within his, and that the particular will therefore has far more influence on the acts of the government than on those of the Sovereign. Each magistrate is almost always charged with some governmental function, whereas no citizen, taken individually, exercises any function of sovereignty. Furthermore, as a State grows larger, its real force increases, though not in direct proportion to its size. But if the State stays the same, the number of magistrates can increase indefinitely without the government gaining any real force, since its force is the force of the State, whose size has not changed. The government's relative force, or activity, therefore declines, while its absolute, or real, force cannot increase.

It is also certain that execution becomes slower as more people are put in charge of it. When too much weight is given to prudence, too little is left to chance; opportunities slip away, and deliberation ends by losing the very thing it was meant to secure.

I have just shown that the government grows slack as the number of magistrates increases, and I showed earlier that the more numerous the people, the greater the force of restraint must be. It follows that the ratio of the magistrates to the government should vary inversely with the ratio of the subjects to the Sovereign. In other terms, the larger the State, the more tightly the government should be drawn together, so that the number of rulers decreases as the population increases.

I should add that I am speaking here of the government's relative strength, not of its rectitude. For on the other side, the more numerous the magistracy, the closer the corporate will comes to the general will, whereas under a single magistrate the corporate will is, as I said, nothing more than a particular will. What is gained on one side is thus lost on the other. The legislator's art lies in knowing how to fix the point at which the government's force and its will, which always stand in inverse proportion, combine in the ratio most advantageous to the State.

### Chapter III: The Division of Governments

The previous chapter showed why the various kinds or forms of government are distinguished by the number of members composing them. It remains to show in this chapter how the division is made.

First, the Sovereign may entrust the government to the whole people or to a majority of it, so that more citizens are magistrates than are simple private individuals. This form of government is called democracy.

Alternatively, it may confine the government to a small number, so that private citizens outnumber magistrates. This form is called aristocracy.

Finally, it may concentrate the entire government in the hands of a single magistrate, from whom all the others derive their power. This third form is the most common, and it is called monarchy, or royal government.

All these forms, or at least the first two, admit of degrees, and even of very wide variation. Democracy may embrace the whole people or be limited to half of it. Aristocracy, in turn, may be narrowed indefinitely, from half the people down to the smallest possible number. Even royalty can be shared to some extent. Sparta always had two kings, as its constitution required, and the Roman Empire at one point had as many as eight emperors at once, though no one could say that the Empire had been divided. There is thus a point at which each form of government shades into the next, and it becomes clear that, under three broad names, government can in fact take as many different forms as the State has citizens.

There are even more. Since the government can also, in certain respects, be divided into parts, each administered in a different way, combining the three forms can produce a multitude of mixed forms, each of which can be multiplied again by all the simple forms.

People have always argued about the best form of government without recognizing that each form is the best in some cases and the worst in others.

If the number of supreme magistrates in different States should be in inverse proportion to the number of citizens, it follows that, in general, democratic government suits small States, aristocratic government suits States of middling size, and monarchy suits large ones. This rule follows directly from the principle already established. But it is impossible to enumerate the countless circumstances that may produce exceptions to it.

### Chapter IV: Democracy

The one who makes the law knows better than anyone how it should be carried out and interpreted. It would seem, then, that no constitution could be better than one in which the executive and legislative powers are united. Yet that very union makes such a government inadequate in certain respects, because things that ought to be kept separate are merged. With the prince and the Sovereign being the same person, the result is, so to speak, a government without government.

It is not good for the one who makes the laws to carry them out, nor for the people as a body to turn its attention away from general considerations and toward particular matters. Nothing is more dangerous than the influence of private interests in public affairs. The abuse of the laws by the government is a lesser evil than the corruption of the legislator, which follows inevitably from attending to particular concerns. When that happens, the State is altered in its very substance, and all reform becomes impossible. A people that would never misuse the powers of government would never misuse its independence either; a people that would always govern well would have no need to be governed.

If we take the term in its strict sense, there has never been a true democracy, and there never will be. It runs against the natural order for the many to govern and the few to be governed. One cannot imagine the people remaining permanently assembled to attend to public affairs, and it is plain that they cannot appoint commissions for that purpose without changing the form of the administration.

Indeed, I can confidently lay it down as a principle that when the functions of government are divided among several bodies, the smallest of them will sooner or later acquire the greatest authority, if only because they are able to dispatch business quickly, and power therefore passes naturally into their hands.

Besides, how many conditions, each difficult to reconcile with the others, such a government presupposes! First, a very small State, where the people can be gathered together easily and where every citizen can readily know all the others. Second, great simplicity of manners, so that business does not multiply and give rise to thorny questions. Next, a large measure of equality in rank and fortune, without which equality of rights and authority cannot long survive. Finally, little or no luxury, for luxury either springs from wealth or makes wealth necessary. It corrupts rich and poor alike, the rich through possession and the poor through covetousness. It sells the nation to softness and vanity, and it strips the State of all its citizens, making them slaves to one another and, every one of them, slaves to public opinion.

This is why a famous writer made virtue the fundamental principle of republics, since none of these conditions could exist without virtue. But for want of the necessary distinctions, that great thinker was often imprecise and sometimes obscure. He failed to see that, because the sovereign authority is everywhere the same, the same principle ought to be found in every well-constituted State. It will be present to a greater or lesser degree, admittedly, depending on the form of the government.

It may be added that no government is so prone to civil war and internal unrest as democratic or popular government, because none has so strong and constant a tendency to change into another form, and none demands more vigilance and courage to keep it as it is. Under such a constitution above all, the citizen must arm himself with strength and steadfastness, and repeat every day of his life what a virtuous Count Palatine declared before the Diet of Poland. This was the Palatine of Posen, father of the King of Poland who later became Duke of Lorraine, and his words were: *Malo periculosam libertatem quam quietum servitium*, "I prefer liberty with danger to peace with slavery."

If there were a nation of gods, it would govern itself democratically. So perfect a government is not suited to men.

### Chapter V: Aristocracy

Here we have two entirely distinct moral persons, the government and the Sovereign, and consequently two general wills. One is general in relation to all the citizens; the other is general only in relation to the members of the administration. Thus, although the government may arrange its internal affairs as it sees fit, it can never address the people except in the name of the Sovereign, which is to say in the name of the people itself, and this must never be forgotten.

The first societies governed themselves aristocratically. The heads of families deliberated together on public matters, and the young deferred without question to the authority of experience. Hence such titles as *priests*, *elders*, *senate*, and *gerontes*, the Greek word for old men. The savages of North America still govern themselves this way, and their government is admirable.

But as the artificial inequality produced by institutions came to outweigh natural inequality, wealth or power was ranked above age, and aristocracy became elective. The ancients' own vocabulary shows the shift: the word *optimates* meant among them not "the best" but "the most powerful." Finally, the passing of a father's power to his children along with his property created patrician families, made government hereditary, and produced senators of twenty.

There are, then, three kinds of aristocracy: natural, elective, and hereditary. The first suits only simple peoples; the third is the worst of all governments; the second is the best, and it is aristocracy in the proper sense of the word.

Besides the advantage of keeping the two powers distinct, elective aristocracy has the further advantage of choosing its members. In popular government all citizens are born magistrates, but here magistracy is confined to a few, who attain it only through election. In this way integrity, understanding, experience, and every other claim to distinction and public esteem become so many additional guarantees of wise government. For this reason, it is of the greatest importance that the manner of electing magistrates be fixed by law. If it is left to the discretion of the prince, a slide into hereditary aristocracy cannot be avoided, as happened in the republics of Venice and Berne. Venice has consequently long been a State in dissolution. Berne, however, is preserved by the extraordinary wisdom of its senate, and it remains an honorable but highly dangerous exception.

Moreover, assemblies are more easily convened, business is better discussed and conducted with greater order and diligence, and the State's reputation abroad is better upheld by venerable senators than by a multitude that is unknown or despised.

In short, it is the best and most natural arrangement for the wisest to govern the multitude, provided it is certain that they will govern for its benefit and not for their own. There is no need to multiply the machinery of government, or to have twenty thousand men do what a hundred chosen men can do even better. But it must not be forgotten that here corporate interest begins to direct the public power with less regard to the rule of the general will, and that a further, inevitable tendency withdraws part of the executive power from the control of the laws.

As for the particular conditions this form requires, the State should be neither so small, nor its people so simple and upright, that the execution of the laws follows directly from the public will, as it does in a good democracy. Nor should the nation be so large that its rulers must disperse to govern it, and can each play the Sovereign in his own province, beginning by making themselves independent and ending by making themselves masters.

But if aristocracy does not require all the virtues that popular government demands, it requires others peculiar to itself, such as moderation among the rich and contentment among the poor. Strict equality seems out of place in it; it was not found even in Sparta.

Furthermore, if this form of government entails a certain inequality of fortune, that inequality is justified so that the administration of public affairs may, as a rule, be entrusted to those best able to devote all their time to it. It is not justified, as Aristotle maintains, so that the rich may always be preferred. On the contrary, it is important that an opposite choice should occasionally teach the people that personal merit offers weightier claims to preference than wealth.

### Chapter VI: Monarchy

Up to this point we have considered the prince as a moral and collective person, unified by the force of the laws and entrusted with the executive power of the State. We must now consider this power when it is gathered into the hands of a natural person, a real man, who alone has the right to exercise it in accordance with the laws. Such a person is called a monarch or king.

In the other forms of administration, a collective being represents an individual; in this form, an individual represents a collective being. The moral unity that constitutes the prince is therefore at the same time a physical unity, and all the qualities that the law can bring together only with difficulty in the other forms are here found naturally united.

Thus the will of the people, the will of the prince, the public force of the State, and the particular force of the government all answer to a single moving power. All the springs of the machine are in the same hands, and the whole moves toward the same end. There are no opposing motions to cancel one another out, and no constitution can be imagined in which a smaller effort produces a greater amount of action. Archimedes, as the story goes, sat calmly on the shore and, with a system of pulleys, easily drew a great ship afloat by himself. To my mind he represents a skillful monarch who governs vast states from his study and sets everything in motion while appearing motionless himself.

But if no government has more vigor than this one, there is also none in which the particular will holds greater sway and more easily dominates the rest. Everything does indeed move toward the same end, but that end is by no means the public happiness, and the very strength of the administration continually works to the detriment of the State.

Kings wish to be absolute, and from a distance men keep crying out to them that the best way to become so is to make themselves loved by their people. The maxim is very fine, and even in some respects very true. Unfortunately, it will always be laughed at in court. The power that comes from a people's love is undoubtedly the greatest, but it is precarious and conditional, and princes will never be satisfied with it. The best kings want to be able to do wrong, if they please, without ceasing to be masters. Political moralizers may tell them as often as they like that, since the people's strength is their own, their first interest is for the people to be prosperous, numerous, and formidable. Kings know perfectly well that this is untrue. Their first personal interest is for the people to be weak, wretched, and incapable of resisting them. I grant that, if the subjects remained always submissive, the prince's interest would indeed be for the people to be powerful, so that its power, being his own, would make him formidable to his neighbors. But since this interest is merely secondary and subordinate, and since strength is incompatible with submission, princes naturally always prefer the principle that offers them the more immediate advantage. This is what the prophet Samuel forcefully set before the Hebrews when they demanded a king, and what Machiavelli has clearly demonstrated. Machiavelli claimed to be teaching kings, but it was the people he really taught. His *Prince* is the handbook of republicans.

Machiavelli was an honest man and a good citizen, but, being attached to the court of the Medici, he could not help veiling his love of liberty amid the oppression of his country. His choice of so detestable a hero as Cesare Borgia reveals his hidden aim clearly enough, and the contradiction between the teaching of *The Prince* and that of his *Discourses on Livy* and *History of Florence* shows that this profound political thinker has so far been studied only by superficial or corrupt readers. The papal court in Rome sternly banned his book. I can well believe it, for that court is the one it portrays most clearly.

We have found on general grounds that monarchy suits only large States, and this is confirmed when we examine it on its own terms. The more numerous the public administration, the smaller the ratio between prince and subjects becomes, and the closer it approaches equality, so that in a democracy the ratio is one to one, which is absolute equality. Conversely, as the government shrinks in number, the ratio increases, reaching its maximum when the government rests in the hands of a single person. There is then too great a distance between prince and people, and the State lacks a bond of union. To create such a bond there must be intermediate orders, with princes, great personages, and a nobility to fill them. But none of this suits a small State, which is ruined by every distinction of class.

If, however, it is difficult for a large State to be well governed, it is far more difficult for it to be well governed by a single man, and everyone knows what happens when kings appoint others to govern in their place.

An essential and unavoidable flaw, which will always rank monarchy below republican government, is that in a republic the public voice almost never raises to the highest offices any but enlightened and capable men, who fill those offices with honor. In monarchies, by contrast, those who rise to the top are most often nothing but petty blunderers, petty swindlers, and petty intriguers, whose petty talents carry them to the highest positions at court, but which, once they arrive, only expose their incompetence to the public. The people is far less often mistaken in its choices than the prince, and a man of real merit among a king's ministers is almost as rare as a fool at the head of a republican government. So when, by some lucky chance, one of these born rulers takes the helm of State in a monarchy nearly ruined by swarms of so-called "gentleman" administrators, everyone is astonished at the resources he finds, and his arrival marks an epoch in his country's history.

For a monarchical State to have any chance of being well governed, its population and extent must be proportioned to the abilities of the one who governs it. It is easier to conquer than to rule. With a long enough lever, a single finger could move the world; to hold the world up requires the shoulders of Hercules, who in the myth took the weight of the heavens from Atlas. However small a State may be, its prince is almost never big enough for it. When, on the other hand, the State happens to be too small for its ruler, it is badly governed in these rare cases too. The ruler, forever pursuing his grand designs, forgets the people's interests, and he makes them no less miserable by misusing the talents he has than a lesser ruler would by lacking the talents he had not. A kingdom ought, so to speak, to expand or contract with each reign according to the prince's abilities; but because the abilities of a senate are more constant in measure, a republic can keep permanent borders without its administration suffering.

The disadvantage most keenly felt in monarchical government is the lack of the continuous succession that, in the other two forms, provides an unbroken bond of union. When one king dies, another is needed. Elections leave dangerous intervals and are stormy affairs, and unless the citizens possess a disinterestedness and integrity that very seldom accompany this kind of government, intrigue and corruption run rampant. The man to whom the State has sold itself can hardly avoid selling it in his turn, recovering at the expense of the weak the money that the powerful have extorted from him. Under such an administration, venality sooner or later spreads through every part, and the peace enjoyed under such a king is worse than the disorder of an interregnum.

What has been done to prevent these evils? Crowns have been made hereditary in certain families, and an order of succession has been established to prevent disputes when kings die. That is, the drawbacks of regencies have been substituted for those of elections, apparent calm has been preferred to wise administration, and men have chosen to risk having children, monsters, or imbeciles as rulers rather than having to quarrel over the choice of good kings. No one has considered that by exposing ourselves to such risks, we stack nearly all the odds against ourselves. There was sound sense in what the younger Dionysius of Syracuse said to his father, who had seized power himself. When the father reproached his son for some shameful act, asking, "Did I set you that example?" the son replied, "No, but your father was not a king."

Everything conspires to strip a man placed in authority over others of his sense of justice and reason. Great pains are taken, we are told, to teach young princes the art of reigning, yet their education seems to do them no good. It would be better to begin by teaching them the art of obeying. The greatest kings whose praises history sings were not raised to reign. Reigning is a science we are never further from mastering than when we have studied too much of it, and one we acquire better by obeying than by commanding. As Tacitus writes in his *Histories*: *Nam utilissimus idem ac brevissimus bonarum malarumque rerum delectus cogitare quid aut nolueris sub alio principe, aut volueris*, "For the best and also the shortest way of distinguishing good from bad is to consider what you would have wished, or not wished, to happen had someone other than you been emperor."

One consequence of this lack of continuity is the instability of royal government. Guided now by one plan and now by another, according to the character of the reigning prince or of those who reign on his behalf, it cannot long maintain a fixed aim or a consistent course. This variability, absent from the other forms of government, where the prince is always the same, keeps the State forever shifting from principle to principle and from project to project. So we may say that, in general, while a court may be more cunning in intrigue, a senate has more wisdom, and republics pursue their ends by steadier and better-considered policies. In a monarchy, by contrast, every upheaval in the royal ministry produces an upheaval in the State, for the maxim shared by all ministers and nearly all kings is to do in every respect the opposite of what their predecessors did.

This same incoherence also exposes a fallacy much favored by royalist political writers. Not only do they compare civil government to the government of a household, and the prince to the father of a family, an error already refuted, but they freely credit the prince with every virtue he ought to have and assume he is always what he should be. Once this assumption is made, royal government is plainly preferable to all others, since it is unquestionably the strongest, and it needs only a corporate will more in keeping with the general will to be the best as well.

But if, as Plato says in the *Statesman*, the "king by nature" is so rare a figure, how often will nature and fortune combine to crown him? And if a royal upbringing necessarily corrupts those who receive it, what can be hoped from a succession of men raised to reign? It is willful self-deception, then, to confuse royal government with government by a good king. To see this form of government as it truly is, we must consider it under princes who are incompetent or wicked, for either they will come to the throne that way, or the throne will make them so.

These difficulties have not escaped our writers, yet they are not troubled by them. The remedy, they say, is to obey without complaint: God sends bad kings in His wrath, and they must be endured as scourges from Heaven. Such talk is no doubt edifying, but it belongs in a pulpit rather than in a book on politics. What should we think of a physician who promises miracles and whose entire art consists in exhorting the sufferer to be patient? We know well enough that we must put up with a bad government when we have one; the question is how to find a good one.

### Chapter VII: Mixed Governments

Strictly speaking, there is no such thing as a simple government. A single ruler must have subordinate magistrates; a popular government must have a head. In the distribution of executive power, therefore, there is always a gradation from the larger number to the smaller, with this difference: sometimes the larger number depends on the smaller, and sometimes the smaller depends on the larger.

Sometimes the distribution is equal. This happens either when the constituent parts depend on one another, as in the government of England, or when the authority of each part is independent but incomplete, as in Poland. This latter form is bad, because it gives the government no unity and leaves the State without a bond of union.

Is a simple or a mixed government better? Political writers are forever debating the question, and it must be answered as we have already answered the similar question about all forms of government.

Simple government is better in itself, precisely because it is simple. But when the executive power is not sufficiently dependent on the legislative power, that is, when the prince stands in a closer relation to the Sovereign than the people do to the prince, this lack of proportion must be corrected by dividing the government. All its parts then retain no less authority over the subjects, while their division makes them, taken together, weaker against the Sovereign.

The same disadvantage can also be prevented by appointing intermediate magistrates, who leave the government intact and whose sole effect is to balance the two powers and uphold their respective rights. The government is then not mixed but tempered.

The opposite disadvantages can be remedied in a similar way: when the government is too slack, tribunals may be established to concentrate it. This is done in every democracy. In the first case the government is divided to weaken it; in the second, to strengthen it. For the extremes of both strength and weakness are found in simple governments, while the mixed forms yield a middling strength.

### Chapter VIII: That All Forms of Government Do Not Suit All Countries

Liberty, not being a fruit that grows in every climate, is not within the reach of every people. The longer one reflects on this principle, which Montesquieu laid down, the more its truth is felt; the more it is disputed, the more opportunity there is to confirm it with fresh proofs.

In every government that exists, the public person consumes without producing. Where, then, does it obtain what it consumes? From the labor of its members. The needs of the public are supplied from the surplus of individuals. It follows that the civil State can survive only so long as men's labor yields them more than they themselves need.

This surplus is not the same in every country. In some it is considerable, in others moderate, in others nonexistent, and in some even negative. The ratio of production to subsistence depends on the fertility of the climate, the kind of labor the land requires, the nature of its products, the strength of its inhabitants, the greater or lesser consumption they find necessary, and several other factors that together make up the whole relation.

On the other side, not all governments are of the same nature. Some are less voracious than others, and the differences among them rest on this second principle: the farther public contributions travel from their source, the more burdensome they become.

The burden should be measured not by the amount of the taxes, but by the distance they must travel to return to the people who paid them. When that circulation is quick and well established, it makes no difference whether much or little is paid: the people remains rich, and the finances are sound. But however little the people gives, if that little never comes back, the constant giving soon exhausts it. The State is then never rich, and the people is always a nation of beggars.

It follows that the greater the distance between the people and the government, the heavier taxation becomes. In a democracy the people bears the lightest burden; in an aristocracy, a heavier one; in a monarchy, the heaviest of all. Monarchy therefore suits only wealthy nations, aristocracy suits States of moderate size and wealth, and democracy suits States that are small and poor.

The more one reflects on it, the more clearly the difference between free States and monarchies appears. In free States, everything is directed toward the common good. In monarchies, the strength of the public and the strength of private individuals work against each other, so that one grows only as the other weakens. In the end, despotism does not govern its subjects in order to make them happy; it makes them miserable in order to govern them.

In every climate, then, there are natural causes by which we can determine the form of government that climate requires, and we can even say what kind of inhabitants it ought to have.

Hostile and barren lands, where the yield does not repay the labor, ought to remain deserted and uncultivated, or else be peopled only by savages. Lands where human labor produces no more than the bare minimum needed to survive ought to be inhabited by barbarous peoples, and in such places no political order is possible. Lands where the surplus of produce over labor is moderate are suited to free peoples. Lands where the soil is rich and fertile, yielding a great harvest for little labor, call for monarchical government, so that the subjects' surplus may be consumed by the luxury of the prince. It is better for this excess to be absorbed by the government than squandered among private individuals. I know there are exceptions, but those exceptions confirm the rule, for sooner or later they produce revolutions that restore things to their natural order.

General laws must always be distinguished from particular causes that may modify their effects. Even if the entire South were covered with republics and the entire North with despotic States, it would remain true that, as a matter of climate, despotism suits hot countries, barbarism suits cold ones, and good political order suits temperate regions. I also recognize that, once the principle is granted, its application may still be disputed. Some will object that there are cold countries that are very fertile and tropical countries that are very unproductive. But this difficulty exists only for those who fail to consider the question from every side. As I have already said, we must take into account labor, strength, consumption, and similar factors.

Take two tracts of land of equal size, one yielding five and the other ten. If the inhabitants of the first consume four and those of the second consume nine, the surplus of the first will be one-fifth of its produce, and that of the second one-tenth. The ratio between the two surpluses will then be the inverse of the ratio between their yields, and the tract producing only five will give a surplus twice as large as the tract producing ten.

But there is no question of a doubled yield here, and I doubt that anyone would claim, as a general rule, that cold countries are as fertile as hot ones. Let us nonetheless suppose that they are. Let us, if you like, place England on the same footing as Sicily, and Poland on the same footing as Egypt. Farther south lie Africa and the Indies; farther north, nothing at all. Yet to obtain this equal yield, consider the difference in cultivation. In Sicily one need only scratch the soil; in England, how hard men must toil! And wherever more hands are needed to obtain the same yield, the surplus must necessarily be smaller.

Consider, too, that the same number of people consume far less in hot countries. The climate demands moderation for the sake of health, and Europeans who try to live there as they do at home all die of dysentery and indigestion. The traveler Chardin writes: "Compared with the Asiatics, we are carnivorous animals, wolves. Some attribute the sobriety of the Persians to the fact that their country is less cultivated, but I believe, on the contrary, that their country produces fewer goods because its inhabitants need fewer. If their frugality," he continues, "were the result of the poverty of the land, only the poor would eat little, whereas in fact everyone does. Likewise, people would eat more or less in different provinces according to the fertility of the soil, yet the same sobriety is found throughout the kingdom. The Persians take great pride in their way of life, saying that one need only look at their complexion to see how much better it is than that of the Christians. And indeed, the Persians have an even complexion, with fair, fine, smooth skin, while their subjects the Armenians, who live in the European manner, have rough, blotchy skin and heavy, ungainly bodies."

The closer one comes to the equator, the less people live on. They scarcely touch meat; rice, maize, couscous, millet, and cassava are their everyday foods. In the Indies there are millions of people whose daily food costs less than a halfpenny. Even within Europe there are marked differences of appetite between northern and southern peoples. A Spaniard could live for a week on a German's dinner. In countries where people eat more heavily, luxury accordingly takes the form of consumption. In England, luxury shows itself in a well-laden table; in Italy, one feasts on sugar and flowers.

Luxury in dress shows similar differences. In climates where the seasons change suddenly and violently, people wear clothing that is better made and plainer. Where they dress only for ornament, what is striking counts for more than what is useful, and clothing itself becomes a luxury. In Naples, along the Posillipo, you may see men strolling every day in gold-embroidered coats and nothing else. The same holds for buildings: where there is nothing to fear from the weather, splendor is the only consideration. In Paris and London, people want to be housed warmly and comfortably. In Madrid, they have magnificent reception rooms but not a single window that shuts, and they sleep in little more than a hole.

In hot countries, food is also far more substantial and flavorful, and this third difference cannot fail to affect the second. Why do Italians eat so many vegetables? Because there they are good, nourishing, and delicious. In France, where vegetables are grown on little but water, they offer scant nourishment and are held in low regard at table. Yet they occupy just as much ground and cost at least as much effort to grow. It is an established fact that the wheat of Barbary, though inferior to French wheat in other respects, yields much more flour, and that French wheat in turn yields more than the wheat of northern countries. From this one may infer that a similar gradation runs generally in the same direction, from the equator to the pole. And is it not a clear disadvantage for an equal harvest to contain less nourishment?

To all these considerations another may be added, one that both follows from them and reinforces them. Hot countries need fewer inhabitants than cold countries, yet they can support more. This produces a double surplus, entirely to the advantage of despotism. The larger the territory occupied by a given number of inhabitants, the harder rebellion becomes, because swift or secret coordination is impossible, and the government can easily uncover plots and cut off communications. But the more a numerous people is crowded together, the less the government can usurp the place of the Sovereign. The people's leaders can deliberate as safely in their own houses as the prince in his council, and a crowd can gather in the public squares as quickly as the prince's troops in their barracks. The advantage of tyrannical government therefore lies in acting across great distances. With the help of the strongholds it establishes as rallying points, its strength increases with distance, as the force of a lever increases with its length. The strength of the people, by contrast, acts only when concentrated. When dispersed, it dissipates and is lost, like gunpowder scattered on the ground, which catches fire only one grain at a time. The most thinly populated countries are thus the best suited to tyranny: wild beasts reign only in deserts.

This does not contradict what I said earlier, in Book II, about the disadvantages of large states. There we were concerned with the government's authority over its own members; here we are concerned with its force against its subjects. The government's scattered members give it rallying-points from which to act against the people at a distance, but it has no rallying-point from which to act directly on those members themselves. The length of the lever is therefore its weakness in the one case and its strength in the other.

### Chapter IX: The Marks of a Good Government

The question "What, in the absolute, is the best government?" can never be answered, because it is indeterminate. Or rather, it has as many good answers as there are possible combinations in the absolute and relative situations of every nation on earth.

If, however, we ask by what sign we can tell whether a particular people is well or badly governed, the matter is different. That is a question of fact, and it admits of an answer.

Yet it goes unanswered, because everyone wants to answer it in his own way. Subjects praise public tranquility; citizens praise individual liberty. The one group prefers security of property, the other security of person. The one considers the harshest government the best, while the other insists that the mildest is best. The one wants crimes punished, the other wants them prevented. The one wants the state to be feared by its neighbors, the other would rather it went unnoticed. The one is satisfied as long as money circulates, while the other demands that the people have bread. And even if agreement were reached on these and similar points, would we be any further along? Moral qualities cannot be measured precisely, so agreement about the sign would not mean agreement about how to weigh it.

For my part, I am constantly astonished that so simple a sign goes unrecognized, or that men act in such bad faith as to refuse to admit it. What is the purpose of political association? The preservation and prosperity of its members. And what is the surest sign that they are being preserved and are prospering? Their number and their population. Look nowhere else for the sign that is in dispute. All other things being equal, the government under which the citizens increase and multiply most, without outside help, without naturalizing foreigners, and without colonies, is beyond question the best. The government under which a people dwindles and diminishes is the worst. You who calculate, it is now your task to count, to measure, and to compare.

The same principle should decide which centuries deserve to be preferred for human prosperity. Those in which literature and the arts have flourished have been admired too much, because no one has fathomed the hidden purpose of their culture or taken account of its fatal effects. As Tacitus put it, "The ignorant called it humanity, when it was in fact a part of their servitude." Will we never see, in the maxims that books lay down, the base self-interest that makes their authors speak? No: whatever they may say, when a country is losing its population, it is not true that all is well, however brilliant its reputation, and the fact that a poet enjoys an income of a hundred thousand francs is not enough to make his age the best of all. We should pay less attention to the apparent calm and tranquility of rulers than to the well-being of their nations as a whole, and above all of the most populous states. A hailstorm may lay waste to several districts, but it rarely causes a famine. Riots and civil wars give rulers violent shocks, but they are not the real afflictions of peoples, who may even enjoy some relief while the question of who is to tyrannize over them is being fought out. Their true prosperity and their true calamities arise from their permanent condition. It is when the whole nation lies crushed beneath the yoke that decay sets in, and that rulers destroy their people at will, and, in Tacitus's words, "make a desert and call it peace."

When the quarrels of the great threw the kingdom of France into turmoil, and the Coadjutor of Paris, the city's assistant archbishop, went to the Parlement with a dagger in his pocket, none of this prevented the French people from prospering and multiplying in dignity, comfort, and freedom. Long ago, Greece flourished in the midst of the most savage wars: blood ran in torrents, and yet the whole country teemed with inhabitants. Machiavelli says of Florence that "in the midst of murders, banishments, and civil wars, our republic seemed only to grow more powerful; the virtue, the morals, and the independence of its citizens did more to strengthen it than all their quarrels did to weaken it." A little unrest gives the soul its spring. What makes the human race truly prosper is not so much peace as liberty.

### Chapter X: The Abuse of Government and Its Tendency to Degenerate

Just as the particular will acts constantly against the general will, so the government exerts itself continually against the Sovereign. The greater this effort grows, the more the constitution is altered; and since in this case there is no other corporate will to establish an equilibrium by resisting the will of the prince, the prince must sooner or later overpower the Sovereign and break the social treaty. This is the unavoidable defect built into the body politic, which from the moment of its birth works ceaselessly to destroy it, just as old age and death eventually destroy the human body.

There are two general ways in which a government degenerates: either it contracts, or the state dissolves.

A government contracts when it passes from the many to the few, that is, from democracy to aristocracy, and from aristocracy to monarchy. This is its natural tendency. If it moved backward, from the few to the many, it could be said to relax; but that reverse sequence is impossible.

Governments, in fact, never change their form except when their energy is spent and leaves them too weak to hold on to what they have. If a government were both to enlarge its scope and to loosen its grip at once, its force would fall to nothing, and it would be even less able to survive. As the spring of government slackens, then, it must be wound up again and its hold tightened, or the state it supports will collapse.

The dissolution of the state can come about in either of two ways.

The first occurs when the prince ceases to administer the state according to the laws and usurps the sovereign power. A remarkable change then takes place: it is not the government but the state that contracts. The great state, that is, dissolves, and another forms within it, made up solely of the members of the government, which now stands toward the rest of the people as nothing more than master and tyrant. So the moment the government usurps sovereignty, the social compact is broken, and every private citizen recovers by right his natural liberty. He is compelled to obey, but he is no longer obliged to.

The same thing happens when the members of the government individually usurp the power they ought to exercise only as a body. This violates the laws no less, and it produces even greater disorder. There are then, so to speak, as many princes as there are magistrates, and the state, as divided as its government, either perishes or changes its form.

When the state dissolves, the abuse of government, whatever form it takes, goes by the common name of anarchy. To distinguish among the cases: democracy degenerates into ochlocracy, or mob rule, and aristocracy into oligarchy. I would add that monarchy degenerates into tyranny, but this last word is ambiguous and needs explaining.

In ordinary usage, a tyrant is a king who governs by violence, without regard for justice or law. In the precise sense, a tyrant is an individual who claims royal authority for himself without having any right to it. This is how the Greeks understood the word: they applied it without distinction to good princes and bad ones alike, so long as their authority was not legitimate. "Tyrant" and "usurper" are thus perfectly synonymous terms.

So that I may give different things different names, I call the man who usurps royal authority a tyrant, and the man who usurps sovereign power a despot. The tyrant forces his way in against the laws in order to govern according to the laws; the despot sets himself above the laws themselves. The tyrant, therefore, cannot be a despot, but the despot is always a tyrant.

The slow formation and development of the Republic of Venice among its lagoons offer a striking example of the sequence I have described. It is most remarkable that, after more than twelve hundred years of existence, the Venetians appear to be still at the second stage, which they reached with the closing of the Great Council in 1198. As for the ancient doges who are cited against this view, it has been proved, whatever the *Squittinio della libertà veneta* may say of them, that they were in no sense sovereigns.

One case will certainly be raised against my view: the Roman Republic, which, it will be said, followed exactly the opposite course, passing from monarchy to aristocracy and from aristocracy to democracy. I see it quite differently.

What Romulus first established was a mixed government, which soon degenerated into despotism. For particular reasons, that state died before its time, as newborn children sometimes die without reaching adulthood. The expulsion of the Tarquins marked the true birth of the Republic. At first, however, it took no settled form, because in failing to abolish the patriciate it left half its work undone. Hereditary aristocracy, the worst of all legitimate forms of administration, thus remained in conflict with democracy, and the form of government, as Machiavelli has shown, was fixed only with the creation of the tribunate. Only then did Rome have a true government and a genuine democracy. The people was then not only sovereign but also magistrate and judge; the senate was merely a subordinate body meant to moderate and concentrate the government; and the consuls themselves, though patricians, chief magistrates, and absolute commanders in war, were within Rome itself nothing more than presiding officers of the people.

From that point on, the government followed its natural tendency and leaned strongly toward aristocracy. The patriciate, one might say, abolished itself, and aristocracy was no longer found in the body of patricians, as at Venice and Genoa, but in the body of the senate, which included both patricians and plebeians, and even in the body of the tribunes once they began to usurp an active role. Names do not change facts, and whenever a people has rulers who govern on its behalf, whatever they are called, the government is an aristocracy.

The abuse of aristocracy led to the civil wars and the triumvirate. Sulla, Julius Caesar, and Augustus became monarchs in fact, and finally, under the despotism of Tiberius, the state was dissolved. Roman history, then, confirms the principle I have laid down rather than refuting it.

Cornelius Nepos, in his *Life of Miltiades*, writes: "All those are called and considered tyrants who hold perpetual power in a state that has known liberty." It is true that Aristotle, in the eighth book of the *Nicomachean Ethics*, distinguishes the tyrant from the king by saying that the tyrant governs in his own interest and the king solely for the good of his subjects. But not only did Greek writers in general use the word "tyrant" in a different sense, as Xenophon's *Hiero* shows most clearly; it would also follow from Aristotle's distinction that, from the beginning of the world until now, there has never yet been a single king.

### Chapter XI: The Death of the Body Politic

Such is the natural and inevitable tendency of even the best-constituted governments. If Sparta and Rome perished, what state can hope to last forever? If we want to establish a lasting form of government, we must not even dream of making it eternal. To succeed, we must not attempt the impossible, or flatter ourselves that we can give the works of man a permanence that the human condition does not allow.

The body politic, like the human body, begins to die the moment it is born, and carries within itself the causes of its own destruction. Yet each may have a constitution that is more or less robust, and more or less fitted to preserve it for a longer or shorter time. The constitution of a man is the work of nature; that of a state is the work of art. It is not in men's power to lengthen their own lives, but it is in their power to lengthen the life of the state as far as possible by giving it the best constitution it can have. Even the best-constituted state will come to an end, but it will end later than any other, unless some unforeseen accident destroys it before its time.

The principle of life in the body politic lies in the sovereign authority. The legislative power is the heart of the state; the executive power is its brain, which sets all the parts in motion.
