The brain may be paralyzed and the individual still live. A man may be reduced to idiocy and go on living, but the moment the heart stops performing its functions, the creature is dead.

The state survives not by its laws but by its legislative power. Yesterday's law does not bind today. But silence is taken as tacit consent, and the Sovereign is presumed to be constantly reaffirming every law that it could repeal and does not. Whatever it has once declared that it wills, it continues to will, unless it revokes the declaration.

Why, then, are old laws held in such respect? For precisely this reason. We must suppose that only the excellence of those ancient acts of will could have preserved them so long; if the Sovereign had not recognized them as consistently beneficial, it would have revoked them a thousand times. This is why, in any well-constituted state, the laws, far from weakening, continually gain fresh strength, and the authority of age makes them more venerable every day. Wherever laws weaken as they grow old, by contrast, this proves that there is no longer any legislative power, and that the state is dead.

### Chapter XII: How the Sovereign Authority Maintains Itself

The Sovereign has no force other than the legislative power, and so it acts only through the laws. And since the laws are nothing but authentic acts of the general will, the Sovereign can act only when the people is assembled. The people assembled, I will be told, is a mere fantasy. It is a fantasy today, but two thousand years ago it was not. Has human nature changed?

In moral matters, the limits of the possible are less narrow than we suppose. It is our weaknesses, our vices, and our prejudices that shrink them. Petty souls do not believe in great men, and abject slaves smile in mockery at the word "liberty."

Let us judge what can be done by what has been done. I will say nothing of the republics of ancient Greece. But the Roman Republic was, in my view, a great state, and the city of Rome a great city. The last census recorded four hundred thousand citizens in Rome capable of bearing arms, and the last count of the Empire's population showed more than four million citizens, not counting subjects, foreigners, women, children, and slaves.

One might imagine the obstacles to assembling, frequently, the enormous population of this capital and its surrounding territory. Yet hardly a week passed without the Roman people meeting in assembly, often several times. It exercised not only the rights of sovereignty but also part of the rights of government. It dealt with certain kinds of business and judged certain cases, and the whole people appeared in the public forum almost as often in the role of magistrates as in that of citizens.

If we went back to the earliest history of nations, we would find that most ancient governments, even monarchical ones such as those of the Macedonians and the Franks, had similar councils. In any event, the single undeniable fact I have cited answers every objection. It is sound logic to reason from what is actual to what is possible.

### Chapter XIII: The Same Subject, Continued

It is not enough for the assembled people to have fixed the constitution of the state once by sanctioning a body of laws. It is not enough for it to have established a permanent government, or to have provided once and for all for the election of magistrates. In addition to the extraordinary assemblies that unforeseen circumstances may require, there must be fixed, periodic assemblies that can be neither abolished nor postponed, so that on the appointed day the people is lawfully convened by the law itself, without any need for a formal summons.

Apart from these assemblies, which are lawful by their date alone, any assembly of the people not convened by the magistrates appointed for that purpose, and according to the prescribed forms, should be considered unlawful, and everything it does should be considered null and void, because the order to assemble must itself come from the law.

How often lawful assemblies should be held depends on so many considerations that no precise rule can be given. One can only say, in general, that the stronger the government, the more often the Sovereign ought to show itself.

This may work for a single town, I will be told, but what is to be done when the state includes several? Is the sovereign authority to be divided? Or is it to be concentrated in a single town, to which all the others are made subject?

Neither, I reply. In the first place, the sovereign authority is single and indivisible, and cannot be divided without being destroyed. In the second place, one town can no more be legitimately made subject to another than one nation can, because the essence of the body politic lies in reconciling obedience with liberty, and the words "subject" and "Sovereign" are exact correlatives whose meanings meet in the single word "citizen."

I answer further that uniting several towns into a single political community is always bad, and that anyone who wishes to make such a union should not expect to escape its natural disadvantages. It is pointless to raise the abuses of large states against someone who wants only small ones. But how can small states be given enough strength to resist large ones, as the Greek cities once resisted the Great King of Persia, and as Holland and Switzerland have more recently resisted the House of Austria?

If, nevertheless, the state cannot be reduced to its proper size, one resource still remains: to have no capital at all, to move the seat of government from town to town, and to convene the Provincial Estates, the assemblies of the country's regions, in each town in turn.

Populate the territory evenly, extend the same rights everywhere, and carry abundance and life to every part of it. In this way the state will become at once as strong and as well governed as it can possibly be. Remember that the walls of towns are built from the ruins of country houses. For every palace I see rising in the capital, I see in my mind's eye an entire countryside laid waste.

### Chapter XIV: The Same Subject, Continued

The moment the people is lawfully assembled as a sovereign body, the jurisdiction of the government lapses entirely and the executive power is suspended. The person of the humblest citizen becomes as sacred and inviolable as that of the highest magistrate, because where the one represented is present, there is no longer any representative. Most of the disturbances that broke out in the *comitia*, the popular assemblies of Rome, arose from ignorance or neglect of this rule. In those assemblies the consuls were merely the presiding officers of the people, the tribunes were merely speakers, and the senate counted for nothing at all.

These intervals of suspension, during which the prince recognizes, or ought to recognize, a superior actually present, have always alarmed him. These assemblies of the people, which are the shield of the body politic and the check upon the government, have at all times been the dread of rulers, who spare no effort, objection, obstacle, or promise to keep the citizens from holding them. When the citizens are greedy, cowardly, and fainthearted, and love their comfort more than their liberty, they do not long withstand the government's redoubled efforts. And so, as the opposing force grows without pause, the sovereign authority finally vanishes, and most states fall and perish before their time.

Between the sovereign authority and arbitrary government, however, there sometimes arises an intermediate power, of which something must now be said.

By "speakers" I mean something close to the sense the word has in the English Parliament. The similarity of these functions would have brought the consuls and the tribunes into conflict, even if all jurisdiction had been suspended.

### Chapter XV: Deputies or Representatives

As soon as public service ceases to be the citizens' chief business, and they would rather serve with their purses than with their persons, the state is already close to ruin. When they must march off to war, they pay troops and stay home; when they must meet in council, they appoint deputies and stay home. Through idleness and money, they end up with soldiers to enslave their country and representatives to sell it.

It is the bustle of commerce and the arts, the greedy pursuit of profit, and the softness and love of comfort that cause personal service to be replaced by payments of money. Men give up part of their gains in order to have leisure to increase them. Hand out money, and you will soon be in chains. The word "finance" is a slave's word; it is unknown in a true city-state. In a genuinely free country, the citizens do everything with their own hands and nothing with money. Far from paying to be excused from their duties, they would pay for the privilege of performing them in person. I am far from sharing the common opinion: I hold that the *corvée*, compulsory labor on public works, is less contrary to liberty than taxes are.

The better a state is constituted, the more public affairs take precedence over private ones in the minds of its citizens. Private affairs even matter much less, because the sum of the common happiness supplies a larger share of each individual's happiness, leaving him less to seek through private efforts. In a well-ordered city, everyone hurries to the assemblies. Under a bad government, no one cares to take a single step to attend them: no one takes any interest in what happens there, everyone foresees that the general will is not going to prevail, and, finally, household concerns absorb everything. Good laws lead to the making of better ones; bad laws bring about worse. The moment anyone says of the affairs of the state, "What does it matter to me?" the state must be given up for lost.

The cooling of patriotism, the activity of private interest, the vast size of states, conquest, and the abuse of government gave rise to the practice of having deputies or representatives of the people in national assemblies. In some countries, men have had the audacity to call these representatives the Third Estate. The particular interests of two orders, the clergy and the nobility, are thus placed first and second, while the public interest comes only third.

Sovereignty cannot be represented, for the same reason that it cannot be alienated. It consists essentially in the general will, and the will cannot be represented: either it is the same will or it is a different one; there is no middle ground. The deputies of the people, therefore, are not and cannot be its representatives. They are merely its stewards, and they can conclude nothing with final authority. Any law that the people has not ratified in person is null and void; it is, in fact, no law at all. The English people believes itself free, but it is badly mistaken. It is free only while it is electing members of Parliament. Once they are elected, it is enslaved again and counts for nothing. The use it makes of those brief moments of liberty fully shows that it deserves to lose them.

The idea of representation is a modern one. It comes to us from feudal government, that unjust and absurd system which degrades the human race and dishonors the very name of man. In the ancient republics, and even in the ancient monarchies, the people never had representatives; the word itself was unknown. It is remarkable that in Rome, where the tribunes were held so sacred that their persons could not be touched, no one ever imagined they might take over the functions of the people. Surrounded by so vast a multitude, they never once tried to pass a single *plebiscitum*, a measure of the popular assembly, on their own authority. We can still gain some idea of the difficulties that the sheer size of the citizen body sometimes created from what happened in the time of the Gracchi, when some citizens had to cast their votes from the rooftops.

Where right and liberty are everything, inconveniences count for nothing. This wise people gave everything its proper weight. It allowed its lictors, the attendants who carried out the magistrates' orders, to do what its tribunes would never have dared attempt, because it had no fear that its lictors would try to represent it.

To explain, however, how the tribunes did sometimes represent the people, it is enough to consider how the government represents the Sovereign. Since law is nothing but the declaration of the general will, it is clear that the people cannot be represented in the exercise of legislative power. In the exercise of executive power, which is merely the force applied to give the law effect, it both can and should be represented. If we looked into the matter closely, then, we would find that very few nations have any laws at all. Be that as it may, it is certain that the tribunes, who held no executive power, could never represent the Roman people by virtue of the powers entrusted to them, but only by usurping those of the Senate.

In Greece, whatever the people had to do, it did for itself. It was continually assembled in the public square. The Greeks lived in a mild climate; they were not naturally greedy; slaves did their work; and their chief concern was liberty. Without the same advantages, how can you hope to keep the same rights? Your harsher climates multiply your needs, and to import into cold countries the luxury and softness of the East is to invite its chains, and indeed to submit to them far more inescapably than the Eastern peoples themselves. For half the year your public squares cannot be used. Your languages, flat and muted, do not carry in the open air. You give more for gain than for liberty, and you fear poverty more than slavery.

What follows, then? Can liberty be maintained only with the support of slavery? Perhaps it can. Extremes meet. Everything outside the course of nature has its drawbacks, and civil society more than anything else. There are some unhappy situations in which we can keep our own liberty only at the expense of others, and in which the citizen can be perfectly free only if the slave is utterly enslaved. Such was the case at Sparta. As for you, modern peoples, you have no slaves, but you are slaves yourselves: you pay for their freedom with your own. You may boast of this choice, but in vain. I see more cowardice in it than humanity.

I do not mean by any of this that slaves are necessary, or that the right of slavery is legitimate. I am simply giving the reasons why modern peoples, who believe themselves free, have representatives, while ancient peoples had none. In any case, the moment a people allows itself to be represented, it is no longer free. It no longer exists.

All things considered, I do not see how the Sovereign can henceforth preserve the exercise of its rights among us unless the city is very small. But if it is very small, will it not be conquered? No. I will show later how the external strength of a great people can be combined with the easy governance and good order of a small State. I had meant to do this in the sequel to this work, when, in treating external relations, I came to the subject of confederations. That subject is entirely new, and its principles have yet to be established.

### Chapter XVI: That the Institution of Government Is Not a Contract

Once the legislative power has been firmly established, the next task is to establish the executive power on an equally sound footing. The executive operates only through particular acts. It is not of the essence of the legislative power, and it is therefore naturally separate from it. If the Sovereign, as such, could hold the executive power, right and fact would become so confused that no one could tell what was law and what was not. The body politic, thus disfigured, would soon fall prey to the very violence it was instituted to prevent.

Since the social contract makes all citizens equal, all may prescribe what all shall do, but no one has the right to demand that another do what he does not do himself. It is precisely this right, indispensable for giving the body politic life and motion, that the Sovereign confers on the prince when it institutes the government.

Some have held that this act of establishment is a contract between the people and the rulers it places over itself, a contract setting conditions between the two parties and binding one to command and the other to obey. It will be granted, I am sure, that this is a strange sort of contract. But let us see whether the view can be defended.

First, the supreme authority can no more be modified than it can be alienated; to limit it is to destroy it. It is absurd and self-contradictory for the Sovereign to set a superior above itself. To bind itself to obey a master would be to return to a state of absolute liberty.

Moreover, a contract between the people and particular persons would clearly be a particular act. It follows that it could be neither a law nor an act of Sovereignty, and would therefore be illegitimate.

It is also plain that the contracting parties would stand toward each other under the law of nature alone, with no guarantee whatever for their mutual commitments, a situation wholly at odds with the civil state. Whoever holds force is always in a position to control how such an agreement is carried out. One might just as well apply the word "contract" to the act of a man who said to another: "I give you all my goods, on condition that you give me back as much of them as you please."

There is only one contract in the State, the act of association, and by its very nature it excludes the existence of any other. No public contract can be conceived that would not violate the first.

### Chapter XVII: The Institution of Government

Under what general idea, then, should we conceive the act by which government is instituted? I will begin by noting that it is a complex act, composed of two others: the establishment of the law, and its execution.

By the first, the Sovereign decrees that there shall be a governing body established in this or that form. This act is clearly a law.

By the second, the people names the rulers who are to be entrusted with the government so established. This nomination, being a particular act, is clearly not a second law, but merely a consequence of the first and a function of government.

The difficulty is to understand how there can be an act of government before any government exists, and how the people, which is only ever Sovereign or subject, can in certain circumstances become a prince or a magistrate.

Here one of the astonishing properties of the body politic comes to light, a property by which it reconciles operations that seem contradictory. This is accomplished through a sudden conversion of Sovereignty into democracy. Without any perceptible change, and simply by virtue of a new relation of all to all, the citizens become magistrates and pass from general acts to particular ones, from legislation to the execution of the law.

This change of relation is no speculative subtlety without examples in practice. It happens every day in the English Parliament, where on certain occasions the House of Commons turns itself into a Grand Committee, a committee of the whole house, in order to discuss business more freely. In this way, what was a sovereign court one moment becomes a mere commission the next. Afterward it reports to itself, as the House of Commons, the results of its work in Grand Committee, and debates again under one name what it has already settled under another.

Indeed, it is the peculiar advantage of democratic government that it can be brought into actual existence by a simple act of the general will. This provisional government then either remains in power, if that is the form adopted, or establishes in the name of the Sovereign the government that the law prescribes, and so the entire proceeding is regular. There is no other way to set up a government legitimately and in keeping with the principles laid down so far.

### Chapter XVIII: How to Check the Usurpations of Government

What we have just said confirms Chapter XVI. It makes clear that the institution of government is not a contract but a law; that those entrusted with executive power are not the people's masters but its officers; that the people can appoint and remove them whenever it pleases; that for them it is a matter not of contract but of obedience; and that in taking on the functions the State assigns them, they are merely fulfilling their duty as citizens, without the slightest right to dispute the terms.

When, therefore, the people establishes a hereditary government, whether monarchical and confined to one family or aristocratic and confined to one class, it does not enter into any binding commitment. It simply gives the administration a provisional form, which lasts until the people chooses to order things differently.

It is true that such changes are always dangerous, and that an established government should never be disturbed unless it has become incompatible with the public good. But this caution is a maxim of policy, not a rule of right. The State is no more bound to leave civil authority in the hands of its rulers than to leave military authority in the hands of its generals.

It is also true that in such cases one cannot be too careful to observe every formality needed to distinguish a regular and legitimate act from a seditious uprising, and the will of an entire people from the clamor of a faction. Here above all, no more allowance should be made for this unwelcome possibility than strict logic makes unavoidable. Yet this very obligation gives the prince a great advantage. He can keep his power against the people's will without anyone being able to say he has usurped it. While appearing only to exercise his rights, he finds it easy to extend them and, on the pretext of keeping the peace, to prevent the assemblies whose purpose is to restore good order. He then exploits a silence he does not allow to be broken, or irregularities he himself provokes, to claim the support of those whom fear keeps quiet, and to punish those who dare to speak. This is how the decemvirs, the ten magistrates appointed at Rome to draw up its laws, first elected for one year and then kept on for a second, tried to make their power permanent by forbidding the comitia, the popular assemblies, to meet. By this easy method, every government in the world, once invested with public power, sooner or later usurps the sovereign authority.

The periodic assemblies I spoke of earlier are designed to prevent or delay this calamity, especially when they require no formal summons. In that case the prince cannot stop them without openly declaring himself a lawbreaker and an enemy of the State.

These assemblies, whose sole purpose is to maintain the social treaty, should always open with two propositions that can never be suppressed, each to be voted on separately.

The first: "Does it please the Sovereign to preserve the present form of government?"

The second: "Does it please the people to leave the administration in the hands of those who now hold it?"

I am assuming here what I believe I have demonstrated: that there is no fundamental law in the State that cannot be revoked, not even the social compact itself. If all the citizens assembled and agreed together to break the compact, there can be no doubt that it would be quite legitimately broken. Grotius even holds that each man may renounce his membership in his own State and recover his natural liberty and his property by leaving the country. This holds, of course, only if he does not leave to escape his obligations and avoid serving his country in its hour of need. Flight in such a case would be criminal and punishable; it would be not withdrawal but desertion. It would surely be absurd if all the citizens in assembly could not do what each can do alone.

## Book IV

### Chapter I: That the General Will Is Indestructible

So long as a number of men assembled together regard themselves as a single body, they have only one will, directed to their common preservation and general welfare. In that condition, all the springs of the State are vigorous and simple, and its principles clear and luminous. There are no tangled or conflicting interests; the common good is plainly evident everywhere, and only good sense is needed to perceive it. Peace, unity, and equality are the enemies of political subtleties. Upright and simple men are hard to deceive precisely because of their simplicity. Lures and clever pretexts make no impression on them, and they are not even sophisticated enough to be dupes. When we see, among the happiest people in the world, groups of peasants settling the affairs of State beneath an oak tree and always acting wisely, can we help but scorn the refinements of other nations, which make themselves illustrious and miserable with so much art and mystery?

A State governed in this way needs very few laws, and when new ones become necessary, everyone sees the necessity. The first man to propose them merely puts into words what all have already felt. No factions, intrigues, or eloquence are needed to pass into law what each man has already resolved to do, as soon as he is sure the others will do the same.

Theorists go wrong because they see only States that were badly constituted from the start, and they are struck by the impossibility of applying such a policy to them. They make great sport of all the absurdities a clever scoundrel or a persuasive speaker might get the people of Paris or London to believe. They do not know that the people of Berne would have sentenced Cromwell to the bells, the public labor gangs whose convicts were made to wear them, and that the Genevans would have put the Duc de Beaufort to hard labor on the treadmill.

But when the social bond begins to loosen and the State to weaken, when private interests begin to make themselves felt and smaller associations begin to sway the larger one, the common interest is altered and meets with opposition. Opinion is no longer unanimous; the general will is no longer the will of all; conflicting views and disputes arise; and even the best advice is not accepted without argument.

Finally, the State reaches the brink of ruin and maintains only an empty, illusory, merely formal existence. The social bond is broken in every heart, and the basest interest brazenly appropriates the sacred name of the "public good." Then the general will falls silent. Everyone is moved by hidden motives, and no one gives his opinion as a citizen any longer, as if the State had never existed. Unjust decrees aimed solely at private interest are passed under the name of laws.

Does it follow that the general will has been destroyed or corrupted? Not at all. It remains constant, unalterable, and pure, but it is subordinated to other wills that encroach upon its domain. Each man, in detaching his own interest from the common interest, sees clearly that he cannot separate the two entirely. Yet his share of the public harm seems trivial to him beside the exclusive advantage he hopes to secure for himself. Apart from this private good, he wills the general good for his own sake as strongly as anyone else. Even when he sells his vote for money, he does not extinguish the general will within himself; he merely evades it. His fault lies in changing the question and answering something other than what he was asked. Instead of saying with his vote, "This is advantageous to the State," he says, "It is advantageous to this man or that party that this or that view should prevail." The rule of public order in assemblies, then, is not so much to preserve the general will within them as to ensure that the question is always put to it, and that it is always the general will that gives the answer.

I could add many reflections here on the simple right to vote in every act of Sovereignty, a right no one can take away from the citizens. I could also say much about the rights of stating opinions, making proposals, dividing questions, and debating, which the government always takes great care to reserve to its own members. But this important subject would require a treatise of its own, and no single work can say everything.

### Chapter II: Voting

It follows from the last chapter that the manner in which public business is conducted can give a fairly reliable indication of the actual state of morals and the health of the body politic. The more harmony reigns in the assemblies, that is, the closer opinion comes to unanimity, the more the general will prevails. Long debates, dissension, and uproar, on the other hand, announce the ascendancy of private interests and the decline of the State.

This seems less clear when the constitution includes two or more orders, as Rome had its patricians and plebeians. Quarrels between these two orders often disturbed the comitia, even in the Republic's best days. But the exception is more apparent than real. Owing to a defect inherent in that body politic, there were in effect two States in one, and what is not true of the two together holds true of each taken separately. Indeed, even in the stormiest periods, the plebiscites of the common people, when the Senate did not interfere with them, always passed peacefully and by large majorities. Because the citizens had only one interest, the people had only one will.

At the other end of the circle, unanimity returns. This happens when citizens have fallen into servitude and lost both their liberty and their will. Fear and flattery then turn voting into acclamation; deliberation ceases, and nothing remains but adoration or abuse. Such was the degraded way the Senate expressed its views under the Emperors, sometimes with absurd precautions. Tacitus records that under Otho, the senators, while heaping curses on Vitellius, took care at the same time to make a deafening noise, so that if Vitellius ever became their master, he could not tell what any one of them had said.

These various considerations determine the rules for counting votes and weighing opinions, according to whether the general will is more or less easy to discern and the State more or less advanced in its decline.

There is only one law that by its nature requires unanimous consent, and that is the social compact. Civil association is the most voluntary act there is. Since every man is born free and master of himself, no one, under any pretext whatever, can subject him without his consent. To decide that the son of a slave is born a slave is to decide that he is not born a man.

If, then, there are opponents when the social compact is made, their opposition does not invalidate the contract; it merely excludes them from it. They are foreigners among the citizens. Once the State is instituted, residence constitutes consent: to live within its territory is to submit to the Sovereign. This must of course be understood to apply only to a free State. Elsewhere, family, property, the lack of any refuge, necessity, or violence may keep a man in a country against his will, and then his living there no longer implies, by itself, that he consents either to the contract or to its violation.

Apart from this original contract, the vote of the majority always binds all the rest; this follows from the contract itself. But it may be asked how a man can be free and yet forced to conform to wills not his own. How can those in opposition be both free and subject to laws they have not agreed to?

I answer that the question is wrongly posed. The citizen consents to all the laws, including those passed over his opposition, and even those that punish him if he dares to break any of them. The constant will of all the members of the State is the general will, and it is by virtue of this will that they are citizens and free. When a law is proposed in the popular assembly, the people is not asked, strictly speaking, whether it approves or rejects the proposal, but whether the proposal conforms to the general will, which is their own will. Each man, in casting his vote, gives his opinion on that question, and the general will is found by counting the votes. When, therefore, the opinion contrary to mine prevails, this proves only that I was mistaken, and that what I took to be the general will was not. Had my private opinion carried the day, I would have done the opposite of what I willed, and it is then that I would not have been free.

This assumes, of course, that all the characteristics of the general will still reside in the majority. When they cease to do so, liberty is no longer possible, whichever side a man may take.

In showing earlier how private wills come to replace the general will in public deliberations, I indicated sufficiently the practical means of preventing this abuse, and I shall have more to say about them later. I have also given the principles for determining the proportion of votes needed to declare that will. A difference of a single vote breaks a tie, and a single opponent breaks unanimity. Between an even split and unanimity, however, lie several degrees of unequal division, and the required proportion may be fixed at any of them, according to the condition and needs of the body politic.

Two general rules can help regulate this proportion. First, the graver and more important the question under discussion, the closer the prevailing opinion should come to unanimity. Second, the more urgently the matter demands a decision, the smaller the required margin may be; where a decision must be reached on the spot, a majority of one vote should suffice. The first rule seems better suited to legislation, the second to practical business. In any case, it is by combining the two that we arrive at the best proportions for fixing the majority required.

At Genoa, the word "Liberty" can be read over the doors of the prisons and on the chains of the galley slaves. This use of the motto is fitting and just. In truth, it is only wrongdoers of every rank who prevent the citizen from being free. In a country where all such men were chained to the oars of the galleys, people would enjoy the most perfect liberty.

### Chapter III: Elections

The election of the prince and of the magistrates is, as I have said, a complex act, and there are two ways of carrying it out: by choice or by lot. Both have been used in various republics, and a highly complicated mixture of the two still survives in the election of the Doge of Venice.

"Election by lot," Montesquieu writes, "is in the nature of democracy." I agree that it is, but in what sense? "The lot," he continues, "is a way of choosing that wrongs no one; it leaves every citizen a reasonable hope of serving his country." These are not reasons.

If we remember that electing rulers is a function of government and not of sovereignty, we will see why the lot is the method more natural to democracy, where the administration is better the fewer acts it performs.

In every true democracy, public office is not an advantage but a heavy burden, and it cannot justly be imposed on one individual rather than another. Only the law can lay that burden on the person whom the lot selects. When the conditions are the same for everyone and the choice depends on no human will, there is no particular application of the law that could compromise its universality.

In an aristocracy, the prince chooses the prince, the government perpetuates itself, and voting is the proper method.

The election of the Doge of Venice confirms this distinction rather than undermining it, because a mixed form of election suits a mixed government. It is a mistake to regard the government of Venice as a true aristocracy. If the people have no share in governing, then the nobility itself becomes the people. A crowd of impoverished nobles, the Barnabotes, never comes near any office. Their nobility consists of nothing more than the empty title of "Excellency" and the right to sit in the Great Council. Since that Great Council is as large as our General Council at Geneva, its illustrious members have no more privileges than our ordinary citizens. Leaving aside the enormous difference in scale between the two republics, the burghers of Geneva correspond exactly to the patricians of Venice. Our "natives" and "inhabitants," the residents without full political rights, correspond to the townsmen and the common people of Venice, and our peasants correspond to the Venetian subjects on the mainland. However one regards that republic, if its size is left out of account, its government is no more aristocratic than ours. The whole difference is that, because we have no ruler for life, we have no need, as Venice does, to use the lot.

Election by lot would have few drawbacks in a true democracy. There, equality would prevail in morals and talents as well as in principles and fortunes, so it would hardly matter who was chosen. But I have already said that a true democracy is only an ideal.

When choice and lot are combined, positions that require special talents, such as military commands, should be filled by choice. The lot is suitable for positions such as judicial offices, where good sense, justice, and integrity are enough, because in a well-constituted State these qualities are common to all citizens.

Neither lot nor vote has any place in a monarchy. Since the monarch is by right the sole prince and the only magistrate, the choice of his lieutenants belongs to him alone. When the Abbé de Saint-Pierre proposed multiplying the councils of the King of France and electing their members by ballot, he did not see that he was proposing to change the form of government.

I ought now to discuss how opinions are given and counted in the assembly of the people. Perhaps, though, an account of how the Roman constitution handled this matter will illustrate all the rules I could set down more forcefully. A thoughtful reader will find it worthwhile to follow in some detail how public and private business was conducted in a council of two hundred thousand men.

### Chapter IV: The Roman Comitia

We have no reliable records of the earliest period of Rome's existence, and it seems very likely that most of the stories told about it are fables. Generally speaking, the most instructive part of a people's history, the part that deals with its founding, is the part we know least about. Experience teaches us every day what causes bring about the revolutions of empires, but because no new peoples are formed anymore, we have little beyond conjecture to explain how they came into being.

The customs we find established show at least that these customs had an origin. Among the traditions that reach back to those origins, the ones supported by the greatest authorities and confirmed by the strongest proofs should be accepted as the most certain. These are the rules I have tried to follow in examining how the freest and most powerful people on earth exercised its supreme power.

After Rome was founded, the newborn republic, which was really the army of its founder, composed of Albans, Sabines, and foreigners, was divided into three groups. Because of this division, they took the name of *tribes*. Each tribe was subdivided into ten *curiae*, and each curia into *decuriae*, led by officers called *curiones* and *decuriones*.

In addition, a body of one hundred *equites*, or knights, called a *century*, was drawn from each tribe. This shows that these divisions, which a town hardly needed, were at first purely military. Yet an instinct for greatness seems to have led the little township of Rome to equip itself in advance with a political system suited to the capital of the world.

This original division soon produced an awkward situation. The tribes of the Albans (the Ramnenses) and of the Sabines (the Tatienses) stayed the same size, while the tribe of the foreigners (the Luceres) kept growing as more and more foreigners settled in Rome, so that it soon outstripped the others in strength. Servius corrected this dangerous flaw by changing the basis of the division. He abolished the division by race and replaced it with one based on the quarter of the city each tribe inhabited. Instead of three tribes he created four, each occupying one of the hills of Rome and named after it. In this way he remedied the inequality of the moment and also provided for the future. To make the division one of persons as well as of places, he forbade the inhabitants of one quarter to move to another, and so prevented the races from mixing.

He also doubled the three old centuries of knights and added twelve more, while keeping the old names. By this simple and prudent method he set the body of knights apart from the people without provoking a murmur from the people.

To the four urban tribes Servius added fifteen others, called rural tribes because they consisted of those who lived in the countryside, which was divided into fifteen districts. Later, fifteen more were created, and the Roman people were finally divided into thirty-five tribes, a number that remained fixed until the end of the Republic.

The distinction between urban and rural tribes had one effect that deserves mention, because it has no parallel elsewhere and because Rome owed to it both the preservation of her morals and the growth of her empire. One would expect the urban tribes to have quickly monopolized power and honors and to have lost no time in bringing the rural tribes into disrepute. Exactly the opposite happened. The early Romans' fondness for country life is well known. They owed it to their wise founder, who joined rural and military labor to liberty and, in a sense, banished to the city the arts, the crafts, intrigue, fortune, and slavery.

Since all of Rome's most illustrious citizens lived in the fields and worked the land, people grew used to looking there alone for the pillars of the republic. Because this was the way of life of the best patricians, everyone honored it. The simple, hardworking life of the villager was preferred to the lazy, idle life of the townspeople of Rome, and a man who in the city would have been a wretched proletarian became a respected citizen as a laborer in the fields. As Varro says, it was not without reason that our great-souled ancestors made the village the nursery of the sturdy and valiant men who defended them in war and fed them in peace. Pliny states plainly that the country tribes were honored because of the men who composed them, while cowards whom the Romans wished to disgrace were transferred to the town tribes as a public humiliation. When the Sabine Appius Claudius came to settle in Rome, he was showered with honors and enrolled in a rural tribe, which later took his family's name. Finally, freedmen always entered the urban tribes and never the rural ones, and in the entire history of the Republic there is not a single example of a freedman, even one who had become a citizen, holding any magistracy.

This was an excellent rule, but it was pushed so far that it eventually produced a change, and certainly an abuse, in the political system. First, the censors, after claiming for a long time the right to transfer citizens arbitrarily from one tribe to another, allowed most people to enroll in whatever tribe they pleased. This permission certainly did no good, and it also deprived the censorship of one of its most important powers. Furthermore, since the great and powerful all had themselves enrolled in the country tribes, while freedmen who had become citizens stayed with the populace in the town tribes, the tribes soon lost any local or territorial meaning. Everyone became so mixed together that members of one tribe could be told apart from those of another only by consulting the registers. As a result, the idea conveyed by the word *tribe* shifted from a place to a person, or rather became little more than a fiction.

It also happened that the town tribes, being close at hand, were often the stronger in the assemblies, and they sold the State to anyone willing to stoop to buying the votes of the rabble who made them up.

Since the founder had set up ten curiae in each tribe, the whole Roman people, who then lived within the city walls, made up thirty curiae. Each had its own temples, gods, officers, priests, and festivals. These festivals were called *compitalia*, and they corresponded to the *paganalia*, the village festivals held in later times by the rural tribes.

When Servius made his new division, the thirty curiae could not be shared out equally among his four tribes, and he did not want to tamper with them. They therefore became a further division of the inhabitants of Rome, entirely independent of the tribes. In the rural tribes and among their members, however, curiae played no part. By then the tribes had become a purely civil institution, and since a new method of raising troops had been introduced, the military divisions of Romulus were no longer needed. So although every citizen was enrolled in a tribe, a great many did not belong to a curia.

Servius made yet a third division, quite distinct from the other two, and in its effects it became the most important of all. He distributed the entire Roman people into six classes, distinguished neither by place nor by person but by wealth. The first classes included the rich, the last the poor, and those in between people of moderate means. These six classes were subdivided into one hundred and ninety-three smaller bodies called centuries, distributed so that the first class alone contained more than half of them, while the last contained only one. Thus the class with the fewest members had the most centuries, and the entire last class counted as a single subdivision, even though it alone included more than half the inhabitants of Rome.

To keep the people from seeing too clearly what this arrangement implied, Servius tried to give it a military appearance. Into the second class he inserted two centuries of armorers, and into the fourth two centuries of makers of instruments of war. In every class except the last, he distinguished the young from the old, that is, those obliged to bear arms from those whom age legally exempted. It was this distinction, more than the one based on wealth, that made it necessary to repeat the census, or count, so often. Finally, he ordered that the assembly be held in the Campus Martius, the Field of Mars, and that all men of military age come there armed.

He did not divide the last class into young and old because the populace who made it up were not given the right to bear arms for their country. A man had to possess a hearth to earn the right to defend it. Of all the troops of beggars who today lend luster to the armies of kings, there is perhaps not one who would not have been driven in disgrace out of a Roman cohort in the days when soldiers were the defenders of liberty.

Within this last class, however, a distinction was drawn between the *proletarians* and the *capite censi*, those "counted by heads." The proletarians, not yet reduced to nothing, at least gave the State citizens, and sometimes, in times of pressing need, even soldiers. Those who had nothing at all and could be counted only by their heads were regarded as of no account whatever, and Marius was the first to stoop to enlisting them.

Without deciding here whether this third arrangement was good or bad in itself, I think I may say that it could have worked only because of the simple morals, the lack of self-interest, the love of agriculture, and the contempt for commerce and profit that marked the early Romans. Where is the modern people among whom ravenous greed, restlessness, intrigue, constant changes of residence, and endless reversals of fortune would allow such a system to last twenty years without turning the State upside down? It must also be noted that morals and the censorship, being stronger than this institution, corrected its defects at Rome, and that a rich man who flaunted his wealth too freely could find himself demoted to the class of the poor.

All of this makes it easy to understand why only five classes are almost always mentioned, even though there were in fact six. The sixth supplied neither soldiers to the army nor votes in the Campus Martius and had almost no function in the State, so it was seldom considered of any account.

These were the various ways in which the Roman people were divided. Let us now look at their effect on the assemblies. When lawfully summoned, these assemblies were called *comitia*. They were usually held in the public square at Rome or in the Campus Martius, and they were distinguished as the Comitia Curiata, the Comitia Centuriata, and the Comitia Tributa, according to the form in which they were convened. Romulus founded the Comitia Curiata, Servius the Comitia Centuriata, and the tribunes of the people the Comitia Tributa. No law was ratified and no magistrate was elected except in the comitia. Since every citizen was enrolled in a curia, a century, or a tribe, no citizen was excluded from the right to vote, and the Roman people were truly sovereign, both in law and in fact.

For the comitia to be lawfully assembled and for their acts to have the force of law, three conditions had to be met. First, the body or magistrate convening them had to possess the necessary authority. Second, the assembly had to be held on a day permitted by law. Third, the auguries, the signs read by the priests, had to be favorable.

The reason for the first rule needs no explanation. The second was a matter of practical policy. The comitia could not be held, for example, on festivals or market days, when country people who came to Rome on business had no time to spend the day in the public square. Through the third rule, the senate kept a proud and restless people in check and duly restrained the zeal of seditious tribunes, though the tribunes found more than one way around this obstacle.

Laws and the election of rulers were not the only questions submitted to the judgment of the comitia. Since the Roman people had taken on themselves the most important functions of government, it may be said that the fate of Europe was decided in their assemblies. The variety of business they handled gave rise to the various forms they took, depending on the matters on which they had to decide.

To judge these various forms, it is enough to compare them. When Romulus established the curiae, his aim was to check the senate by means of the people and the people by means of the senate, while keeping his own ascendancy over both. Through this assembly, therefore, he gave the people all the authority of numbers to counterbalance the authority of power and wealth, which he left to the patricians. In keeping with the spirit of monarchy, however, he still gave the patricians the greater advantage, through the influence their clients exerted over the majority of votes. This admirable institution of patron and client was a masterpiece of statesmanship and humanity, and without it the patriciate, which flatly contradicted the republican spirit, could not have survived. Rome alone has the honor of having given the world this great example, which never led to any abuse and yet has never been imitated.

Since the assemblies by curiae persisted under the kings until the time of Servius, and the reign of the later Tarquin was not regarded as legitimate, the royal laws were generally called *leges curiatae*.

Under the Republic, the curiae, still confined to the four urban tribes and including only the populace of Rome, suited neither the senate, which led the patricians, nor the tribunes, who, though plebeians themselves, led the well-to-do citizens. The curiae therefore fell into disrepute, and their decline went so far that thirty lictors, the attendants who carried the magistrates' rods of office, would meet and do what the Comitia Curiata should have done.

The division by centuries was so favorable to the aristocracy that it is hard to see at first how the senate could ever have failed to prevail in the assemblies named after them, which elected the consuls, the censors, and the other curule magistrates, the high officials entitled to the ivory chair of state. Of the one hundred and ninety-three centuries into which the six classes of the whole Roman people were divided, the first class held ninety-eight, and since voting was counted only by centuries, this class alone had a majority over all the others. When all of its centuries agreed, the remaining votes were not even taken. The decision of the smallest number passed for the decision of the multitude, and it may be said that in the Comitia Centuriata, outcomes were determined far more by the depth of men's purses than by the number of their votes.

This extreme authority was tempered in two ways. First, the tribunes as a rule, and always a large number of plebeians, belonged to the class of the rich, and so offset the influence of the patricians in the first class.

The second was this. Instead of having the centuries vote in order, which would have meant always beginning with the first, the Romans chose one century by lot, and that century alone proceeded to the election. Afterward all the centuries were called on another day according to their rank, the same election was repeated, and as a rule the first result was confirmed. In this way, the authority of example was taken from rank and given to the lot, according to a democratic principle.

This custom brought a further advantage. Between the two elections, citizens from the countryside had time to learn about the merits of the provisionally chosen candidate, and so did not have to vote without knowing the facts. But under the pretext of speeding things up, this custom was abolished, and both elections were held on the same day.

The Comitia Tributa were, properly speaking, the council of the Roman people. Only the tribunes could convene them. In these assemblies the tribunes were elected and passed their *plebiscita*, the resolutions of the common people. The senate not only had no standing there but no right even to attend, and the senators, forced to obey laws on which they could not vote, were in this respect less free than the humblest citizens. This injustice was thoroughly ill-conceived, and it alone was enough to invalidate the decrees of a body from which some of its members were excluded. Had all the patricians attended these assemblies by right of citizenship, they would have had, as private individuals, little influence over a vote counted by heads, in which the lowliest proletarian counted for as much as the *princeps senatus*, the senator whose name stood first on the senate's roll.

It is clear, then, that these different ways of dividing so large a people and collecting its votes did more than impose order. The methods were not mere formalities, indifferent in themselves. Each produced results related to the purposes for which it was preferred.

Without going into further detail here, we may conclude from what has been said that the Comitia Tributa were the most favorable to popular government and the Comitia Centuriata to aristocracy. The Comitia Curiata, in which the populace of Rome made up the majority, were fit only to serve tyranny and evil designs, and so they naturally fell into disrepute. Even seditious men avoided a method that exposed their schemes too plainly. It is beyond dispute that the full majesty of the Roman people lay in the Comitia Centuriata alone, because they alone included everyone. The Comitia Curiata excluded the rural tribes, and the Comitia Tributa excluded the senate and the patricians.

The method of voting among the ancient Romans was as simple as their morals, though not as simple as at Sparta. Each man declared his vote aloud, and a clerk wrote it down. The majority within each tribe determined the vote of the tribe, the majority of the tribes determined the vote of the people, and the same held for the curiae and the centuries. This custom worked well as long as honesty prevailed among the citizens and each man was ashamed to vote in public for an unjust proposal or an unworthy candidate. But once the people grew corrupt and votes were bought, it was right that voting should become secret, so that buyers would be held back by mistrust and scoundrels would have a way to avoid becoming traitors.

I know that Cicero attacks this change and blames it in part for the ruin of the Republic. Though I feel the weight that Cicero's authority must carry on such a question, I cannot agree with him. I hold, on the contrary, that it was for want of enough such changes that the State's destruction was hastened. Just as the regimen of a healthy man does not suit the sick, we should not try to govern a corrupt people by the laws a good people requires. There is no better proof of this rule than the long life of the Republic of Venice, whose shadow still survives solely because its laws are suited only to wicked men.

The citizens were therefore given tablets with which each man could vote without anyone knowing how he had voted. New procedures were also introduced for collecting the tablets, counting the votes, comparing the totals, and so on. Yet all these precautions did not prevent the good faith of the officials charged with these duties from being frequently called into question. Finally, edicts were issued to prevent intrigue and the buying and selling of votes, but their sheer number proves how little they accomplished.

Toward the close of the Republic, the Romans often had to resort to extraordinary expedients to make up for the inadequacy of the laws. Sometimes they claimed that a miracle had occurred, a device that might impress the people but could not deceive those who governed. Sometimes an assembly was hastily summoned before the candidates had time to organize their factions. Sometimes, when it became clear that the people had been won over and were about to take a wrong position, an entire session was consumed with speeches. In the end, however, ambition slipped past every attempt to restrain it. The most remarkable fact of all is that, amid all these abuses, this enormous populace, thanks to its ancient regulations, never ceased to elect magistrates, pass laws, judge cases, and conduct business both public and private almost as easily as the senate itself could have done.

Two further points on the matters discussed above. I specified "in the Campus Martius" because that was where the *comitia* assembled when the people voted by centuries. In the assembly's two other forms, the people gathered in the Forum or elsewhere, and there the *capite censi*, the poorest citizens, counted only by head because they owned no property, had as much influence and authority as the leading citizens. As for the officers charged with handling the votes, they were the *custodes*, *diribitores*, and *rogatores suffragiorum*: the guardians of the ballots, the sorters who counted them, and the collectors who gathered them.

### Chapter V: The Tribunate

When an exact proportion cannot be established among the constituent parts of the State, or when causes that cannot be removed keep altering the relation of one part to another, a special magistracy is instituted that forms no corporate unity with the rest. This magistracy restores each term to its proper relation with the others, and it provides a link or middle term between the prince and the people, between the prince and the Sovereign, or, if necessary, between both at once.

This body, which I shall call the tribunate, is the guardian of the laws and of the legislative power. Sometimes it protects the Sovereign against the government, as the tribunes of the people did at Rome. Sometimes it upholds the government against the people, as the Council of Ten now does at Venice. And sometimes it maintains the balance between the two, as the Ephors did at Sparta.

The tribunate is not a constituent part of the city, and it should have no share in either legislative or executive power. That very fact, however, makes its own power greater, for while it can do nothing, it can prevent anything from being done. As the defender of the laws, it is more sacred and more revered than the prince who executes them or the Sovereign who enacts them. This was plainly visible at Rome, when the proud patricians, for all their contempt for the people, were forced to bow before one of the people's officers, a man who possessed neither the right to take the auspices nor any judicial authority.

The tribunate, wisely tempered, is the strongest support a good constitution can have. But if its strength exceeds the proper measure even slightly, it overturns the whole State. Weakness, by contrast, is not in its nature: so long as it amounts to anything at all, it is never less than it ought to be.

It degenerates into tyranny when it seizes the executive power, which it should only restrain, and when it tries to set aside the laws, which it should only protect. The immense power of the Ephors was harmless so long as Sparta preserved its moral character, but once corruption had begun, that power hastened it. The blood of King Agis, slaughtered by these tyrants, was avenged by his successor, and both the crime of the Ephors and their punishment hastened the destruction of the republic; after Cleomenes, Sparta ceased to count for anything. Rome perished in the same way. The excessive power of the tribunes, which they had usurped by degrees, finally became, with the help of laws designed to secure liberty, a safeguard for the emperors who destroyed it. As for the Venetian Council of Ten, it is a tribunal of blood, an object of horror to patricians and common people alike. Far from offering the laws a lofty protection, it does nothing, now that the laws have been degraded, but strike blows in the dark that no one dares to notice.

The tribunate, like the government, grows weaker as the number of its members increases. When the tribunes of the Roman people, who at first numbered only two and later five, wished to double their number, the senate allowed it, confident that it could use one tribune to check another, as indeed it afterward freely did.

The best way to prevent usurpation by so formidable a body, though no government has yet adopted it, would be to make the tribunate not permanent but subject to regular intervals during which it would remain suspended. These intervals should not be long enough to allow abuses to take root, and they could be fixed by law in such a way that they might easily be shortened, when necessary, by extraordinary commissions.

This method seems to me to have no disadvantages, because, as I have said, the tribunate forms no part of the constitution and can therefore be removed without affecting it. It also seems effective, because a newly restored magistrate begins not with the power his predecessor exercised but only with the power the law grants him.

### Chapter VI: The Dictatorship

The inflexibility of the laws, which prevents them from adapting to circumstances, can in certain cases make them disastrous and cause them, in a time of crisis, to bring about the ruin of the State. The order and deliberateness of the procedures they require take time, and circumstances sometimes do not allow it. A thousand situations for which the legislator made no provision may arise, and a vital part of foresight is recognizing that not everything can be foreseen.

It is a mistake, therefore, to try to make political institutions so rigid that their operation can never be suspended. Even Sparta allowed its laws to lapse.

Still, only the gravest dangers can outweigh the danger of altering the public order, and the sacred power of the laws should never be halted except when the very existence of the country is at stake. In these rare and unmistakable cases, the public safety is secured by a special act that entrusts it to the person most worthy of the charge. This commission may take one of two forms, depending on the nature of the danger.

If increasing the activity of the government is a sufficient remedy, power is concentrated in the hands of one or two of its members. In that case, what changes is not the authority of the laws but only the manner of administering them. If, on the other hand, the peril is such that the formal machinery of the laws stands in the way of their own preservation, a supreme ruler is appointed who silences all the laws and briefly suspends the sovereign authority. In such a case there can be no doubt about the general will: it is evident that the people's first intention is that the State should not perish. The suspension of legislative authority is therefore in no sense its abolition. The magistrate who silences it cannot make it speak; he dominates it but cannot represent it. He can do anything except make laws.

The Roman senate used the first method when, in a solemn and consecrated formula, it charged the consuls to see to the safety of the Republic. The second method was used when one of the two consuls named a dictator, a custom Rome borrowed from the city of Alba. The nomination was made in secret and at night, as though there were something shameful in placing a man above the laws.

During the early period of the Republic, Rome turned to the dictatorship very often, because the State did not yet rest on a foundation firm enough to sustain itself by the strength of its constitution alone. The moral character of the people at that time made many precautions unnecessary that would have been essential in other periods, so there was no fear that a dictator would abuse his authority or try to keep it beyond his term. On the contrary, so much power seemed a burden to the man who held it, and he hurried to lay it down, as if standing in place of the laws were too troublesome and too dangerous a position to keep.

It is therefore not the danger of its abuse but the danger of its cheapening that leads me to criticize the careless use of this supreme magistracy in the earliest times. As long as it was freely employed for elections, dedications of temples, and purely formal occasions, there was a risk that it would grow less formidable when it was truly needed, and that people would come to regard as empty a title used only for empty ceremony.

Toward the end of the Republic, the Romans, having grown more cautious, were as unreasonably sparing with the dictatorship as they had once been lavish with it. Their fears were plainly groundless. The very weakness of the capital protected it against the magistrates within its walls; a dictator might in certain cases defend public liberty but could never endanger it; and the chains of Rome would be forged not in Rome itself but in her armies. The feeble resistance Marius offered to Sulla, and Pompey to Caesar, showed clearly what could be expected of authority at home when it faced force from abroad.

This misconception led the Romans into serious errors, among them the failure to name a dictator during the conspiracy of Catiline. Since the affair concerned only the city itself and at most a single province of Italy, the unlimited authority the laws granted a dictator would have allowed him to crush the conspiracy quickly. In fact, it was put down only through a combination of lucky accidents that no human prudence had any right to expect.

Instead, the senate contented itself with handing its whole power to the consuls. As a result, Cicero, in order to act effectively, was forced to exceed his powers on a matter of capital importance: the execution of citizens. Though his conduct was approved in the first rush of public joy, he was later rightly called to account for the blood of citizens shed in violation of the laws. No such charge could ever have been brought against a dictator. But the consul's eloquence carried the day. And Cicero himself, Roman though he was, loved his own glory more than his country, and sought not so much the most lawful and secure means of saving the State as the means of securing for himself the entire honor of having saved it. He could not have been sure of that honor had he proposed a dictator, since he dared not nominate himself and could not be certain that his colleague would nominate him. He was therefore rightly honored as the liberator of Rome and rightly punished as a lawbreaker. However glorious his recall from exile may have been, it was unquestionably an act of pardon.

However this momentous trust is conferred, its duration must be fixed at a very short period that can never be extended. In the crises that call for it, the State is either soon lost or soon saved, and once the immediate need has passed, the dictatorship becomes either tyrannical or idle. At Rome, where dictators held office for only six months, most of them resigned before their term expired. Had the term been longer, they might well have tried to extend it further still, as the decemvirs did when they were appointed for a year. The dictator had only enough time to deal with the emergency for which he had been chosen; he had none to devise further projects.

### Chapter VII: The Censorship

Just as the law is the declaration of the general will, the censorship is the declaration of the public judgment. Public opinion is the kind of law the censor administers, and, like the prince, he only applies it to particular cases.

Far from being the arbiter of the people's opinion, the censorial tribunal merely declares it, and the moment the two diverge, its decisions are null and void.

It is pointless to distinguish a nation's morals from the things it esteems, since both rest on the same principle and cannot be separated. There is no people on earth whose choice of pleasures is not determined by opinion rather than by nature. Correct people's opinions, and their morals will purify themselves. People always love what is good, or what they take to be good; it is in judging what is good that they go astray. It is this judgment, then, that must be regulated. Whoever judges morals judges honor, and whoever judges honor takes his law from opinion.

A people's opinions arise from its constitution. Although the law does not regulate morals, it is legislation that brings them into being. When legislation weakens, morals degenerate, and in such cases the judgment of the censors will not accomplish what the force of the laws has failed to achieve.

It follows that the censorship can be useful for preserving morals but never for restoring them. Establish censors while the laws are still vigorous; once the laws have lost their vigor, all hope is gone, for no legitimate power can retain its force when the laws have lost theirs.

The censorship upholds morals by keeping opinion from becoming corrupt, by preserving its soundness through wise application, and sometimes even by settling it while it is still unformed. In the kingdom of France, the practice of bringing seconds to duels had been carried to absurd extremes, and it was abolished by nothing more than a phrase in a royal edict: "As for those who are cowardly enough to call upon seconds." By anticipating the public's judgment, this pronouncement instantly decided it. But when later edicts from the same source tried to declare dueling itself an act of cowardice, which indeed it is, the public ignored the ruling, because common opinion did not regard dueling as cowardly and had already made up its mind on the question.

I have argued elsewhere, at greater length in my *Letter to M. d'Alembert*, that because public opinion is subject to no constraint, the tribunal established to represent it should bear no trace of constraint either. One cannot sufficiently admire the skill with which the Romans, and still more the Spartans, employed this resource, which we moderns have entirely lost.

When a man of bad character made a good proposal in the Spartan council, the Ephors disregarded it and had the same proposal put forward by a virtuous citizen. What an honor for the one and what a disgrace for the other, without a word of praise or blame for either! Some drunkards from Samos once fouled the tribunal of the Ephors; in truth they came from another island, which the delicacy of our language forbids me to name here. The next day a public edict granted the Samians permission to be filthy. An actual punishment would not have been as severe as such an impunity. When Sparta had pronounced on what was or was not right, Greece did not appeal her judgments.

### Chapter VIII: Civil Religion

At first, men had no kings but the gods and no government but theocracy. They reasoned as Caligula did, holding that their rulers were of a different nature from those they ruled, and in that age they reasoned correctly. It takes a long transformation of sentiment before people can bring themselves to accept their equals as masters in the hope of benefiting by it.

From the simple fact that a god was placed at the head of every political society, it followed that there were as many gods as there were peoples. Two peoples who were strangers to each other, and nearly always enemies, could not long acknowledge the same master, any more than two armies in battle could obey the same commander. National divisions thus produced polytheism, and polytheism in turn produced theological and civil intolerance, which, as we shall see, are by nature the same thing.

The Greeks' fondness for rediscovering their own gods among the barbarians arose from their habit of regarding themselves as the natural sovereigns of those peoples. But nothing is more absurd than the scholarship of our own day that identifies and confuses the gods of different nations. As if Moloch, Saturn, and Cronus could be the same god! As if the Phoenician Baal, the Greek Zeus, and the Latin Jupiter could be the same! As if imaginary beings bearing different names could still have anything in common!

If it is asked why, in pagan times, when each State had its own cult and its own gods, there were no wars of religion, I answer that it was precisely because each State, having its own cult as well as its own government, made no distinction between its gods and its laws. Political war was also theological war; the domains of the gods were, so to speak, fixed by the boundaries of nations. The god of one people had no rights over another. The pagan gods were not jealous gods; they divided the rule of the world among themselves. Even Moses and the Hebrews sometimes lent themselves to this view when they spoke of "the God of Israel." It is true that they regarded as powerless the gods of the Canaanites, a proscribed people condemned to destruction, whose land they were to take. But consider how they spoke of the territories of neighboring peoples they were forbidden to attack! "Is not what belongs to your god Chamos lawfully yours?" said Jephthah to the Ammonites. "By the same right, we possess the lands our conquering God has made his own." Here, I think, is an acknowledgment that the rights of Chamos and those of the God of Israel are of the same kind.

But when the Jews, under the rule first of the kings of Babylon and later of the kings of Syria, stubbornly refused to recognize any god but their own, their refusal was treated as rebellion against their conquerors and brought down upon them the persecutions recorded in their history, persecutions without parallel until the coming of Christianity.

Since every religion, then, was bound exclusively to the laws of the State that prescribed it, there was no way to convert a people except by subjugating it, and there could be no missionaries other than conquerors. Because the obligation to change one's cult was a law imposed on the defeated, one had to win before proposing such a change. Far from men fighting for the gods, the gods, as in Homer, fought for men; each side asked its god for victory and repaid him with new altars. Before taking a city, the Romans would summon its gods to depart. When they left the people of Tarentum their angered gods, they regarded those gods as subordinate to their own and obliged to pay them homage. They left the conquered their gods just as they left them their laws, and a wreath offered to Jupiter of the Capitol was often the only tribute they demanded.

Finally, when the Romans had spread their cult and their gods along with their empire, and had often adopted the gods of the conquered as well, granting the rights of the city to both alike, the peoples of that vast empire gradually found themselves with multitudes of gods and cults that were nearly the same everywhere. In this way paganism throughout the known world at last became a single religion.

It was under these circumstances that Jesus came to establish on earth a spiritual kingdom. By separating the theological system from the political one, it ended the unity of the State and set off the internal divisions that have never ceased to trouble Christian peoples. Since the new idea of a kingdom of the other world could never have occurred to pagans, they always regarded the Christians as true rebels who, while pretending to submit, were only waiting for the chance to make themselves independent and masters, and to seize by cunning the authority they professed, in their weakness, to respect. This was the cause of the persecutions.

What the pagans had feared came to pass. Then everything took on a different appearance. The humble Christians changed their tone, and soon this supposed kingdom of the other world became, under a visible leader, the most violent of earthly despotisms.

Yet since there have always been a prince and civil laws as well, this double power and conflict of jurisdiction have made any sound political order impossible in Christian States, and people have never managed to determine whether they were bound to obey the ruler or the priest.

Several peoples, even in Europe and its vicinity, have tried without success to preserve or restore the old system, but the spirit of Christianity has prevailed everywhere. The sacred cult has always remained, or has again become, independent of the Sovereign, without any necessary connection to the body of the State.

Muhammad held very sound views and bound his political system tightly together. As long as his form of government endured under the caliphs who succeeded him, that government was truly unified and, to that extent, good. But the Arabs, having become prosperous, learned, refined, soft, and cowardly, were conquered by barbarians. The division between the two powers then began again, and although it is less apparent among Muslims than among Christians, it nonetheless exists, especially in the sect of Ali, and there are States, such as Persia, where it makes itself felt continually.

Among us, the kings of England have made themselves heads of the Church, and the Czars have done the same. But this title has made them less its masters than its ministers. They have acquired not so much the right to change it as the power to maintain it; they are not its legislators but only its princes. Wherever the clergy forms a corporate body, it is master and legislator in its own domain. There are thus two powers, two Sovereigns, in England and in Russia, just as elsewhere.
