Of all Christian writers, the philosopher Hobbes alone saw the evil and its remedy, and dared to propose reuniting the two heads of the eagle, the spiritual and the temporal powers, and restoring political unity throughout, without which no State or government will ever be properly constituted. But he should have seen that the domineering spirit of Christianity is incompatible with his system, and that the interest of the priesthood would always prove stronger than that of the State. It is not so much what is false and terrible in his political theory as what is just and true in it that has brought hatred upon it.

I believe that if history were studied from this point of view, it would be easy to refute the opposing positions of Bayle and Warburton, the first of whom holds that religion can be of no use to the body politic, while the second maintains on the contrary that Christianity is its strongest support. We could show the former that no State has ever been founded without a religious basis, and the latter that the law of Christianity, at bottom, does more harm by weakening the constitution of the State than good by strengthening it. To make myself understood, I need only make somewhat more precise the overly vague ideas of religion as they bear on this subject.

Religion, considered in relation to society, which is either general or particular, can likewise be divided into two kinds: the religion of the man and the religion of the citizen. The first has neither temples, nor altars, nor rites, and is confined to the purely inward worship of the supreme God and the eternal obligations of morality. It is the religion of the Gospel, pure and simple, the true theism, and it may be called natural divine right or law. The second is codified within a single country, which it provides with its gods and its own guardian patrons. It has its dogmas, its rites, and its outward forms of worship prescribed by law. Outside the single nation that follows it, the whole world is, in its eyes, infidel, alien, and barbarous; for this religion, the rights and duties of man extend no farther than its own altars. All the religions of the earliest peoples were of this kind, and we may call it civil or positive divine law.

There is a third kind of religion, stranger than the others, which gives men two codes of law, two rulers, and two homelands, binds them to contradictory duties, and makes it impossible for them to be faithful at once to their religion and to their citizenship. Such are the religions of the Lamas and of the Japanese, and such is Roman Christianity. This may be called the religion of the priest, and it produces a kind of mixed and antisocial law that has no name.

Considered politically, all three kinds of religion have their defects. The third is so plainly bad that it would be a waste of time to stop and prove it. Whatever destroys social unity is worthless; every institution that sets a man at odds with himself is worthless.

The second is good insofar as it unites the worship of God with love of the laws and, by making the homeland the object of the citizens' devotion, teaches them that to serve the State is to serve its guardian god. It is a form of theocracy, in which there can be no high priest but the prince and no priests but the magistrates. To die for one's country then becomes martyrdom; to break its laws becomes impiety; and to hold a guilty man up to public execration is to deliver him to the wrath of the gods, as in the old Roman formula of condemnation: *Sacer estod*, "Let him be accursed."

On the other hand, it is bad because, being founded on falsehood and error, it deceives men, makes them credulous and superstitious, and drowns the true worship of the Divinity in empty ceremony. It is bad, too, when it becomes tyrannical and exclusive and makes a people bloodthirsty and intolerant, so that it breathes fire and slaughter and regards the killing of anyone who does not believe in its gods as a sacred act. The result is to place such a people in a natural state of war with all others, so that its own security is gravely endangered.

There remains, then, the religion of man, or Christianity: not the Christianity of today, but that of the Gospel, which is something entirely different. Through this holy, sublime, and true religion, all men, as children of one God, recognize one another as brothers, and the society that unites them is not dissolved even by death.

But because this religion has no particular relation to the body politic, it leaves the laws with only the force they possess on their own, adding nothing to it, and so one of the great bonds that hold a particular society together fails to operate. Worse still, far from attaching the citizens' hearts to the State, it detaches them from all earthly things. I know of nothing more contrary to the social spirit.

We are told that a nation of true Christians would form the most perfect society imaginable. I see only one great difficulty in this supposition: a society of true Christians would not be a society of men.

I would go further and say that such a society, for all its perfection, would be neither the strongest nor the most durable. Its very perfection would deprive it of the bond that holds it together; the flaw that would destroy it would lie in that perfection itself.

Everyone would do his duty. The people would obey the laws, the rulers would be just and moderate, the magistrates upright and incorruptible, the soldiers contemptuous of death, and there would be neither vanity nor luxury. So far, so good; but let us hear more.

Christianity is a wholly spiritual religion, concerned only with the things of heaven; the Christian's homeland is not of this world. He does his duty, it is true, but he does it with profound indifference to whether his efforts succeed or fail. So long as he has nothing to reproach himself for, it matters little to him whether things go well or badly here on earth. If the State prospers, he hardly dares share in the public happiness, for fear of growing proud of his country's glory; if the State declines, he blesses the hand of God that lies heavy on His people.

For the State to be peaceful and for harmony to be maintained, every citizen without exception would have to be a good Christian. If, by bad luck, there were a single self-seeker or hypocrite among them (a Catiline, say, or a Cromwell), he would certainly get the better of his pious fellow citizens. Christian charity does not easily allow a man to think ill of his neighbors. Once such a man has found, by some trick, the art of imposing on them and seizing a share of public authority, he becomes a man of rank, and it is God's will that he be respected. Soon he becomes a power, and it is God's will that he be obeyed. If he abuses that power, it is the scourge with which God punishes His children. People would have scruples about driving out the usurper: it would mean disturbing the public peace, resorting to violence, and shedding blood, all of which sits poorly with Christian meekness. And after all, in this vale of tears, what does it matter whether we are free men or serfs? What counts is getting to heaven, and resignation is only one more means of getting there.

If war breaks out with another State, the citizens march readily into battle, and not one of them thinks of running away. They do their duty, but they have no passion for victory; they know better how to die than how to win. What does it matter to them whether they win or lose? Does Providence not know better than they what is good for them? Imagine what use a proud, impetuous, and passionate enemy could make of their stoicism! Set against them those generous peoples who were consumed by a burning love of glory and of country; imagine your Christian republic face to face with Sparta or Rome. The pious Christians would be beaten, crushed, and destroyed before they knew what had happened, or would owe their survival only to the contempt their enemy conceived for them. The soldiers of Fabius, in my view, swore a fine oath: they swore not to conquer or die, but to come back victorious, and they kept their word. Christians would never have sworn such an oath; they would have regarded it as tempting God.

But I am wrong to speak of a Christian republic; the two terms cancel each other out. Christianity preaches nothing but servitude and dependence. Its spirit is so favorable to tyranny that it always profits from such a regime. True Christians are made to be slaves; they know it, and they hardly mind, for this short life counts for too little in their eyes.

I will be told that Christian troops are excellent. I deny it. Show me an example. For my part, I know of no Christian troops. I will be told of the Crusades. Without disputing the Crusaders' valor, I reply that, far from being Christians, they were the priests' soldiers, citizens of the Church. They fought for their spiritual homeland, which the Church had somehow turned into a temporal one. Properly understood, this is a return to paganism: since the Gospel establishes no national religion, a holy war is impossible among Christians.

Under the pagan emperors, Christian soldiers were brave. Every Christian writer says so, and I believe it: they were engaged in an honorable rivalry with the pagan troops. Once the emperors became Christian, that rivalry ceased, and when the Cross had driven out the eagle, the standard of the Roman legions, Roman valor vanished entirely.

But setting political considerations aside, let us return to the question of right and settle our principles on this important point. The right that the social compact gives the Sovereign over its subjects does not, as we have seen, extend beyond the bounds of public utility. The subjects, then, owe the Sovereign an account of their opinions only insofar as those opinions matter to the community. Now, it matters a great deal to the community that each citizen should have a religion, for that will make him love his duty. But the dogmas of that religion concern the State and its members only insofar as they bear on morality and on the duties the believer is bound to fulfill toward others. Beyond that, each person may hold whatever opinions he pleases, and it is no business of the Sovereign to take notice of them. The Sovereign has no authority in the other world; whatever becomes of its subjects in the life to come is none of its concern, provided they are good citizens in this one.

There is, therefore, a purely civil profession of faith, whose articles the Sovereign should fix, not strictly as religious dogmas, but as social sentiments without which a person cannot be a good citizen or a loyal subject. The Sovereign cannot compel anyone to believe them, but it can banish from the State anyone who does not believe them. It banishes him not for impiety, but as an antisocial being, incapable of sincerely loving the laws and justice, or of sacrificing his life to his duty when the need arises. And if anyone, after publicly acknowledging these dogmas, behaves as though he does not believe them, let him be punished with death: he has committed the worst of all crimes, that of lying before the law.

The dogmas of the civil religion should be few, simple, and precisely worded, without explanation or commentary. The existence of a powerful, intelligent, and benevolent Deity who foresees and provides; the life to come; the happiness of the just; the punishment of the wicked; the sanctity of the social contract and the laws: these are its positive dogmas. Its negative dogmas I limit to one. It forbids intolerance, which belongs to the religions we have rejected.

Those who distinguish civil intolerance from theological intolerance are, in my view, mistaken. The two are inseparable. It is impossible to live at peace with people we believe to be damned; to love them would be to hate the God who punishes them. We must absolutely either reclaim them or torment them. Wherever theological intolerance is admitted, it inevitably produces some civil effect; and once it does, the Sovereign is no longer sovereign even in temporal matters. From then on, priests are the real masters, and kings are merely their ministers.

Now that there is no longer, and can no longer be, an exclusive national religion, all religions that tolerate others should themselves be tolerated, so long as their dogmas contain nothing contrary to the duties of citizenship. But whoever dares to say "Outside the Church there is no salvation" ought to be driven from the State, unless the State is the Church and the prince is its pontiff. Such a dogma is good only under a theocratic government; under any other, it is fatal. Henry IV is said to have embraced the Roman faith because Protestant ministers granted that a Catholic could be saved while Catholic theologians denied that a Protestant could, which made Catholicism the safer wager. That very reason ought to make every honest man abandon it, and still more any prince who knows how to reason.

*Note:* In the Vulgate, the text reads: *Nonne ea quae possidet Chamos deus tuus, tibi jure debentur?* (Judges 11:24), "Is not what your god Chemosh possesses rightfully yours?" Father de Carrières translates it: "Do you not regard yourselves as having a right to what your god possesses?" I do not know the force of the Hebrew original, but I can see that in the Vulgate Jephthah plainly acknowledges the right of the god Chemosh, and that the French translator weakened this admission by inserting an "according to you" that does not appear in the Latin.

*Note:* It is quite clear that the Phocian War, which was called "the Sacred War," was not a war of religion. Its purpose was to punish acts of sacrilege, not to conquer unbelievers.

*Note:* It should be observed that the clergy find their bond of union less in formal assemblies than in the communion of the Churches. Communion and excommunication are the social compact of the clergy, a compact that will always make them masters of peoples and kings. All priests who share communion are fellow citizens, even if they come from opposite ends of the earth. This invention is a masterpiece of statecraft. Nothing like it exists among pagan priests, who have therefore never formed a clerical corporate body.

*Note:* See, for instance, the letter Grotius wrote to his brother on April 11, 1643, in which that learned man sets out what he found to praise and what he found to censure in Hobbes's *De Cive*. It is true that, being inclined to leniency, he seems to forgive the author the good for the sake of the bad; but not everyone is so forgiving.

*Note:* "In a republic," says the Marquis d'Argenson, "each man is perfectly free in whatever does not harm others." This is the invariable limit, and it cannot be defined more precisely. I have not been able to deny myself the pleasure of occasionally quoting from this manuscript, though it is unknown to the public, in order to honor the memory of a good and distinguished man who kept, even as a minister of state, the heart of a good citizen, along with sound and just views on the government of his country.

*Note:* Caesar, pleading for Catiline, tried to establish the dogma that the soul is mortal. Cato and Cicero, in refuting him, wasted no time on philosophy. They were content to show that Caesar spoke like a bad citizen and was advancing a doctrine that would harm the State. That, and not a question of theology, was in fact what the Roman Senate had to judge.

*Note:* Marriage, for example, being a civil contract, has civil effects without which society cannot even survive. Suppose a body of clergy claims the sole right to permit this act, a right that every intolerant religion must necessarily claim. Is it not clear that, in establishing the Church's authority on this point, it will destroy the prince's, who will thereafter have only as many subjects as the clergy choose to allow him? Able to marry people or refuse to marry them according to whether they accept this doctrine or that, admit or reject this formula or that, show more piety or less, the Church alone, by exercising prudence and firmness, will dispose of all inheritances, offices, and citizens, and even of the State itself, which could not survive if it were composed entirely of bastards. But, I will be told, there will be appeals to the secular courts against ecclesiastical abuse, summonses, and decrees; the Church's revenues and property will be seized. How sad! The clergy, if it has even a little (I will not say courage, but sense), will pay no attention and go its own way. It will calmly allow the appeals, summonses, decrees, and seizures, and in the end it will remain master. It is no great sacrifice, I think, to give up a part when one is sure of securing the whole.

### Chapter IX: Conclusion

Having now laid down the true principles of political right and tried to give the State a foundation of its own to rest on, I ought next to strengthen it through its external relations. That would include the law of nations, commerce, the right of war and conquest, public law, alliances, negotiations, treaties, and so on. But all this forms a new subject, far too vast for my limited scope. I ought throughout to have confined myself to a narrower sphere.

## A Discourse on the Arts and Sciences

*Which won the prize at the Academy of Dijon in 1750*

On this question proposed by the Academy: Has the restoration of the arts and sciences had a purifying effect upon morals?

*Barbarus hic ego sum, qui non intelligor illis.* (Ovid: "Here I am the barbarian, because no one understands me.")

### Preface

The pages that follow take up one of the loftiest and most compelling of all moral questions. They are not concerned, however, with the metaphysical subtleties that have lately crept into every branch of literature, and from which even our academic curricula are not always free. We are dealing here with one of those truths on which the happiness of mankind depends.

I foresee that I will not easily be forgiven for the position I have taken. Setting myself against everything most admired today, I can expect nothing less than a universal outcry, and the approval of a few sensible people is not enough to make me count on that of the public. But I have taken my stand, and I will go to no trouble to please either intellectuals or fashionable society. In every age there are people born to be in bondage to the opinions of the society they live in. Not a few of those who today play the freethinker and the philosopher would, had they lived in the time of the League, the militant Catholic faction of France's wars of religion, have been nothing more than fanatics. No author who means to outlive his own age should write for such readers.

One word more and I am done. Since I did not expect the honor the Academy conferred on me, I had, after submitting my Discourse, revised and enlarged it so thoroughly as to make it almost a new work. Under the circumstances, however, I have felt obliged to publish it exactly as it stood when it won the prize. I have only added a few notes and kept two easily recognizable changes of which the Academy might perhaps not have approved. The respect, gratitude, and even justice I owe that body seemed to me to require this acknowledgment.

### Moral Effects of the Arts and Sciences

*Decipimur specie recti.* (Horace: "We are deceived by the appearance of what is right.")

The question before me is "whether the restoration of the arts and sciences has had the effect of purifying morals or corrupting them." Which side am I to take? The side, gentlemen, that befits an honest man who is aware of his own ignorance and thinks himself none the worse for it.

I feel how difficult it is to treat this subject fittingly before the tribunal that is to judge what I put forward. How can I presume to disparage the sciences before one of the most learned assemblies in Europe, to praise ignorance in a famous Academy, and to reconcile my contempt for study with the respect owed to the truly learned?

I saw these contradictions, but they did not discourage me. It is not science I am attacking, I told myself; it is virtue I am defending, and before virtuous men. Goodness is dearer still to the good than learning is to the learned.

What, then, have I to fear? The discernment of the assembly before which I am pleading? That, I admit, is to be feared, but more on account of flaws in how my argument is constructed than of the views it holds. Just sovereigns have never hesitated to rule against themselves in doubtful cases, and indeed the most favorable position a just claim can occupy is to be laid before an arbiter who is both just and enlightened, and who is judge in his own cause.

To this encouraging motive I may add another that finally decided me. Since I have upheld the cause of truth as well as my natural abilities allow, there is one reward that cannot fail me, whatever success I appear to have. I will find that reward at the bottom of my own heart.

### The First Part

It is a noble and beautiful sight to see man raise himself, so to speak, out of nothing by his own efforts; dispel, by the light of reason, the thick clouds in which nature had wrapped him; rise above himself; soar in thought to the heavenly regions; stride like the sun across the vast expanse of the universe; and, what is grander and more wonderful still, turn back into himself, there to study man and come to know his own nature, his duties, and his purpose. All these marvels we have seen revived within the last few generations.

Europe had sunk back into the barbarism of the earliest ages. A few centuries ago, the inhabitants of this part of the world, now so highly enlightened, lived in a condition worse than ignorance. A scientific jargon, more contemptible than plain ignorance, had usurped the name of knowledge and placed an almost insurmountable obstacle in the way of its restoration.

Things had reached such a point that it took a complete revolution to bring people back to common sense. It came at last from the quarter where it was least expected. It was the obtuse Muslim, the eternal scourge of letters, who was the immediate cause of their revival among us. The fall of Constantine's throne at Constantinople brought the relics of ancient Greece to Italy, and France in turn was enriched by these precious spoils. The sciences soon followed literature, and the art of thinking joined the art of writing, an order that may seem strange but is perhaps only too natural. The world now began to perceive the chief advantage of keeping company with the Muses: it makes people more sociable by inspiring them with the desire to please one another through works worthy of each other's approval.

The mind has its needs, just as the body does. The needs of the body are the foundation of society; those of the mind are its ornaments.

So long as government and law provide for people's security and well-being in their common life, the arts, literature, and the sciences, less despotic though perhaps more powerful, fling garlands of flowers over the chains that weigh them down. They stifle in people's hearts that sense of original liberty for which they seem to have been born, make them love their own slavery, and so turn them into what is called a civilized people.

Necessity raised up thrones; the arts and sciences have made them strong. Powers of the earth, cherish every talent and protect those who cultivate it! Civilized peoples, cultivate these pursuits! To them, happy slaves, you owe the delicacy and refinement of taste you so pride yourselves on, and that gentleness of temper and urbanity of manners which make your dealings with one another so easy and agreeable. In a word, you owe them the appearance of every virtue without the possession of a single one.

It was for this kind of polish, all the more captivating for seeming so unaffected, that Athens and Rome were so distinguished in the celebrated days of their splendor and magnificence; and it is doubtless in this same respect that our own age and nation will surpass all times and peoples. A philosophical air without pedantry; manners at once natural and engaging, as far removed from Teutonic clumsiness as from Italian pantomime: these are the effects of a taste formed by liberal studies and refined by conversation with the world. How happy those who live among us would be if our outward appearance always faithfully mirrored our hearts; if propriety were virtue itself; if the maxims we profess were the rules we lived by; and if true philosophy were inseparable from the title of philosopher! But so many good qualities too seldom go together, and virtue rarely travels with so much pomp and ceremony.

Rich clothing may announce a man of fortune, and elegance a man of taste, but true health and manly vigor are known by other signs. It is under the laborer's coarse homespun cloth, not beneath the courtier's gilt and tinsel, that we should look for strength and vigor of body.

Outward ornament is no less foreign to virtue, which is the strength and vigor of the mind. The good man is like an athlete who prefers to wrestle stripped bare, as the wrestlers of antiquity did. He scorns all those vile trappings that hamper the use of his strength, most of which were invented only to hide some deformity.

Before art had shaped our behavior and taught our passions to speak an artificial language, our manners were rough but natural, and the differences in our conduct revealed at a glance the differences in our characters. Human nature was not fundamentally better then than it is now; but people found their security in the ease with which they could see through one another, and this advantage, whose value we no longer feel the value, spared them a great many vices.

Today, more subtle inquiry and a more refined taste have reduced the art of pleasing to a set of rules, and our manners are governed by a servile, deceptive uniformity, as though every mind had been cast in the same mold. Politeness demands one thing, propriety another; ceremony has its forms and fashion its laws, and these we must always obey, never the promptings of our own nature. We no longer dare to appear as we really are. We live under perpetual constraint, and the herd of human beings we call society, placed in the same circumstances, all behave exactly alike unless some very particular and powerful motive intervenes. So we never know whom we are dealing with. Even to know our friends, we must wait for some critical and urgent occasion, which is to say until it is too late, since it is precisely on such occasions that this knowledge would have been useful.

What a train of vices must follow from this uncertainty! Sincere friendship, genuine esteem, and complete trust are banished from human life. Jealousy, suspicion, fear, coldness, reserve, hatred, and treachery lie permanently hidden beneath that uniform and deceitful veil of politeness, beneath the much-vaunted candor and urbanity we owe to the enlightenment of our age. We will no longer take the name of our Creator in vain by swearing oaths; instead we will insult Him with blasphemies, and our fastidious ears will take no offense. We have grown too modest to boast of our own merits, yet we do not hesitate to disparage the merits of others. We no longer insult even our enemies crudely; we slander them with skill. Our hatred of other nations is fading, but love of our own country is dying along with it. Ignorance is held in contempt, but a dangerous skepticism has taken its place. Some vices are now condemned, and others have become disreputable, yet we still have many that are honored with the names of virtues, and it has become necessary either to possess them or at least to pretend to. Let anyone who wishes praise the moderation of our modern sages. I see in it only a more refined form of intemperance, as undeserving of my praise as their artificial simplicity.

Such is the purity our morals have attained; this is the virtue we have made our own. Let the arts and sciences claim their share in this wholesome achievement. I will add only one further reflection. Suppose an inhabitant of some distant country tried to form an idea of European morals from the state of our sciences, the perfection of our arts, the decorum of our public entertainments, the politeness of our behavior, the affability of our conversation, our constant professions of goodwill, and those bustling crowds of people of every rank who seem, from morning until night, to have no concern but to oblige one another. Such a stranger, I maintain, would form an entirely false picture of our morals.

Where there is no effect, there is no point in searching for a cause. But here the effect is certain and the depravity real: our minds have been corrupted in exact proportion as the arts and sciences have advanced. Will it be said that this misfortune belongs to our own age alone? No, gentlemen. The evils that spring from our idle curiosity are as old as the world. The daily ebb and flow of the tides is not governed more regularly by the moon than the morals of a people are by the progress of the arts and sciences. As their light has risen above our horizon, virtue has fled, and the same phenomenon has been observed in every time and place.

Consider Egypt, the first school of humankind, that ancient land famous for its fertility beneath a hard, rainless sky of brass, the place from which Sesostris once set out to conquer the world. Egypt became the mother of philosophy and the fine arts, and soon afterward she was conquered by Cambyses, and then in turn by the Greeks, the Romans, the Arabs, and finally the Turks.

Consider Greece, once peopled by heroes who twice defeated Asia. While letters were still in their infancy, they had not yet corrupted the character of its people. But the progress of the sciences soon brought a dissolution of morals and the imposition of the Macedonian yoke. From that time on, Greece, always learned, always pleasure-loving, and always enslaved, has experienced through all its upheavals nothing more than a change of masters. Not all the eloquence of Demosthenes could breathe life back into a body that luxury and the arts had once drained of its strength.

Rome, founded by a shepherd and made illustrious by farmers, did not begin to degenerate until the days of Ennius and Terence. But after the appearance of an Ovid, a Catullus, a Martial, and the whole crowd of obscene authors whose very names are enough to make modesty blush, Rome, once the temple of virtue, became the theater of vice, the scorn of nations, and a laughingstock even to barbarians. So the capital of the world at last bowed beneath the yoke of slavery it had imposed on others, and the very day of its fall came on the eve of the day it conferred on one of its citizens the title of Arbiter of Good Taste.

What shall I say of the capital of the Eastern Empire, whose location seemed to destine it to be the capital of the world, that refuge of the arts and sciences when they had been banished from the rest of Europe, perhaps more by wisdom than by barbarism? The most shameless debauchery, the most depraved villainy, the most atrocious crimes, conspiracies, murders, and assassinations are the threads from which the whole history of Constantinople is woven. Such is the pure spring from which the flood of knowledge that our age so prides itself on has reached us.

But why look to past ages for proof of a truth for which the present supplies ample evidence? There is in Asia a vast empire where learning is honored and leads to the highest offices of state. If the sciences improved our morals, if they inspired us with courage and taught us to give our lives for our country, the Chinese ought to be wise, free, and invincible. But if there is no vice they do not practice and no crime with which they are unfamiliar; if neither the shrewdness of their ministers, nor the supposed wisdom of their laws, nor the multitude of people who fill that immense empire could save them from the yoke of the crude and ignorant Tartars, then what use were all their scholars and men of letters? What benefit has that country gained from the honors it heaps upon its learned men? Could it be the benefit of being peopled by a race of scoundrels and slaves?

Set against these examples the morals of those few nations that, protected from the contagion of useless knowledge, have by their virtues made themselves happy and set an example for the rest of the world. Such were the first inhabitants of Persia, a nation so remarkable that virtue was taught among them the way the sciences are taught among us. They subdued Asia with great ease, and they alone enjoy the distinction of having the history of their political institutions regarded as a philosophical romance. Such were the Scythians, of whom such marvelous praises have come down to us. Such were the Germans, whose simplicity, innocence, and virtue offered a most welcome relief to the pen of a historian weary of describing the baseness and villainies of an enlightened, wealthy, and pleasure-loving nation. Such, too, had Rome been in the days of its poverty and ignorance. And such, even in our own time, has proved to be that rustic nation whose justly celebrated courage not even adversity could conquer, and whose loyalty no example could corrupt.

It was not out of stupidity that these peoples preferred other kinds of activity to those of the mind. They were well aware that in other countries there were men who spent their time disputing idly about the supreme good, and about vice and virtue. They knew that these useless thinkers lavished praise on themselves and contemptuously branded other nations as barbarians. But they observed the morals of these people, and so learned what to think of their learning.

Can we forget that in the very heart of Greece there arose a city as famous for the happy ignorance of its citizens as for the wisdom of its laws, a republic of demigods rather than of men, so far did their virtues seem to surpass ordinary humanity? Sparta, eternal proof of the emptiness of learning! While the vices, led in by the fine arts, were making their way into Athens, and while that city's tyrant was carefully assembling the works of Homer, the prince of poets, Sparta was driving from her walls the artists and the arts, the learned and their learning.

The difference showed in the outcome. Athens became the home of politeness and taste, the land of orators and philosophers. The elegance of its buildings matched that of its language; everywhere one saw marble and canvas brought to life by the hands of the most skillful masters. From Athens came those astonishing works that every corrupt age will take as its models. The portrait of Sparta is less vividly colored. There, the neighboring peoples used to say, "men are born virtuous, and the very air of the country seems to inspire virtue." Yet its citizens left us nothing but the memory of their heroic deeds, monuments that should count for no less in our eyes than the most curious relics of Athenian marble.

It is true that among the Athenians a few wise men resisted the general current and kept their integrity even in the company of the Muses. But listen to the judgment that the foremost, and most unfortunate, of them passed on the artists and scholars of his day.

"I have examined the poets," he says, "and I regard them as people whose talents deceive both themselves and others. They present themselves as wise and are taken for wise, but in reality they are anything but."

"From the poets," Socrates continues, "I turned to the artists. No one knew less about the arts than I did; no one was more thoroughly convinced that artists possessed remarkable knowledge. I soon discovered, however, that they were in as bad a state as the poets, and that both had fallen into the same error. Because the most skillful among them excel others in their particular craft, they believe themselves wiser than the rest of humanity. This arrogance spoiled all their skill in my eyes. So, putting myself in the oracle's place and asking myself whether I would rather be what I am or what they are, know what they know or know that I know nothing, I answered readily, for myself and for the god, that I would rather remain as I am.

"None of us, neither the sophists, nor the poets, nor the orators, nor the artists, nor I, knows the nature of the true, the good, or the beautiful. But there is this difference between us: though none of these people knows anything, they all believe they know something, whereas I, if I know nothing, am at least in no doubt about my ignorance. So the superior wisdom the oracle attributed to me comes down simply to this: I am fully convinced that I do not know what I do not know."

So we find Socrates, the wisest of men in the judgment of the god and the most learned of Athenians in the opinion of all Greece, speaking in praise of ignorance. Were he alive today, there is little reason to think our modern scholars and artists would make him change his mind. No, gentlemen, that honest man would still persist in despising our vain sciences. He would do nothing to swell the flood of books pouring in from every side. He would leave us, as he left his disciples, only the example and the memory of his virtues, and that is the noblest way to instruct humankind.

What Socrates had begun in Athens, Cato the Elder carried on in Rome, railing against those seductive and subtle Greeks who were corrupting the virtue and sapping the courage of his fellow citizens. Culture nonetheless prevailed. Rome filled with philosophers and orators; military discipline was neglected and agriculture despised; men formed sects and forgot their country. The sacred names of liberty, selflessness, and obedience to the law gave way to the names of Epicurus, Zeno, and Arcesilaus. Their own philosophers even had a saying that since learned men had appeared among them, honest men had gone into eclipse. Before that time the Romans had been content to practice virtue; they were ruined when they began to study it.

What would the great soul of Fabricius have felt had it been his misfortune to be called back to life and to see the pomp and magnificence of that Rome which his arm had saved from ruin, and which his honored name had made more illustrious than all its conquests? "Gods!" he would have said. "What has become of those thatched roofs and rustic hearths that were once the dwellings of temperance and virtue? What fatal splendor has replaced the old Roman simplicity? What is this foreign language, this effeminacy of manners? What is the meaning of these statues, these paintings, these buildings? Fools, what have you done? You, the masters of the earth, have made yourselves the slaves of the frivolous nations you conquered. You are governed by rhetoricians. It was only to enrich architects, painters, sculptors, and actors that you soaked Greece and Asia with your blood. Even the spoils of Carthage have become the prize of a flute player. Romans! Romans! Hurry and tear down those amphitheaters, smash those statues, burn those paintings. Drive out these slaves who hold you in subjection and whose fatal arts are corrupting your morals. Let other hands win fame through such empty talents; the only talent worthy of Rome is that of conquering the world and making virtue reign in it. When Cineas took the Roman Senate for an assembly of kings, it was not useless pomp or studied elegance that struck him. He heard none of that hollow eloquence that is now the study and the delight of frivolous orators. What, then, was the majesty Cineas beheld? Fellow citizens, he saw the noblest sight that ever existed under heaven, a sight that all your riches and all your arts can never show: an assembly of two hundred virtuous men, worthy to command in Rome and to govern the world."

But let us set aside the distance of time and place and look at what has happened in our own time and country. Or rather, let us spare ourselves odious descriptions that might offend our delicacy, and save ourselves the trouble of repeating the same things under different names. It was not for nothing that I summoned the shade of Fabricius, for what have I put in his mouth that could not have come just as fittingly from Louis XII or Henry IV? It is true that in France Socrates would not have drunk the hemlock. But he would have drunk from a far more bitter cup: insult, mockery, and contempt a hundred times worse than death.

So it is that luxury, dissolution, and slavery have in every age been the punishment for our proud attempts to emerge from the happy ignorance in which the wisdom of Providence placed us. The thick veil with which it has covered all its workings seems sufficient proof that it never meant us for such fruitless inquiries. But is there a single lesson it has taught us that we have properly profited from, or that we have neglected without paying for it? Let men learn, for once, that nature wished to protect them from science as a mother snatches a dangerous weapon from her child's hands. Let them know that all the secrets she hides from them are so many evils from which she shields them, and that the very difficulty they find in acquiring knowledge is not the least of her kindnesses toward them. Men are perverse, but they would have been far worse had they had the misfortune to be born learned.

How humiliating these reflections are for humanity, and how they ought to mortify our pride! "What!" someone will ask. "Is integrity the child of ignorance? Are virtue and learning incompatible? What consequences might not follow from such suppositions?" But to reconcile these apparent contradictions, we need only examine closely the emptiness and vanity of those grand titles so freely bestowed on human knowledge, titles that so blind our judgment. Let us consider, then, the arts and sciences in themselves. Let us see what must result from their progress, and let us not hesitate to accept the truth of every point on which our reasoning agrees with the conclusions we can draw from history.

Sovereigns are always pleased to see their subjects develop a taste for the arts of amusement and superfluity, provided those arts do not drain the country of its gold and silver. They understand perfectly well that, besides nourishing the pettiness of mind that suits a condition of slavery, every new artificial want simply fastens another chain upon the people. Alexander, wishing to keep the Ichthyophagi, a coastal people who lived on fish, in a state of dependence, forced them to give up fishing and live on the ordinary food of civilized nations. The savages of America, who go naked and live entirely on what they hunt, have always proved impossible to subdue. And indeed, what yoke can be imposed on people who need nothing?

"I love to converse and to argue," said Montaigne, "but only with very few people, and only for my own satisfaction. To do so as entertainment for the great, or to make a show of one's talents, is in my opinion a trade quite unbecoming to a man of honor." It is the trade of all our intellectuals, save one.

I dare not speak of those happy nations that did not even know the names of many vices we find it hard to suppress. I mean the savages of America, whose simple and natural form of government Montaigne preferred without hesitation not only to the laws of Plato but to the most perfect visions of government that philosophy could ever devise. He cites many examples, striking to anyone capable of appreciating them. And yet, he says, what of it? They don't even wear breeches!

What are we to suppose the Athenians themselves really thought of eloquence, when they took such care to banish rhetorical display from that upright tribunal against whose verdicts even their gods made no appeal? What did the Romans think of physicians, when they expelled medicine from the republic? And when the remnant of humanity left among the Spaniards led them to forbid their lawyers to set foot in America, what must they have thought of jurisprudence? Might it not be said that they hoped, by this single measure, to make amends for all the outrages they had committed against the unhappy Indians?

### The Second Part

An ancient tradition passed from Egypt into Greece that the sciences were invented by a god who was hostile to the peace of mankind. What, then, must the Egyptians, among whom the sciences first arose, have thought of them? They, after all, saw close at hand the sources from which the sciences sprang. And whether we turn to the annals of the world or supplement the uncertain chronicles of history with philosophical inquiry, we will not find that human knowledge had an origin to match the flattering idea we now like to hold of it. Astronomy was born of superstition; eloquence of ambition, hatred, falsehood, and flattery; geometry of avarice; physics of idle curiosity; and even moral philosophy of human pride. The arts and sciences, then, owe their birth to our vices. We would have fewer doubts about their benefits if they had sprung from our virtues.

Their evil origin is reflected all too plainly in their objects. What would become of the arts if luxury did not nourish them? If men were not unjust, what use would there be for jurisprudence? What would become of history if there were no tyrants, no wars, no conspiracies? In short, who would spend his life in barren speculation if everyone, attentive only to the duties of humanity and the needs of nature, devoted his whole life to serving his country, helping his friends, and relieving the unfortunate? Are we really made to live and die at the edge of that well at whose bottom, as the ancient saying goes, Truth lies hidden? This reflection alone, in my view, should be enough to discourage at the very outset anyone who seriously sets out to educate himself through the study of philosophy.

What dangers surround us! How many false paths open before us in the pursuit of the sciences! Through how many errors, each more dangerous than truth is useful, must we pass before we reach it? Our disadvantage is obvious: falsehood can take an infinite variety of forms, while truth has only one way of being. Besides, where is the man who sincerely wants to find it? And even granting his good will, by what distinguishing marks can he be sure he has recognized it? Amid the endless diversity of opinions, where is the criterion by which we can judge it with certainty? And, harder still, if we should be lucky enough to discover it, which of us will know how to use it rightly?

If our sciences are futile in the aims they set themselves, they are no less dangerous in the effects they produce. Born of idleness, they breed idleness in turn, and the first harm they necessarily inflict on society is an irreparable loss of time. In the political world as in the moral one, to live without doing any good is a great evil, and so every useless citizen should be regarded as a harmful one. Tell me, then, illustrious philosophers, you who have taught us the ratios by which attraction acts in a vacuum; the relation, in the revolutions of the planets, between the areas swept out in equal times; which curves have conjugate points, points of inflection, and cusps; how the soul and the body keep in step, like two clocks set to the same time, without any actual communication between them; which planets might be inhabited; and which insects reproduce in extraordinary ways. Answer me, I say, you from whom we have received all this sublime information: had you taught us none of these fine things, would we have been fewer in number, worse governed, less formidable, less prosperous, or more corrupt?

Reconsider, then, the importance of your productions. And since the labors of our most enlightened scholars and best citizens are of so little use, tell us what we ought to think of that vast herd of obscure writers and idle men of letters who devour the substance of the State and give nothing in return.

Idle, did I say? Would to God they were! Society would be more peaceful and morals less corrupt. But these vain and frivolous declaimers go out in every direction, armed with their deadly paradoxes, to undermine the foundations of faith and bring virtue to nothing. They smile with contempt at such old-fashioned words as patriotism and religion, and dedicate their talents and their philosophy to destroying and defaming everything that men hold sacred. It is not that they truly hate virtue or doctrine; they are enemies of public opinion and nothing else. To bring them to the foot of the altar, it would be enough to exile them to a land of atheists. To what extravagances will the craving to be different not drive men!

Wasting time is certainly a great evil, but still greater evils follow in the wake of literature and the arts. One of them is luxury, which, like them, is born of idleness and vanity. Luxury rarely appears without the arts and sciences, and they never appear without luxury. I know that our philosophy, so fertile in paradoxes, maintains, against the experience of every age, that luxury adds to the splendor of States. But leaving aside the question of whether sumptuary laws, which restrict extravagant spending, are necessary, can anyone deny that sound morals are essential to the survival of empires, and that luxury is directly opposed to sound morals? Let us grant that luxury is a sure sign of wealth, and even, if you like, that it helps to increase wealth. What conclusion follows from this paradox, so worthy of our times? And what will become of virtue if riches must be gained at any price? The statesmen of the ancient world spoke constantly of morals and virtue; ours speak of nothing but commerce and money. One will tell you that in a certain country a man is worth exactly what he would fetch at the slave market in Algiers. Another, applying the same method of calculation, finds that in some countries a man is worth nothing, and in others less than nothing. They value men as they would herds of cattle. By their reckoning, a man is worth to the State only what he consumes, so that a single Sybarite, from that famously pleasure-loving city, would be worth at least thirty Spartans. Let these writers tell me, then, which of the two republics, Sybaris or Sparta, was conquered by a handful of peasants, and which became the terror of Asia.

The monarchy of Cyrus was conquered by thirty thousand men led by a prince poorer than the humblest of the Persian satraps, as the provincial governors were called. In the same way the Scythians, the poorest of all nations, held out against the most powerful monarchs in the world. When two famous republics, one rich and the other poor, contended for the empire of the world, the rich one was conquered by the poor. The Roman Empire in its turn, after swallowing up all the riches of the world, fell prey to peoples who did not even know what riches were. The Franks conquered Gaul, and the Saxons England, with no treasures but their courage and their poverty. A band of poor mountaineers, whose entire greed extended no further than a few sheepskins, first checked the arrogance of Austria and then went on to crush the wealthy and formidable House of Burgundy, which at the time made the rulers of Europe tremble. And finally, all the power and wisdom of the heir of Charles V, backed by all the treasures of the Indies, shattered against a few herring fishermen. Let our statesmen be good enough to set aside their calculations for a moment and reflect on these examples. Let them learn, for once, that money buys everything except morals and citizens. What, then, is the real question at issue in the debate over luxury? It is whether it is better for empires to be brilliant and short-lived, or virtuous and lasting. I say brilliant, but with what kind of brilliance? A taste for ostentation never dwells in the same minds as a taste for honesty. No, it is impossible that minds debased by a multitude of trivial concerns should ever rise to anything truly great and noble. Even if they had the strength, they would lack the courage.

Every artist loves applause, and the praise of his contemporaries is the most precious part of his reward. What, then, will he do to win it if he has the misfortune to be born among a people, and in an age, where learning is fashionable and the frivolity of youth sets the tone; where men have sacrificed their taste to those who tyrannize over their liberty, and one sex dares approve nothing but what suits the timidity of the other; where the greatest masterpieces of dramatic poetry are condemned and the noblest works of music neglected? This is what he will do. He will lower his genius to the level of his age, and will sooner resign himself to producing mediocre works that are admired in his lifetime than labor at sublime achievements that will not be admired until long after his death. Let the famous Voltaire tell us how many strong and vigorous beauties he has sacrificed to our false refinement, and how much that was great and noble has been taken from him by that spirit of gallantry which delights in the frivolous and the petty.

In this way the decay of morals, the inevitable result of luxury, brings with it in turn the corruption of taste. And if, among men of ordinary ability, there happens to be one with enough strength of mind to refuse to follow the spirit of the age and degrade himself with childish productions, his fate will be hard. He will die in poverty and obscurity. This is less a prediction than a fact already confirmed by experience. Yes, Carle and Pierre Van Loo, the time has already come when your brushes, which were meant to enhance the majesty of our churches with sublime and sacred images, must either fall from your hands or be prostituted to decorating the panels of carriages with lewd pictures. And you, inimitable Pigalle, rival of Phidias and Praxiteles, whose chisel the ancients would have employed to carve their gods, and whose statues almost excuse their idolatry in our eyes: even your hand must stoop to shaping the belly of an ape, or else stand idle.

We cannot reflect on the morals of mankind without dwelling with pleasure on the picture of the simplicity that prevailed in the earliest times. That image may fairly be compared to a beautiful shore, adorned by the hand of nature alone, toward which our eyes keep turning and which we watch recede with regret. While men were innocent and virtuous, they liked to have the gods as witnesses to their actions, and they lived together with them in the same huts. But once they became wicked, they grew tired of such inconvenient spectators and banished them to magnificent temples. Finally they drove the gods out even from these, so that they could live there themselves; or at least, the temples of the gods were no longer more magnificent than the houses of private citizens. This was the height of degeneracy, and vice could go no further than when it was seen propped up, so to speak, at the doors of the great on marble columns and carved into Corinthian capitals.

As the comforts of life multiply, as the arts are perfected and luxury spreads, true courage weakens and the virtues disappear. All of this is the work of the sciences and of those arts that are practiced in the privacy of men's homes. When the Goths ravaged Greece, the libraries escaped the flames only because of an opinion that spread among them: that it was best to leave the enemy possessions so well suited to distracting them from military exercise and keeping them occupied with idle and sedentary pursuits.

Charles VIII found himself master of Tuscany and the Kingdom of Naples almost without drawing his sword, and his entire court attributed this unexpected success to the fact that the princes and nobles of Italy devoted themselves more eagerly to cultivating their minds than to vigorous and martial pursuits. Indeed, says the sensible man who records these facts, experience plainly teaches us that in military matters, and in everything like them, the study of the sciences tends to make men soft and cowardly rather than resolute and vigorous.

The Romans admitted that military virtue died out among them as they became connoisseurs of painting, engraving, and goldsmith's work, and began to cultivate the fine arts. And as if that famous country were destined to stand forever as an example to other nations, the rise of the Medici and the revival of letters destroyed once again, and this time perhaps for good, the martial reputation that Italy seemed to have recovered a few centuries earlier.

The ancient republics of Greece, with the wisdom so evident in most of their institutions, forbade their citizens to take up any of those inactive and sedentary occupations that, by weakening and corrupting the body, also sap the vigor of the mind. And in fact, with what courage can we expect hunger and thirst, fatigue, danger, and death to be faced by men whom the slightest want overwhelms and the smallest difficulty repels? With what resolve can soldiers endure the extreme hardships of war when they are wholly unaccustomed to them? With what spirit can they make forced marches under officers who lack the strength even to travel on horseback? It is no answer to point to the celebrated valor of all our scientifically trained modern warriors. I hear a great deal about their bravery on the day of battle, but nothing about how they bear extreme fatigue, or how they endure harsh seasons and bad weather. A little sun or snow, or the lack of a few superfluities, is enough to cripple and destroy one of our finest armies within days. Intrepid warriors, allow me for once to tell you the truth, which you so seldom hear. I am fully convinced of your bravery. I have no doubt you would have triumphed with Hannibal at Cannae and at Trasimene, or that you would have crossed the Rubicon with Caesar and helped him enslave his country. But you could never have crossed the Alps with the one, or, with the other, conquered your own ancestors, the Gauls.

A war does not always turn on the outcome of battles. There is an art of generalship higher than the art of winning victories. A man may show great fearlessness under fire and still be a very bad officer. Even in the common soldier, a little more strength and endurance might be more useful than so much courage, which, after all, is no protection against death. And what does it matter to the State whether its troops die of cold and fever or by the enemy's sword?

If the cultivation of the sciences harms military qualities, it harms moral qualities even more. From our earliest childhood, an absurd system of education decorates our wit and corrupts our judgment. On every side we see enormous institutions where our young people are educated at great expense and taught everything except their duties. Your children will not know their own language, yet they will speak others that are spoken nowhere. They will be able to compose verses they can scarcely understand, and, without being able to tell truth from error, they will master the art of disguising both with plausible arguments until neither can be recognized. But magnanimity, fairness, temperance, humanity, and courage will be words whose meaning they do not know. The dear name of their country will never reach their ears, and if they ever hear of God, it will be less to revere Him than to be frightened of Him. "I would just as soon," said a wise man, "that my pupil had spent his time on the tennis court; at least his body would have had some exercise."

I know perfectly well that children must be kept occupied, and that idleness is the danger most to be feared for them. But what should they be taught? That is certainly an important question. Let them be taught what they will need to practice as grown men, not what they will need to forget.

Our gardens are adorned with statues and our galleries with paintings. What would you suppose these masterpieces, displayed for public admiration, depict? The great men who defended their country, or the still greater men who enriched it with their virtues? Not at all. They are images of every perversion of heart and mind, carefully chosen from ancient mythology and set before the early curiosity of our children, no doubt so that they may have models of vicious conduct before their eyes even before they can read.

Where do all these abuses come from, if not from the fatal inequality introduced among men by the distinction of talents and the debasement of virtue? This is the most obvious effect of all our studies, and the most dangerous of all their consequences. We no longer ask whether a man is honest, but whether he is clever. We do not ask whether a book is useful, but whether it is well written. Rewards are showered on wit and ingenuity, while virtue goes unhonored. There are a thousand prizes for fine speeches and none for good deeds. I should like to know, however, whether the honor attached to the best discourse ever to win this Academy's prize can compare with the merit of having founded the prize.

A wise man does not chase after fortune, but he is by no means indifferent to glory. And when he sees glory so unjustly distributed, his virtue, which a little emulation might have quickened and turned to the benefit of society, languishes and dies away in obscurity and poverty. This is why, in the long run, the pleasing arts must everywhere be preferred to the useful ones, and this truth has been confirmed all too thoroughly since the revival of the arts and sciences. We have physicists, geometers, chemists, astronomers, poets, musicians, and painters in abundance, but we no longer have citizens. Or if a few remain, scattered across our abandoned countryside, they are left to perish there, unnoticed and neglected. Such is the state to which we have been reduced, and such are our feelings toward those who give us our bread and our children their milk.

I admit, however, that the evil is not as great as it might have become. Eternal Providence, by placing healing herbs beside poisonous plants and making venomous creatures carry their own antidote, has taught the sovereigns of the earth, who are its ministers, to imitate its wisdom. Following this example, the truly great monarch, whose glory every age will only enhance, drew forth from the very heart of the arts and sciences, the source of a thousand moral failings, those famous learned societies which, while they hold the dangerous trust of human knowledge, are also the sacred guardians of morals. They fulfill this role through the care they take to preserve morals in all their purity among their own members, and through the standards they demand of everyone they admit.

These wise institutions, confirmed by his august successor and imitated by every king in Europe, will at least act as a restraint on men of letters, who, all aspiring to the honor of admission to these Academies, will keep watch over themselves and try to make themselves worthy of it through useful work and blameless conduct. Those Academies, too, that in offering prizes for literary merit choose subjects designed to awaken the love of virtue in the hearts of citizens, show that this love reigns among themselves, and they must give people the rare and genuine pleasure of seeing learned societies devote themselves to enlightening mankind not only through pleasing exercises of the intellect but also through useful instruction.

An objection that might be raised here is in fact only further proof of my argument. So many precautions show all too clearly how necessary they are. No one seeks remedies for evils that do not exist. And why must these remedies, like ordinary ones, bear every mark of ineffectiveness? The many establishments created for the benefit of the learned serve only to mislead men about the true objects of the sciences and to draw their attention toward cultivating them. Judging by the precautions taken everywhere, one would think we had too many farmers and were afraid of running short of philosophers. I will not venture here into a comparison between agriculture and philosophy; it would not bear examination. I will only ask: What is philosophy? What do the writings of the most celebrated philosophers contain? What are the lessons of these friends of wisdom? To hear them, would we not take them for a troupe of quack doctors hawking their remedies in the public square, each crying out, "Here, here, come to me, I am the only true physician"? One of them teaches that there is no such thing as matter, and that everything exists only as a representation in the mind. Another declares that there is no substance but matter, and no God but the world itself. A third tells you that virtue and vice do not exist, and that moral good and evil are mere fantasies; while a fourth informs you that men are nothing but beasts of prey and may devour one another with a clear conscience. Why, my great philosophers, do you not reserve these wise and profitable lessons for your friends and your children? You would soon reap the rewards, and the rest of us would have no fear of seeing our own friends and children become your disciples.

Such are the remarkable men whom their contemporaries held in the highest esteem while they lived, and to whom immortality has been granted since their death. Such are the wise maxims we have received from them and pass down, from age to age, to our descendants. Paganism, for all that it was abandoned to every extravagance of human reason, left behind nothing to compare with the shameful monuments that the art of printing has prepared under the reign of the Gospel. The impious writings of Leucippus and Diagoras perished with their authors, because in their day the world did not yet know how to immortalize the errors and absurdities of the human mind. But thanks to printing, and to the use we make of it, the pernicious reflections of Hobbes and Spinoza will last forever.

Go, then, you famous writings, of which the ignorance and rustic simplicity of our forefathers would have been incapable. Go to our descendants, together with those still more pernicious works that reek of the corrupt manners of our present age, and let them carry to posterity a faithful history of the progress and benefits of our arts and sciences. If they are read, they will leave no doubt about the question we are now debating; and unless mankind is by then even more foolish than we are, our descendants will lift their hands to Heaven and cry out in bitterness of heart: "Almighty God, you who hold the minds of men in your hand, deliver us from the fatal arts and sciences of our forefathers; give us back ignorance, innocence, and poverty, which alone can make us happy and are precious in your sight."

But if the progress of the arts and sciences has added nothing to our real happiness, if it has corrupted our morals, and if that corruption has debased our taste, what are we to make of the crowd of textbook writers who have cleared away the obstacles that nature deliberately placed on the road to the Temple of the Muses? Nature set those obstacles there to guard the approach and to test the strength of anyone tempted to seek knowledge. What are we to make of those compilers who have rashly broken open the doors of the sciences and ushered into their sanctuary a populace unworthy to approach it? It would have been far better if everyone found incapable of making real progress in learning had been turned away at the entrance, and so directed toward the arts that are useful to society. A man who will spend his whole life as a bad versifier or a third-rate geometer might nonetheless have made an excellent clothier. Those whom nature intended for her disciples have never needed masters. Bacon, Descartes, and Newton, those teachers of mankind, had no teachers themselves. Indeed, what guide could have led them as far as their sublime genius carried them? Ordinary masters would only have cramped their intelligence by confining it within the narrow limits of their own capacity. It was from the obstacles they first encountered that they learned to exert themselves, and they roused their energies to cross the vast field they eventually covered. If some men are to be permitted to devote themselves to the arts and sciences, it should be only those who feel strong enough to walk alone in the footsteps of such pioneers and to outstrip them. To these few alone belongs the task of raising monuments to the glory of the human understanding. But if we want nothing to lie beyond their genius, then nothing must lie beyond their hopes. That is the only encouragement they require. The soul gradually adapts itself to the objects on which it is employed, and so great occasions produce great men. The greatest orator in the world, Cicero, was a Consul of Rome, and perhaps the greatest of philosophers, Bacon, was Lord Chancellor of England. Can anyone imagine that if the first had been merely a professor at some university, and the second a pensioner of some academy, their works would not have suffered from their situation? Let princes, then, not disdain to admit into their councils those most capable of giving them good advice. Let them renounce the old prejudice, invented by the pride of the great, that the art of governing mankind is harder than the art of instructing it, as if it were easier to induce men to do good willingly than to compel them to it by force. Let learned men of the first rank find an honorable refuge at their courts, and let them enjoy there the only reward worthy of them: that of using their influence to promote the happiness of the peoples they have enlightened with their wisdom. Only in this way are we likely to see what virtue, science, and authority can accomplish when they are animated by the noblest rivalry and work in concert for the happiness of mankind.

But so long as power stands alone on one side, and knowledge and understanding alone on the other, the learned will seldom turn their minds to great subjects, princes will still more rarely perform great deeds, and peoples will go on being what they are now: base, corrupt, and miserable.

As for us, ordinary men on whom Heaven has not chosen to bestow such great talents and who are not destined for such glory, let us remain in our obscurity. Let us not covet a reputation we could never attain, and which, as things now stand, would never repay us for the trouble it had cost, even if we were fully qualified to win it. Why should we build our happiness on the opinions of others when we can find it in our own hearts? Let us leave to others the task of teaching mankind its duties, and confine ourselves to performing our own. We have no need of any knowledge beyond this.

O Virtue, sublime science of simple souls, must we take such pains and make such preparations in order to know you? Are your principles not engraved on every heart? To learn your laws, is it not enough to look within ourselves and listen to the voice of conscience while the passions are silent?

This is the true philosophy, and we must learn to be content with it, without envying the fame of those celebrated men whose names are immortal in the republic of letters. Instead of envying them, let us try to establish between them and ourselves the honorable distinction once seen between two great peoples, the Athenians and the Spartans: that the one knew how to speak well, and the other how to act well.

---

*On the fable of Prometheus.* The allegory in the fable of Prometheus is easy to see, and the Greeks who chained him to the Caucasus do not seem to have thought any better of him than the Egyptians did of their god Theuth, the deity credited with inventing the sciences. According to an ancient fable, the first time the Satyr saw fire, he wanted to kiss and embrace it; but Prometheus cried out to him to stop, or his beard would pay for it. "It burns," he said, "everything that touches it."

*On false confidence in knowledge.* The less we know, the more we think we know. The Peripatetics, the followers of Aristotle, doubted nothing. Did not Descartes build the universe out of cubes and vortices? And is there a single physicist in all of Europe who does not boldly explain the inexplicable mysteries of electricity, which will perhaps remain forever the despair of true philosophers?

*On the influence of women.* I am far from thinking that the ascendancy women have gained over men is an evil in itself. It is a gift that nature has given them for the good of mankind, and if it were better directed it could do as much good as it now does harm. We do not sufficiently appreciate how much society would gain from giving a better education to that half of our species which governs the other. Men will always be what women choose to make them. If you want men to be noble and virtuous, then teach women what greatness of soul and virtue are. The reflections this subject prompts, which Plato once made, deserve to be developed more fully by a pen worthy of following so great a master and of defending so great a cause.

*On the quotation from Diderot.* The passage is taken from Diderot's *Pensées philosophiques*, or *Philosophical Thoughts*.

*On the education of princes.* Such was the education the Spartans gave to one of the greatest of their kings. It is well worth noting, says Montaigne, that the excellent institutions of Lycurgus, which were truly miraculous in their perfection, paid as much attention to the upbringing of the young as if that were their chief purpose. And yet, in Greece, the very home of the Muses, they make so little mention of learning that it seems as though their noble-spirited youth scorned every other restraint, and required, instead of masters of the sciences, only instructors in valor, prudence, and justice.

Let us next hear what the same writer says of the ancient Persians. Plato, he tells us, relates that the heir to the throne was raised in this way. At birth he was entrusted not to women but to eunuchs of the highest authority, who were placed close to the king's person on account of their virtue. They took charge of making his body handsome and healthy. At seven, they taught him to ride and to hunt. At fourteen he was placed in the hands of four men: the wisest, the most just, the most temperate, and the bravest in the kingdom. The first instructed him in religion; the second taught him to hold inviolably to the truth; the third, to master his passions; and the fourth, to fear nothing. All of them, I may add, taught him to be a good man; not one taught him to be learned.

In Xenophon, Astyages asks Cyrus to tell him about his last lesson. "It was this," Cyrus answered. "One of the big boys at school, who had a small coat, gave it to a little boy and took the little boy's coat, which was larger. Our master made me judge of the dispute, and I ruled that things should stay as they were, since each boy seemed better suited than before. But the master reproved me. He said I had considered only convenience, when justice ought to have been my first concern, and justice teaches that no one should suffer forcible interference with what belongs to him." Cyrus added that he had been punished for his wrong decision, just as boys in our village schools are punished when they forget the first aorist of τύπτω (*typtō*, "I strike"), the stock verb of every Greek grammar drill. My tutor will have to deliver me a fine oration *in genere demonstrativo*, a formal showpiece speech in the ceremonial style of rhetoric, before he persuades me that his school is as good as that one.

*On printing.* If we consider the terrible disorders that printing has already caused in Europe, and judge the future by the daily progress of its evils, it is easy to foresee that sovereigns will one day take as much trouble to banish this dreadful art from their dominions as they ever took to encourage it. The Sultan Achmet, yielding to the persistent demands of certain self-styled men of taste, agreed to have a printing press set up at Constantinople; but it had scarcely begun to operate before they were obliged to destroy it and throw the equipment down a well.

It is said that when the Caliph Omar was asked what should be done with the library at Alexandria, he replied: "If the books in the library contain anything contrary to the Qur'an, they are evil and ought to be burned; if they contain only what the Qur'an teaches, they are superfluous." Our men of letters have cited this reasoning as the height of absurdity. Yet if Gregory the Great had stood in Omar's place, with the Gospel in place of the Qur'an, the library would still have been burned, and that might well have been the finest act of his life.

---

## A Discourse on the Origin of Inequality

*A discourse on a subject proposed by the Academy of Dijon:*

*What is the origin of inequality among men, and is it authorized by natural law?*

*Non in depravatis, sed in his quae bene secundum naturam se habent, considerandum est quid sit naturale.*

We should consider what is natural not in things that are depraved, but in those that are rightly ordered according to nature.

Aristotle, *Politics*, Book I, Chapter 2.

### Dedication to the Republic of Geneva

Most honorable, magnificent, and sovereign lords: Convinced that only a virtuous citizen can confer on his country honors it can accept, I have spent the past thirty years working to make myself worthy of offering you some public tribute. Now that this fortunate occasion makes up, in some measure, for the shortcomings of my efforts, I have felt entitled to seize it, following the promptings of the zeal that inspires me rather than waiting for the right that ought to have authorized me. Having had the good fortune to be born among you, how could I reflect on the equality that nature has established among men, and on the inequality they have introduced, without also reflecting on the profound wisdom by which, in this State, the two are happily combined and made to coincide? They coincide here in the way most in keeping with natural law and most favorable to society, to the maintenance of public order, and to the happiness of individuals. In searching for the best rules that common sense can lay down for the constitution of a government, I was so struck to find them all actually in force in yours that, even had I not been born within your walls, I would have thought it essential to offer this picture of human society to the people that, of all peoples, seems to me to possess its greatest advantages and to have guarded best against its abuses.

If I had been free to choose the place of my birth, I would have chosen a society of a size proportionate to the limits of human faculties, that is, to the possibility of being well governed. In such a society each person would be equal to his task, and no one would be obliged to entrust to others the functions with which he himself had been charged. It would be a State in which all the individuals knew one another, so that neither the secret schemes of vice nor the modesty of virtue could escape the notice and judgment of the public, and in which the pleasant habit of seeing and knowing one another would make love of country a love of one's fellow citizens rather than of its soil.
