I would have wished to be born in a country where the interest of the sovereign and that of the people were one and the same, so that every movement of the machine would always tend toward the general happiness. And since this could not be so unless the sovereign and the people were one and the same person, it follows that I would have wished to be born under a democratic government, wisely tempered.

I would have wished to live and die free: that is, so far subject to the laws that neither I nor anyone else could throw off their honorable yoke. It is a gentle and wholesome yoke, and even the proudest necks bear it all the more docilely because they are made to bear no other.

I would have wished, then, that no one within the State could claim to be above the law, and that no one outside it could impose his will in such a way that the State would be obliged to recognize his authority. For whatever the constitution of a government may be, if there is a single man within its jurisdiction who is not subject to the law, all the rest are necessarily at his mercy. And if there is a national ruler within and a foreign ruler without, however they may divide their authority between them, it is impossible for both to be properly obeyed or for the State to be well governed.

I would not have chosen to live in a newly founded republic, however excellent its laws. I would fear that the government, perhaps framed differently from what the circumstances of the moment required, might not suit the new citizens, or they might not suit it, and that the State would run the risk of being overthrown and destroyed almost as soon as it was born. For liberty is like those solid, rich foods or those full-bodied wines that nourish and strengthen robust constitutions accustomed to them, but ruin and intoxicate weak and delicate ones to which they are unsuited. Peoples once accustomed to masters are in no condition to do without them. If they try to shake off the yoke, they only move further from freedom, because they mistake for it an unbridled license that is its very opposite, and so they nearly always end up, through their revolutions, delivering themselves into the hands of seducers who only make their chains heavier than before. The Roman people itself, the model for all free peoples, was wholly incapable of governing itself when it first escaped the oppression of the Tarquins, the last kings of Rome. Degraded by slavery and by the shameful labors imposed upon it, it was at first nothing but a stupid mob that had to be managed and governed with the greatest wisdom. Only in this way could minds weakened, or rather brutalized, under tyranny grow accustomed by degrees to breathing the healthful air of liberty, and gradually acquire the austere morals and strength of spirit that finally made the Romans the people most worthy of respect. I would therefore have sought as my country some peaceful and happy republic whose origins were lost, as it were, in the night of time; one that had suffered only such shocks as served to display and strengthen the courage and patriotism of its people; and whose citizens, long accustomed to a wise independence, were not only free but worthy of being so.

I would have wished to choose a country turned away from the brutal love of conquest by a fortunate lack of power, and protected by an even more fortunate position from the fear of itself becoming the conquest of another State. It would be a free city lying among several nations, none of which had any interest in attacking it, while each had an interest in preventing the others from doing so; in short, a republic that offered nothing to tempt the ambition of its neighbors and could reasonably count on their help in case of need. It follows that a republic so happily placed would have nothing to fear except from itself. If its citizens trained in the use of arms, it would be less from any need to provide for their defense than to keep alive the martial ardor and courageous spirit so becoming in free men, which help sustain their taste for liberty.

I would have sought a country in which the right of legislation belonged to all the citizens, for who can judge better than they the conditions under which they had best live together in the same society? Not that I would have approved of plebiscites like those of the Romans, resolutions voted by the assembly of the common people. In those assemblies the leaders of the State, the very men most concerned with its preservation, were excluded from deliberations on which its safety often depended, and, by the most absurd inconsistency, the magistrates were deprived of rights enjoyed by the humblest citizens.

On the contrary, to prevent self-interested and ill-conceived schemes, and all such dangerous innovations as finally ruined the Athenians, I would have wanted no one to be free to propose new laws at will. This right should belong exclusively to the magistrates, and even they should use it with such caution, the people for its part should be so restrained in granting its consent, and the promulgation of laws should be surrounded with such solemnity, that before the constitution could be overturned there would be time enough for everyone to become convinced of several things: that it is above all the great antiquity of laws that makes them sacred and venerable; that men soon learn to despise laws they see altered every day; and that States, by growing used to neglecting their ancient customs on the pretext of improving them, often introduce greater evils than the ones they set out to remove.

Above all I would have avoided, as necessarily ill governed, a republic in which the people, imagining they could do without magistrates or at least leave them only a precarious authority, had imprudently kept in their own hands the administration of civil affairs and the execution of their own laws. Such must have been the crude constitution of the first governments as they emerged directly from the state of nature, and this was another of the vices that contributed to the downfall of the Athenian republic.

Instead I would have chosen a community in which individuals were content to sanction their laws and to decide the most important public matters in general assembly on the proposal of their leaders. They would have established respected courts, carefully separated the various branches of government, and elected each year some of the most capable and upright of their fellow citizens to administer justice and govern the State. It would be a community, in short, in which the virtue of the magistrates bore witness to the wisdom of the people, and each did honor to the other. In such a community, if fatal misunderstandings ever arose to disturb the public peace, even these intervals of blindness and error would bear the marks of moderation, mutual esteem, and a shared respect for the laws, which are sure signs and pledges of a reconciliation as lasting as it is sincere. Such, most honorable, magnificent, and sovereign lords, are the advantages I would have sought in the country I would have chosen for my birth. And if Providence had added to all these a delightful setting, a temperate climate, a fertile soil, and the most beautiful countryside under Heaven, I would have wished for nothing more, to complete my happiness, than the peaceful enjoyment of all these blessings in the bosom of this happy homeland. I would have wished to live peacefully in the pleasant society of my fellow citizens, practicing toward them, after their own example, the duties of friendship, humanity, and every other virtue, and to leave behind me the honorable memory of a good man and an upright and virtuous patriot.

But if, less fortunate or grown wise too late, I had found myself reduced to ending an infirm and languishing life in other lands, vainly regretting the peace and repose I had forfeited through the imprudence of youth, I would at least have cherished the same feelings in my heart, though denied the chance to put them to use in my native country. Filled with a tender and disinterested love for my distant fellow citizens, I would have addressed them from my heart in something like the following words:

"My dear fellow citizens, or rather my brothers, since the ties of blood as well as the laws unite almost all of us, it gives me pleasure that I cannot think of you without thinking at the same time of all the blessings you enjoy, and of which none of you, perhaps, feels their value more deeply than I, who have lost them. The more I reflect on your civil and political condition, the less I can imagine that the nature of human affairs could admit of a better one.

"In every other government, whenever the question is how to secure the greatest good of the State, nothing ever advances beyond plans and ideas, or at best bare possibilities. Your happiness, by contrast, is already complete, and you have nothing to do but enjoy it; to be made perfectly happy, you need only know how to be content with being so.

"Your sovereignty, won or recovered by the sword and maintained for the past two centuries by your courage and wisdom, is at last fully and universally acknowledged. Honorable treaties have fixed your borders, confirmed your rights, and secured your peace. Your constitution is excellent: it was dictated by the deepest wisdom, and it is guaranteed by great and friendly powers. Your State enjoys perfect tranquility. You have neither wars nor conquerors to fear, and you have no master other than the wise laws you made yourselves, which are administered by upright magistrates of your own choosing. You are neither so wealthy that soft indulgence enfeebles you, and that in the pursuit of frivolous pleasures you lose your taste for true happiness and solid virtue, nor so poor that you need more help from abroad than your own industry can supply. Meanwhile, the precious privilege of liberty, which great nations preserve only by submitting to the most exorbitant burdens, costs you almost nothing to keep.

"May a Republic so wisely and happily constituted endure forever, as an example to other nations and for the happiness of its own citizens! This is the only prayer left for you to make, the only precaution that remains to be taken. From now on it depends on you alone, not to make yourselves happy, for your ancestors have spared you that trouble, but to make that happiness last through the wisdom with which you enjoy it. Your preservation depends on your constant unity, your obedience to the laws, and your respect for those who administer them. If the smallest trace of bitterness or distrust remains among you, hurry to destroy it, as you would an accursed leaven that works its way through the whole loaf and must sooner or later bring misfortune and ruin upon the State. I implore every one of you to look into your hearts and to listen to the secret voice of conscience. Can any of you find, anywhere in the world, a body more upright, more enlightened, and more honorable than your magistracy? Do not all its members set you an example of moderation, of simple manners, of respect for the laws, and of the most sincere harmony? Place in such wise leaders, then, without reservation, that wholesome confidence which reason always owes to virtue. Remember that they are your own choice, that they justify that choice, and that the honors due to those you have raised to office necessarily reflect back upon you. None of you is so ignorant as not to know that when the laws lose their force, and those who defend them lose their authority, neither security nor liberty is possible for anyone. Why, then, should you hesitate to do gladly and with well-founded confidence what your true interest, your duty, and reason itself would in any case have obliged you to do?

"Never let a culpable and harmful indifference to the preservation of the constitution lead you to neglect, when the need arises, the prudent advice of the most enlightened and devoted of your fellow citizens. Instead, let fairness, moderation, and firm resolve continue to govern all your conduct, and let them display you to the whole world as an example of a brave and modest people, equally jealous of their honor and of their liberty. Above all, and this is the last advice I shall give you, beware of malicious interpretations and venomous rumors, whose hidden motives are often more dangerous than the actions they attack. A whole household wakes and heeds the first alarm of a good and trusty watchdog that barks only when thieves approach; but we hate the pestering of those noisy curs that are forever disturbing the public peace, and whose constant, ill-timed warnings keep us from listening to them on the occasions when they may actually be needed."

And you, most honorable and magnificent lords, worthy and revered magistrates of a free people, allow me to offer you in particular my duty and homage. If there is any rank in the world capable of conferring honor on those who hold it, it is surely the one that virtue and talent combine to bestow: the rank of which you have made yourselves worthy, and to which your fellow citizens have raised you. Their merit adds fresh luster to your own. And since you have been chosen to govern by men who are themselves capable of governing others, I cannot help regarding you as standing as far above all other magistrates as a free people, and above all the people over whom you have the honor to preside, stands above the populace of other States in wisdom and reason.

Allow me to cite an example of which better records ought to have survived, and which will always be close to my heart. I cannot recall without the tenderest emotion the memory of that virtuous citizen to whom I owe my existence, and who often taught me, as a child, the respect that is owed to you. I see him still, living by the work of his hands and nourishing his soul on the loftiest truths. I see the works of Tacitus, Plutarch, and Grotius lying before him amid the tools of his trade. At his side stands his beloved son, receiving, alas with too little profit, the tender instruction of the best of fathers. Yet if the follies of youth made me forget his wise lessons for a time, I have at last the happiness of knowing that, however strong one's inclination toward vice, an education into which love has entered is not easily thrown away entirely.

Such, most honorable and magnificent lords, are the citizens, and even the ordinary inhabitants, of the State you govern. Such are those intelligent and sensible men of whom other nations, calling them workmen and the common people, habitually hold a low and false opinion. My father, I gladly admit, was in no way distinguished among his fellow citizens. He was simply what they all are; and yet, such as he was, there is no country where his acquaintance would not have been sought, and cultivated with profit, even by men of the highest standing. It would not become me, nor, thank Heaven, is it at all necessary, to remind you of the regard such men have a right to expect from their magistrates. They are your equals both by education and by the rights of nature and birth, and your inferiors only by their own will, through the preference they owe to your merit; and for having granted you that preference, they may claim some measure of acknowledgment in return. It gives me lively satisfaction to learn that in all your dealings with them you temper the gravity befitting ministers of the law with the greatest openness and affability, and that you repay them so well, through your esteem and attentiveness, for the respect and obedience they owe you. This conduct is not only just but prudent, since it happily helps to erase the memory of many unfortunate events that ought to be buried in eternal oblivion. It is all the wiser because it leads this generous and fair-minded people to take pleasure in their duty and to love honoring you as a matter of course, and because it makes those who are most zealous in defending their own rights the most disposed to respect yours.

It is not surprising that the rulers of a civil society should have the welfare and glory of their communities at heart. But it is uncommonly fortunate for the peace of mankind when those who regard themselves as the magistrates, or rather the masters, of a holier and more sublime country show some love for the earthly country that sustains them. I am happy to be able to make so rare an exception in our favor, and to count among our best citizens those zealous guardians of the sacred articles of faith established by law, those venerable shepherds of souls whose powerful and captivating eloquence is all the better suited to carry the maxims of the Gospel into men's hearts because they are the first to practice those maxims themselves. Everyone knows how successfully the art of preaching is cultivated in Geneva. But people are so accustomed to hearing clergymen preach one thing and practice another that few have the chance to learn how thoroughly the spirit of Christianity, with its holiness of conduct, its severity toward oneself, and its indulgence toward one's neighbor, prevails throughout the whole body of our ministers. Perhaps Geneva alone has been granted the edifying example of so perfect a union between its clergy and its men of letters. It is largely on their wisdom, their acknowledged moderation, and their zeal for the prosperity of the State that I build my hopes for its lasting peace. At the same time, I note with pleasure, mingled with surprise and reverence, how deeply they detest the hideous maxims of those accursed and barbarous men, of whom history offers more than one example, who, in order to uphold the so-called rights of God, which is to say their own interests, were all the less sparing of human blood because they expected their own to be always respected.

I must not forget that precious half of the Republic which makes the happiness of the other, and whose gentleness and prudence preserve its peace and virtue. Lovable and virtuous daughters of Geneva, it will always be the lot of your sex to govern ours. Fortunate are we, so long as your chaste influence, exercised solely within the bounds of marriage, is exerted only for the glory of the State and the happiness of the public. It was in this way that women held command in Sparta, and in this way you deserve to command in Geneva. What man could be so barbarous as to resist the voice of honor and reason coming from the lips of a loving wife? Who would not scorn the vanities of luxury on seeing the simple and modest dress which, from the luster you lend it, seems the most becoming to beauty? It is your task to sustain, through your quiet persuasion and your innocent and gracious rule, respect for the laws of the State and harmony among its citizens. It is yours to reunite divided families through happy marriages, and above all to correct, by the persuasive sweetness of your lessons and the modest grace of your conversation, the extravagances our young men pick up in other countries. Instead of the many useful things by which they might have profited there, they bring home little more than a childish air and absurd manners learned among loose women, together with an admiration for some vague so-called grandeur and for the paltry rewards of servitude, which can never approach the true greatness of liberty. Continue, then, always to be what you are: the chaste guardians of our morals and the gentle guarantee of our peace, exercising on every occasion the privileges of the heart and of nature in the service of duty and virtue.

I flatter myself that I shall never be proved wrong in resting my hopes for the general happiness of the citizens and the glory of the Republic on such a foundation. It must be admitted, however, that with all these advantages the Republic will not shine with the kind of brilliance that dazzles most men's eyes; and a childish and fatal taste for such brilliance is the deadliest enemy of happiness and liberty.

Let our dissolute youth look elsewhere for easy pleasures and long regrets. Let our would-be connoisseurs of taste go elsewhere to admire grand palaces, handsome carriages, sumptuous furnishings, the pomp of public spectacles, and all the refinements of luxury and softness. Geneva has nothing to boast of but men. Such a sight nonetheless has a value of its own, and those who have a taste for it are well worth all the admirers of the rest.

Deign, most honorable, magnificent, and sovereign lords, to receive, all of you with equal kindness, this respectful testimony of the interest I take in your common prosperity. And if I have been so unfortunate as to fall into any indiscreet rapture in this heartfelt outpouring, I beg you to forgive me, and to attribute it to the tender affection of a true patriot and to the ardent and legitimate zeal of a man who can imagine no greater happiness for himself than to see you happy.

Most honorable, magnificent, and sovereign lords, I am, with the most profound respect,

Your most humble and obedient servant and fellow citizen,

J. J. ROUSSEAU

Chambéry,
June 12, 1754

### Preface

Of all the human sciences, the most useful and the least advanced seems to me to be the knowledge of man. I venture to say that the single inscription on the Temple of Delphi, "Know thyself," contained a precept more difficult and more important than anything in all the massive volumes moralists have ever written. I consider the subject of this discourse one of the most interesting questions philosophy can pose and, unfortunately for us, one of the thorniest that philosophers can be called upon to solve. For how can we know the source of inequality among men without first knowing man himself? And how can man hope to see himself as nature made him, through all the changes that the succession of places and times must have produced in his original constitution? How can he separate what is fundamental to his nature from the changes and additions that his circumstances and his own advances have introduced to modify his primitive condition? Think of the statue of the sea-god Glaucus, so disfigured by time, sea, and storms that it looked more like a wild beast than a god. In the same way, the human soul has been altered in society by a thousand constantly recurring causes: by the acquisition of a multitude of truths and errors, by changes in the constitution of the body, and by the ceaseless clash of the passions. It has, so to speak, changed its appearance until it is scarcely recognizable. Instead of a being that acts always on fixed and unchanging principles, instead of the heavenly and majestic simplicity its divine Author stamped upon it, we find only the hideous contrast of passion mistaking itself for reason and of an understanding grown delirious.

It is crueler still that, since every advance of the human species carries it farther from its primitive state, the more discoveries we make, the more we deprive ourselves of the means of making the most important discovery of all. Thus, in a sense, it is through our very study of man that the knowledge of him has been placed beyond our reach.

It is easy to see that we must look to these successive changes in man's constitution for the origin of the differences that now distinguish men from one another. By common agreement, men are naturally as equal among themselves as were the animals of every species before physical causes introduced the variations now observable in some of them.

It is indeed inconceivable that these first changes, however they came about, could have altered every individual of the species at once and in the same way. It is natural to suppose that while the condition of some improved or worsened, and they acquired various good or bad qualities not inherent in their nature, others remained longer in their original state. This was no doubt the first source of inequality among men, a source far easier to point out in such general terms than to trace precisely to its actual causes.

My readers, then, should not imagine that I flatter myself with having seen what seems to me so hard to discover. I have entered upon certain lines of reasoning and ventured certain conjectures, less in the hope of resolving the difficulty than with the aim of shedding some light on it and reducing the question to its proper form. Others may easily go farther along the same road, yet no one will find it easy to reach the end. For it is no light undertaking to distinguish properly between what is original and what is artificial in man's present nature, or to form an accurate idea of a state that no longer exists, that perhaps never existed, and that probably never will exist, yet of which we must nevertheless have accurate ideas if we are to judge our present state soundly. It would indeed take more philosophy than one might suppose for anyone to determine exactly what precautions he ought to take in order to make solid observations on this subject. A good solution to the following problem seems to me not unworthy of the Aristotles and Plinys of our own age: What experiments would be needed to discover natural man? And how could those experiments be carried out within society?

So far am I from undertaking to solve this problem that I believe I have considered the subject enough to declare in advance that our greatest philosophers would not be too good to direct such experiments, nor our most powerful sovereigns to carry them out. We have very little reason to expect such a combination, especially when it would have to be joined to the perseverance, or rather the succession of intelligence and good will, that success would require on both sides.

These inquiries, so difficult to carry out and so little considered until now, are nevertheless the only means left to us of removing a multitude of difficulties that conceal from us the real foundations of human society. It is this ignorance of the nature of man that casts so much uncertainty and obscurity over the true definition of natural right. "The idea of right," says Burlamaqui, "and still more that of natural right, are ideas manifestly relative to the nature of man. It is therefore from this very nature," he continues, "from the constitution and state of man, that we must deduce the first principles of this science."

We cannot observe without surprise and dismay how little agreement exists among the various authors who have treated this great subject. Among the most important writers, scarcely two hold the same view of it. To say nothing of the ancient philosophers, who seem to have done their utmost to contradict one another on the most fundamental principles, the Roman jurists subjected man and the other animals indiscriminately to the same natural law. They did so because by that name they meant the law that nature imposes on herself rather than the law she prescribes to others; or rather, because of the particular sense the term "law" had for those jurists, who on this occasion seem to have understood by it nothing more than the general relations nature has established among all living beings for their common preservation. The moderns, understanding by "law" only a rule prescribed to a moral being, that is, to a being who is intelligent, free, and considered in his relations with other beings, accordingly confine the jurisdiction of natural law to man, as the only animal endowed with reason. But each defines this law in his own fashion, and they ground it on such metaphysical principles that very few people among us are capable of understanding them, let alone discovering them on their own. The definitions of these learned men, which differ on everything else, therefore agree only in this: that it is impossible to understand the law of nature, and consequently to obey it, without being a highly subtle casuist, skilled in resolving the finest points of conscience, and a profound metaphysician. This amounts to saying that, in founding society, mankind must have employed a capacity that is acquired only with great difficulty, and by very few people, even within society.

Knowing so little of nature, and agreeing so poorly on the meaning of the word "law," we would find it hard to settle on a good definition of natural law. Thus all the definitions we find in books, besides lacking uniformity, share another fault: they are drawn from many kinds of knowledge that men do not possess by nature, and from advantages of which men can have no idea until they have already left that natural state. Modern writers begin by asking what rules it would be expedient for men to agree upon for their common interest, and then give the name "natural law" to a collection of such rules, with no proof beyond the good that would follow from their universal practice. This is certainly a simple way of composing definitions, and of explaining the nature of things by almost arbitrary conveniences.

But so long as we are ignorant of natural man, it is useless to try to determine either the law originally prescribed to him or the law best suited to his constitution. All we can know with any certainty about this law is that, if it is to be a law, the wills of those it binds must be conscious of submitting to it; and also that, to be natural, it must speak directly through the voice of nature.

Setting aside, therefore, all those scientific books that teach us only to see men as they have made themselves, and contemplating the first and simplest operations of the human soul, I believe I can perceive in it two principles prior to reason. One gives us an ardent interest in our own well-being and preservation; the other inspires in us a natural repugnance at seeing any sentient being, and especially any of our own species, suffer pain or death. It is from the agreement and combination that the understanding is able to establish between these two principles, without any need to introduce the principle of sociability, that all the rules of natural right seem to me to derive. These are rules that reason is afterward obliged to reestablish on other foundations, once its successive developments have led it to suppress nature itself.

By proceeding in this way, we are not obliged to make man a philosopher before he is a man. His duties toward others are not dictated to him solely by the belated lessons of wisdom; and so long as he does not resist the inner impulse of compassion, he will never harm another man, or even any sentient being, except in those legitimate cases where his own preservation is at stake and he is obliged to give himself preference. This method also puts an end to the age-old disputes about whether animals share in natural law. For it is clear that, lacking intelligence and freedom, they cannot recognize that law; but since they share, to some degree, in our nature through the sensitivity with which they are endowed, they ought to share in natural right as well, so that human beings bear a kind of obligation even toward animals. Indeed, if I am bound to do no harm to my fellow creatures, this seems to be less because they are rational than because they are sentient; and since sentience is common to humans and animals alike, it ought at least to entitle animals to the privilege of not being needlessly mistreated by us.

The study of original man, of his real needs, and of the fundamental principles of his duty is, moreover, the only proper method for resolving the many difficulties that arise concerning the origin of moral inequality, the true foundations of the body politic, the reciprocal rights of its members, and a host of other questions as important as they are obscure.

If we look at human society with a calm and disinterested eye, it seems at first to reveal nothing but the violence of the powerful and the oppression of the weak. The mind recoils from the cruelty of the one, or is moved to lament the blindness of the other. And since nothing in life is less permanent than those external relations we call weakness or power, wealth or poverty, which arise more often from accident than from wisdom, all human institutions seem at first glance to rest on nothing firmer than shifting sand. Only by looking more closely, and by clearing away the dust and sand that surround the building, do we discover the immovable base on which it stands and learn to respect its foundations. Without a serious study of man, of his natural faculties and their successive development, we shall never be able to draw these necessary distinctions, or to separate, in the present constitution of things, what is the work of divine will from what human ingenuity has attempted to add. The political and moral inquiries to which this important question leads are therefore useful in every respect, and the hypothetical history of governments offers a lesson no less instructive to mankind.

By considering what we would have become had we been left to ourselves, we should learn to bless Him whose gracious hand, by correcting our institutions and giving them an immovable foundation, has prevented the disorders that would otherwise have sprung from them, and has made our happiness flow from the very sources that seemed likely to plunge us into misery.

*Quem te deus esse*
*Jussit, et humanâ quâ parte locatus es in re,*
*Disce.*

*Learn what God has commanded you to be, and what place you hold in the human order.*
— Persius, *Satires*, III, 71

### A Dissertation on the Origin and Foundation of the Inequality of Mankind

It is of man that I must speak, and the question I am examining tells me that I must address myself to men, for questions of this kind are not posed by those who are afraid to honor the truth. I shall therefore defend the cause of humanity with confidence before the wise men who invite me to do so, and I shall be content if I prove myself worthy of my subject and of my judges.

I see two kinds of inequality in the human species. The first I call natural or physical, because it is established by nature; it consists in differences of age, health, bodily strength, and qualities of mind or soul. The second may be called moral or political inequality, because it depends on a kind of convention and is established, or at least authorized, by the consent of men. It consists of the various privileges that some enjoy at the expense of others: being richer, more honored, more powerful, or even able to compel obedience.

There is no point in asking what the source of natural inequality is, because the simple definition of the term already answers the question. It would be even more pointless to ask whether there is any essential connection between the two kinds of inequality, for that would amount to asking whether those who command are necessarily better than those who obey, and whether strength of body or mind, wisdom or virtue, are always found in individuals in proportion to their power or wealth. Such a question might perhaps be fit for slaves to debate within earshot of their masters, but it is wholly unworthy of reasonable and free men in search of the truth.

The subject of this discourse, then, is more precisely the following: to mark the moment in the course of events when right took the place of violence and nature was brought under the rule of law, and to explain by what chain of miracles the strong came to submit to serving the weak, and the people to buy an imaginary peace at the cost of real happiness.

The philosophers who have examined the foundations of society have all felt the need to go back to the state of nature, but not one of them has actually arrived there. Some have not hesitated to attribute to man in that state the idea of just and unjust, without troubling to show that he must have possessed such an idea, or that it would have been of any use to him. Others have spoken of every man's natural right to keep what belongs to him, without explaining what they meant by "belongs." Still others began by granting the strong authority over the weak and moved straight on to the birth of government, without considering how much time must have passed before the words "authority" and "government" could have meant anything to human beings. All of them, in short, forever speaking of need, greed, oppression, desire, and pride, have carried into the state of nature ideas that were acquired in society; in describing the savage, they described social man. Most of our writers have not even thought to doubt whether the state of nature ever existed. Yet it is clear from Holy Scripture that the first man, having received his understanding and his commandments directly from God, was not himself in such a state; and if we give the writings of Moses the credence every Christian philosopher owes them, we must deny that men were ever in the pure state of nature, even before the Flood, unless they fell back into it through some extraordinary event, a paradox that would be very awkward to defend and quite impossible to prove.

Let us begin, then, by setting the facts aside, since they have no bearing on the question. The inquiries we may undertake on this subject must not be taken as historical truths, but only as conditional and hypothetical reasoning, better suited to explaining the nature of things than to establishing their actual origin, much like the hypotheses our physicists form every day about the formation of the world. Religion commands us to believe that God Himself took men out of the state of nature immediately after the Creation, and that they are unequal only because He wills them to be so. But it does not forbid us to form conjectures, drawn solely from the nature of man and of the beings around him, about what might have become of the human race had it been left to itself. This is the question put to me, and the one I propose to examine in the following discourse. Since my subject concerns all of humanity, I shall try to use a language suited to every nation, or rather, forgetting time and place, to think only of the people I am addressing. I shall imagine myself in the Lyceum of Athens, repeating the lessons of my masters, with Plato and Xenocrates as my judges and the whole human race as my audience.

O man, whatever your country and whatever your opinions, listen: here is your history as I believe I have read it, not in the books of your fellow men, who are liars, but in nature, which never lies. Everything that comes from her will be true; you will find nothing false except what I may have added of my own without meaning to. The times I am about to describe are very distant. How greatly you have changed from what you once were! It is, so to speak, the life of your species that I shall write, according to the qualities you received, which your education and habits may have corrupted but cannot have wholly destroyed. There is, I feel, an age at which the individual would wish to stop; you are about to search for the age at which you would have wished your whole species to stand still. Discontented with your present condition, for reasons that promise your unhappy descendants still greater discontent, you will perhaps wish you could go back; and that wish should stand as a tribute to your earliest ancestors, a reproach to your contemporaries, and a source of dread for the unfortunate generations who will come after you.

### The First Part

However important it may be, if we are to judge the natural state of man correctly, to consider him from his origin and to examine him, as it were, in the embryo of his species, I shall not trace his physical organization through its successive stages of development, nor pause to ask what his animal constitution must have been at the beginning in order to become at last what it now is. I shall not ask whether his long nails were at first, as Aristotle supposes, merely hooked claws; whether his whole body was covered with hair like a bear's; or whether, walking on all fours, with his gaze fixed on the ground and confined to a horizon of a few paces, he did not reveal by that very posture both the character and the limits of his ideas. On such matters I could offer only vague and almost imaginary conjectures. Comparative anatomy has made too little progress as yet, and the observations of naturalists are too uncertain, to provide a basis for any solid reasoning. So, without appealing to the supernatural knowledge we have been given on this point, and without considering the changes that must have occurred in man's inner and outer structure as he put his limbs to new uses and fed on new kinds of food, I shall assume that he was always formed as we see him today: that he always walked on two legs, used his hands as we do, cast his gaze across the whole of nature, and measured with his eyes the vast expanse of the heavens.

If we strip this being, so constituted, of all the supernatural gifts he may have received and all the artificial faculties he could only have acquired through a long process; if we consider him, in short, exactly as he must have come from the hands of nature, we see an animal weaker than some and less agile than others, but, all things considered, the most advantageously organized of all. I see him satisfying his hunger beneath the first oak and quenching his thirst at the first stream, finding his bed at the foot of the same tree that gave him his meal, and with that, all his needs are met.

So long as the earth was left to its natural fertility and covered with immense forests never cut by the axe, it would offer food and shelter on every side to every species of animal. Men, scattered among the other creatures, would observe and imitate their skills and so raise themselves to the level of animal instinct, with this advantage: whereas each species of animal is limited to a single instinct of its own, man, who perhaps has no instinct peculiar to himself, would make all of them his own. He would live on most of the various foods that other animals divide among themselves, and would therefore find his subsistence far more easily than any of them.

Accustomed from infancy to harsh weather and the severity of the seasons, hardened to fatigue, and forced, naked and unarmed, to defend themselves and their prey against other fierce animals or else to escape them by running, men would develop a robust and almost unshakable constitution. Their children, bringing into the world the excellent constitution of their parents and strengthening it through the very exertions that had first produced it, would thus acquire all the vigor the human body is capable of. Nature treats them precisely as Sparta treated the children of its citizens, where infants were examined at birth and the weak left to die: those born well formed she makes strong and robust, and all the rest she destroys. In this she differs from our modern societies, where the State, by making children a burden to their parents, kills them indiscriminately before they are even born.

The savage's body is the only instrument he knows, and he puts it to many uses of which ours, for lack of practice, are incapable, since our ingenuity robs us of the strength and agility that necessity compels him to acquire. Had he possessed an axe, could he have torn so thick a branch from a tree with his bare arm? Had he possessed a sling, could he have thrown a stone with such force? Had he possessed a ladder, would he have climbed a tree so nimbly? Had he possessed a horse, would he himself have been so swift of foot? Give civilized man time to gather all his machines around him, and he will no doubt easily defeat the savage. But if you want to see a still more unequal contest, set the two against each other naked and unarmed, and you will soon recognize the advantage of having all one's powers constantly at hand, of being always ready for anything, and of carrying oneself, so to speak, whole and entire wherever one goes.

Hobbes maintains that man is naturally fearless and bent only on attacking and fighting. Another distinguished philosopher holds the opposite view, and Cumberland and Pufendorf likewise assert that nothing is more timid and fearful than man in the state of nature, that he is always trembling and ready to flee at the slightest noise or movement. This may be true where unfamiliar things are concerned, and I do not doubt that he is frightened by every new sight, so long as he cannot tell what physical good or harm to expect from it, or weigh his own strength against the dangers before him. Such situations, however, are rare in the state of nature, where everything proceeds in a uniform way and the face of the earth is not subject to the sudden and constant changes produced by the passions and whims of people living together in groups. Savage man, living scattered among the other animals and soon finding himself in a position to test his strength against theirs, quickly comes to compare himself with them; and seeing that he surpasses them in skill more than they surpass him in strength, he learns to fear them no longer. Set a bear or a wolf against a savage who is robust, agile, and resolute, as all savages are, and who is armed with stones and a good club, and you will see that the danger is at least equal on both sides. After a few such encounters, wild beasts, which have no fondness for attacking one another, will be in no hurry to attack man, whom they will have found to be as wild and fierce as themselves. As for animals whose strength genuinely exceeds man's skill, he is in the same position as all weaker animals, which nonetheless manage to survive, except that he has a further advantage: being just as fast a runner and finding an almost certain refuge in any tree, he is free at every encounter to engage or to withdraw, to fight or to flee as he chooses. Add to this that no animal appears to make war on man by nature, except in self-defense or in extreme hunger, or to show any of those violent antipathies that seem to mark one species as intended by nature to be food for another.

This is no doubt why Africans and savages are so little afraid of the wild beasts they may meet in the woods. The Caribs of Venezuela, among others, live in perfect security in this respect and without the slightest trouble. Although they go almost naked, François Coréal reports, they venture freely into the woods armed only with bows and arrows, yet no one has ever heard of one of them being devoured by wild animals.

But man has other, more formidable enemies against which he has no such means of defense: the natural infirmities of infancy, of old age, and of illness of every kind, sad proofs of our weakness. The first two are common to all animals; the last belongs chiefly to man living in society. As for infancy, we may observe that the human mother, carrying her child with her wherever she goes, can nurse it far more easily than the females of many other species, which must constantly go back and forth, at great cost of effort, finding food in one place and suckling or feeding their young in another. It is true that if the mother dies, the infant is in great danger of dying with her; but this risk is shared by many other species whose young take a long time to become self-sufficient. And if our infancy is longer than theirs, our lives are proportionately longer, so that in this respect things are fairly balanced, though there are other principles, concerning the length of early life and the number of offspring, that have no bearing on the present subject. In old age, when people are less active and perspire little, the need for food declines along with the ability to obtain it. And since the savage way of life spares them gout and rheumatism, and since old age is, of all ills, the one human aid can least relieve, they simply cease to be, without others noticing that they are gone, and almost without noticing it themselves.

As for illness, I shall not repeat the empty and false tirades against medicine that most healthy people deliver. I shall ask instead whether any reliable observations have been made from which one could fairly conclude that, in countries where the art of medicine is most neglected, the average human lifespan is shorter than in countries where it is most cultivated. And how could that be so, if we bring upon ourselves more diseases than medicine can supply remedies for? Consider the great inequality in ways of living: the extreme idleness of some and the excessive labor of others; the ease with which we arouse and satisfy our sensual appetites; the overly refined foods of the rich, which inflame them and fill them with indigestion; and, on the other side, the unwholesome food of the poor, which, bad as it is, is often too scanty for their needs, so that when the chance arises they eat ravenously and overload their stomachs. Add to all this late nights and excesses of every kind, the immoderate outbursts of every passion, fatigue, mental exhaustion, and the countless sorrows and anxieties that attend every station in life and endlessly torment the human mind. These are all too grim proofs that most of our ills are of our own making, and that we could have avoided nearly all of them by keeping to the simple, regular, and solitary way of life that nature prescribed for us. If nature intended man to be healthy, I venture to say that the state of reflection is a state contrary to nature, and that a thinking man is a depraved animal. When we consider the sound constitution of savages, at least of those we have not ruined with our strong liquor, and reflect that they suffer from hardly any ailments other than wounds and old age, we are tempted to believe that in tracing the history of civil society we would also be tracing the history of human illness. Such, at least, was the view of Plato, who concluded from certain remedies that Podalirius and Machaon prescribed or approved at the siege of Troy that several illnesses those remedies would have caused in his own day were then unknown to mankind. And Celsus tells us that dietary regimen, now so necessary, was first invented by Hippocrates.

Exposed, then, to so few causes of illness, man in the state of nature can have no need of remedies, and still less of physicians. Nor is the human race worse off in this respect than other animals, as anyone can learn by asking hunters whether they come across many sickly animals in the course of the chase. They certainly find many that bear the marks of serious wounds, and many that have had bones or even limbs broken, yet have healed with no surgeon but time and no regimen but their ordinary way of life. Nor do their cures appear any less complete for their not having been tormented by incisions, poisoned with drugs, or weakened by fasting. In short, however useful medicine may be among us when properly administered, it is certain that if the sick savage, left to himself, has nothing to hope for except from nature, he also has nothing to fear except from his illness, which often makes his situation preferable to ours.

We must therefore take care not to confuse savage man with the people we see around us every day. Nature treats all the animals left in her care with a partiality that seems to show how jealously she guards that right. The horse, the cat, the bull, and even the donkey are generally larger, and always sturdier, more vigorous, stronger, and bolder when they run wild in the forests than when they are raised in the stable. Once domesticated, they lose half these advantages, and it seems as though all our efforts to feed and care for them well serve only to degrade them. So it is with man. As he becomes sociable and a slave, he grows weak, fearful, and servile, and his soft and pampered way of life saps both his strength and his courage. We may add that the difference between savage and civilized man must be even greater than that between wild and tame animals; for although nature treated humans and beasts alike, the many comforts people grant themselves beyond what they grant their animals are so many further causes of their deeper degeneration.

It is therefore no great misfortune for these first men, nor any great obstacle to their survival, that they go naked, have no dwellings, and lack all the superfluities we consider so necessary. If their skin is not covered with hair, they have no need of such covering in warm climates, and in cold ones they soon learn to take the skins of the beasts they have overcome. If they have only two legs to run with, they have two arms with which to defend themselves and provide for their needs. Their children learn to walk late and with difficulty, but their mothers can carry them with ease, an advantage other animals lack, since the mother, when pursued, must either abandon her young or slow her pace to theirs. In short, unless we assume a singular and chance combination of circumstances, which I shall discuss later and which was most unlikely ever to occur, it is plain in every state of the case that the man who first made himself clothing or a shelter was supplying himself with things he did not need at all. He had done without them until then, and there is no reason he could not have endured, as a grown man, the same kind of life he had lived as a child.

Solitary, idle, and always surrounded by danger, the savage is bound to be fond of sleep, and his sleep must be light, like that of animals, which think very little and may be said to doze whenever they are not thinking. Since self-preservation is his chief and nearly his only concern, he must exercise most the faculties that bear on attack and defense, whether to overpower his prey or to avoid becoming the prey of other animals. The organs that are refined only through ease and sensual indulgence, by contrast, will remain in a crude and undeveloped state, incapable of any delicacy. His senses are therefore divided: his touch and taste will be extremely coarse, while his sight, hearing, and smell will be exceptionally keen and subtle. Such is the condition of animals generally, and such, according to the accounts of travelers, is the condition of most savage nations. It is no surprise, then, that the Hottentots of the Cape of Good Hope can make out ships at sea with the naked eye at as great a distance as the Dutch can with their telescopes; that the savages of America could track the Spaniards by scent as well as the best hunting dogs; that these barbarous peoples feel no discomfort in going naked; or that they season their food heavily with hot peppers and drink the strongest European liquors as if they were water.

So far I have considered only physical man. Let us now look at him from the metaphysical and moral side.

In every animal I see nothing but an ingenious machine, to which nature has given senses so that it can keep itself running and protect itself, to a certain extent, against anything that might disorder or destroy it. I see exactly the same things in the human machine, with one difference: in the workings of the beast, nature alone is the agent, whereas man takes part in his own workings as a free agent. The beast chooses and rejects by instinct; man does so by an act of free will. As a result, the beast cannot depart from the rule prescribed to it even when departing would benefit it, while man often departs from such rules to his own harm. A pigeon would starve to death beside a dish of the finest meats, and a cat on a heap of fruit or grain, although either could certainly find nourishment in the food it so disdainfully refuses, if only it thought of trying it. This is also why dissolute men run into excesses that bring on fever and death: the mind corrupts the senses, and the will keeps speaking after nature has fallen silent.

Every animal has ideas, since it has senses; it even combines those ideas to some degree, and in this respect man differs from the beast only in degree. Some philosophers have even claimed that there is a greater difference between one man and another than between certain men and certain beasts. It is not so much understanding, then, that marks the specific difference between man and beast, as man's quality of being a free agent. Nature commands every animal, and the beast obeys. Man feels the same impulse, but he knows that he is free to comply or to resist, and it is above all in his awareness of this freedom that the spiritual nature of his soul reveals itself. Physics may explain, to some extent, the mechanism of the senses and the formation of ideas; but in the power of willing, or rather of choosing, and in the feeling of that power, we find only acts that are purely spiritual and wholly beyond explanation by the laws of mechanics.

Even if the difficulties surrounding all these questions should leave some room for dispute about this difference between men and beasts, there is another, highly specific quality that distinguishes them and that admits of no dispute at all. This is the faculty of self-improvement, which, with the help of circumstances, gradually develops all our other faculties, and which belongs to the species as well as to the individual. An animal, by contrast, is at the end of a few months everything it will ever be for the rest of its life, and its species, at the end of a thousand years, is exactly what it was in the first year of that thousand. Why is man alone liable to sink into senility? Is it not because in old age he returns to his primitive condition? The beast, having acquired nothing, has nothing to lose, and it keeps the strength of its instinct to the end; but man, when age or accident strips him of everything his capacity for improvement had allowed him to gain, falls lower than the beasts themselves. It would be a melancholy thing to have to admit that this distinctive and nearly unlimited faculty is the source of all human misery; that it is this faculty which, over time, draws man out of his original condition, in which he would have passed his days peacefully and innocently, without noticing them go by; and that it is this faculty which, bringing forth in different ages his discoveries and his errors, his vices and his virtues, at last makes him a tyrant over both himself and nature. It would be distressing to have to regard as a benefactor the man who first gave the Indians of the Orinoco the idea of binding boards against the temples of their children, a practice that preserves for them at least some portion of their dullness of mind and their original happiness.

Savage man, left by nature to the guidance of instinct alone, or rather compensated for whatever instinct he may lack by faculties that can at first take its place and later raise him far above it, must therefore begin with purely animal functions. Seeing and feeling must be his first condition, one he would share with all other animals. To will and not to will, to desire and to fear, must be the first and nearly the only operations of his soul, until new circumstances bring about new developments of his faculties.

Whatever the moralists may say, human understanding owes a great deal to the passions, which, as everyone agrees, owe a great deal to the understanding in turn. It is through the activity of the passions that our reason is refined. We seek knowledge only because we wish to enjoy, and it is impossible to imagine why a being with neither fears nor desires would take the trouble to reason. The passions, for their part, arise from our needs, and their growth depends on the growth of our knowledge, since we can desire or fear things only through the ideas we have of them or through the simple impulse of nature. Savage man, lacking every kind of enlightenment, can have only passions of the second kind; his desires never reach beyond his physical needs. The only goods he recognizes in the universe are food, a female, and sleep; the only evils he fears are pain and hunger. I say pain, and not death, because no animal can know what it is to die. Knowledge of death and its terrors is one of the first things man acquires as he moves away from the animal condition.

It would be easy, if it were necessary, to support this view with facts, and to show that among all the nations of the world the progress of the understanding has corresponded exactly to the needs that peoples either received from nature or had imposed on them by circumstance, and therefore to the passions that drove them to provide for those needs. I might point to the arts arising in Egypt and spreading with the flooding of the Nile. I might trace their progress into Greece, where they took root again, grew, and rose to the heavens among the rocks and sands of Attica, yet could not sprout on the fertile banks of the Eurotas in Sparta. I might observe that, in general, the peoples of the North are more industrious than those of the South because they cannot manage as well without being so, as if nature wished to even things out by granting their minds the fertility she had denied their soil.

But without appealing to the uncertain evidence of history, who can fail to see that everything seems to deprive savage man of both the temptation and the means to change his condition? His imagination paints no pictures; his heart asks nothing of him. His few needs are so easily met, and he is so far from possessing the knowledge that would lead him to want more, that he can have neither foresight nor curiosity. The spectacle of nature grows indifferent to him as it grows familiar. He sees in it always the same order and the same cycles; he lacks the understanding to marvel at its greatest wonders, and it is not in his mind that we should expect to find the kind of philosophy a man needs in order to notice, for once, what he sees every day. His soul, disturbed by nothing, is wholly absorbed in the feeling of its present existence, with no idea of the future, however near. His plans, as narrow as his outlook, scarcely reach the end of the day. Even now, such is the extent of the Carib's foresight: he will heedlessly sell you his cotton hammock in the morning and come back in tears that evening to buy it again, having failed to foresee that he would need it that night.

The more we reflect on this subject, the wider the distance appears between pure sensation and even the simplest knowledge, and it becomes impossible to conceive how a man, by his own powers alone, without the help of communication and the spur of necessity, could have crossed so great a gap. How many ages may have passed before men were able to see any fire other than the fire sent from the heavens? How many chance events must have occurred to teach them the most ordinary uses of that element? How many times must they have let it go out before they learned the art of making it again? And how often might such a secret have died with the one who discovered it? What can we say of agriculture, an art that demands so much labor and foresight, and depends so heavily on other arts, that it could clearly be practiced only in a society that had at least begun to form? Its purpose is not so much to draw the means of subsistence from the earth, which would yield them on its own, as to force the earth to produce what best suits our tastes. But suppose that men had multiplied so greatly that the earth's natural produce could no longer sustain them, a supposition which, incidentally, would show that such a life was highly favorable to the human race. Suppose further that, without forges or workshops, the tools of farming had dropped from the sky into the savages' hands; that they had overcome their natural aversion to steady labor; that they had learned to anticipate their needs; that they had guessed how to cultivate the soil, sow grain, and plant trees; and that they had discovered the arts of grinding grain and fermenting grapes, all things that the gods must have taught them, since it is impossible to see how they could have discovered them on their own. Even then, what man among them would be foolish enough to take the trouble of cultivating a field that could be stripped of its crop by the first passerby, man or beast, who happened to fancy it? And how could any of them resolve to spend his life in wearisome toil when, the more he needed the reward of his labor, the more certain he would be of never receiving it? In short, how could such a situation lead men to cultivate the earth until it had been formally divided among them, which is to say, until the state of nature had been abolished?

Suppose we imagined savage man to be as skilled in the art of thinking as the philosophers make him out to be. Suppose we followed them in imagining him a philosopher in his own right, capable of investigating the loftiest truths and of deriving, through highly abstract chains of reasoning, principles of reason and justice from the love of order in general or from the known will of his Creator. Suppose, in short, that we imagined him as intelligent and enlightened as he must in fact have been, and is actually found to have been, dull and stupid. What benefit would the species gain from all this metaphysics, which could not be passed from one person to another but would perish with the one who conceived it? What progress could mankind make while scattered through the forests among the other animals? And how far could men improve or enlighten one another when, having no fixed dwelling and no need of each other's help, the same two individuals would scarcely meet twice in their lives, and even then perhaps without recognizing each other or exchanging a word?

Consider how many ideas we owe to the use of speech, and how much grammar exercises the understanding and eases its operations. Reflect on the unimaginable effort and the immense span of time that the first invention of languages must have cost. Add these reflections to what came before, and then judge how many thousands of centuries must have passed in the gradual development, within the human mind, of the operations of which it is capable.

I will take the liberty here of pausing for a moment to consider the difficulties surrounding the origin of languages. On this subject I might be content simply to repeat the investigations of the Abbé de Condillac, since they fully confirm my system and may even have first suggested it to me. But the way this philosopher resolves the difficulties he himself raises about the origin of arbitrary signs makes it clear that he assumes what I call into question: namely, that some kind of society must already have existed among the first inventors of language. While referring the reader to his observations on this point, therefore, I think it right to offer my own, in order to present the same difficulties in a light suited to my subject.

The first difficulty is to understand how language could have become necessary. Since men had no communication with one another and no need of any, we can conceive neither why this invention was needed nor how it was possible, if it was not in some way indispensable. I might claim, along with many others, that languages arose in the domestic dealings between parents and their children. But this explanation would not remove the difficulty, and it would also repeat the error of those who, in reasoning about the state of nature, always carry into it ideas drawn from the state of society. They invariably picture families living together under one roof, their members bound by a union as close and lasting as the one that exists among us, where so many shared interests hold them together. In that primitive state, however, men had neither houses nor huts nor property of any kind. Each lived wherever he happened to be, rarely for more than a single night. The sexes came together without intention, as chance, opportunity, or desire brought them together, and they had little need of words to convey their purposes to each other; they parted with the same indifference. The mother nursed her children at first for her own relief, and later, once habit had made them dear to her, for their sake. But as soon as they were strong enough to find food on their own, they left her of their own accord, and since they had scarcely any way of keeping track of one another except by staying constantly within sight, they soon became wholly unable to recognize each other when they happened to meet again.

It should also be noted that the child, who had all his needs to make known and therefore more to say to his mother than she could have to say to him, must have borne the main burden of invention. The language he used would be of his own devising, so that there would be as many languages as there were individuals speaking them, and this variety would be increased by their wandering, roving life, which allowed no time for any idiom to become fixed. To say that the mother taught her child the words he was to use when asking her for one thing or another explains how languages already formed are taught, but it does nothing to explain how languages were first formed.

Let us suppose, however, that this first difficulty has been overcome. Let us place ourselves for a moment on the far side of the vast gulf that must separate the pure state of nature from the condition in which languages had become necessary, and, granting that they were necessary, let us ask how they could first have been established. Here we face a new and still greater difficulty. If men needed speech in order to learn to think, they must have needed the art of thinking even more in order to invent the art of speaking. And even if we could understand how the articulate sounds of the voice came to be accepted as the agreed-upon interpreters of our ideas, we would still have to ask what could have served to interpret this agreement itself for ideas that correspond to no object of the senses and so could be indicated neither by gesture nor by voice. We can therefore scarcely form any reasonable conjecture about the origin of this art of communicating our thoughts and establishing a connection between minds. It is an art so exalted that, far as it now stands from its origins, philosophers still see it as so immeasurably distant from perfection that none of them would dare to claim it will ever arrive there, even if the upheavals that time inevitably brings were suspended in its favor, even if prejudice were banished from our academies or condemned to silence, and even if those learned societies devoted themselves without interruption, for entire ages, to this thorny question.

The first language of mankind, the most universal and the most forceful, and indeed the only language man needed before he had occasion to persuade assembled crowds, was the simple cry of nature. But because this cry was drawn out only by a kind of instinct in moments of urgency, to beg for help in danger or for relief in suffering, it was of little use in the ordinary course of life, where gentler feelings prevail. As men's ideas began to expand and multiply, and closer communication arose among them, they sought to invent more numerous signs and a richer language. They multiplied the inflections of the voice and added gestures, which are by nature more expressive and depend less on a prior agreement for their meaning. Visible and moving objects were thus expressed by gestures, and audible ones by imitative sounds. But gestures can indicate little beyond objects that are present or easily described and actions that can be seen; they are not always usable, since darkness or an intervening object renders them useless; and they invite attention rather than compel it. So men eventually thought of replacing them with the articulate sounds of the voice, which, though they bear no natural relation to any particular idea, are better suited to express all ideas as agreed-upon signs. Such a convention could only have been established by common consent, and it must have been accomplished with considerable difficulty by men whose crude vocal organs were unaccustomed to any such exercise. It is in itself even harder to conceive, since such a general agreement must have had reasons behind it, and speech itself seems to have been necessary to establish the use of speech.

It is reasonable to suppose that the first words men used had a much broader meaning than words in languages already formed, and that, knowing nothing of how speech divides into its component parts, they at first gave each single word the meaning of a whole proposition. When they began to distinguish subject from predicate and noun from verb, itself no ordinary feat of genius, nouns were at first nothing but proper names, the present infinitive was the only verb tense, and the very idea of adjectives must have developed only with great difficulty, because every adjective is an abstract idea, and abstraction is a laborious and unnatural operation.

At first every object received its own particular name, without regard to genus or species, which these first inventors were in no position to distinguish. Each individual thing appeared to their minds in isolation, just as things stand in the picture nature presents. If one oak was called A, another was called B, for our first idea of two things is that they are not the same, and it often takes a long time to perceive what they have in common. Thus the narrower their knowledge of things, the larger their vocabulary must have been. The awkwardness of such a vocabulary could not easily be remedied, because arranging things under common and general names required knowing their distinguishing properties. Observation and definition became necessary; that is, natural history and metaphysics far more advanced than men could have possessed at that time.

Moreover, general ideas cannot enter the mind without the help of words, and the understanding can grasp them only through propositions. This is one of the reasons animals cannot form such ideas, or ever acquire the capacity for self-improvement that depends on them. When a monkey moves from one nut to another, are we to believe that he holds some general idea of that kind of fruit and compares its ideal model with the two particular nuts? Certainly not. Rather, the sight of one nut recalls to his memory the sensations he received from the other, and his eyes, affected in a certain way, signal to his palate the sensation it is about to receive. Every general idea is purely intellectual; let the imagination intrude upon it in the slightest degree, and the idea at once becomes particular. Try to picture in your mind the image of a tree in general, and you will never succeed. Whatever you do, you will see it as large or small, bare or in leaf, pale or dark, and if you could see in it only what is common to all trees, it would no longer resemble a tree at all. Purely abstract entities are perceived in the same way, or can be conceived only with the help of language. The definition of a triangle alone gives you a true idea of it; the moment you picture a triangle in your mind, it is one particular triangle and not another, and you cannot avoid giving it visible lines and a colored surface. We must therefore use propositions and language in order to form general ideas. For as soon as the imagination stops working, than the understanding can proceed only with the help of words. If, then, the first inventors of speech could give names only to ideas they already had, it follows that the first nouns could have been nothing more than proper names.

But when our new grammarians began, by means I cannot conceive, to extend their ideas and generalize their terms, the ignorance of these inventors must have confined the method within very narrow limits. At first they had gone too far in multiplying the names of individual things, because they knew nothing of genus and species. Afterward they made too few general names, because they had not considered beings in all their specific differences. Carrying these distinctions as far as they properly ought to go would have required more knowledge and experience than they could have possessed, and more effort and inquiry than they would have been willing to spend. If even today we are continually discovering new species that have so far escaped observation, consider how many must have escaped men who judged things merely by their first appearance! It hardly needs adding that the most basic classes and the most general notions must also have escaped their notice. How, for instance, could they have understood or even thought of such words as *matter*, *spirit*, *substance*, *mode*, *figure*, and *motion*, when our philosophers, who have been using them for so long, have the greatest difficulty understanding them, and when the ideas attached to them are purely metaphysical, so that no model for them can be found anywhere in nature?

I stop at this point and ask my judges to set their reading aside for a moment and consider how far there still is to go, even after the invention of nouns for physical things, which is the easiest part of language to invent, before human thought finds a perfect means of expression and a stable form, one that could meet the demands of public speech and have its effect on society. I ask them to consider how much time must have been spent, and how much knowledge was needed, to discover numbers, abstract terms, the aorists (the simple past forms that mark an action without regard to its duration) and all the other tenses of verbs, the particles, syntax, the means of linking one proposition to another, the forms of reasoning, and the whole logic of speech. For my own part, I am so dismayed by the mounting difficulties, and so convinced that it is almost demonstrably impossible for languages to have been first established by merely human means, that I leave to anyone willing to take it up the discussion of a difficult problem: which was more necessary, an existing society for the invention of language, or an invented language for the establishment of society.

Whatever the origin of language and society may be, one inference can at least be drawn. Nature has taken so little care to bind men together through mutual needs, or to make the use of speech easy for them, that she has done little to make them sociable and has contributed little of her own to anything they have done to create such bonds. It is impossible, in fact, to conceive why, in a state of nature, one man should need the help of another any more than a monkey or a wolf needs the help of another of its kind; or, granting that he did, what motive could induce the other to help him; or, even then, how the two could agree on the terms. I know it is endlessly repeated that man in such a state would have been the most miserable of creatures. And if it is true, as I believe I have proved, that he must have lived for many ages before he could have had either the desire or the opportunity to leave that state, this would be an accusation against nature, not against the being she had so unhappily constituted. As I understand the word *miserable*, however, it either has no meaning at all or signifies only a painful deprivation, a state of suffering in body or in soul. I should be glad to have someone explain to me what kind of misery a free being can suffer whose heart is at ease and whose body is in health. I would also ask whether social life or natural life is more likely to become unbearable to those who live it.
