Around us we see hardly a single person in civil society who does not lament his existence; we even see many who deprive themselves of as much of it as they can, and all the laws of men and of God together can scarcely put a stop to the practice. I ask whether anyone has ever heard of a savage, living in freedom, who took it into his head to complain of life or to kill himself. Let us judge, then, with less vanity, on which side the real misery lies. Nothing, on the other hand, could be more unhappy than a savage dazzled by learning, tormented by passions, and reasoning about a condition different from his own. Providence, it seems, very wisely arranged that the faculties man possessed in potential should develop only as occasions arose to exercise them, so that they would neither burden and perplex him by appearing before their time nor arrive too slowly to be of use when he needed them. In instinct alone he had everything he required for living in the state of nature, and in a cultivated understanding he has only just enough to sustain life in society.

At first glance it would seem that men in a state of nature, having no moral relations and no fixed obligations toward one another, could be neither good nor bad, neither virtuous nor vicious. The exception would be if we took these terms in a physical sense, calling those qualities in an individual vices that might harm his self-preservation and those virtues that might promote it. In that case, the most virtuous man would be the one who least resisted the pure impulses of nature. Without departing from the ordinary sense of these words, however, it is wise to suspend any judgment we might be tempted to pass on such a state, and to guard against our own prejudices, until we have weighed the matter impartially. We must first see whether virtues or vices prevail among civilized men, and whether their virtues do them more good than their vices do them harm. We must discover whether the progress of the sciences adequately compensates them for the injuries they inflict on one another the better informed they become of the good they ought to do. And we must ask whether, on the whole, they would not be far happier with nothing to fear or hope from anyone than they are now, subjected to universal dependence and obliged to accept everything from those who undertake to give them nothing in return.

Above all, let us not conclude with Hobbes that because man has no idea of goodness he must be naturally wicked; that he is vicious because he does not know virtue; that he always refuses to render his fellow creatures any service he does not believe they have a right to demand; or that, on the strength of the right he rightly claims to everything he needs, he foolishly imagines himself the sole owner of the entire universe. Hobbes saw clearly the defects of all the modern definitions of natural right, but the conclusions he draws from his own definition show that he understands it in a sense no less false. Reasoning from the principles he himself lays down, he ought to have said that the state of nature, being the condition in which care for our own preservation does least harm to the preservation of others, was consequently the best suited to peace and the most fitting for mankind. He says exactly the opposite, because he improperly included in the savage's concern for self-preservation the gratification of a multitude of passions that are the product of society and are what made laws necessary in the first place. A wicked man, he says, is a robust child. It remains to be proved, however, that man in a state of nature is this robust child. And even if we grant that he is, what would Hobbes conclude? Only that if this man, once robust and strong, were as dependent on others as he is when weak, there is no excess he would not commit: he would beat his mother when she was too slow to give him the breast, strangle a younger brother who annoyed him, or bite the arm of another who got in his way. But to say that man in the state of nature is both strong and dependent is to make two contradictory assumptions. Man is weak when he is dependent, and he becomes his own master before he becomes strong. Hobbes failed to see that the same cause that prevents a savage from using his reason, as our jurists maintain, also prevents him from abusing his faculties, as Hobbes himself concedes. It may therefore justly be said that savages are not wicked precisely because they do not know what it is to be good. What keeps them from doing harm is neither the development of their understanding nor the restraint of law, but the calm of their passions and their ignorance of vice. As the ancient historian Justin wrote of the Scythians, "so much more does ignorance of vice profit them than knowledge of virtue profits others."

There is another principle that Hobbes overlooked. It was given to mankind to soften, on certain occasions, the fierceness of egoism, or, before egoism was born, the desire for self-preservation, and it tempers the ardor with which man pursues his own welfare by means of an innate repugnance at seeing a fellow creature suffer. I do not think I need fear contradiction in granting man the one natural virtue that even the most violent detractor of human virtue could not deny him. I am speaking of compassion, a disposition well suited to creatures as weak and as exposed to so many evils as we certainly are. It is all the more universal and useful to mankind because it comes before any kind of reflection, and it is so natural that even animals sometimes give clear evidence of it. Setting aside the tenderness of mothers toward their young and the dangers they brave to protect them, it is well known that horses are reluctant to trample a living body. An animal never passes the corpse of one of its own species without disturbance; some even give their dead a kind of burial; and the mournful lowing of cattle as they enter the slaughterhouse reveals the impression the horrible spectacle before them makes upon them. It is gratifying to see the author of *The Fable of the Bees* forced to admit that man is a compassionate and feeling being. In the example he offers, he abandons his cold and subtle style to give us the moving description of a man who, shut up in a place from which he cannot escape, is forced to watch a wild beast tear a child from its mother's arms, crush its tender limbs in its murderous jaws, and rip out its still-quivering entrails with its claws. What dreadful agitation the witness of such a scene must feel, even though he himself is in no danger! What anguish he must suffer at being unable to help the fainting mother or the dying child!

Such is the pure impulse of nature, prior to all reflection. Such is the force of natural compassion, which even the deepest corruption of morals has so far scarcely been able to destroy. Every day in our theaters we see men moved, even to tears, by the sufferings of some unfortunate character, men who, if they were in the tyrant's place, would likely add to the torments of their own enemies. They resemble the bloodthirsty Sulla, so sensitive to suffering he had not caused himself, or Alexander of Pherae, who dared not attend the performance of any tragedy for fear of being seen weeping with Andromache and Priam, yet who could listen without emotion to the cries of all the citizens strangled every day on his orders. As Juvenal writes in his fifteenth satire, "Nature confesses that she gave the human race the tenderest of hearts, since she gave it tears."

Mandeville knew very well that, for all their moral codes, men would never have been anything but monsters if nature had not given them compassion to support their reason. What he failed to see is that from this single quality flow all the social virtues he denied that man possesses. For what are generosity, clemency, and humanity but compassion directed toward the weak, toward the guilty, or toward the human race in general? Even benevolence and friendship, rightly understood, are only the products of a compassion steadily fixed on a particular object, for what difference is there between wishing that someone may not suffer pain or distress and wishing that he may be happy? Even if it were true that pity is nothing more than a feeling that puts us in the place of the sufferer, a feeling obscure but intense in the savage and developed but weak in civilized man, this would only confirm my argument. Compassion must be stronger in proportion as the creature witnessing distress identifies itself with the creature that suffers. And such identification must clearly have been far more complete in the state of nature than in the state of reason. It is reason that gives rise to self-regard and reflection that strengthens it; it is reason that turns a man's mind back upon itself and separates him from everything that might trouble or distress him. It is philosophy that isolates him and prompts him to say, at the sight of another's misfortune, "Perish if you will; I am safe." Nothing but dangers threatening the whole community can disturb the philosopher's peaceful sleep or drag him from his bed. A man may be murdered with impunity beneath his window; he has only to put his hands over his ears and argue with himself a little to keep nature, which rebels within him, from identifying with the victim. The savage has no such admirable talent. Lacking reason and wisdom, he always foolishly gives way to the first promptings of humanity. In riots and street fights it is the common people who crowd together, while the wise man prudently slips away; it is the rabble and the market women who separate the combatants and keep respectable people from cutting each other's throats.

It is certain, then, that compassion is a natural feeling which, by moderating the force of love of self in each individual, contributes to the preservation of the whole species. It is compassion that sends us, without reflection, to the aid of those in distress. In the state of nature it takes the place of laws, morals, and virtues, with the added advantage that no one is tempted to disobey its gentle voice. It is compassion that will always keep a sturdy savage from robbing a weak child or a frail old man of the food they have gathered with great pain and difficulty, so long as he sees some chance of providing for himself by other means. Instead of the sublime maxim of rational justice, "Do unto others as you would have them do unto you," it inspires in all men this other maxim of natural goodness, far less perfect but perhaps more useful: "Do good to yourself with as little harm as possible to others." In short, it is in this natural feeling, rather than in any subtle argument, that we must look for the source of the repugnance every man would feel at doing evil, even apart from the precepts of education. Socrates and minds of his stamp may be able to acquire virtue through reason, but the human race would long ago have ceased to exist if its preservation had depended solely on the reasoning of the individuals who compose it.

With passions so little roused and so effective a check upon them, men were wild rather than wicked, more concerned to protect themselves from harm than to harm others, and so they were by no means prone to dangerous quarrels. Since they had no regular dealings with one another, they knew nothing of vanity, deference, esteem, or contempt. They had not the slightest notion of *mine* and *yours*, and no true conception of justice. They regarded any violence done to them as an injury easily made good rather than as a crime that ought to be punished, and they never thought of revenge, except perhaps mechanically and on the spot, the way a dog will sometimes bite the stone thrown at it. Their quarrels, therefore, would seldom have had bloody consequences, since the only thing at stake would have been food. There remains, however, one greater danger, which I must now address.

Among the passions that stir the human heart, there is one, ardent and impetuous, that makes each sex necessary to the other. It is a terrible passion that defies danger and overcomes every obstacle, and in its frenzies it seems likely to destroy the human race it is actually meant to preserve. What would become of men abandoned to this brutal and unbridled fury, without modesty or shame, fighting every day for their loves at the cost of their blood?

It must be admitted, first of all, that the more violent the passions are, the more necessary laws become to restrain them. But setting aside the fact that laws are inadequate to this task, as is evident from the crimes and disorders these passions cause among us every day, we would do well to ask whether these evils did not arise together with the laws themselves. If so, then even if the laws were capable of suppressing them, the least we could expect is that they should check an evil that would never have existed without them.

Let us begin by distinguishing between the physical and the moral elements in the feeling of love. The physical element is the general desire that drives the sexes to unite. The moral element is what fixes that desire exclusively on a single object, or at least gives it greater intensity toward the object preferred. It is easy to see that this moral element is an artificial feeling, born of social custom and cultivated by women with great care and skill in order to establish their dominion and place in power the sex that ought to obey. This feeling rests on certain ideas of beauty and merit that a savage has no means of acquiring, and on comparisons he is incapable of making, so for him it must be almost nonexistent. Just as his mind cannot form abstract ideas of proportion and regularity, his heart is not susceptible to the feelings of love and admiration that arise, even imperceptibly, from applying those ideas. He follows only the disposition nature has given him, not tastes he could never have acquired, and so any woman suits him equally well.

Confined to the physical side of love, and fortunate enough to know nothing of those refinements that sharpen desire while making it harder to satisfy, men in the state of nature must have experienced fewer and less violent fits of passion, and consequently fewer and less violent disputes. Imagination, which wreaks such havoc among us, never speaks to the hearts of savages. They wait calmly for the promptings of nature, yield to them without deliberation, with more pleasure than frenzy, and once their need is satisfied, the desire is gone. It is therefore beyond dispute that love, like all the other passions, acquired only in society the burning intensity that so often makes it fatal to mankind. It is all the more absurd to portray savages as constantly cutting each other's throats to gratify their brutal appetites, because this view flatly contradicts experience. The Caribs, who of all peoples have strayed least from the state of nature, are in fact the most peaceable in their loves and the least prone to jealousy, even though they live in a hot climate that would seem always to inflame the passions.

As for the conclusions that might be drawn from the many animal species whose males fill our poultry yards with blood and slaughter, or make the forests ring every spring with their fights over females, we must first set aside all those species in which nature has plainly established a different balance of strength between the sexes from the one that exists among us. Nothing can be concluded about men from the habits of fighting cocks. In species where the proportion more closely resembles ours, these battles must arise entirely from a scarcity of females relative to males, or, what comes to the same thing, from the periods during which the female consistently refuses the male. If each female accepts the male for only two months of the year, the effect is the same as if there were five-sixths fewer females. Neither of these conditions applies to the human species, in which females usually outnumber males, and in which no one has ever observed, even among savages, that women have fixed seasons of desire and indifference like the females of other animals. Moreover, in several of these species all the individuals are seized by passion at once, and there comes a terrible moment of general frenzy, tumult, and disorder, a scene never witnessed among humans, whose love is not bound to a season. We must not conclude, then, from the battles of such animals over their females, that the same would hold for mankind in the state of nature. And even if we did draw that conclusion, we can see that such contests do not wipe out other species, and we have no reason to think they would prove more destructive to ours. Clearly they would do even less harm than they do in society, especially in countries where morals still count for something, and where the jealousy of lovers and the vengeance of husbands are the daily cause of duels, murders, and worse; where the duty of eternal fidelity serves only to produce adultery; and where the very laws of honor and chastity inevitably spread debauchery and multiply abortions.

Let us conclude, then, that man in the state of nature wandered through the forests without industry, without speech, and without a home, a stranger alike to war and to every bond, with no need of his fellow men and no desire to harm them, perhaps without even recognizing any of them individually. Let us conclude that, being self-sufficient and subject to so few passions, he had only the feelings and knowledge suited to his condition; that he felt only his present needs and paid no attention to anything he did not believe immediately concerned him; and that his understanding advanced no further than his vanity. If by chance he made some discovery, he was all the less able to share it, since he did not even know his own children. Every art would necessarily die with its inventor, since there was no education of any kind among men, and generation followed generation without the least advance; when everyone set out from the same starting point, centuries must have passed in the barbarism of the first ages; when the race was already old, and man was still a child.

If I have dwelt at such length on this supposed primitive state, it is because I had so many ancient errors and deep-rooted prejudices to remove. I therefore thought it my duty to dig down to their very root and to show, through a true picture of the state of nature, how far even the natural inequalities of mankind are from having the reality and influence that modern writers attribute to them.

It is easy to see, in fact, that many of the differences that distinguish one man from another are simply the effect of habit and of the different ways of life that men adopt in society. A robust or delicate constitution, and the strength or weakness that goes with it, more often results from a hardy or a pampered upbringing than from the body's original endowment. The same holds for the powers of the mind. Education not only creates a difference between those who are cultivated and those who are not; it also widens the differences among the cultivated themselves, in proportion to how far each has been cultivated, just as the distance between a giant and a dwarf walking the same road grows with every step they take. Compare the enormous variety in the education and way of life of the different classes in society with the uniformity and simplicity of animal and savage life, where everyone eats the same kind of food, lives in exactly the same manner, and does exactly the same things. It then becomes easy to see how much smaller the difference between one man and another must be in the state of nature than in society, and how greatly the inequalities of social institutions must magnify the natural inequality of mankind.

But even if nature really did show, in distributing her gifts, the partiality that is attributed to her, what advantage would her greatest favorites gain from it at the expense of others, in a state that allows for almost no relations between people? Where there is no love, what good is beauty? What use is wit to those who never converse, or cunning to those who have no dealings with one another? I constantly hear it said that in such a state the strong would oppress the weak. But what is meant here by oppression? Some, we are told, would dominate others by violence, and the others would groan in servile submission to their whims. This is precisely what I observe among ourselves, but I do not see how it can be inferred of men in the state of nature, who could scarcely be made to understand what we mean by domination and servitude. One man might, it is true, seize the fruit another had gathered, the game he had killed, or the cave he had chosen for shelter. But how would he ever manage to compel obedience, and what bonds of dependence could exist among men who own nothing? If I am driven from one tree, I can go to the next; if I am disturbed in one place, what stops me from going somewhere else? Suppose, further, that I meet a man not only much stronger than I am but also so depraved, so lazy, and so savage as to force me to provide his food while he himself does nothing. He would have to make sure never to take his eyes off me for a single moment, and he would have to tie me up securely before he went to sleep, or I would certainly either knock him on the head or run away. In other terms, he would be voluntarily taking on far more trouble than the trouble he hoped to avoid, or than he could cause me. And after all this, let him relax his guard ever so slightly, let him merely turn his head at some sudden noise, and I would instantly be twenty paces away, lost in the forest, my bonds broken, and he would never see me again.

Without my dwelling uselessly on such details, anyone can see that the bonds of servitude are formed only through men's mutual dependence on one another and the reciprocal needs that bind them together. It is therefore impossible to make any man a slave unless he has first been reduced to a condition in which he cannot do without the help of others. Since no such condition exists in the state of nature, everyone there is his own master, and the law of the strongest has no effect.

Having shown that inequality is barely felt in the state of nature, and that its influence there is next to nothing, I must now show its origin and trace its progress through the successive developments of the human mind. I have shown that perfectibility, the social virtues, and the other faculties that natural man possessed in potential could never have developed on their own. They required the chance convergence of many outside causes, which might never have arisen, and without which man would have remained forever in his primitive condition. I must now gather and examine the various accidents that may have improved human understanding while corrupting the species, and made man wicked while making him sociable, so as to bring both him and the world from that distant era to the point at which we see them now.

I admit that, since the events I am about to describe could have happened in various ways, I have nothing but conjecture to guide my choice among them. Yet conjectures become reasons when they are the most probable that can be drawn from the nature of things and the only means available for discovering the truth. The consequences I intend to draw, however, will not be merely conjectural. On the principles I have just established, it would be impossible to construct any other theory that did not yield the same results, and from which I could not draw the same conclusions.

This will be enough to excuse me from dwelling on several matters: on the way the passage of time makes up for the slight probability of particular events; on the surprising power of trivial causes when they act constantly; on the fact that certain hypotheses cannot be overthrown, even though we cannot give them the certainty of established fact; on the principle that, when two facts are given as real and must be connected by a series of intermediate facts that are unknown or presumed unknown, it is the business of history to supply the facts that connect them, and, when history is silent, the business of philosophy to determine similar facts that serve the same purpose; and finally, on the influence of similarity, which, where events are concerned, reduces the facts to far fewer distinct classes than is commonly supposed. It is enough for me to offer these points for the consideration of my judges, and to have arranged matters so that the general reader need not consider them at all.

As Justin wrote: "So much more did ignorance of vice profit the one people than knowledge of virtue profited the other."

Egoism must not be confused with self-love, for the two differ both in their nature and in their effects. Self-love is a natural feeling that leads every animal to look after its own preservation; in human beings, guided by reason and tempered by compassion, it produces humanity and virtue. Egoism is a purely relative and artificial feeling. It arises only in the state of society, leads each individual to rate himself above everyone else, causes all the harm that people inflict on one another, and is the true source of what we call the "sense of honor." With this distinction in mind, I maintain that egoism did not exist in our primitive condition, in the true state of nature. Each man regarded himself as the only observer of his own actions, the only being in the universe who took any interest in him, and the sole judge of his own merit. No feeling that depends on comparison could take root in his soul, because he had no occasion to make comparisons. For the same reason he could know neither hatred nor the desire for revenge, since these passions can spring only from a sense of having been injured. What constitutes an injury is not the harm done but the contempt or the intention to hurt behind it, and so men who neither valued nor compared themselves could do one another a good deal of violence, when it suited them, without either side feeling injured. Each man regarded his fellows much as he regarded animals of other species. He might seize the prey of someone weaker or surrender his own to someone stronger, and still look on these acts of violence as ordinary natural events, without the slightest stirring of insolence or spite, and with no feeling beyond the joy of success or the disappointment of failure.

"Nature confesses that she gave the human race the tenderest of hearts, for she gave it tears."

### The Second Part

The first man who fenced off a piece of ground, took it into his head to say "This is mine," and found people simple enough to believe him was the true founder of civil society. How many crimes, wars, and murders, how many horrors and miseries, might the human race have been spared if someone had pulled up the stakes or filled in the ditch and cried out to his fellows: "Do not listen to this impostor. You are lost if you forget that the fruits of the earth belong to everyone, and the earth itself to no one." Yet in all likelihood things had already reached a point where they could no longer go on as they were. The idea of property depends on many earlier ideas, which could only have been acquired one after another and could not have formed in the human mind all at once.

Humanity must have made very considerable progress, and acquired a good deal of knowledge and skill, passing it on and adding to it from one age to the next, before arriving at this final stage of the state of nature. We must therefore go further back and try to gather into a single view that slow succession of events and discoveries, arranged in their most natural order.

Man's first feeling was the sense of his own existence, and his first concern was to preserve it. The earth supplied everything he needed, and instinct taught him how to use it. Hunger and his other appetites led him, at different times, through different modes of existence. Among these was one that drove him to reproduce his kind: a blind impulse, with nothing of the heart in it, which produced a purely animal act. Once the need was satisfied, the two sexes no longer recognized each other, and even the child meant nothing to its mother as soon as it could manage without her.

Such was the condition of man in his infancy. His was the life of an animal, limited at first to bare sensation, barely making use of the gifts nature offered him and still further from any thought of wresting something more from her. But difficulties soon arose, and he had to learn to overcome them. The height of trees kept their fruit out of his reach; other animals competed with him for the same fruit; and the fiercest creatures threatened his very life in pursuit of their own survival. All of this obliged him to train his body. He had to become agile, quick on his feet, and strong in a fight. Natural weapons, such as stones and branches, were close at hand. He learned to overcome the obstacles nature put in his way, to fight other animals when he had to, to contend even with other men for the means of survival, and to make up for whatever he was forced to yield to someone stronger.

As the human race grew more numerous, its cares multiplied. Differences of soil, climate, and season must have produced differences in how people lived. Barren years, long and bitter winters, and scorching summers that withered the fruits of the earth must have called for new kinds of ingenuity. Along the seashore and the riverbanks, people invented the hook and line and became fishermen and eaters of fish. In the forests they made bows and arrows and became hunters and warriors. In cold regions they clothed themselves in the skins of the animals they had killed. Lightning, a volcano, or some lucky accident introduced them to fire, a new defense against the harshness of winter. They learned first to keep it alive, then to kindle it themselves, and finally to cook with it the meat they had previously eaten raw.

This repeated application of various things to himself, and of one thing to another, would naturally lead the human mind to perceive certain relations among them. The relations we express by words such as *large*, *small*, *strong*, *weak*, *fast*, *slow*, *timid*, and *bold*, compared almost without his noticing whenever need arose, must eventually have produced in him a kind of reflection, or rather a mechanical prudence, that pointed him toward the precautions most necessary to his safety.

The new intelligence that grew out of this development increased his advantage over other animals by making him aware of it. He began to set traps for them and to play a thousand tricks on them, and although many surpassed him in speed or strength, in time he became master of some and the scourge of others. So it was that the first time he looked at himself, he felt the first stirring of pride. At a stage when he could barely tell the different orders of beings apart, he regarded his own species as the highest of them, and in doing so prepared the way for claiming superiority as an individual.

Other men, it is true, were not to him what they are to us now, and he had no more dealings with them than with other animals. Still, they did not escape his notice. The resemblances he gradually observed among them, and between himself and his female, led him to infer others that he could not yet perceive directly. Finding that they all behaved as he would have done in the same circumstances, he naturally concluded that they thought and acted exactly as he did. Once this important truth was firmly fixed in his mind, it must have led him, through an intuitive sense surer and far quicker than any reasoning, to adopt the rules of conduct toward them that best served his own safety and advantage.

Experience taught him that the love of well-being is the sole motive of human action. With that understanding, he could recognize the few occasions on which shared interest made it reasonable to count on the help of his fellows, and the still fewer occasions on which competing interests gave him grounds to distrust them. In the first case he joined them in a common herd, or at most in some loose association that placed no restraint on its members and lasted only as long as the passing need that brought it about. In the second case, each pursued his own advantage, by open force if he thought himself strong enough, or by skill and cunning if he felt himself the weaker.

In this way men may gradually have acquired some crude notion of mutual commitments and of the benefits of keeping them, but only so far as their immediate and visible interest required. They knew nothing of foresight. Far from troubling themselves about the distant future, they scarcely gave a thought to the next day. If a deer was to be caught, each man understood that he had to hold his assigned post faithfully if the hunt was to succeed. But if a hare happened to pass within reach of one of them, there is no doubt he would have chased it without hesitation, and, once he had caught it, would have cared very little that his companions lost their quarry as a result.

Dealings of this kind clearly required no language much more refined than that of rooks or monkeys, which band together for much the same purposes. For a long time, the universal language must have consisted of inarticulate cries, abundant gestures, and some imitative sounds. In each region, certain agreed-upon articulate sounds were added to these, and from them particular languages arose, although, as I have already suggested, it is not easy to explain how such conventions were first established. These languages were crude and imperfect, much like those still found among some savage peoples today.

Carried along by the swift passage of time, by the sheer quantity of what I have to say, and by the nearly imperceptible pace of progress in its early stages, I pass over a multitude of ages in a moment. The more slowly events followed one another, the more quickly they can be described.

These first advances allowed men to make others more rapidly. As they became more enlightened, they became more industrious. They no longer fell asleep under the first tree or in the first cave that offered shelter. They invented various tools of hard, sharp stone, which they used to dig the earth and cut wood. Then they built huts of branches and later learned to coat them with mud and clay. This was the period of a first revolution, which established families and set them apart from one another, and which introduced a kind of property that was itself the source of a thousand quarrels and fights. Since the strongest were probably the first to build huts they felt able to defend, it is reasonable to conclude that the weak found it far easier and safer to imitate them than to try to drive them out. And among those who already had huts, none had any reason to take his neighbor's, not so much because it did not belong to him as because it would have been of no use to him, and he could not have seized it without a desperate battle against the family living in it.

The first expansions of the human heart came from this new situation, which brought husbands and wives, fathers and children, together under one roof. The habit of living together soon gave rise to the finest feelings known to humanity: married love and parental affection. Each family became a small society, all the more united because freedom and mutual attachment were its only bonds. The sexes, which until then had lived in the same way, now began to follow different ways of life. The women grew more settled and became accustomed to keeping the hut and caring for the children, while the men went out in search of food for all. Living more comfortably, both sexes also began to lose some of their strength and ferocity. But if individuals became somewhat less able to face wild beasts on their own, they found it easier, by the same token, to gather and resist them together.

In this new condition, the simplicity and isolation of man's life, the small number of his needs, and the tools he had invented to meet them left him a great deal of free time, which he used to provide himself with many comforts his ancestors had never known. This was the first yoke he unknowingly placed on himself, and the first source of the evils he was preparing for his descendants. Besides continuing to weaken both body and mind, these comforts lost nearly all their power to please once people grew used to them, and they even degenerated into real needs, until going without them became far more painful than having them had ever been pleasant. People were unhappy to lose them, though possessing them did not make them happy.

Here we can see a little more clearly how speech became established and gradually improved within each family, and we can also form some idea of how various causes may have spread language and hastened its development by making it more and more necessary. Floods or earthquakes surrounded inhabited regions with cliffs or water; upheavals of the earth broke off pieces of the continent and turned them into islands. Plainly, among people thrown together in this way and forced to live in one another's company, a common tongue must have developed far more easily than among those who still roamed the continental forests. It is quite possible, then, that after their first attempts at navigation the islanders brought the use of speech to the mainland, and it is at least highly probable that societies and languages first arose on islands and even reached maturity there before they were known on the continent.

From this point on, everything begins to take on a new appearance. Men who had until now wandered through the woods, having adopted a more settled way of life, gradually come together and form separate groups, until in every region a distinct nation emerges, united in character and customs not by regulations or laws but by a shared way of life and diet and the common influence of climate. Living permanently near one another, families could not help forming some ties over time. Among young people of opposite sexes living in neighboring huts, the passing encounters that nature demands soon led, through frequent contact, to another kind of relationship, no less pleasant and more lasting. People now began to notice the differences between things and to compare them. Without realizing it, they acquired the ideas of beauty and merit, and these soon gave rise to feelings of preference. Having seen one another often, they could no longer bear not to see one another constantly. A tender and pleasant feeling crept into their hearts, and the slightest obstacle turned it into a violent fury. Jealousy arose along with love; discord triumphed; and human blood was shed for the gentlest of all passions.

As ideas and feelings followed one upon another, and both heart and mind came into play, people continued to shed their original wildness, and their personal ties grew more intimate every day even as they reached further. They grew accustomed to gathering in front of their huts around a great tree. Singing and dancing, the true offspring of love and leisure, became the entertainment, or rather the occupation, of the men and women gathered there with nothing else to do. Each began to look at the others and to want to be looked at in turn, and so public esteem came to have a value. Whoever sang or danced best, whoever was handsomest, strongest, most skillful, or most eloquent came to be the most highly regarded. This was the first step toward inequality and, at the same time, toward vice. From these first distinctions arose vanity and contempt on one side, shame and envy on the other; and the ferment these new elements set working eventually produced combinations fatal to innocence and happiness.

As soon as people began to value one another and the idea of regard had taken hold in their minds, everyone laid claim to it, and no one could be denied it without consequence. This gave rise to the first duties of courtesy, even among savages, and every deliberate wrong became an affront. Beyond whatever harm it caused, the injured party was sure to see in it a contempt for his person that was often harder to bear than the harm itself.

Since each man now punished the contempt shown him in proportion to his own opinion of himself, revenge became terrible, and men became bloodthirsty and cruel. This is precisely the stage that most of the savage peoples known to us have reached. Because so many writers failed to draw the proper distinctions and did not see how far these peoples already are from the state of nature, they hastily concluded that man is cruel by nature and needs civil institutions to soften him. In fact, nothing is gentler than man in his primitive state, placed by nature at an equal distance from the stupidity of the beasts and the fatal ingenuity of civilized man. Confined equally by instinct and by reason to the single task of protecting himself from the dangers that threaten him, he is held back by natural compassion from harming others and is not moved to do so even in return for harm he has suffered. For, as the wise Locke put it in his maxim, there can be no injury where there is no property.

It must be noted, however, that the society now formed, and the relations now established among people, required qualities different from those they possessed by their original constitution. Morality began to enter into human actions. Before there were laws, each person was the sole judge and avenger of the wrongs done to him, so that the goodness suited to the pure state of nature was no longer suited to society in its infancy. Punishments had to grow harsher as opportunities for offense grew more frequent, and the fear of revenge had to take the place of the severity of law. Thus, although people had become less patient and their natural compassion had already weakened somewhat, this period in the development of human faculties, holding a proper middle course between the idleness of the primitive state and the restless activity of our egoism, must have been the happiest and most stable of all eras. The more we consider it, the more we find that this condition was the least subject to upheaval and the very best that man could know, so that he can only have left it through some fatal accident which, for the common good, ought never to have occurred. The example of savages, most of whom have been found in this condition, seems to show that humanity was meant to remain in it, that it is the true youth of the world, and that every later advance has been, in appearance, a step toward the perfection of the individual, but in reality a step toward the decline of the species.

As long as people were content with their rough huts, as long as they were satisfied with clothing made of animal skins stitched together with thorns and fish bones, adorned themselves only with feathers and shells, went on painting their bodies in various colors, improved and decorated their bows and arrows, and used sharp stones to carve fishing canoes or simple musical instruments; as long, in short, as they took on only tasks that one person could complete and practiced only crafts that did not require several pairs of hands working together, they lived free, healthy, decent, and happy lives, as far as their nature permitted, and continued to enjoy the pleasures of dealing with one another as independent equals. But from the moment one man needed another's help, from the moment it seemed advantageous for one person to have provisions enough for two, equality vanished, property appeared, and labor became a necessity. Vast forests were transformed into smiling fields that had to be watered with human sweat, and slavery and misery were soon seen sprouting and growing alongside the harvest.

Metalworking and agriculture were the two arts that brought about this great revolution. The poets say it was gold and silver; but in the philosopher's view, it was iron and grain that first civilized human beings and ruined the human race. Both were unknown to the savages of America, who for that reason have remained savages. Other peoples, too, seem to have stayed in a state of barbarism as long as they practiced only one of these arts. Perhaps one of the strongest reasons why Europe has been, if not civilized longer than the rest of the world, at least more consistently and more highly civilized, is that it is at once the richest in iron and the most fertile in grain.

It is hard to guess how people first came to know and use iron. It is impossible to suppose that they would have thought on their own to dig ore out of the ground and prepare it for smelting before they knew what would come of it. Nor is there much reason to attribute the discovery to an accidental fire, since ore deposits form only in barren places, bare of trees and plants, as though nature had gone out of her way to keep this fatal secret from us. That leaves only the extraordinary chance of a volcano spewing out metals already molten, which might have given onlookers the idea of imitating nature's process. Even then, we would have to imagine them possessed of uncommon courage and foresight to undertake so laborious a task with so remote a prospect of benefit, and those qualities are found together only in minds more advanced than we can suppose these first discoverers to have had.

As for agriculture, its principles were understood long before they were put into practice. People who constantly drew their food from trees and plants could hardly fail to learn how nature propagates vegetation. Yet it was probably a very long time before their ingenuity turned in that direction. Perhaps the trees, which together with hunting and fishing supplied their food, needed no tending; perhaps they did not know how to use grain or lacked the tools to grow it; perhaps they had no foresight about future needs; or perhaps, finally, they had no way to stop others from stealing the fruits of their labor.

As they became more industrious, they likely began, with sharp stones and pointed sticks, to grow a few vegetables or roots around their huts. But it was a long time before they knew how to prepare grain or had the tools needed to raise it in any quantity. Farming also requires accepting a present loss for the sake of a future gain, a calculation entirely foreign to the savage mind, since, as I have said, the savage hardly foresees in the morning what he will need by nightfall.

The invention of other arts must therefore have been necessary to force humanity to take up agriculture. As soon as workers were needed to smelt and forge iron, others were needed to feed them. The more hands were employed in manufacturing, the fewer remained to supply the common food, even though the number of mouths to be fed stayed the same. And since some wanted goods in exchange for their iron, the rest eventually discovered how to make iron serve to multiply the goods of the earth. In this way the arts of husbandry and agriculture took root on one side, and on the other the art of working metals and extending their uses.

The cultivation of the earth inevitably led to its division, and property, once recognized, gave rise to the first rules of justice: to secure to each person what was his, each person first had to be able to have something. Moreover, as people began to look toward the future, and as everyone now had something to lose, each had reason to fear that any injury he did to another would be returned in kind. This account of the origin of property is all the more natural because it is impossible to conceive how property could arise from anything but manual labor. What else can a man add to things he did not originally create, so as to make them his own? It is the farmer's labor alone that, by giving him a title to the produce of the ground he has tilled, also gives him a claim to the land itself, at least until the harvest. Repeated from year to year, this becomes continuous possession, and continuous possession is easily transformed into property. When the ancients, Grotius tells us, gave Ceres, the goddess of grain, the title of Lawgiver, and named a festival held in her honor the Thesmophoria, a name that refers to the giving of laws, they meant that the division of land had produced a new kind of right: the right of property, which differs from any right that can be derived from the law of nature.

In this state of affairs, equality might have been maintained if the talents of individuals had been equal, and if, for example, the use of iron and the consumption of foodstuffs had always balanced each other exactly. But since nothing preserved this balance, it was soon upset. The strongest did the most work; the most skillful made the best use of his labor; the most ingenious found ways to reduce his labor. The farmer needed more iron, or the smith more grain, and though both worked equally hard, one earned a great deal while the other could barely support himself. In this way natural inequality imperceptibly unfolds alongside the inequality produced by circumstance, and the differences among men, sharpened by their different situations, become more noticeable and more lasting in their effects, and begin to shape the fortunes of individuals in the same proportion.

Once matters had reached this point, the rest is easy to imagine. I will not detain the reader with an account of the successive invention of other arts, the development of language, the testing and employment of talents, the inequality of fortunes, the use and abuse of wealth, and all the details connected with them, which the reader can readily supply for himself. I will confine myself to a brief look at humanity in this new situation.

Here, then, are all the human faculties developed: memory and imagination fully at work, self-love engaged, reason active, and the mind close to the highest point of perfection of which it is capable. Here are all the natural qualities set in motion, and every person's rank and condition fixed, not only by his share of property and his power to help or harm others, but also by his wit, beauty, strength, or skill, his merit or his talents. Since these were the only qualities capable of commanding respect, it soon became necessary either to possess them or to pretend to.

It was now in people's interest to appear to be what they were not. Being and seeming became two entirely different things, and from this division arose arrogant display and deceitful cunning, together with all the vices that follow in their wake. At the same time, people who had once been free and independent were now, through a host of new needs, made subject to the whole of nature, and above all to one another. Each became in some measure a slave even as he became the master of others. If rich, he needed their services; if poor, their help; and even a middling condition did not allow anyone to do without others. A man must therefore have been constantly occupied in getting others to take an interest in his fortunes, and in making them find, or at least seem to find, their own advantage in advancing his. This required him to be sly and calculating with some and overbearing and harsh with others, under a kind of necessity to mistreat everyone he depended on whenever he could not frighten them into compliance and did not judge it worth his while to be useful to them. Insatiable ambition, the craving to raise one's fortune less out of genuine need than out of the desire to outdo others, filled everyone with a base inclination to harm one another, and with a hidden jealousy that is all the more dangerous because it wears the mask of goodwill in order to achieve its ends more safely. In short, there arose rivalry and competition on one side and conflicting interests on the other, and on both sides a concealed desire to profit at someone else's expense. All these evils were the first effects of property and the inseparable companions of growing inequality.

Before the invention of tokens to represent wealth, riches could consist of little besides land and livestock, the only real possessions human beings can have. But once inherited estates had grown so numerous and so large that they covered the whole of the land and bordered on one another, a man could enlarge his holdings only at another's expense. Meanwhile, the surplus population, those who had been too weak or too idle to acquire land, had grown poor without having lost anything, because while everything around them changed, they alone stayed the same. They were forced either to receive their living from the rich or to steal it from them, and from this there soon arose, depending on the characters involved, domination and servitude, or violence and plunder. The wealthy, for their part, had no sooner tasted the pleasure of command than they scorned every other pleasure. Using the slaves they already had to acquire new ones, they thought of nothing but subduing and enslaving their neighbors, like ravenous wolves that, having once tasted human flesh, refuse all other food and from then on hunt only men.

So, as the most powerful and the most wretched each regarded their strength or their misery as a kind of right to the possessions of others, equivalent in their minds to the right of property, the breakdown of equality was followed by the most terrible disorder. The usurpations of the rich, the robberies of the poor, and the unchecked passions of both stifled the cries of natural compassion and the still faint voice of justice, and filled people with greed, ambition, and vice. Between the claim of the strongest and the claim of the first occupant there arose endless conflicts, which never ended except in battle and bloodshed. The newborn society thus gave way to a horrible state of war. People so harried and corrupted could no longer retrace their steps or renounce the fatal gains they had made, and by misusing the very faculties that do them honor, they worked only toward their own undoing and brought themselves to the brink of ruin.

*Attonitus novitate mali, divesque miserque,*
*Effugere optat opes; et quæ modô voverat odit.*

Stunned by this strange new evil, at once rich and wretched, he longs to escape his wealth, and hates what he had only lately prayed for.

It is impossible that people should not eventually have reflected on so miserable a situation and on the disasters that were overwhelming them. The rich in particular must have felt how much they suffered from a perpetual state of war in which they bore all the costs, and in which, though everyone risked his life, they alone risked their property as well. Besides, however plausibly they might disguise their usurpations, they knew these rested on precarious and false titles, so that if others took from them by force what they themselves had gained by force, they would have no grounds for complaint. Even those who had grown rich through their own industry could hardly base their ownership on better claims. It was useless for them to say, "I built this well; I earned this spot by my own work." Someone might answer: Who gave you your standing here? And by what right do you demand that we pay you for work we never asked you to do? Do you not know that a multitude of your fellow human beings are starving for lack of what you have in excess? You would have needed the express and unanimous consent of the human race before taking for yourself more of the common means of life than you needed to live. Lacking valid reasons to justify himself and sufficient strength to defend himself; able to crush any individual with ease, yet easily crushed himself by a band of robbers; alone against all; and unable, because of mutual jealousy, to unite with his equals against numerous enemies bound together by the common hope of plunder, the rich man, driven by necessity, at last conceived the most cunning plan that ever entered the human mind. It was to turn to his own advantage the very forces that threatened him, to make allies of his adversaries, to instill in them different principles, and to give them other institutions, as favorable to himself as the law of nature had been unfavorable.

With this aim, after describing to his neighbors the horror of a situation that armed every man against all the others, that made their possessions as burdensome to them as their needs, and in which no one could find safety in either wealth or poverty, he readily devised persuasive arguments to win them over to his design. "Let us unite," he said, "to protect the weak from oppression, restrain the ambitious, and secure to every man the possession of what belongs to him. Let us establish rules of justice and peace that all, without exception, will be obliged to obey, rules that may in some measure make up for the whims of fortune by binding the powerful and the weak alike to mutual obligations. In short, instead of turning our strength against one another, let us gather it into a supreme power that will govern us by wise laws, protect and defend every member of the association, drive off our common enemies, and preserve lasting harmony among us."

Far fewer words to this effect would have been enough to deceive men so crude and so easily led, especially since they had too many quarrels among themselves to do without arbitrators, and too much ambition and greed to go long without masters. They all rushed headlong into their chains, believing they were securing their liberty. They had just enough intelligence to see the advantages of political institutions, but not enough experience to foresee their dangers. Those best able to foresee the dangers were precisely the people who expected to profit from them, and even the most prudent judged it reasonable to sacrifice one part of their freedom to preserve the rest, as a wounded man has his arm cut off to save the rest of his body.

Such was, or may well have been, the origin of society and of law. They bound the poor with new fetters and gave the rich new powers. They destroyed natural liberty beyond recovery, fixed the law of property and inequality for all time, turned shrewd usurpation into an unalterable right, and, for the benefit of a few ambitious individuals, subjected the entire human race to perpetual labor, servitude, and misery. It is easy to see how the founding of one community made the founding of all the others necessary, and how, in order to stand against united forces, the rest of humanity had to unite in turn. Societies quickly multiplied and spread across the earth, until there was scarcely a corner of the world where a man could escape the yoke and draw his head out from beneath the sword he saw forever hanging over him by a thread. Civil law having thus become the common rule among the members of each community, the law of nature kept its place only between different communities. There, under the name of the law of nations, it was tempered by certain unspoken agreements meant to make commerce possible and to stand in for natural compassion. For compassion, when applied to whole societies, lost nearly all the influence it had exerted over individuals, and survived only in a few great cosmopolitan spirits who break through the imaginary barriers that divide one people from another and, following the example of the Sovereign Creator, embrace the whole human race in their benevolence.

But political bodies, remaining in a state of nature with respect to one another, soon felt the same disadvantages that had forced individuals to leave it, and this state proved even more fatal to these great bodies than it had been to the individuals who composed them. From it arose national wars, battles, murders, and reprisals that shock nature and offend reason, along with all those dreadful prejudices that rank the shedding of human blood among the virtues and the marks of honor. The most distinguished men learned to regard cutting one another's throats as a duty. In the end, people slaughtered their fellow human beings by the thousands without even knowing why, and committed more murders in a single day of battle, and more atrocities in the sack of a single town, than had been committed in the state of nature over whole ages across the entire earth. Such were the first visible effects of the division of humanity into separate communities. But let us return to their institutions.

I know that some writers have explained the origin of political societies differently, attributing it either to conquest by the powerful or to an alliance of the weak. Which of these causes we choose makes no difference to my argument. The account I have just given nonetheless seems to me the most natural, for the following reasons. First, in the case of conquest, the so-called right of conquest is no right at all and so could not serve as the foundation for any other right. Conqueror and conquered would remain in a state of war with each other unless the conquered people, restored to full possession of their liberty, freely chose their conqueror as their leader. Until then, whatever terms of surrender might have been agreed upon, being founded on violence, would be void by that very fact, and on this hypothesis there could be neither a true society nor a body politic, nor any law but that of the strongest. Second, in the case of an alliance of the weak, the words "strong" and "weak" are ambiguous. In the period between the establishment of a right of property, or first occupancy, and the establishment of political government, these words are better expressed by "rich" and "poor," because, before laws were instituted, people had no other means of bringing their equals into submission than by attacking their goods or by granting them a share of their own. Third, since the poor had nothing to lose but their freedom, it would have been utterly absurd for them to give up voluntarily the only good they still possessed and receive nothing in return. The rich, by contrast, were sensitive, so to speak, in every part of their possessions, so it was far easier to harm them and therefore far more necessary for them to take precautions against harm. And, finally, it is more reasonable to suppose that a thing was invented by those it would benefit than by those it was bound to injure.

In its infancy, government had no regular or settled form. Lacking experience and philosophy, people could see only the inconveniences immediately before them, and they thought of guarding against others only as these arose. Despite the efforts of the wisest lawgivers, the political order remained imperfect, because it was little more than the product of chance. Having begun badly, it never had its original faults corrected, even as time revealed its defects and suggested remedies. It was constantly being patched, when the first task should have been to clear the ground and haul away all the old materials, as Lycurgus did at Sparta, if a sound and lasting structure was to be built. At first, society consisted of nothing more than a few general agreements that every member pledged to observe, with the whole community standing as guarantor to each individual that these agreements would be kept. Only experience could reveal how weak such a constitution was, and how easily it could be violated without punishment, since it was hard to convict anyone of wrongdoing when the public alone was to be both witness and judge. The laws were bound to be evaded in countless ways, and disorders and difficulties were bound to multiply, until it became necessary to entrust the dangerous charge of public authority to private persons, and to give magistrates the task of enforcing obedience to the decisions of the people. For to claim that leaders were chosen before the community was formed, and that the administrators of the laws existed before the laws themselves, is too absurd a supposition to be taken seriously.

It would be equally unreasonable to suppose that people began by throwing themselves irrevocably and unconditionally into the arms of an absolute master, and that the first remedy proud and unconquered men devised for their common safety was to rush headlong into slavery. Why, after all, did they give themselves superiors at all, if not to defend themselves against oppression and to protect their lives, their liberties, and their property, which are, so to speak, the constituent elements of their being? In relations between one person and another, the worst that can happen is to find oneself at another's mercy. It would therefore have defied common sense to begin by handing over to a leader the very things they needed his help to preserve. What could he have offered them in exchange for so great a right? And had he dared to demand it on the pretext of defending them, would he not have received the answer recorded in the fable: "What more can the enemy do to us?" It is therefore beyond dispute, and indeed the fundamental principle of all political right, that peoples have set up leaders to protect their liberty and not to enslave them. "If we have a prince," Pliny said to Trajan, "it is so that we may be spared from having a master."

Political theorists commit the same fallacy about the love of liberty that philosophers commit about the state of nature. They judge things very different from anything they have seen by what they happen to see, and they credit human beings with a natural inclination to servitude because the slaves within their view bear the yoke patiently. They fail to consider that liberty is like innocence and virtue: its value is known only to those who possess it, and the taste for it vanishes the moment it is lost. When a Persian satrap, a provincial governor, compared life in Sparta unfavorably with life in Persepolis, the Spartan general Brasidas replied, "I know the delights of your country, but you cannot know the pleasures of mine."

An unbroken horse raises its mane, paws the ground, and rears back violently at the sight of the bridle, while a horse that has been properly trained patiently submits even to whip and spur. In the same way, the savage will not bend his neck to the yoke that civilized man accepts without complaint, and prefers the most turbulent liberty to the most peaceful slavery. We cannot, therefore, judge humanity's natural disposition toward or against slavery from the servility of peoples already enslaved. We should judge it instead by the tremendous efforts every free people makes to save itself from oppression. I know that the enslaved never tire of praising the tranquillity they enjoy in their chains, and that they call a state of wretched servitude a state of peace: *miserrimam servitutem pacem appellant*, "they give the name of peace to the most miserable slavery." But when I see free peoples sacrificing pleasure, peace, wealth, power, and life itself to preserve the one treasure so scorned by those who have lost it; when I see animals born free dash their heads against the bars of their cages out of an innate impatience with captivity; when I see multitudes of naked savages who despise European pleasures braving hunger, fire, the sword, and death simply to preserve their independence, I feel that it is not for slaves to reason about liberty.

As for paternal authority, from which some writers have derived absolute government and indeed all society, it is enough, without returning to the opposing arguments of Locke and Sidney, to note several points. Nothing on earth is further removed from the savage spirit of despotism than the gentleness of an authority that looks more to the good of the one who obeys than to the good of the one who commands. By the law of nature, the father is master of the child only so long as the child needs his help. After that, the two are equal: the son becomes entirely independent of the father and owes him respect, not obedience. Gratitude is a duty that ought to be rendered, not a right that can be demanded. So rather than saying that civil society derives from paternal authority, we should say that paternal authority draws its chief force from civil society. No individual was ever recognized as the father of many people until his sons and daughters remained settled around him. The father's goods, of which he is truly the master, are the ties that keep his children dependent on him, and he may, if he chooses, give them no share of his property unless they earn it through constant deference to his wishes. The subjects of an arbitrary despot, however, are so far from having any such favor to expect from their ruler that they themselves and everything they own are his property, or at least are treated by him as such. They are forced to accept as a favor whatever small portion of their own he chooses to leave them. When he strips them of their goods, he is merely doing justice, and when he allows them to live, he is showing mercy.

If we go on in this way, testing fact against right, we will find no more reason than truth in the idea that tyranny was established voluntarily. It would also be no easy matter to prove the validity of a contract that binds only one of the parties, in which all the risk lies on one side and none on the other, so that the only person who could suffer from it is the one who bound himself. Even in modern times, this odious system is far from being that of wise and good monarchs, and especially of the kings of France, as several passages in their edicts show. A notable example is the following passage from a celebrated edict published in 1667 in the name and by order of Louis XIV:

"Let it not be said, therefore, that the Sovereign is not subject to the laws of his State, since the contrary is a true principle of the law of nations, one that flattery has sometimes attacked but that good princes have always defended as the guardian deity of their realms. How much more legitimate is it to say, with the wise Plato, that the perfect happiness of a kingdom consists in the obedience of subjects to their prince, and of the prince to the laws, and in the laws being just and always directed toward the public good!"

I will not pause here to ask whether, since liberty is the noblest faculty of human beings, it is not a degradation of our very nature, a lowering of ourselves to the level of beasts enslaved to instinct, and even an insult to the Author of our being, to renounce without reservation the most precious of all His gifts, and to submit to the necessity of committing every crime He has forbidden, merely to gratify a mad or cruel master; nor whether this sublime craftsman ought not to be less angered at seeing His work destroyed altogether than at seeing it so dishonored. I will set aside, if my opponents wish, the authority of Barbeyrac, who, following Locke, flatly declares that no one can sell his liberty so far as to submit to an arbitrary power that may treat him however it pleases, since, he adds, this would amount to selling his own life, of which he is not the master. I ask only this: what right could those who were unafraid to degrade themselves in this way have had to subject their descendants to the same disgrace, and to renounce on their behalf blessings that those descendants do not owe to the generosity of their ancestors, and without which life itself must be a burden to everyone worthy of it?

Pufendorf argues that we may give up our liberty in favor of other people just as we transfer our property to one another by contracts and agreements. This seems a very weak argument. In the first place, the property I give away becomes entirely foreign to me, and I cannot suffer from its misuse; but it concerns me intimately that my liberty not be abused, and I cannot expose myself to becoming an instrument of crime without taking on the guilt of the crimes I may be forced to commit. Moreover, since the right of property is merely a convention of human making, people may dispose of what they own as they see fit. The same is not true of nature's essential gifts, such as life and liberty, which every man is permitted to enjoy, and which it is at the very least doubtful that anyone has the right to give up. By surrendering liberty we degrade our being; by surrendering life we do our best to annihilate it; and since no worldly good can compensate us for the loss of either, it would be an offense against both reason and nature to renounce them at any price whatsoever. But even if we could transfer our liberty as we transfer our property, there would be a great difference where children are concerned. Children enjoy their father's wealth only because his right passes down to them, whereas liberty is a gift they hold from nature by virtue of being human, and their parents have no right whatsoever to strip them of it. Just as establishing slavery required doing violence to nature, so perpetuating such a right would require changing nature itself. The jurists who have solemnly ruled that the child of a slave is born a slave have, in effect, ruled that a human being is born something other than human.

I take it as certain, then, that government did not begin with arbitrary power. Arbitrary power is rather the corruption of government, its furthest extremity, and it finally returns government to nothing more than the law of the strongest, which government was originally designed to remedy. But even supposing government had begun this way, such power, being illegitimate in itself, could not have provided a foundation for the laws of society, nor, therefore, for the inequality those laws established.

Without entering here into the inquiries that still remain to be made into the nature of the fundamental compact underlying all government, I will content myself with adopting the common view of it, and treat the establishment of the political body as a genuine contract between the people and the leaders they have chosen: a contract by which both parties bind themselves to observe the laws it sets out, which form the bonds of their union. Since the people, with respect to their social relations, have merged all their wills into one, the various articles in which this will is expressed become so many fundamental laws, binding on every member of the State without exception. One of these articles governs the selection and powers of the magistrates appointed to oversee the execution of the rest. This power extends to everything that may preserve the constitution, but stops short of altering it. It is accompanied by honors, so that the laws and those who administer them will command respect, and the officials themselves are granted personal privileges to reward them for the cares and labor that good administration requires. The magistrate, for his part, binds himself to use the power entrusted to him only in keeping with the intentions of those who granted it, to secure each of them in the peaceful possession of what is theirs, and on every occasion to put the public interest ahead of his own.

Before experience had revealed the unavoidable abuses of such a constitution, or knowledge of the human heart had allowed men to foresee them, it must have seemed all the more excellent because those charged with preserving it had the greatest stake in doing so. Since the magistracy and its attendant rights rested entirely on the fundamental laws, the magistrates would lose their legitimacy the moment those laws ceased to exist; the people would no longer owe them obedience; and because it is the laws, not the magistrates, that are essential to the existence of a State, its members would recover their right to natural liberty.

If we reflect on this subject with even a little attention, we will find further arguments to confirm this truth, and we will be convinced, from the very nature of the contract, that it cannot be irrevocable. For if there were no higher power able to guarantee the good faith of the contracting parties or to compel them to fulfill their mutual obligations, each party would be the sole judge in its own case, and each would always have the right to renounce the contract as soon as it found that the other had broken its terms, or that those terms no longer suited it. It is on this principle that the right of abdication may perhaps be founded. Now, if we consider only the human side of this institution, as we are doing here, it is certain that if the magistrate, who holds all the power and takes for himself all the advantages of the contract, nonetheless has the right to renounce his authority, then the people, who pay for all their leader's faults, must have a far better right to renounce their dependence. Yet the terrible and countless quarrels and disorders that such a dangerous privilege would inevitably produce show, more clearly than anything else, how badly human governments needed a firmer foundation than reason alone, and how necessary it was for public peace that the divine will should intervene to give sovereign authority a sacred and inviolable character, depriving subjects of the fatal right to dispose of it. If religion had brought the world no other benefit, this alone would oblige men to embrace and cultivate it, abuses and all, since it has saved more blood than fanaticism has ever spilled. But let us return to the thread of our hypothesis.

The various forms of government owe their origin to the differing degrees of inequality among individuals at the moment those governments were instituted. Where one man stood out above the rest in power, virtue, wealth, or personal influence, he was made sole magistrate, and the State became a monarchy. Where several men of roughly equal standing rose above the rest, they were elected together and formed an aristocracy. And among a people who had strayed less from the state of nature, and whose fortunes and talents were less unequal, supreme authority was held in common, and a democracy was formed. Over time it became clear which of these forms suited men best. Some peoples remained subject only to the laws; others soon came to obey their magistrates instead. Citizens worked to preserve their liberty, while subjects, galled at seeing others enjoy a blessing they themselves had lost, thought only of enslaving their neighbors. In short, on one side arose riches and conquests, and on the other, happiness and virtue.

Under these various governments, all offices were at first elective. Where wealth carried no weight, preference went to merit, which confers a natural authority, and to age, which brings experience in affairs and deliberation in council. The Elders of the Hebrews, the Gerontes of Sparta, the Senate of Rome, and the very origin of our word *seigneur*, which comes from the Latin for "elder," all show how highly old age was once revered. But the more often the choice fell on old men, the more often elections had to be held, and the more troublesome they became. Intrigues began, factions formed, partisan feeling turned bitter, and civil wars broke out; the lives of individuals were sacrificed to the supposed happiness of the State, and at last men stood on the verge of relapsing into their original anarchy.
