Ambitious leaders took advantage of these circumstances to make their offices permanent within their own families, while the people, already accustomed to dependence, ease, and the comforts of life, and already unable to break their chains, consented to deepen their servitude for the sake of their tranquility. So it was that magistrates, once their offices became hereditary, grew used to regarding those offices as family property, and themselves as the owners of communities whose mere officers they had originally been. They came to treat their fellow citizens as slaves, to count them like cattle among their possessions, and to call themselves the equals of the gods and kings of kings.

If we trace the progress of inequality through these various upheavals, we find that its first stage was the establishment of laws and of the right of property; its second, the institution of magistracy; and its third and last, the transformation of legitimate power into arbitrary power. The condition of rich and poor was sanctioned by the first stage, that of powerful and weak by the second, and only by the third that of master and slave. This is the final degree of inequality, the point at which all the others ultimately arrive and remain, until the government is either dissolved entirely by new revolutions or restored to legitimacy.

To see why this progression was inevitable, we must consider not so much the motives behind the founding of the body politic as the forms it actually takes and the defects that necessarily accompany it, for the very flaws that make social institutions necessary also make their abuse unavoidable. Setting aside Sparta, where the laws were chiefly concerned with the education of children, and where Lycurgus instilled such moral habits that laws became almost unnecessary (for laws, being generally weaker than the passions, restrain men without changing them), it would not be hard to prove that any government which faithfully fulfilled the purposes for which it was founded, and guarded carefully against change and corruption, was established needlessly. A country in which no one evaded the laws or abused the powers of office would need neither laws nor magistrates.

Political distinctions inevitably give rise to civil distinctions. The growing inequality between the leaders and the people soon makes itself felt among individuals, and is modified in a thousand ways by passions, talents, and circumstances. The magistrate could not seize any illegitimate power without elevating the creatures with whom he had to share it. Besides, individuals allow themselves to be oppressed only insofar as they are driven by blind ambition; looking downward rather than upward, they come to prize domination over independence, and submit to servitude so that they may in turn enslave others. It is no easy thing to force into obedience a man who has no wish to command, and the shrewdest politician could never enslave a people whose only desire was to be independent. But inequality spreads easily among cowardly and ambitious souls, who are always ready to take their chances with fortune and are almost indifferent whether they command or obey, depending on whether fortune favors or frowns on them. There must therefore have been a time when the people's eyes were so dazzled that their rulers had only to say to the humblest of men, "Be great, you and all your descendants," for him to appear great at once in everyone's eyes as well as in his own. His descendants exalted themselves still further the more distant they were from him: the more obscure and uncertain the cause, the greater the effect, and the more idlers a family could count among its members, the more illustrious it was considered.

If this were the place for details, I could easily explain how, even without the intervention of government, inequality of standing and authority became unavoidable among private individuals as soon as their union in a single society led them to compare themselves with one another and to take account of the differences they discovered through the constant dealings each man had to have with his neighbors. These differences are of several kinds, but since wealth, nobility or rank, power, and personal merit are the chief distinctions by which people judge one another in society, I could prove that the harmony or conflict among these different forces is the surest sign of whether a State is well or badly constituted. I could show that of these four kinds of inequality, although personal qualities are the origin of all the others, wealth is the one to which they are all reduced in the end; for since riches contribute most directly to individual well-being and are the easiest to transfer, they are used to buy every other distinction. This observation allows us to judge fairly precisely how far a people has strayed from its original constitution, and how far it has advanced toward the final stage of corruption. I could explain how this universal craving for reputation, honors, and advancement, which consumes us all, exercises our faculties and powers and sets them in comparison with one another; how it stirs up and multiplies our passions; and how, by turning all men into competitors and rivals, or rather enemies, it causes countless failures, successes, and upheavals of every kind by setting so many contenders running the same race. I could show that it is to this desire to be talked about, this relentless urge to distinguish ourselves, that we owe both the best and the worst of what we have: our virtues and our vices, our sciences and our errors, our conquerors and our philosophers, which is to say a great many bad things and very few good ones. In short, I could prove that if we see a handful of rich and powerful men at the summit of fortune and grandeur while the multitude grovels in poverty and obscurity, it is because the former value what they enjoy only insofar as others are deprived of it, and because, without any change in their own condition, they would cease to be happy the moment the people ceased to be miserable.

These details alone, however, would supply material for a substantial work, in which one could weigh the advantages and disadvantages of every form of government in relation to man in the state of nature, and at the same time lay bare all the different guises in which inequality has appeared up to now, or may appear in ages to come, according to the nature of the various governments and the changes that time must inevitably bring about in them. We would then see the multitude oppressed from within as a result of the very precautions it had taken against foreign tyranny. We would see oppression steadily gaining ground, without the oppressed being able to know where it would stop or what legitimate means remained to them for halting its advance. We would see the rights of citizens and the freedom of nations gradually extinguished, and the complaints, protests, and appeals of the weak dismissed as seditious grumbling. We would see statecraft confine the honor of defending the common cause to a mercenary portion of the people. We would see taxes made necessary by this arrangement, and the disheartened farmer abandoning his fields even in peacetime, leaving the plow to strap on the sword. We would see deadly and capricious codes of honor established, and the champions of the country sooner or later becoming its enemies, forever holding their daggers to the breasts of their fellow citizens. A time would come when they could be heard saying to the oppressor of their country, in the words the poet Lucan gives to a Roman soldier pledging himself to Caesar:

*Pectore si fratris gladium juguloque parentis*
*Condere me jubeas, gravidæque in viscera partu*
*Conjugis, invitâ peragam tamen omnia dextrâ.*

"Should you order me to bury my sword in my brother's breast, in my father's throat, or in the womb of my pregnant wife, I would carry out every command, though my hand were unwilling."

From vast inequalities of fortune and condition, from the great variety of passions and talents, of useless and harmful arts, and of empty sciences, would arise a host of prejudices equally opposed to reason, happiness, and virtue. We would see the magistrates encouraging everything that might weaken men united in society by fostering dissension among them: everything that might sow the seeds of real division while lending society an appearance of harmony, and everything that might inspire the different ranks of people with mutual hatred and distrust by setting the rights and interests of one against those of another, thereby strengthening the power that held them all.

It is out of this disorder and these upheavals that despotism, gradually raising its hideous head and devouring whatever remained sound and uncorrupted in any part of the State, would finally trample both the laws and the people underfoot and establish itself on the ruins of the republic. The period immediately preceding this final transformation would be one of turmoil and calamity, but in the end the monster would swallow everything, and the people would no longer have leaders or laws, only tyrants. From that moment on, there would be no question of morals or virtue. Despotism, *cui ex honesto nulla est spes*, under which honest conduct offers no hope of anything, tolerates no other master wherever it reigns. The moment it speaks, integrity and duty lose all their force, and blind obedience is the only virtue left for slaves to practice.

This is the final stage of inequality, the extreme point that closes the circle and meets the point from which we began. Here all private individuals become equal once again, because they are nothing; and since subjects have no law but their master's will, and the master no restraint but his own passions, every notion of goodness and every principle of justice vanishes once more. Here we return completely to the law of the strongest, and so to a new state of nature, different from the one we started from, because that was the state of nature in its original purity, while this one is the product of extreme corruption. In every other respect there is so little difference between the two states, and despotism so thoroughly dissolves the contract of government, that the despot is master only as long as he remains the strongest; as soon as he can be driven out, he has no grounds to complain of violence. The popular uprising that ends with a sultan's death or overthrow is just as lawful as the acts by which, only the day before, he disposed of the lives and fortunes of his subjects. As force alone kept him in power, force alone brings him down. Everything thus proceeds according to the natural order, and whatever the outcome of these frequent and sudden revolutions, no one has cause to complain of another's injustice, only of his own misfortune or imprudence.

If the reader thus discovers and retraces the lost and forgotten road by which man must have passed from the state of nature to the state of society; if he carefully restores, along with the intermediate stages I have just described, those I have had to omit for lack of time or that my imagination failed to supply, he cannot help being struck by the immense distance that separates the two states. It is in tracing this slow succession that he will find the answer to a number of moral and political problems that philosophers cannot resolve. He will sense that, because men differ from one age to another, the reason Diogenes, searching with his lamp, could not find a man was that he was looking among his contemporaries for a man of an earlier time. He will see that Cato perished with Rome and with liberty because he was out of place in his own age; the greatest of men only astonished a world that he would surely have ruled had he lived five hundred years earlier. In short, he will understand how the human soul and its passions imperceptibly alter their very nature; why our needs and our pleasures eventually fasten on new objects; and why, as original man gradually disappeared, society now presents us with nothing but an assembly of artificial men and contrived passions, which are the product of all these new relationships and have no real foundation in nature. On this subject, reflection teaches us nothing that observation does not fully confirm.

The savage and the civilized man differ so profoundly in their hearts and inclinations that what would bring supreme happiness to one would drive the other to despair. The savage longs only for peace and freedom; he wants only to live and to be spared labor, and even the *ataraxia* of the Stoics, their ideal of a perfectly untroubled mind, falls far short of his deep indifference to everything else. Civilized man, by contrast, is forever bustling, sweating, toiling, and racking his brains in search of still more laborious occupations. He drudges on to his final moment; he even courts death in order to put himself in a position to live, or renounces life to win immortality. He pays court to powerful men whom he hates and to rich men whom he despises; he will stop at nothing for the honor of serving them; he is not ashamed to pride himself on his own servility and on their patronage; and, proud of his slavery, he speaks with contempt of those who do not share the honor of it. What a spectacle the bewildering and envied labors of a European minister of state would present to the eyes of a Carib! How many cruel deaths would this easygoing savage not prefer to the horrors of such a life, which is rarely sweetened even by the pleasure of doing good! Yet for him to grasp the motives behind all this anxious striving, the words *power* and *reputation* would have to mean something to him. He would need to know that there are men who place great value on the opinion of the rest of the world, and who can be made happy and content with themselves on the testimony of others rather than on their own. The true source of all these differences is that the savage lives within himself, while social man lives always outside himself and knows how to live only in the opinion of others, so that he seems to derive the very sense of his own existence from their judgment of him. It is not my present purpose to dwell on the indifference to good and evil that arises from this disposition, in spite of all our fine treatises on morality; or to show how, with everything reduced to appearances, honor, friendship, virtue, and often vice itself become mere artifice and play-acting, until at last we learn the secret of boasting even of our vices; or to show, in short, how, always asking others what we are and never daring to ask ourselves, in the midst of so much philosophy, humanity, and civilization, and such lofty codes of morality, we have nothing to show for ourselves but a frivolous and deceitful appearance: honor without virtue, reason without wisdom, and pleasure without happiness. It is enough that I have proved that this is by no means the original state of man, and that it is only the spirit of society, together with the inequality society produces, that transforms and distorts all our natural inclinations in this way.

I have tried to trace the origin and progress of inequality, and the founding and abuse of political societies, as far as these can be deduced from the nature of man by the light of reason alone, independently of the sacred dogmas that give sovereign authority the sanction of divine right. Two conclusions follow from this survey. First, since there is hardly any inequality in the state of nature, all the inequality that now prevails owes its strength and growth to the development of our faculties and the advance of the human mind, and finally becomes permanent and legitimate through the establishment of property and laws. Second, moral inequality, authorized by positive law alone, conflicts with natural right whenever it is not proportionate to physical inequality. This distinction settles what we ought to think of the kind of inequality that prevails in every civilized country. However the law of nature is defined, it is plainly contrary to it that children should command old men, that fools should rule the wise, and that a privileged few should gorge themselves on superfluities while the starving multitude lacks the bare necessities of life.

Distributive justice would itself oppose the rigorous equality of the state of nature, even if such equality were practicable in civil society. Since every member of the State owes it services in proportion to his talents and abilities, each ought in turn to be distinguished and favored in proportion to the services he has actually rendered. This is the sense in which we must understand the passage of Isocrates praising the early Athenians for having determined which of two kinds of equality was more useful: the kind that divides the same advantages indiscriminately among all citizens, or the kind that distributes them to each according to his merits. These skillful statesmen, the orator adds, banished the unjust equality that draws no distinction between good men and bad, and held fast to the equality that rewards and punishes every man according to what he deserves.

But in the first place, no society has ever existed, however corrupt some may have become, in which no distinction was made between the good and the bad. And where morality is concerned, the law cannot prescribe measures exact enough to give a magistrate a practical rule. It is therefore with great prudence that the law, so as not to leave the fortunes or standing of citizens to the magistrate's discretion, forbids him to pass judgment on persons and confines him to judging actions. Only morals as pure as those of the ancient Romans can bear censors; such a tribunal among us would throw everything into confusion. The difference between good and bad men is determined by public esteem. The magistrate is strictly a judge of law alone, whereas the public is the truest judge of morals, and on this point it has such integrity and insight that, though it may sometimes be deceived, it can never be corrupted. The rank of citizens ought therefore to be regulated not by their personal merit, which would let the magistrate apply the law almost arbitrarily, but by the actual services they have done the State, which can be estimated far more exactly.

### Appendix

A famous author, tallying up the good and evil of human life and comparing the totals, finds that our pains greatly exceed our pleasures, so that, all things considered, human life is hardly a valuable gift. His conclusion does not surprise me, for he drew all his arguments from man in civilization. Had he gone back to the state of nature, his inquiry would clearly have reached a different result, and man would have been seen to suffer very few evils not of his own making. It has cost us no small effort to make ourselves as wretched as we are. Consider, on one side, the immense labors of mankind: the many sciences brought to perfection, the arts invented, the forces put to work, the chasms filled in, the mountains leveled, the rocks shattered, the rivers made navigable, the land cleared, the lakes emptied, the marshes drained, the enormous structures raised on land, and the crowded ships that cover the sea. Then weigh, with even a little thought, the real benefits all these works have brought to mankind. We cannot help being astonished at the vast disproportion between the two, and lamenting the folly of man, who, to gratify his foolish pride and empty self-admiration, eagerly pursues every misery he is capable of feeling, though a kindly nature had placed them out of his way.

That men are in fact wicked, long and sad experience proves beyond doubt; yet I believe I have shown that man is naturally good. What, then, can have corrupted him so thoroughly, if not the changes in his constitution, the advances he has made, and the knowledge he has acquired? We may admire human society as much as we like; it remains true that society necessarily leads men to hate one another in proportion as their interests clash, and to render one another apparent services while actually doing every imaginable harm. What are we to think of a relationship in which each individual's interest dictates rules directly opposed to those that public reason dictates to the community as a whole, and in which every man profits from his neighbor's misfortune? There is perhaps no man of comfortable means without greedy heirs, and perhaps even children, secretly wishing for his death; no ship at sea whose loss would not be good news to some merchant; no house that some dishonest debtor would not gladly see burned to ashes along with all the papers it contains; no nation that does not rejoice at the disasters that befall its neighbors. So it is that we find our advantage in the misfortunes of our fellow creatures, and that one man's loss almost always makes another man's prosperity. Worse still, public calamities are the object of the hopes and expectations of countless individuals. Some wish for sickness, some for mortality, some for war, and some for famine. I have seen men wicked enough to weep with sorrow at the prospect of a plentiful harvest. The great and fatal fire of London, which cost so many unhappy people their lives or their fortunes, made the fortunes of perhaps ten thousand others. I know that Montaigne criticizes Demades the Athenian for having punished a workman who sold his coffins at a high price and so profited handsomely from the deaths of his fellow citizens. But the reason Montaigne gives is that, by the same logic, everybody would have to be punished, and that reason plainly confirms my point. Let us look past the surface appearances of benevolence, then, and examine what happens in the inmost recesses of the heart. Let us consider what the state of things must be when men are forced to flatter and destroy one another at the same time, when they are born enemies by duty and swindlers by interest.

It may be said that society is so arranged that every man gains by serving others. That would be well enough, if he did not gain still more by injuring them. No legitimate profit is so great that it cannot be far exceeded by what can be made illegitimately; we always gain more by harming our neighbors than by doing them good. All that is required is knowing how to act with impunity, and to that end the powerful use all their strength and the weak all their cunning.

Savage man, once he has eaten, is at peace with all of nature and the friend of all his fellow creatures. If a dispute arises over a meal, he rarely comes to blows without first weighing the difficulty of defeating his opponent against the trouble of finding food elsewhere; and since pride plays no part, the matter ends with a few blows. The victor eats, the loser looks for food somewhere else, and peace returns. The case is entirely different for man in society. He must first provide himself with necessities, then superfluities; next come delicacies, then immense wealth, then subjects, and then slaves. He never enjoys a moment's rest. Stranger still, the less natural and pressing his wants, the more headstrong his passions, and, worse yet, the greater his power to gratify them. So after a long run of prosperity, after swallowing up treasures and ruining multitudes, the hero ends by cutting every throat until he finds himself at last sole master of the world. Such, in miniature, is the moral portrait, if not of human life, then at least of the secret ambitions of the civilized heart.

Compare impartially the condition of the citizen with that of the savage, and trace, if you can, how many doors the citizen has opened to pain and death beyond those of his vices, his wants, and his misfortunes. Reflect on the mental afflictions that prey on us, the violent passions that wear us down and exhaust us, the excessive labor that burdens the poor, and the still more dangerous idleness to which the rich abandon themselves, so that the poor die of want and the rich of excess. Reflect for a moment on the odd mixtures and harmful seasonings of our food, and the spoiled condition in which it is often eaten; on the adulteration of medicines, the tricks of those who sell them, the errors of those who administer them, and the poisonous vessels in which they are prepared; on the epidemics bred by foul air where great numbers of people are crowded together, or caused by our delicate habits of living, by our passing from heated houses into the open air and back again, by putting on or taking off our clothes too carelessly, and by all the precautions that sensuality has turned into necessary habits, so that neglecting them can cost us our life or health. Take into account the fires and earthquakes that consume or bury whole cities and kill their inhabitants by the thousands. In short, add together all the dangers these causes perpetually hold over us, and you will see how dearly nature makes us pay for the contempt with which we have treated her lessons.

I will not repeat here what I have said elsewhere about the calamities of war. I only wish that those with enough knowledge were willing, or bold enough, to make public the details of the villainies committed in armies by the contractors who supply provisions and run the hospitals. We would see plainly that their monstrous frauds, already poorly concealed, which cripple the finest armies in no time at all, kill more soldiers than the enemy's swords.

The number of people who die every year at sea, from famine, scurvy, pirates, fire, and shipwreck, offers material for another shocking calculation. We must also charge to the establishment of property, and therefore to the founding of society, all assassinations, poisonings, and highway robberies, and even the punishments inflicted on the wretches guilty of these crimes. Those punishments, though necessary to prevent greater evils, make the murder of one man cost the lives of two or more, and so double the loss to the human race.

What shameful methods are sometimes used to prevent the birth of human beings and to cheat nature! There are the brutal and depraved appetites that insult her most beautiful work, appetites unknown to savages or to animals, which can spring only from the corrupt imagination of people in civilized countries. There are secret abortions, the fitting fruits of debauchery and twisted notions of honor. There is the abandonment or murder of multitudes of infants who fall victim to their parents' poverty or their mothers' cruel shame. And finally there is the castration of unhappy wretches, part of whose existence, along with all hope of descendants, is sacrificed to idle singing or, worse still, to the brutal jealousy of other men; in this last case the mutilation becomes a double outrage against nature, both in the treatment of those who suffer it and in the use for which they are destined. But is it not a thousand times more common, and more dangerous, for paternal authority to offend openly against humanity? How many talents have been wasted, and how many inclinations forced, by the unwise constraint of fathers? How many men who would have distinguished themselves in a suitable calling have died dishonored and miserable in another for which they had no taste! How many happy but unequal marriages have been broken or troubled, and how many chaste wives dishonored, by a social order perpetually at odds with the order of nature! How many good and virtuous husbands and wives are punished by each other for having been badly matched! How many young and unhappy victims of their parents' greed sink into vice, or spend their melancholy days in tears, groaning in unbreakable bonds that their hearts reject and that gold alone has forged! Fortunate, sometimes, are those whose courage and virtue take them from life before inhuman violence forces them to spend it in crime or despair. Forgive me, father and mother, whom I shall always mourn: my complaint deepens your grief. But may your sorrows stand as an eternal and terrible example to everyone who dares, in the name of nature, to violate her most sacred right.

I have spoken only of the ill-fated unions produced by our social system; but should we suppose that those presided over by love and sympathy are free of harm? What if I set out to show humanity attacked at its very source, even in the most sacred of all bonds, where fortune is consulted before nature, and where, since the disorders of society confuse all virtue and vice, chastity becomes a criminal precaution and the refusal to give life to another human being an act of humanity? But without drawing back the veil that hides all these horrors, let us be content to point out the evil that others must remedy.

Add to all this the multitude of unhealthy trades that shorten men's lives or ruin their bodies, such as work in the mines and the processing of metals and minerals, particularly lead, copper, mercury, cobalt, and arsenic. Add the other dangerous trades that every day claim the lives of roofers, carpenters, masons, and miners. Put all these together, and we can see in the establishment and refinement of societies the causes of that decline in our numbers which many philosophers have observed.

Luxury, which cannot be prevented among people who cling to their own comfort and to the regard of others, soon completes the evil that society began. Under the pretext of giving bread to the poor, whom it should never have made poor in the first place, it impoverishes everyone else and sooner or later depopulates the State. Luxury is a remedy far worse than the disease it claims to cure; or rather, it is itself the greatest of all evils for every State, large or small. To support the hordes of servants and idlers it creates, it brings oppression and ruin upon the citizen and the laborer. It is like those scorching winds that cover trees and plants with devouring insects, rob useful animals of their food, and spread famine and death wherever they blow.

From society and the luxury it breeds arise the liberal and mechanical arts, commerce, letters, and all those superfluities that make industry flourish and that both enrich and ruin nations. The reason for this ruin is plain. The very nature of agriculture makes it the least profitable of all the arts: because its products are the most universally necessary, their price must be kept within reach of the very poorest. From the same principle follows a general rule: the arts are more profitable the less useful they are, and in the end the most useful becomes the most neglected. From this we may learn what to think of the real advantages of industry and the actual effects of its progress.

These are the evident causes of all the miseries into which wealth finally plunges the most celebrated nations. As the arts and industry flourish, the despised farmer, burdened with the taxes needed to support luxury and condemned to spend his days between labor and hunger, abandons his native fields to seek in the towns the bread he ought to be bringing to them. The more our capital cities dazzle the common eye, the more reason we have to grieve at the sight of the abandoned countryside, the great tracts of land left uncultivated, and the roads crowded with unfortunate citizens turned beggars or highwaymen, doomed to end their wretched lives on a dunghill or on the gallows. Thus the State grows rich on one side and weak and depopulated on the other. The mightiest monarchies, after taking enormous pains to enrich and depopulate themselves, finally fall prey to some poor nation that has yielded to the fatal temptation to invade them; that nation in turn grows rich and weak, and is itself invaded and ruined by another.

Let someone explain to us what produced the swarms of barbarians who overran Europe, Asia, and Africa for so many centuries. Was their prodigious increase due to their industry and arts, to the wisdom of their laws, or to the excellence of their political system? Let the learned tell us why these fierce and brutal men, without understanding or science, without education or restraint, multiplied to such a degree instead of destroying one another hourly in quarrels over the produce of their fields and forests. Let them tell us how these wretches could presume to resist such clever people as we were, so well trained in military discipline and possessed of such excellent laws and institutions. And let them tell us why, now that society has been perfected in the northern countries and so much effort has gone into teaching their inhabitants their social duties and the art of living together happily and peacefully, those countries no longer send forth the countless hosts they once did, to be the plague and terror of other nations. I fear someone may finally answer that all these fine things, the arts, the sciences, and the laws, were wisely invented by men as a beneficial plague, to keep mankind from multiplying too greatly, lest the world given us for a dwelling should in time become too small for its inhabitants.

What, then, is to be done? Must societies be abolished altogether? Must *meum* and *tuum*, mine and thine, be destroyed, and must we go back to the forests to live among the beasts? This is the kind of inference my adversaries draw, and I would rather anticipate it myself than leave them the shame of drawing it. You who have never heard the voice of heaven, who believe man is destined only to live out this brief life and die in peace; you who are able, in the midst of crowded cities, to give up your fatal acquisitions, your restless minds, your corrupt hearts, and your endless desires: resume your ancient and original innocence, since it depends entirely on yourselves. Retire to the woods, there to lose the sight and the memory of your contemporaries' crimes, and do not fear that you degrade your species by renouncing its advances in order to renounce its vices. As for men like me, whose passions have destroyed their original simplicity and who can no longer live on plants and acorns, or live without laws and magistrates; those who were honored, in the person of their first father, Adam, with instruction from above; those who see in the divine command given to him, a precept in itself indifferent and inexplicable on any other system, the purpose of giving human actions from the very beginning a morality they would otherwise have taken so long to acquire; those, in short, who are convinced that God has called all mankind to share in the happiness and perfection of heavenly beings: all these will strive to earn the eternal reward they expect from practicing the virtues they learn to follow by learning to know them. They will respect the sacred bonds of their communities; they will love their fellow citizens and serve them with all their strength; they will scrupulously obey the laws and all those who make or administer them. They will especially honor those wise and good princes who find ways to prevent, cure, or even soften the evils and abuses that constantly threaten us, and they will kindle the zeal of their worthy rulers by showing them, without flattery or fear, the importance of their office and the rigor of their duty. But they will feel no less contempt for a constitution that cannot sustain itself without the help of so many distinguished men, far more often wished for than found, and from which, despite all their care and devotion, there always arise more real calamities than even apparent advantages.

## A Discourse on Political Economy

The word *economy*, or *œconomy*, comes from the Greek οἶκος, meaning "house," and νόμος, meaning "law." Originally it meant only the wise and lawful management of a household for the common good of the whole family. The term was later extended to the government of that great family, the State. To distinguish the two senses, the latter is called general or political economy, and the former domestic or private economy. Only the first is treated in this discourse.

Even if the analogy between the State and the family were as close as many writers claim, it would not follow that the rules of conduct suited to one of these societies would suit the other. They differ too greatly in size to be governed in the same way. There will always be a vast difference between domestic government, in which a father can see everything for himself, and civil government, in which the ruler sees hardly anything except through the eyes of others. To put the two on an equal footing in this respect, the father's talents, strength, and every other faculty would have to grow in proportion to the size of his family, and the soul of a powerful monarch would have to stand to that of an ordinary man as the extent of his empire stands to a private person's estate.

But how could the government of the State resemble that of the family, when the two rest on such different foundations? Because a father is physically stronger than his children, his paternal authority may reasonably be said to be established by nature, at least for as long as they need his protection. In the great family, however, whose members are all naturally equal, political authority is purely arbitrary in its origin. It can be founded only on conventions, and the magistrate can hold no authority over the others except by virtue of the laws. A father's duties are dictated to him by natural feeling, and in a way that rarely allows him to neglect them. Rulers have no such principle. They are truly bound to the people only by what they themselves have promised to do, and the people therefore have the right to demand that they do it. A second and more important difference concerns property. Since children have nothing except what they receive from their father, all rights of property plainly belong to him or derive from him. In the great family the reverse is true: the general administration exists only to secure individual property, which came before it. The principal aim of a household's labor is to preserve and enlarge the father's patrimony, so that he may one day divide it among his children without leaving them poor. The wealth of the public treasury, by contrast, is only a means, and an often misunderstood one, of keeping individuals in peace and plenty. In short, the small family is destined to come to an end and eventually to break up into several new families of the same kind. The great family, constituted to endure forever in the same condition, has no need to grow in order to multiply, as the small one does. It needs only to sustain itself, and it can easily be shown that any increase does it more harm than good.

Within the family, it is clear for several reasons rooted in its very nature that the father ought to command. First, authority should not be divided equally between father and mother. Government must be single, and whenever opinions differ there must be one voice that decides. Second, however lightly we may regard the disadvantages peculiar to women, they necessarily bring periods of inactivity, and this alone is reason enough to exclude women from the supreme authority, for when the balance is perfectly even, a straw is enough to tip the scale. Moreover, the husband ought to be able to oversee his wife's conduct, because it matters to him to be certain that the children he is obliged to acknowledge and support are his and no one else's. Third, children should obey their father, at first out of necessity and later out of gratitude. Having had their needs met by him for one half of their lives, they ought to devote the other half to providing for his. Fourth, servants owe him their labor in exchange for the provision he makes for them, though they may end the bargain as soon as it no longer suits them. I say nothing here of slavery, because it is contrary to nature and can be authorized by no right or law.

None of this holds in political society. There the chief is so far from having any natural interest in the happiness of individuals that he not uncommonly seeks his own happiness in their misery. If the magistracy is hereditary, a community of men is often governed by a child. If it is elective, the elections themselves give rise to countless difficulties. In either case, every advantage of fatherhood is lost. If you have a single ruler, you are at the mercy of a master who has no reason to love you; if you have several, you must endure both their tyranny and their quarrels. In short, abuses are inevitable, and their consequences fatal, in every society where the public interest and the laws have no natural force and are perpetually assailed by the personal interests and passions of both ruler and members.

Although the functions of the father of a family and those of the chief magistrate ought to aim at the same end, they must pursue it in such different ways, and their duties and rights are so fundamentally distinct, that we cannot confuse the two without forming very false ideas about the basic laws of society and falling into errors that are fatal to mankind. If the voice of nature is the best counselor a father can heed in carrying out his duty, for the magistrate it is a false guide. It constantly keeps him from performing his own duty, and sooner or later, unless he is restrained by the most exalted virtue, it leads him to ruin both himself and the State. The only precaution a father needs is to guard himself against depravity and keep his natural inclinations from being corrupted. In the magistrate, however, it is those very inclinations that do the corrupting. To act rightly, the father has only to consult his heart; the magistrate becomes a traitor the moment he listens to his. He should distrust even his own reason, and follow no rule but the public reason, which is the law. Nature, accordingly, has produced a multitude of good fathers, but it is doubtful whether, since the beginning of the world, human wisdom has produced ten men capable of governing their equals.

From all that has been said, it follows that public economy, my subject here, has rightly been distinguished from private economy. Since the State has nothing in common with the family except that the heads of both are obliged to make them happy, the same rules of conduct cannot apply to both. I have judged these few lines sufficient to overturn the detestable system that Sir Robert Filmer tried to establish in his *Patriarcha*, a work that two celebrated writers have honored far too much by writing books to refute it. The error, moreover, is a very old one, for Aristotle himself saw fit to combat it with arguments that can be found in the first book of his *Politics*.

I must also ask my readers to distinguish between public economy, which is my subject and which I call government, and the supreme authority, which I call sovereignty. The distinction lies in this: sovereignty holds the right to make laws and, in certain cases, binds the body of the nation itself, whereas government holds only the right to carry the laws out and binds only individuals. I shall take the liberty of using a very common comparison, inaccurate in some respects, to make my meaning clear.

The body politic, considered on its own, may be regarded as an organized, living body resembling that of a human being. The sovereign power corresponds to the head. The laws and customs are the brain, the source of the nerves and the seat of understanding, will, and the senses, while the judges and magistrates are its organs. Commerce, industry, and agriculture are the mouth and stomach, which prepare the common sustenance. Public revenue is the blood, which a prudent economy, acting as the heart, sends through the whole body to carry nourishment and life. The citizens are the body and limbs that make the machine live, move, and work. And no part of this machine can be injured without the painful sensation being carried at once to the brain, provided the creature is in good health.

The life of both bodies is the self that is common to the whole: the mutual sensitivity and inner correspondence of all the parts. Where this communication ceases, where formal unity disappears and the adjoining parts are connected only by lying next to one another, the man is dead, or the State is dissolved.

The body politic, therefore, is also a moral being possessed of a will. This general will, which always tends toward the preservation and welfare of the whole and of every part, and which is the source of the laws, constitutes for all members of the State, in their relations with one another and with the State, the rule of what is just and unjust. This truth shows, incidentally, how foolishly some writers have condemned as theft the cunning that Sparta required of its children in obtaining their meager meals, which the boys were expected to take by stealth, as if whatever the law ordained were not lawful.

It is important to note that this rule of justice, though certain with regard to all citizens, may be faulty with regard to foreigners. The reason is clear. The will of the State, though general in relation to its own members, is no longer general in relation to other States and their members. Toward them it becomes a particular and individual will, whose rule of justice is found in the law of nature. Yet this, too, falls within the principle laid down here. In such a case, the great city of the world becomes the body politic, its general will is always the law of nature, and the various States and peoples are its individual members. From these distinctions, applied to each political society and its members, we derive the surest and most universal rules for judging whether a government is good or bad, and, more generally, for judging the morality of all human actions.

Every political society is made up of smaller societies of various kinds, each with its own interests and rules of conduct. But the societies everyone recognizes, because they have an outward and authorized form, are not the only ones that actually exist within the State. All individuals united by a common interest form so many others, whether temporary or permanent, whose influence is no less real for being less visible. To observe properly the varied relations among them is the true knowledge of public morals and manners. Each of these associations, tacit or formal, exerts the force of its will and so modifies the public will in its own way. The will of these particular societies always has two aspects. For the members of the association, it is a general will; for the larger society, it is a particular will. It is often right with respect to the first and wrong with respect to the second. A man may be a devout priest, a brave soldier, or a zealous senator, and yet a bad citizen. A given resolution may benefit the smaller community and harm the greater one. It is true that, since particular societies are always subordinate to the general society, the duty of a citizen takes precedence over that of a senator, and a man's duty over that of a citizen. Unfortunately, personal interest always stands in inverse proportion to duty: it grows stronger as the association grows narrower and the commitment less sacred. This proves beyond dispute that the most general will is always also the most just, and that the voice of the people is in fact the voice of God.

It does not follow that public decisions are always fair. For the reasons I have given, they may not be when they concern foreigners. It is therefore not impossible for a republic, though well governed in itself, to embark on an unjust war. Nor is it any less possible for the council of a democracy to pass unjust decrees and condemn the innocent. But this never happens unless the people has been led astray by private interests, which the influence or eloquence of a few clever men has substituted for those of the State. In that case, the general will is one thing and the outcome of public deliberation another. The Athenian democracy does not contradict this, for Athens was not really a democracy at all, but a highly tyrannical aristocracy governed by philosophers and orators. Examine carefully what happens in any public deliberation, and you will see that the general will always favors the common good. Very often, however, there is a secret split, a tacit alliance, which for particular ends overrides the natural inclination of the assembly. When that happens, the body of society is effectively divided into other bodies. Their members acquire a general will that is good and just with respect to these new bodies but unjust and bad with respect to the whole, from which each of them has thus been torn away.

With the help of these principles, it is easy to explain the apparent contradictions we notice in the conduct of so many people who are scrupulously honest in some respects and cheats and scoundrels in others, who trample on the most sacred duties and yet remain faithful to the death to commitments that are often illegitimate. In this way, even the most depraved of men always pay some kind of homage to public faith, and even robbers, who are the enemies of virtue in the great society, show some respect for its shadow in their hidden caves.

In establishing the general will as the first principle of public economy and the fundamental rule of government, I have not thought it necessary to inquire seriously whether the magistrates belong to the people or the people to the magistrates, or whether public affairs should be conducted for the good of the State or only for that of its rulers. That question has long been settled one way in theory and another in practice, and in general it would be absurd to expect those who are in fact masters to prefer any interest to their own. It would therefore be fitting to divide public economy further into popular and tyrannical. The first is that of every State in which the people and the rulers share a unity of interest and will. The second necessarily exists wherever the government and the people have different interests, and consequently opposing wills. The rules of the tyrannical kind are written out at length in the archives of history and in the satires of Machiavelli. The rules of the popular kind are found only in the writings of those philosophers who dare to proclaim the rights of humanity.

I. The first and most important rule of legitimate or popular government, that is, of government whose aim is the good of the people, is therefore, as I have said, to follow the general will in everything. But to follow this will, one must know it, and above all distinguish it from the particular will, beginning with one's own. This distinction is always very difficult to draw, and only the most exalted virtue can shed enough light to make it. And since one must be free in order to will, a second difficulty arises that is no less great than the first: how to preserve public liberty and the authority of government at the same time. Look into the motives that led men, once united in a general society by their common needs, to bind themselves still more closely in civil societies, and you will find no other motive than to secure the property, life, and liberty of each member through the protection of all. Yet can men be compelled to defend the liberty of any one of their number without infringing on that of others? And how can they provide for public needs without taking away the private property of those who are made to contribute to them? Whatever sophistry may be used to disguise the matter, it is certain that if any constraint can be placed on my will, I am no longer free, and that if anyone else can lay hands on my property, I am no longer its master. This difficulty, which would have seemed insurmountable, has been removed, like the first, by the most exalted of all human institutions, or rather by a divine inspiration that teaches mankind to imitate here on earth the unchanging decrees of the Deity. By what inconceivable art has a way been found to make men free by making them subject; to employ in the service of the State the property, the persons, and even the lives of all its members, without constraining them and without consulting them; to bind their will by their own consent; to overcome their refusal by that very consent; and to make them punish themselves when they act against their own will? How can it be that all obey and yet no one commands, that all serve and yet have no masters, and are all the more free because, under an apparent subjection, each gives up only that part of his liberty which could harm the liberty of another? These wonders are the work of law. It is to law alone that men owe justice and liberty. It is this beneficial instrument of the will of all that restores, in civil right, the natural equality among men. It is this heavenly voice that dictates to each citizen the precepts of public reason, and teaches him to act according to the rules of his own judgment and not to contradict himself. It is with this voice alone that rulers should speak when they command. For the moment one man sets the law aside and claims to subject another to his private will, he leaves the state of civil society and confronts that man face to face in the pure state of nature, where obedience is dictated by necessity alone.

The ruler's most pressing interest, and indeed his most indispensable duty, is therefore to watch over the observance of the laws of which he is the minister and on which his entire authority rests. If he demands that others observe them, he is all the more strongly bound to observe them himself, since he enjoys all their benefits. His example carries such weight that even if the people were willing to let him free himself from the yoke of the law, he should be wary of using so dangerous a privilege, which others might soon try to seize in their turn, and often to his cost. At bottom, since all social commitments are mutual by nature, no one can place himself above the law without giving up its advantages, for no one is under any obligation to a man who claims to have none toward others. For this reason, no exemption from the law will ever be granted, on any grounds whatsoever, under a well-ordered government. Citizens who have served their country well ought to be rewarded with honors, but never with privileges, for a republic is on the eve of its fall when anyone can think it a fine thing not to obey the laws. If the nobility or the army should ever adopt such a maxim, all would be lost beyond recovery.

The power of the laws depends even more on their own wisdom than on the severity of those who administer them, and the public will draws its greatest weight from the reason that dictated it. This is why Plato considered it a most necessary precaution to place at the head of every edict a preamble setting out its justice and usefulness. Indeed, the first of all laws is to respect the laws. Severe punishment is only an empty expedient, devised by small minds to substitute terror for the respect they have no means of winning. It has consistently been observed that in countries where legal punishments are harshest, they are also most frequent. The cruelty of such punishments is therefore proof only of the great number of criminals, and by punishing everything with equal severity, it drives those guilty of minor offenses to commit crimes in order to escape punishment for their faults.

But although the government is not master of the law, it is no small thing to be its guarantor and to have a thousand ways of inspiring love for it. In this alone lies the art of ruling. With force in one's hands, no art is needed to make the whole world tremble, nor even much to win men's hearts, for experience has long since taught the people to give their rulers great credit for all the harm they refrain from doing it, and to adore them if they do not positively hate it. A fool, if he is obeyed, can punish crimes as well as anyone. The true statesman is the one who knows how to prevent them, and his honorable rule extends over the wills of his subjects even more than over their actions. If he could ensure that everyone acted rightly, he would have nothing left to do, and the masterpiece of his labors would be to remain idle. It is certain, at least, that the greatest talent a ruler can possess is to disguise his power in order to make it less odious, and to guide the State so peacefully that it seems to need no guides at all.

I conclude, therefore, that just as the legislator's first duty is to make the laws conform to the general will, the first rule of public economy is that the administration of justice should conform to the laws. It will even be enough to keep the State from being badly governed if the legislator has provided, as he should, for every requirement of place, climate, soil, custom, and neighboring peoples, and for all the other circumstances peculiar to the people he was charged with establishing. There still remains, of course, an infinity of details of administration and economy that must be left to the wisdom of the government. But there are two infallible rules for its conduct in such matters. The first is that the spirit of the law should decide every particular case that could not be foreseen. The second is that the general will, which is both the source of all laws and their supplement, should be consulted wherever the laws fall short. How, I shall be asked, can the general will be known in cases where it has not expressed itself? Must the whole nation be assembled at every unforeseen event? Certainly not. There is all the less reason to assemble it because there is no certainty that its decision would express the general will. Besides, the method would be impractical for a large people, and it is hardly ever necessary when the government means well. The rulers know perfectly well that the general will always lies on the side most favorable to the public interest, that is, the most equitable side, so they need only act justly to be sure of following it. When the general will is defied too openly, it makes itself felt despite the formidable restraint of public authority. I shall cite the closest examples I can of what may be done in such cases.

In China, it is the Prince's constant maxim to rule against his officials in every dispute that arises between them and the people. If bread is too expensive in a province, the intendant of that province is thrown into prison. If there is an uprising in another, the governor is dismissed, and every mandarin answers with his head for any trouble that occurs in his district. These matters are afterward given a proper examination, but long experience has taught that the verdict can safely be reached in advance. There is seldom any injustice to correct. Meanwhile the Emperor, convinced that public outcry never arises without cause, always finds within the seditious clamor he punishes some just grievances to redress.

It is a great thing to maintain peace and order in every part of the republic. It is a great thing for the State to be tranquil and the law respected. But if nothing more is done, there will be more appearance than reality in all this, for a government that limits itself to securing mere obedience will have difficulty getting itself obeyed. It is good to know how to deal with men as they are, but it is far better to make them what they need to be. The most absolute authority is the kind that reaches into a man's inmost being and concerns itself as much with his will as with his actions. In the long run, every people becomes what its government makes it: warriors, citizens, men, when the government so pleases; or a mere populace and rabble, when it chooses to make them that. Every prince who despises his subjects therefore dishonors himself, for he confesses that he does not know how to make them worthy of respect. If you would command men, then, make men. If you would have them obey the laws, make them love the laws, and they will need only to know their duty in order to do it. This was the great art of ancient governments in those distant times when philosophers gave laws to peoples and used their authority only to make them wise and happy. From that art arose the many sumptuary laws restraining luxury and display, the many regulations of morals, and all the public rules of conduct that were adopted or rejected with the greatest care. Even tyrants did not neglect this part of administration; they took as much trouble to corrupt the morals of their slaves as magistrates took to correct those of their fellow citizens. Our modern governments, by contrast, imagine they have done everything once they have raised money, and they consider it unnecessary, and even impossible, to go one step further.

II. The second essential rule of public economy is no less important than the first. If you want the general will to be carried out, bring every particular will into conformity with it. And since virtue is nothing more than this conformity of particular wills with the general will, the rule amounts to this: establish the reign of virtue.

If our politicians were less blinded by ambition, they would see that no institution of any kind can act in the spirit for which it was founded unless it is guided by the law of duty. They would feel that the strongest support of public authority lies in the hearts of the citizens, and that nothing can replace morality in sustaining a government. Upright men are not the only ones who know how to administer the laws, but at bottom only good men know how to obey them. A man who has once overcome remorse will not shrink from punishments that are less severe and less lasting, and that he at least hopes to escape. Whatever precautions are taken, those who need only impunity in order to do wrong will always find ways to evade the law and avoid its penalties. Once that happens, every particular interest combines against the general interest, which no longer belongs to any individual, and public vices do more to weaken the laws than the laws do to repress those vices. In time, the corruption of the people and of their rulers spreads to the government itself, however wise it may be. The worst of all abuses is to pay the laws an outward obedience only in order to break them in safety. When that happens, the best laws soon become the most harmful, and it would be a hundred times better if they did not exist at all. In such a condition it is useless to pile edict upon edict and regulation upon regulation. Each new measure only introduces fresh abuses without correcting the old. The more the laws multiply, the more they are despised, and every new official appointed to enforce them is simply one more person to break them, either sharing the plunder with his predecessors or plundering separately on his own account. The reward of virtue soon becomes the reward of theft. The vilest men rise to the highest standing, and the greater they become, the more contemptible they are; their infamy shows even in their dignities, and their very honors disgrace them. If they buy the influence of those in power or the favor of women, it is only so that they may in turn sell justice, duty, and the State. Meanwhile the people, sensing that its own vices are not the first cause of its misfortunes, murmurs and complains that all its troubles come from the very men it pays to protect it from them.

It is in these circumstances that the voice of duty falls silent in men's hearts, and their rulers are forced to put in its place the cry of terror, or the lure of an apparent interest with which they afterward deceive their own creatures. They are then driven to every petty and contemptible expedient, which they dignify with the names of reasons of state and secrets of the cabinet. Whatever vigor remains in the government is spent by its members in ruining and supplanting one another, while public business is neglected or carried on only as personal interest requires and directs. The entire art of these great politicians comes down to bewitching the people they depend on, so that each man believes he is working for his own interest when he is in fact working for theirs. I say "theirs" only on the false assumption that it is truly in the interest of rulers to destroy a people in order to subject it, and to ruin their own property in order to secure possession of it.

But when citizens love their duty, and when those entrusted with public authority sincerely devote themselves to fostering that love through their own example and diligence, every difficulty disappears. Government becomes so easy that it has no need of that art of darkness whose blackness is its only mystery. Those daring and much-admired spirits who are so dangerous to the State, all those great ministers whose glory cannot be separated from the misery of the people, are no longer missed. Public morality supplies whatever the rulers lack in genius, and the more virtue prevails, the less talent is needed. Even ambition is better served by duty than by usurpation. Once a people is convinced that its rulers work only for its happiness, its deference spares them the trouble of working to strengthen their power, and history shows in a thousand cases that the authority of a ruler who is loved, over those he loves, is a hundred times more absolute than all the tyranny of usurpers. This does not mean that a government should be afraid to use its power, only that it should use it lawfully. History offers a thousand examples of timid or ambitious rulers who were ruined by their slackness or their pride, and not one who suffered for having been strictly just. Yet we must not confuse negligence with moderation, or clemency with weakness. To be just, one must be severe. To tolerate vice when one has the right and the power to suppress it is to be vicious oneself.

It is not enough to tell citizens to be good; they must be taught to be good. Example is the first lesson in this respect, but it is not the only means available, and patriotism is the most effective of all. For, as I have already said, every man is virtuous when his particular will conforms in all things to the general will, and we freely will what is willed by those we love. The feeling of humanity seems to evaporate and grow weak when it is stretched to embrace all mankind; we cannot be moved by calamities in Tartary or Japan as we are by those that strike European nations. Our interest and compassion must be confined and limited to some degree before they can become active. And since this sentiment can be of use only to the people among whom we live, it is fitting that our humanity should concentrate itself on our fellow citizens, gaining fresh strength from our habit of seeing them and from the common interest that binds them together. The greatest miracles of virtue have unquestionably been produced by patriotism. This fine and ardent feeling, which lends the force of self-love all the beauty of virtue, gives it an energy that, without distorting it, makes it the most heroic of all the passions. It is patriotism that has produced so many immortal deeds whose glory dazzles our feeble eyes, and so many great men whose old-fashioned virtues pass for fables now that patriotism has become an object of ridicule. This is not surprising. The raptures of sensitive hearts seem pure fantasy to anyone who has never felt them, and love of country, which is a hundred times more ardent and delightful than love of a mistress, can be understood only by those who have experienced it. Still, in every heart it warms and in every action it inspires, one can easily see a glowing and sublime fervor that does not accompany even the purest virtue when that virtue stands apart from it.

Compare Socrates even with Cato. The first was the greater philosopher; the second was more truly a citizen. Athens was already ruined in Socrates' time, and he had no country but the world at large. Cato always carried the cause of his country in his heart; he lived for it alone and could not bear to outlive it. The virtue of Socrates was that of the wisest of men, but set beside Caesar and Pompey, Cato seems a god among mortals. Socrates taught a handful of individuals, opposed the Sophists, and died for the truth. Cato defended his country, its liberty, and its laws against the conquerors of the world, and when he no longer had a country to serve, he took his leave of the earth. A worthy disciple of Socrates would be the most virtuous man of his time; a worthy follower of Cato would be one of the greatest. The virtue of the first would be his own happiness; the second would seek his happiness in the happiness of all. We should be taught by the one and led by the other, and that alone is enough to decide which to prefer, for no people has ever been turned into a nation of philosophers, but it is not impossible to make a people happy.

Do we want men to be virtuous? Then let us begin by making them love their country. But how can they love it if their country means no more to them than it does to foreigners, and grants them nothing it could refuse to anyone? It would be worse still if they did not enjoy even the benefit of civil security, and if their lives, liberties, and property lay at the mercy of the powerful, with no permission and no possibility of seeking redress from the laws. In that case they would be bound by all the duties of civil society without enjoying even the ordinary rights of the state of nature, and without being able to use their own strength in their defense. They would be in the worst condition in which free men could possibly find themselves, and the word "country" would mean to them something merely odious and absurd. We must not imagine that a man can break or lose an arm without the pain reaching his head. Nor is it any more credible that the general will should consent to any member of the State, whoever he may be, wounding or destroying another, than that the fingers of a sane man should deliberately scratch out his own eyes. The security of individuals is so closely bound up with the public association that, setting aside the allowance that must be made for human weakness, the social contract would by right be dissolved if a single citizen who could have been helped were allowed to perish in the State, or if one were wrongfully imprisoned, or if a single plainly unjust sentence were handed down. For once the fundamental agreements are broken, it is impossible to conceive of any right or interest that could hold the people within the social union, unless they were held there by force, and force alone is what dissolves civil society.

Does not the commitment undertaken by the whole body of the nation oblige it to provide for the security of its least member as carefully as for that of all the rest? Is the welfare of a single citizen any less the common cause than the welfare of the whole State? Some will say that it is good for one to perish for all. I am ready to admire that saying when it comes from the lips of a worthy and virtuous patriot who willingly and dutifully sacrifices himself for the good of his country. But if it means that the government may lawfully sacrifice an innocent man for the good of the many, I regard it as one of the most detestable maxims tyranny ever devised, the greatest falsehood that can be put forward, the most dangerous concession that can be made, and a direct contradiction of the fundamental laws of society. So far is it from true that one person ought to perish for all, that all have pledged their lives and property to the defense of each, so that the weakness of individuals may always be protected by the strength of the public, and each member by the whole State. Suppose we take away from the whole people one individual after another, and then press the defenders of this maxim to explain exactly what they mean by "the body of the State." We will find that it shrinks in the end to a small number of persons who are not the people but the people's officers, and who, having bound themselves by a personal oath to perish for the people's welfare, would conclude from it that the people must perish for theirs.

Do we need examples of the protection the State owes its members and the respect it owes their persons? They are to be found only among the most illustrious and courageous nations; only among free peoples is the dignity of man made real. It is well known how deeply the whole republic of Sparta was troubled whenever the question arose of punishing a guilty citizen. In Macedon, a man's life was a matter of such weight that Alexander the Great, at the height of his glory, would not have dared to put a Macedonian criminal to death in cold blood until the accused had made his defense before his fellow citizens and been condemned by them. But the Romans surpassed all other peoples in the regard their government showed for the individual and in its scrupulous care to preserve the inviolable rights of every member of the State. Nothing was so sacred among them as the life of a citizen, and nothing less than an assembly of the whole people could condemn one. Neither the Senate nor the consuls, for all their majesty, had that right; among the most powerful people in the world, the crime and punishment of a citizen were treated as a public calamity. Indeed, it seemed so grievous to shed blood for any crime whatever that the Porcian Law, the *Lex Porcia*, replaced the death penalty with banishment for anyone willing to go on living after losing so great a country. Everything at Rome and in the Roman armies breathed that love of citizens for one another, and that respect for the Roman name, which raised the courage and inspired the virtue of everyone who had the honor to bear it. The cap that marked a slave newly freed into citizenship, and the civic crown awarded to a man who had saved a fellow citizen's life, were regarded with the greatest delight amid all the splendor of the triumphs. It is worth noting that of all the crowns awarded for brilliant feats in war, only the civic crown and the crown of the triumphant general were made of laurel; all the others were merely gold. This is how Rome was virtuous, and how it became mistress of the world.

Ambitious rulers! A herdsman governs his dogs and cattle, and yet he is the lowliest of men. If there is anything fine in commanding, it is when those who obey us are capable of doing us honor. Respect your fellow citizens, then, and you will make yourselves worthy of respect; respect liberty, and your power will grow by the day. Never overstep your rights, and they will soon become unlimited.

Let our country, then, show itself the common mother of its citizens. Let the advantages they enjoy in it make it dear to them. Let the government leave them a large enough share in public administration to make them feel at home, and let the laws appear to them as nothing other than the guarantees of their common liberty. These rights, great as they are, belong to all men; yet the ill will of rulers can easily reduce them to nothing without ever seeming to attack them directly. The law, so abused, becomes for the powerful both a weapon of attack and a shield against the weak, and the pretext of the public good is always the most dangerous scourge of the people. What is most necessary in government, and perhaps most difficult, is an unbending integrity in doing strict justice to all, and above all in protecting the poor against the tyranny of the rich. The greatest evil has already arrived when there are poor men to be defended and rich men to be restrained. The full force of the law falls only on those of middling fortune; it is equally powerless against the treasure of the rich and the destitution of the poor. The first mocks it; the second escapes it. One tears through the net; the other slips through its mesh.

It is therefore one of the most important tasks of government to prevent extreme inequality of fortunes, not by taking wealth away from those who possess it, but by depriving everyone of the means to accumulate it; not by building hospitals for the poor, but by protecting citizens from becoming poor. Consider the uneven distribution of people across the land, crowded together in one place while other places are emptied; the encouragement of the arts that cater to luxury and of purely industrial occupations at the expense of useful and laborious crafts; the sacrifice of agriculture to commerce; the dependence on tax farmers, those private financiers who buy the right to collect public revenue, which the mismanagement of the State's funds makes unavoidable; and, finally, venality pushed so far that even public esteem is counted in cash and virtue is sold at market price. These are the most evident causes of wealth and poverty, of private interest taking the place of the public interest, of mutual hatred among citizens, of indifference to the common cause, of the corruption of the people, and of the weakening of every mainspring of government. Such evils are hard to cure once they make themselves felt, but a wise administration ought to prevent them if it means to preserve, along with good morals, respect for the laws, patriotism, and the influence of the general will.

Yet all these precautions will fall short unless rulers go still deeper, to the root of the matter. I end this part of public economy where I ought to have begun it. There can be no patriotism without liberty, no liberty without virtue, and no virtue without citizens. Create citizens and you have everything you need; without them you will have nothing but degraded slaves, from the rulers of the State on down. Forming citizens is not the work of a day, and to have men one must educate them while they are children. It may be said that whoever governs men should not seek in them a perfection beyond their nature and beyond their capacity; that he should not wish to destroy their passions; and that such an undertaking is no more desirable than it is possible. I will go further and grant that a man without passions would certainly make a bad citizen. But it must also be granted that unless men are taught not to love certain things, it is impossible to teach them to love one object more than another, to prefer what is truly beautiful to what is ugly and misshapen. If, for example, they were trained from an early age to regard their individuality only in relation to the body of the State, and to be conscious, so to speak, of their own existence only as part of the State's existence, they might at last come to identify themselves in some degree with this greater whole. They would feel themselves members of their country and love it with that exquisite feeling which an isolated person reserves for himself alone. They would raise their spirits continually toward this great object, and so transform into a sublime virtue the very disposition, dangerous in itself, from which all our vices spring. Philosophy shows that feeling can be given these new directions, and history supplies a thousand striking examples. If such examples are so rare among us moderns, it is because no one cares whether citizens exist or not, and still less does anyone think of attending to the matter early enough to form them. It is too late to change our natural inclinations once they have taken their course and habit has hardened our egoism.
