It is too late to draw us out of ourselves once the human self, concentrated in our hearts, has acquired that contemptible restlessness which swallows up all virtue and makes up the whole life and being of small minds. How can patriotism take root amid so many other passions that choke it? And what can be left for one's fellow citizens in a heart already divided among avarice, a mistress, and vanity?

From the first moment of life, men ought to begin learning to deserve to live. Since we share in the rights of citizenship from the instant of birth, that instant ought to mark the beginning of our duties as well. If there are laws for maturity, there ought to be laws for childhood that teach obedience to others. And just as each man's reason is not left to be the sole judge of his duties, so the government ought all the less to abandon the education of children indiscriminately to the understanding and prejudices of their fathers, since that education matters even more to the State than to the fathers. In the ordinary course of nature, a father's death often deprives him of the final fruits of his children's education, but sooner or later his country feels its effects. Families dissolve; the State endures.

If the public authority were to take the father's place and assume this important function, acquiring his rights by fulfilling his duties, he would have all the less reason to complain, since he would merely be changing his title. As a citizen, he would hold in common with others the same authority over his children that he had exercised separately as a father, and he would be obeyed no less when speaking in the name of the law than when he spoke in the name of nature. Public education, therefore, conducted under rules laid down by the government and under magistrates appointed by the Sovereign, is one of the fundamental principles of popular or legitimate government. If children are raised together in a setting of equality; if they are steeped in the laws of the State and the precepts of the general will; if they are taught to respect these above all else; if they are surrounded by examples and objects that constantly remind them of the tender mother who nourishes them, of the love she bears them, of the priceless benefits they receive from her, and of what they owe her in return, then we cannot doubt that they will learn to cherish one another as brothers, to will nothing contrary to the will of society, to replace the empty and idle chatter of sophists with the deeds of men and citizens, and in time to become the defenders and fathers of the country whose children they will so long have been.

I shall say nothing of the magistrates appointed to preside over such an education, which is surely the most important business of the State. If such marks of public trust were granted on slight grounds, and if this exalted office were not, for those who had worthily filled every other post, the reward of their labors, the pleasant and honorable rest of their old age, and the crown of all their honors, then the whole undertaking would be useless and the education would come to nothing. Wherever a lesson is not backed by authority, and a precept by example, all teaching is fruitless; even virtue loses its credit in the mouth of someone who does not practice it. But let illustrious soldiers, bowed beneath the weight of their laurels, preach courage; let upright magistrates, grown gray in the purple robes of office and on the judge's bench, teach justice. Teachers like these would raise up virtuous successors of their own, and would hand down from age to age, to generations still to come, the experience and talents of rulers, the courage and virtue of citizens, and a shared resolve in all to live and die for their country.

I know of only three peoples who once practiced public education: the Cretans, the Spartans, and the ancient Persians. Among all three it met with the greatest success, and among the last two it worked wonders. Since the world has been divided into nations too large to be governed well, the method has ceased to be practicable, and the reader will readily see other reasons why no modern people has ever attempted it. It is remarkable that the Romans managed without it; but for five hundred years Rome was one continuous miracle, which the world cannot hope to see again. The virtue of the Romans, born of their horror of tyranny and of the crimes of tyrants, and of an innate love of country, turned every household into a school of citizenship. At the same time, the unlimited power that fathers held over their children made private authority so stern that a father was feared more than a magistrate; within the tribunal of his own family he was both the censor of morals and the avenger of the laws.

A careful and well-meaning government, constantly watchful to maintain or restore patriotism and good morals among the people, therefore guards in advance against the evils that sooner or later follow when citizens grow indifferent to the fate of the Republic. It keeps within narrow bounds that private interest which so isolates individuals that the State is weakened by their power and can hope for nothing from their goodwill. Wherever people love their country, respect the laws, and live simply, little remains to be done to make them happy; and in public administration, where chance has less sway than in the fortunes of private individuals, wisdom lies so close to happiness that the two aims merge into one.

III. It is not enough to have citizens and to protect them; it is also necessary to see to their subsistence. Providing for public needs follows plainly from the general will, and it is the third essential duty of government. This duty, we should understand, is not to fill the granaries of private individuals and so excuse them from working, but to keep abundance so within their reach that labor is always necessary to obtain it and never useless. The duty also extends to everything concerning the management of the treasury and the expenses of public administration. Having treated general economy as it relates to the government of persons, we must now consider it as it relates to the administration of property.

This part presents no fewer difficulties to resolve, and contradictions to remove, than the one before it. The right of property is certainly the most sacred of all the rights of citizens, and in some respects more important even than liberty itself. This may be because it bears more directly on the preservation of life; or because property, being easier to seize and harder to defend than life, demands the closer attention of the law, which ought to guard most carefully what is most easily taken away; or, finally, because property is the true foundation of civil society and the real guarantee of the commitments citizens make. If property were not answerable for a person's actions, nothing would be easier than to dodge one's duties and laugh at the laws. On the other hand, it is no less certain that maintaining the State and its government involves costs and outlays; and since anyone who agrees to an end must accept the means, it follows that the members of a society ought to contribute from their property to its support. Moreover, it is difficult to secure the property of individuals on one side without encroaching on it on another. All the regulations that govern inheritance, wills, contracts, and the like must inevitably place some constraint on how individuals dispose of their goods, and must therefore restrict the right of property.

Beyond what I have already said about how the authority of the law can be reconciled with the liberty of the citizen, one important observation remains to be made about the disposal of goods, and it removes many difficulties. As Pufendorf has shown, the right of property by its very nature does not extend beyond the life of the owner; the moment a man dies, his goods cease to belong to him. To lay down the conditions under which he may dispose of them is therefore not so much to alter his right, as it might appear, as in fact to extend it.

In general, although only the Sovereign may establish the laws that regulate how individuals may dispose of their own goods, the spirit of these laws, which the government should follow in applying them, is that property should pass from father to son and from relative to relative, leaving the family and being sold off as little as possible. There is an evident reason for this in favor of the children. The right of property would be quite useless to them if their father left them nothing; and since they have often contributed by their own labor to building up their father's wealth, they are in their own right his partners in the ownership of it. But there is another reason, more remote though no less important: nothing is more damaging to morals and to the Republic than the constant shifting of rank and fortune among citizens. Such upheavals are both the evidence and the source of a thousand disorders; they overturn and confuse everything. Those who were raised for one way of life find themselves destined for another, and neither those who rise nor those who fall are able to adopt the rules of conduct or acquire the qualifications their new condition requires, still less to carry out the duties it entails. I now turn to the subject of public finance.

If the people governed itself directly, with no intermediary between the administration of the State and the citizens, they would need only to assess themselves from time to time, in proportion to public needs and to the means of each individual. And since all of them would keep a close eye on how such assessments were collected and spent, no fraud or abuse could creep into their management. The State would never fall into debt, nor would the people be overburdened with taxes; or at least, knowing how the money would be used would console them for the severity of the tax. But things cannot be carried on in this way. However small a State may be, a civil society is always too populous to be governed directly by all its members. Public money must pass through the hands of rulers, all of whom have, alongside the interests of the State, their own private interests, and these are not the last to be heeded. The people, for its part, noticing the greed and absurd extravagance of its rulers more readily than the public needs, grumbles at being stripped of necessities to supply others with luxuries; and once these complaints have reached a certain pitch of bitterness, even the most upright administration will find it impossible to restore confidence. In that situation, voluntary contributions bring in nothing, and forced contributions are illegitimate. This cruel choice, between letting the State perish and violating the sacred right of property on which it rests, is the great difficulty of a just and prudent economy.

The first step the founder of a republic ought to take, once the laws are established, is to set aside a sufficient fund for the upkeep of the magistrates and other officials and for other public expenses. If this fund consists of money, it is called the *aerarium* or fisc, that is, the public treasury; if it consists of land, it is called the public domain. For obvious reasons, the latter is much to be preferred. Anyone who has reflected on the matter must share the view of Bodin, who regards the public domain as the most honorable and reliable means of providing for the needs of the State. It is worth noting, too, that Romulus, in dividing up the land, made it his first concern to set aside a third of it for the use of the State. I admit it is not impossible for the yield of the public domain to dwindle to nothing if it is badly managed; but bad management is not of the essence of public domains.

Before any use is made of this fund, it should be assigned or accepted by an assembly of the people, or of the estates of the country, which should determine its future purpose. After this solemn act, which makes such funds inalienable, their very nature is in a sense transformed, and the revenues become so sacred that to misapply them, or divert them from the purpose for which they were intended, is not merely the most shameful kind of theft but outright treason. It reflects great discredit on Rome that the honesty of Cato the Censor was considered so very remarkable, and that the emperor Galba, rewarding a singer's talent with a few crowns, thought it necessary to point out that the money came from his own private purse and not from the State's. But if we find few Galbas, where are we to look for a Cato? Once vice is no longer a disgrace, what leaders will be scrupulous enough to keep their hands off the public revenues left to their discretion? And what leaders will avoid, in time, deceiving even themselves, by pretending that their own costly and scandalous extravagance is the glory of the State, and that the means of extending their own authority are the means of increasing its power? It is especially in this delicate area of administration that virtue is the only effective instrument, and the integrity of the magistrate the only real check on his greed. Ledgers and audits, rather than exposing fraud, only conceal it, for prudence is never as quick to devise new precautions as dishonesty is to evade them. Never mind account books and papers, then; place the management of finance in honest hands. That is the only way to have it conducted faithfully.

Once public funds have been established, the rulers of the State become their administrators by right, for this administration is a part of government that is always essential, though not always equally so. Its importance grows as other resources decline; and it may fairly be said that a government has reached the last stage of corruption when money is the only sinew it has left. Since every government constantly tends toward slackness, this alone is enough to show why no State can survive unless its revenues continually increase.

The first sense that such an increase is needed is also the first sign of internal disorder in the State. The prudent administrator, while seeking means to meet the present need, will spare no effort to discover the distant cause of the new demand, just as a sailor who finds water gaining on his ship does not neglect, while he works the pumps, to locate and stop the leak.

From this follows the most important rule in the administration of finance: to take more pains to prevent needs than to increase revenues. However diligent one may be, relief that comes after the harm, and more slowly than it, always leaves some damage behind. While a remedy is being found for one ill, another begins to make itself felt, and the remedies themselves create new difficulties, until at last the nation is mired in debt and the people oppressed, while the government loses its influence and can accomplish very little with a great deal of money. I imagine it was through recognizing this rule that the governments of antiquity achieved such wonders, doing more with their thrift than ours do with all their treasure; and perhaps this is the origin of the common meaning of the word *economy*, which signifies the prudent management of what one has rather than ways of acquiring what one lacks.

Apart from the public domain, however, which benefits the State in proportion to the integrity of those who govern, anyone well acquainted with the full power of general administration, especially when it confines itself to legitimate methods, would be astonished at the resources rulers have at their disposal for guarding against public needs without encroaching on the goods of individuals. Since they control the entire commerce of the State, nothing is easier for them than to steer it into channels that provide for every need without seeming to interfere. The distribution of provisions, money, and goods in just proportions, according to time and place, is the true secret of finance and the source of wealth, provided that those who manage it have foresight enough to accept an apparent loss in the present in order to secure, in reality, immense profits in the future. When we see a government paying bounties on the export of grain in times of plenty, and on its import in times of scarcity, instead of collecting duties on it, we need to have such facts before our very eyes to believe them. Had they happened in ancient times, we would dismiss them as idle tales. Suppose that, to prevent shortages in bad years, someone proposed establishing public granaries: in most countries, would not the upkeep of so useful an institution become an excuse for new taxes? In Geneva, such granaries, established and maintained by a prudent administration, are a public resource in bad years and the State's principal source of revenue at all times. *Alit et ditat*, "It feeds and it enriches," reads the inscription on the front of the building, and rightly so. In setting out here the economic system of a good government, I have often turned my eyes to that of this Republic, glad to find in my own country an example of the wisdom and happiness I should like to see prevail in every other.

If we ask how the needs of a State grow, we shall generally find that, like the wants of individuals, they arise less from any real necessity than from the multiplication of useless desires, and that expenses are often increased merely to provide a pretext for raising revenue. The State would therefore sometimes gain by not being rich, and apparent wealth is in reality more burdensome than poverty itself would be. Rulers may indeed hope to keep their peoples in tighter dependence by giving back with one hand what they take with the other. This was in fact Joseph's policy toward the Egyptians, when he stored up grain in the years of plenty and sold it back to them in the years of famine, until they had surrendered their money, their livestock, their land, and finally themselves to Pharaoh. But this political sophistry is all the more ruinous to the State because the money never returns to the hands it left. Such principles only enrich the idle at the expense of the industrious.

The desire for conquest is one of the most obvious and dangerous causes of this growth. Often springing from a different kind of ambition than the one it appears to proclaim, this desire is not always what it seems. Its real motive is less the professed wish to enlarge the nation than a hidden wish to increase the rulers' authority at home, both by enlarging the army and by the distraction that the objects of war create in the minds of citizens.

It is at least certain that no peoples are as oppressed and wretched as conquering nations, and that their successes only add to their misery. Even if history did not teach us this, reason alone would tell us that the larger a State grows, the heavier and more burdensome its expenses become in proportion. Every province must contribute its share to the general cost of government, and in addition must bear the expense of its own administration, which is as great as if it were truly independent. Add to this that great fortunes are always made in one place and spent in another. Production soon ceases to keep pace with consumption, and an entire country is impoverished merely to enrich a single city.

Another source of growing public needs, which depends on the one just described, is this. A time may come when citizens, no longer regarding themselves as having a stake in the common cause, cease to be the defenders of their country, and magistrates prefer commanding mercenaries to commanding free men, if for no other reason than that, when the moment comes, they can use the mercenaries to subdue the free men. Such was the condition of Rome toward the end of the Republic and under the emperors. All the victories of the early Romans, like those of Alexander, had been won by brave citizens who were ready, when needed, to give their blood in their country's service, but who would never sell it. Only at the siege of Veii did the practice of paying the Roman infantry begin. Marius, in the Jugurthine War, dishonored the legions by bringing in freedmen, vagrants, and other mercenaries. Tyrants, the enemies of the very people they were bound to make happy, maintained standing armies, ostensibly to resist foreign enemies but in reality to enslave their fellow countrymen. To raise such troops, men had to be taken from the land; the loss of their labor reduced the supply of food, and the cost of maintaining them brought in taxes that drove up prices. This first disorder provoked discontent among the people; to suppress it, the army had to be enlarged, and the people's misery deepened accordingly; and their growing despair led to still further enlargement of the cause in order to guard against its effects. The mercenaries, meanwhile, whose worth we may judge by the price at which they sold themselves, were proud of their own baseness and despised the laws that protected them, as well as the fellow citizens whose bread they ate. They thought it a greater honor to be Caesar's henchmen than defenders of Rome. Given over to blind obedience, they always held their swords at the throats of their fellow citizens, ready for a general slaughter at the first signal. It would not be hard to show that this was one of the chief causes of the fall of the Roman Empire.

The invention of artillery and fortifications has forced the princes of Europe in modern times to return to the use of standing armies to garrison their towns. But however legitimate their motives, it is to be feared that the effect may prove no less fatal. There is no better reason now than in the past for stripping the countryside of people to form armies and garrisons, nor should the people be oppressed to support them. In short, these dangerous institutions have multiplied so rapidly in recent years in this part of the world that they plainly threaten to depopulate Europe and, sooner or later, to ruin its inhabitants.

Be that as it may, it should be understood that such institutions necessarily overturn the true economic system, which draws the State's principal revenue from the public domain, and leave only the troublesome resource of subsidies and taxes, which remains to be discussed.

It should be remembered that the foundation of the social compact is property, and its first condition is that everyone be maintained in the peaceful possession of what belongs to him. It is true that, by the same agreement, everyone binds himself, at least tacitly, to be assessed for the public needs. But since this commitment cannot override the fundamental law, and presupposes that the need is clearly recognized by all who contribute to meeting it, such an assessment, to be lawful, must plainly be voluntary. It must depend not on a particular will, as if the consent of each individual were required and each gave only what he pleased, but on a general will, decided by majority vote and based on a proportional rate that leaves nothing arbitrary in the levying of the tax.

That taxes can be legitimately established only with the consent of the people or its representatives is a truth acknowledged by every philosopher and jurist of any standing on questions of public right, Bodin included. If some have laid down rules that seem to contradict it, their particular motives are easy enough to see, and they attach so many conditions and restrictions that the argument comes, in the end, to the same thing. As far as right is concerned, it makes no difference whether the people has the power to refuse or the Sovereign has no right to demand; and if the question concerns only power, there is no point in asking whether it is legitimate. Contributions levied on the people are of two kinds: real taxes, levied on commodities, and personal taxes, paid by the head. Both are called taxes or subsidies: when the people fixes the sum to be paid, it is called a subsidy; when it grants the proceeds of a levy, it is called a tax. In Montesquieu's *The Spirit of the Laws* we are told that a head tax is best suited to slavery, and a tax on goods most in keeping with liberty. This would be indisputable if everyone's circumstances were equal; otherwise nothing could be more disproportionate than such a tax, and the spirit of liberty consists precisely in observing exact proportions. But if a tax by head were exactly proportioned to the circumstances of individuals, as what is called the capitation in France might be, it would be the most equitable and therefore the most fitting for free men.

These proportions seem at first very easy to determine, because, being tied to each person's position in the world, they are always matters of public knowledge. But due regard is seldom paid to all the factors that should enter into such a calculation, even setting aside the deceptions that arise from greed, fraud, and self-interest. In the first place, we must consider the relation of quantities, according to which, all else being equal, a person who has ten times as much property as another ought to pay ten times as much to the State. Second, we must consider the relation of use, that is, the distinction between necessities and superfluities. A person who possesses only the ordinary necessities of life should pay nothing at all, while the tax on someone who has superfluities may justly extend to everything he owns beyond bare necessities. To this he may object that, when his rank is taken into account, what may be superfluous to a man of inferior station is necessary for him. But this is false. A grandee has two legs, just as a cowherd does, and like the cowherd he has only one belly. Moreover, these supposed necessities are so little necessary to his rank that if he gave them up for some worthy cause, he would only be held in greater honor. The common people would be ready to adore a minister who walked to the Council on foot because he had sold his carriages to meet some urgent need of the state. Finally, the law commands no one to live in splendor, and decorum is no argument against right.

A third relation is never taken into account, although it ought to be the chief consideration: the advantage each person draws from the social union. That union provides powerful protection for the immense possessions of the rich, while it scarcely allows the poor man to keep peaceful possession of the cottage he has built with his own hands. Are not all the advantages of society for the rich and powerful? Are not all the profitable offices in their hands? Are not all privileges and exemptions reserved for them alone? Is not public authority always on their side? If a man of standing robs his creditors or commits some other fraud, is he not always certain to go unpunished? Are not the beatings, the acts of violence, the assassinations, and even the murders committed by the great hushed up within a few months and then forgotten entirely? But let a great man himself be robbed or insulted, and the whole police force is at once set in motion, and woe even to innocent people who happen to fall under suspicion. If he must travel along a dangerous road, the whole countryside takes up arms to escort him. If the axle of his light carriage breaks, everyone rushes to help. If there is noise outside his door, he says a single word and silence falls. If the crowd jostles him, he waves his hand and everyone makes way. If his coach meets a farm wagon on the road, his servants stand ready to beat the driver's brains out. Better that fifty honest people on foot, going quietly about their business, be knocked on the head than that one idle fop be delayed in his coach. Yet all this deference costs him not a single farthing, the smallest of coins. It is the rich man's right, not something he buys with his wealth. How different it is for the poor man! The more humanity owes him, the more society refuses him. Every door is shut to him, even when he has a right to have it opened, and if he ever obtains justice, it is with far more difficulty than others obtain favors. When the militia is to be raised or the highway repaired, he is always first in line; he always carries the burden from which his richer neighbor has enough influence to be excused. At the slightest misfortune, everyone shuns him. If his cart overturns in the road, far from receiving help, he is lucky not to be horsewhipped by the insolent footmen of some young duke. In short, all free assistance is denied to the poor at the very moment they need it, simply because they cannot pay for it. I regard any poor man as utterly ruined if he has the misfortune to possess an honest heart, a beautiful daughter, and a powerful neighbor.

Another fact, no less important, is that the losses of the poor are much harder to repair than those of the rich, and that acquiring anything grows harder in proportion as one needs it more. "Nothing comes from nothing" holds as true in life as in physics. Money is the seed of money, and the first guinea is sometimes harder to earn than the second million. Add to this that whatever the poor pay is lost to them forever, and either stays in the hands of the rich or flows back into them. And since the entire proceeds of taxation must sooner or later pass to those who share in government, or to their dependents, these people always have a tangible interest in raising taxes, even though they pay their own share.

The terms of the social compact between these two classes of men can be summed up in a few words: "You need me, because I am rich and you are poor. Let us therefore come to an agreement. I will allow you the honor of serving me, on condition that you give me what little you have left, in return for the trouble I shall take in commanding you."

If we weigh all these considerations carefully together, we shall find that to levy taxes in a truly fair and proportionate manner, the tax should not be in simple proportion to the property of those who pay it, but in compound proportion, taking account at once of the difference in their conditions and of the surplus of their possessions. This very important and difficult calculation is performed every day by numbers of honest clerks who know their arithmetic, yet a Plato or a Montesquieu would not dare to attempt it without the greatest diffidence, or without praying to Heaven for understanding and integrity.

Another drawback of personal taxes is that they can be felt too keenly or collected too harshly. This does not, however, stop them from being frequently evaded, for it is far easier for people to escape a tax than for their possessions to do so.

Of all forms of taxation, the tax on land, known as "real" taxation because it falls on things rather than on persons, has always been considered the most advantageous in countries where more attention is paid to how much a tax will yield, and to the certainty of collecting it, than to sparing the people discomfort. Some have even claimed that the peasant must be burdened to rouse him from idleness, and that he would never work if he had no taxes to pay. But experience everywhere refutes this ridiculous notion. In England and Holland the farmer pays very little, and in China nothing at all, yet these are the countries where the land is best cultivated. On the other hand, in countries where the farmer is taxed in proportion to the yield of his fields, he leaves them uncultivated or harvests only enough for bare subsistence. To a man who loses the fruit of his labor, doing nothing is a kind of gain. Taxing industry is a very strange way of banishing idleness.

Taxes on land or grain, especially when excessive, produce two results so destructive that in the long run they cannot fail to depopulate and ruin every country where they are established. The first arises from the faulty circulation of coined money. Industry and commerce draw all the money of the countryside into the capital cities, and because the tax destroys whatever balance might otherwise exist between the farmer's needs and the price of his grain, money is always leaving the countryside and never returning. Thus the richer the city, the poorer the country. The proceeds of taxation pass from the hands of the prince or his financial officers into those of craftsmen and merchants, and the farmer, who receives only the smallest share, is at last exhausted by always paying the same amount while constantly receiving less. How could a human body survive if it had veins but no arteries, or if its arteries carried blood only to within four inches of the heart? The traveler Chardin tells us that in Persia the royal dues on goods are paid in kind, that is, in the goods themselves rather than in money. This custom, which Herodotus reports was already practiced in the same country long ago, up to the time of Darius, might prevent the evil I have been describing. But unless intendants, directors, commissioners, and warehousemen in Persia are a different breed of people from what they are elsewhere, I can hardly believe that even the smallest part of this produce ever reaches the king, or that the grain does not rot in every granary, or that most of the storehouses do not burn down.

The second harmful effect arises from an apparent advantage, which allows the evil to grow worse before anyone notices it. Grain is a commodity whose price is not raised by taxes in the country that produces it, and although it is absolutely necessary, its quantity can shrink without its price rising. As a result, many people die of hunger even while grain stays cheap, and the farmer bears the entire burden of a tax for which he cannot compensate himself through the price of his grain. We must be careful not to reason about a land tax in the same way as about duties on various kinds of merchandise. The effect of such duties is to raise prices, and they are paid by the buyers rather than the sellers. However heavy they may be, they remain voluntary: the merchant pays them only in proportion to the quantity he buys, and since he buys only in proportion to what he sells, he himself determines how the law applies to him. The farmer, by contrast, must pay his rent at fixed times whether he sells or not, and he cannot wait until he can get his own price for his goods. Even if he is not forced to sell simply to live, he must sell to pay the taxes, so that it is often the very weight of the tax that keeps the price of grain low.

It should further be noted that the resources of commerce and industry, far from making the tax more bearable through an abundance of money, only make it more burdensome. I shall not dwell on what is plainly evident: that although a larger or smaller quantity of money in a state may raise or lower its credit in the eyes of foreigners, it makes not the slightest difference to the real fortunes of its citizens and leaves their condition neither more nor less comfortable. But I must make two important observations. First, unless a state has surplus goods to sell and its abundance of money comes from foreign trade, only the trading cities feel that abundance, while the peasant merely grows poorer by comparison. Second, since the price of everything rises as money increases, taxes must rise in proportion, so that the farmer finds himself still more heavily burdened without having any greater means.

It should be observed that the tax on land is in reality a duty on its produce. Everyone agrees that nothing is so dangerous as a tax on grain paid by the purchaser. Why, then, do we fail to see that it is a hundred times worse when the duty is paid by the grower himself? Is this not an attack on the very substance of the state at its source? Is it not the most direct possible way of depopulating a country, and therefore, in the end, of ruining it? For the worst scarcity a nation can suffer is a scarcity of people.

Only the true statesman can rise above the purely financial object when he imposes taxes. He alone can turn heavy burdens into useful regulations, and make the people even wonder whether such measures were not designed for the general good of the nation rather than merely to raise money.

Duties on the import of foreign goods that the inhabitants are fond of but the country does not need; on the export of domestic products that are not overly plentiful and that foreigners cannot do without; on the products of frivolous and excessively lucrative trades; on the import of all pure luxuries; and, in general, on all articles of luxury: such duties will achieve both ends at once. It is by taxes of this kind, which relieve the poor and shift the burden onto the rich, that we can prevent the continual growth of inequality of fortune, the subjection of so great a multitude of artisans and useless servants to the rich, the multiplication of idle people in our cities, and the depopulation of the countryside.

The value of any commodity and the duty laid on it should be so proportioned that the size of the possible profit does not tempt the greed of individuals too strongly toward fraud. To make smuggling difficult, the goods chosen for taxation should be those hardest to conceal. Duties should be paid by the consumer of the taxed article rather than by the seller, because the sheer amount of duty the seller would have to pay would expose him to greater temptations and give him more opportunities for fraud. This is the constant practice in China, a country where taxes are higher, and yet better paid, than anywhere else in the world. There the merchant pays no duty at all; the buyer alone bears the whole charge, without complaint or unrest, because the necessities of life, such as rice and grain, are entirely exempt from taxation, so that the common people are not oppressed and the duty falls only on those who are well off. Precautions against smuggling should be prompted less by the fear that it will occur than by the care a government ought to take to protect individuals from being lured by illegitimate profits, which first make them bad citizens and soon afterward turn them into dishonest men.

Heavy taxes should be laid on servants in livery, the uniformed household staff of great families; on equipages, the carriages with their horses and attendants; on rich furniture and fine clothes; on spacious courtyards and gardens; on public entertainments of every kind; on useless professions such as dancers, singers, and actors; in a word, on that whole multitude of objects of luxury, amusement, and idleness that strike everyone's eyes and can scarcely be hidden, since their entire purpose is to be seen, and without being seen they would be useless. We need not fear that the yield of these taxes will be uncertain merely because they fall on things that are not absolutely necessary. Those who imagine that people, once seduced by luxury, can ever give it up know very little of human nature. Such people would a hundred times sooner give up the common necessities of life, and would much rather die of hunger than of shame. The added expense becomes only one more reason for keeping up these things, since the vanity of appearing wealthy profits both from the price of the article and from the cost of the tax. As long as there are rich people in the world, they will want to distinguish themselves from the poor, and the state can devise no revenue less burdensome or more certain than one drawn from this very distinction.

For the same reason, industry would have nothing to fear from an economic system that increased public revenue, encouraged agriculture by relieving the farmer, and gradually tended to bring all fortunes closer to that middle condition which constitutes the true strength of the state. These taxes, I admit, might bring certain fashionable articles of dress and amusement to an early end, but only to replace them with others, from which the craftsman would profit and the treasury suffer no loss. In short, suppose the guiding spirit of government were always to tax only the superfluities of the rich. One of two things must then happen. Either the rich would turn their superfluous spending toward useful ends that would benefit the state, in which case taxation would have the effect of the best sumptuary laws, those laws that restrain private extravagance in dress, food, and display. The state's expenses would then necessarily shrink along with those of individuals, and the treasury would lose less in revenue than it gained by having fewer expenses to meet. Or, if the rich did not become less extravagant, the treasury would draw from the taxes on their spending resources enough to meet the needs of the state. In the first case, the treasury would be richer by what it saved in having less use for its money; in the second, it would be enriched by the useless spending of private persons.

To all this we may add a very important distinction in matters of political right, one to which governments, always intent on doing everything themselves, ought to pay close attention. It has been observed that personal taxes and duties on the necessities of life, because they directly infringe on the right of property, and therefore on the true foundation of political society, are always liable to have dangerous consequences unless they are established with the express consent of the people or of its representatives. The case is different with articles we can deny ourselves. Since the individual is under no absolute obligation to pay, his contribution may be counted as voluntary, and the particular consent of each contributor then takes the place of the general consent of the whole people. For why should a people oppose a tax that falls only on those who choose to pay it? It seems certain to me that anything not forbidden by law or contrary to morality, yet which the government may prohibit, may also be permitted on payment of a certain duty. If the government may prohibit the use of coaches, for example, it may certainly impose a tax on them, and this is a prudent and useful way of discouraging their use without forbidding it outright. In such a case, the tax may be regarded as a kind of fine, whose proceeds compensate for the abuse it punishes.

It may perhaps be objected that those whom Bodin calls "impostors," by which he means those who impose or devise taxes, belong to the class of the rich and will hardly spare themselves in order to relieve the poor. But this is entirely beside the point. If, in every nation, those to whom the sovereign entrusts the government of the people were, by their very position, its enemies, it would not be worth asking what they ought to do to make the people happy.
